This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the <term>public domain</term> <note>A legal status where a work is no longer protected by copyright and is freely available for use by anyone.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other <term>marginalia</term> <note>Handwritten notes or marks made in the margins of a book by previous readers or owners.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

* **Make non-commercial use of the files** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

* **Refrain from automated querying** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on <term>machine translation</term> <note>The use of software to translate text from one language to another.</note>, <term>optical character recognition</term> <note>Commonly known as OCR; the process of converting images of typed or handwritten text into machine-encoded text.</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

* **Maintain attribution** The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

* **Keep it legal** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

<meta>This page is the Preface of Gustav Theodor Fechner's 1877 work, following the title page and legal notices. Fechner is addressing the intense academic criticism his 1860 foundational work received and explaining the purpose of this new volume.</meta>

# Preface.

Well then, since my *Elements of Psychophysics*, <note>original title: "Elemente der Psychophysik." This 1860 work is considered the founding text of experimental psychology, introducing "Fechner's Law" regarding stimulus and perception.</note> which appeared in the year 1860, can be considered "dismissed"—except for a few points that perhaps could not be ignored—due to the attacks that have since come from all sides, and because the views offered by my opponents as replacements are so scattered that they defy easy oversight or comparison, I am sharing those views in the following pages. I provide them in such a compilation that a choice may be made between them for the purpose of a new foundation <term>Grundlegung<gloss>The fundamental basis or theoretical groundwork for a field of study.</gloss></term> of psychophysics. However, at the same time, foreseeing that choosing between such diverse views—which contradict one another no less than they do that first attempt of mine—will be difficult, I seek to encourage the consideration of whether it might not ultimately be preferable to return to my own views; and to that end, I turn my attention in every direction. That is, in short—expressed somewhat jokingly, yet meant quite seriously—the content and intent of this writing. The following introduction speaks to this in more detail, while the summary <note>original: "Resumé."</note> briefly condenses the content and results; beyond that, I find only a few external matters regarding this work to note here.

->9 0 5 9 2<-

<vocab>Psychophysics, Elements of Psychophysics, Preface, Summary, Foundation</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Gustav Fechner ironicaly acknowledges the widespread criticism of his 1860 work and explains that he has compiled his opponents' contradictory views to demonstrate why his original theories of psychophysics remain the most viable.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Theodor Fechner, psychophysics, scientific debate, Elements of Psychophysics, experimental psychology, 19th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues a sentence from the previous page, concluding a thought about the structural focus of the manuscript.</meta>

...or must have a *central focal point* in view. Following several sections that introduce the work in general, I have placed the consideration of the most urgent objections first. I have done this so that, in later sections, I might engage with my opponents regarding their own views (insofar as what was stated in the 4th section did not already seem sufficient) and to present the current experimental status of *Weber’s Law*. <note>Named after Ernst Heinrich Weber, this law describes the relationship between the physical intensity of a stimulus and the perceived change in that intensity.</note>

*Round brackets* within literal citations indicate insertions made by the original authors themselves; *square* brackets indicate those inserted by me. — With *p.*, I refer to page numbers in the writings of other authors; with *S.* <note>The author uses "S." for "Seite," the German word for page.</note>, I refer to page numbers within this work itself. — Regarding page references appearing with an author’s name but without the title of their specific book or treatise, please see the remark on *page 14.* — I refer to my own work, "*Elements of Psychophysics*," in citations as "*Elem.*" — The term "*Psychophysical*" <term>Psychophysics: The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations they produce.</term> has been abbreviated in the text several times as *ps.-ph.*, and "*Sensation*" has been abbreviated as *E.* <note>Original: "Empfindung."</note>

<meta>This page begins the main text of Gustav Theodor Fechner’s "In Matters of Psychophysics," following the Table of Contents. It introduces the fundamental problem Fechner seeks to solve: how to mathematically measure the intensity of human sensation, which was previously thought to be impossible to quantify.</meta>

Fechner, In Matters of Psychophysics.

## I. Introduction.

It was previously generally assumed that although differences in the intensity of sensations can be identified through internal perception, there is fundamentally no actual psychological measure—specifically, one that would allow us to say a sensation is so many times stronger than another. In the meantime, in my Elements of <term>Psychophysics</term> <note>Psychophysics: The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations and perceptions they evoke.</note> (Vol. I, p. 54 and more generally Vol. II, p. 191), I have sought to show that while it is impossible to obtain such a measure through the internal superposition of sensations in a way similar to how one might externally measure a piece of cloth with a yardstick, such a measure can indeed be established based on the relationship between the intensity of the sensation and the intensity of the <term>stimulus</term> <note>German: "Reiz"; the physical energy (like light or sound) that causes a sensory response.</note> that triggers the sensation. In this way, internal sensation can be measured by an external yardstick. This would even be quite simple if we could assume the intensity of sensation was directly proportional to the intensity of the stimulus, but closer investigation shows this to be impossible; nevertheless, it is sufficient to know the dependency relationship between the two in order to determine the magnitude of the sensation based on the measured magnitude of the stimulus. This is the <term>pivot of the matter</term> <note>original Latin: "Cardo rei"</note> for obtaining a psychological measure.

I have discussed the principles and experimental rules that can lead to the determination of this dependency relationship, and I have established laws and formulas under which this relationship can be categorized, based partly on the experiments of others and partly on my own research conducted in this regard.

Now, the consideration easily presents itself that the external stimulus which triggers a sensation does not do so directly, but rather—

<summary>Fechner introduces his groundbreaking theory that mental sensations can be measured mathematically by looking at their relationship to physical stimuli. He argues that even if we cannot "stack" feelings like we measure cloth, we can use the physical intensity of a stimulus as an external yardstick for the mind.</summary>

<keywords>psychophysics, stimulus, sensation, measurement, Gustav Theodor Fechner, psychological measure, mathematical formulas</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s introductory argument from page 1, specifically explaining how a physical stimulus triggers a "psychophysical" process in the nervous system, which then leads to a conscious sensation. It moves toward his formal definition of Psychophysics.</meta>

...acts by triggering a physical activity—which I call **psychophysical**—within our nervous system, upon which the mental activity of sensation is directly dependent. If one could determine the magnitude of this psychophysical activity (triggered by the stimulus) as proportional to the magnitude of the stimulus itself, or find any other valid relationship between the two besides simple proportionality, then those laws and formulas would, in the first case, apply directly to the relationship of dependency between sensation and psychophysical activity. In the second case, they would at least allow us to infer it. Thus, regardless of the measurement of sensation itself, this would provide an important contribution—indeed, a primary <term>starting point</term> <note>German: "Angriffspunct"; literally a "point of attack" or a place to begin a scientific investigation.</note> for an exact doctrine concerning the immediate relationships between body and soul from a quantitative perspective. This would be all the more significant and profound for psychology on one hand, physiology on the other, and even for philosophy itself, if those laws and formulas governing sensory perception in relation to the underlying physical activity were found to extend even higher. No matter how high a mental activity may rise, the intensity of <term>conscious activity</term> <note>German: "Bewusstseinsthätigkeit"; the energy or effort the mind exerts when we are aware of something.</note> used within it—much like the clarity of the images that even the highest levels of consciousness play with—provides a starting point for the question of the mathematical relationships between the mental and physical sides. I have already dealt with this to some extent regarding the phenomena of memory and attention.

Having said all this, I define "Psychophysics" in general as an exact doctrine of the relationships—primarily **quantitative relationships***)—between the mental (psychic) and material (physical) sides of existence. It is "exact" insofar as it is based on observation, experiment, and the mathematical connection of facts; in short, it follows the same method as other disciplines considered "exact" <note>By "exact," Fechner means the "hard sciences" of his day, like physics and chemistry, which relied on repeatable experiments and math.</note>. To be sure, its results have not yet reached the same degree of certainty as those of...

---

*) The relationships between the quality of mental phenomena and the form of physical phenomena are not fundamentally excluded from psychophysics, though they have been little investigated thus far. <margin>Fechner clarifies here that while he focuses on *how much* we feel (quantity), he doesn't ignore *what* we feel (quality), though it is harder to measure.</margin>

<summary>Fechner explains that stimuli trigger "psychophysical" activities in the nerves which then create sensations, providing a mathematical bridge between the physical and mental worlds. He defines Psychophysics as a rigorous science that uses the methods of physics to study the relationship between the soul and the body.</summary>

<keywords>psychophysical activity, stimulus and sensation, body and soul, definition of Psychophysics, quantitative relationships, exact science, consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s introduction, comparing the rigor of psychophysics to other natural sciences and defining its two primary branches: outer and inner psychophysics. It also begins a discussion on the historical reception of his "Principle of Psychic Measurement" and his disagreement with the physiologist Ewald Hering.</meta>

...of mechanics, physics, and chemistry—as the following will only too clearly prove—and it may even rank below physiology in this respect. This is due to the great difficulties unique to it, which, rather than excluding an exact method, instead challenge all its strengths to overcome them; meanwhile, in the philosophical treatment of the same field, such a method has been entirely disregarded until now.

Psychophysics, thus understood, is divided into two main parts, which I distinguish as *outer* and *inner psychophysics*. The first deals with the relationships between mental phenomena and the *external triggers* of those phenomena, called <term>stimuli</term> <note>original: "Reizen"; the physical energy (like light or sound) that acts upon a sensory organ.</note>. The other deals with the relationships of mental phenomena to the *internal*, so-called *psychophysical, bodily activities* that underlie them *directly*. The first is based primarily on experiments; the other draws conclusions from the first while incorporating anatomical, physiological, and psychological facts.

The principles, laws, and formulas I developed from these most general perspectives in the "*Elements*" <note>original: "Elementen"; referring to Fechner’s 1860 book *Elemente der Psychophysik*, which founded the field of experimental psychology.</note> have generally met the following fate. They have attracted attention in wide circles, and—apart from many philosophers who find psychophysics fundamentally unappealing—it has been favorably noted that an attempt was made at all to approach the problem of the relationship between <term>body and soul</term> in a more exact way than before. Furthermore, I am not aware that the <term>principle of psychic measurement</term> <note>The idea that mental experiences (sensations) can be measured mathematically just as physical objects can.</note> I established—based on the functional relationship between stimulus and sensation, and more deeply between psychophysical activity and sensation—has been contradicted by those who have since engaged in such research. In fact, even those who do not agree, or only partially agree, with the laws and formulas I represent, still apply the same principle when establishing their own measurement formulas.*) And since the possibility of such...

---

*) An exception to this might be *Hering* <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a prominent physiologist who often challenged Fechner’s and Helmholtz’s theories on vision and sensation.</note>; for although he has not yet directly raised an objection to the principle of *psychic measurement* I established, such an objection seems to me to be present in his...

<sig>1 *</sig>

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between 'outer psychophysics' (stimulus vs. sensation) and 'inner psychophysics' (internal neural activity vs. sensation), while defending his mathematical approach against philosophical critics.</summary>
<keywords>outer psychophysics, inner psychophysics, stimuli, psychophysical activity, principle of psychic measurement, body-soul relationship, Ewald Hering, Elements of Psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his work, specifically addressing the reception of his "Principle of Psychic Measurement." He acknowledges that while the terminology of psychophysics has been adopted, the specific laws and formulas he proposed are under heavy fire from the leading scientific minds of his day.</meta>

...of measurement the possibility of psychophysics itself is founded, the name of this science first originated from it and has since come into fairly general use, and mathematical psychology is offered the psychic measure it previously lacked—then I could already be satisfied if, of my entire psychophysics, only this principle with the related processing of experimental methods could be maintained; though this would by no means preserve the whole system.

Granted the validity of the <term>principle of psychic measurement</term> <note>Fechner’s mathematical method for quantifying the intensity of a mental sensation based on the physical intensity of a stimulus.</note> I established, the measurement itself takes on a different form and value depending on which laws and formulas one assumes for the functional relationship between stimulus and sensation in <term>outer psychophysics</term>. Likewise, the relationship between the mental and physical sides within ourselves appears differently depending on how one conceives the functional relationship between the two when transitioning in various ways from outer to inner psychophysics. In both regards, however, I must speak more of opposing than of concurring evaluations of the views, laws, theorems, and formulas I have set forth; indeed, objections have been raised from so many sides and from such diverse—partly theoretical, partly experimental—perspectives that, according to them, not one stone of my entire, laboriously erected psychophysical system would remain upon another, so to speak.

Aside from one objection by **Helmholtz** <note>Hermann von Helmholtz (1821–1894), a polymath who made fundamental contributions to the physics of vision and sound.</note>, which was already considered in an addendum at the conclusion of my *Elements*, more or less profound objections have become known to me from **Aubert**, **Mach**, **Bernstein**, **Plateau**, **Delboeuf**, **Brentano**, **Hering**, and **Langer**. The much-

---

[indirectly founded on a] fundamental conception of psychophysical conditions entirely different from mine, which he <note>Referring to Ewald Hering, a physiologist who challenged Fechner’s theories on color and light perception.</note> already expressed in the way he treated light sensation, although this cannot be seen clearly until the continuation of his psychophysical investigations, which he promised, appears. In philosophical writings, many a contradiction to my principle of psychic measurement may have been raised that has escaped me, because I have followed recent philosophical literature very little. In general, however, philosophical and psychophysical researchers do not yet coincide much.

<summary>Fechner acknowledges that while his term "psychophysics" is widely used, his specific mathematical formulas are under intense scrutiny from prominent scientists like Mach, Helmholtz, and Hering. He admits his system might be dismantled by these critics but remains focused on the fundamental validity of his measurement principle.</summary>
<keywords>psychophysics, psychic measurement, stimulus, sensation, outer psychophysics, inner psychophysics, scientific criticism, 19th-century psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion of the intense scientific opposition to his work. After mentioning Helmholtz on the previous page, he now turns to other major critics like Hering, Langer, Delboeuf, and Brentano, addressing their specific mathematical and philosophical objections to his law of measurement.</meta>

...the most multifaceted and perhaps most apparent [objections] certainly come from **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist who was Fechner’s most significant rival, particularly regarding theories of color vision and spatial perception.</note>, and they have not failed to make a great impression, partly due to the respectable authority of their author, partly due to their ingenuity, and partly due to the decisiveness with which they are expressed. According to these objections, the laws I established as fundamental are not only improbable *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; Latin for "from the beginning" or based on theoretical deduction rather than observation.</note>, but also incorrectly inferred from the facts; they are demonstrably false. Indeed, were they correct, they would establish a confused conception of the external world that contradicts rational <term>teleology</term> <note>The philosophical study of nature's goals or purposes; here, the idea that our senses are designed to give us a functional, "rational" understanding of reality.</note>. The matter, they say, must be handled fundamentally differently. **Langer** <note>Karl Langer (1819–1887), an Austrian anatomist.</note>, who concludes the series of opponents known to me, yields little to **Hering** in the abundance and sharpness of his objections; and **Delboeuf** <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896), a Belgian philosopher and psychologist who praised Fechner but proposed significant mathematical modifications to his work.</note>, while ultimately arriving at a <term>measurement formula</term> <note>The mathematical equation used to relate the intensity of a physical stimulus to the intensity of a mental sensation.</note> identical in form to mine, though entirely different in meaning, summarizes the opposition he raised in his first treatise (the *Étude*) in his third (the *Revue* p. 241) as follows:

> "The **Fechnerian Law** [measurement law] is untenable from a mathematical point of view. It carries absurd consequences with it, and the manner in which it is established (*établie* <note>French: "established."</note>) grants the mind no clear idea of what the quantity of a sensation can be, and therefore also not how it can be represented by a number," etc.

After all this, I can only be grateful to my main opponents and hereby express my thanks to them, as they have generally introduced their opposition to my doctrine with benevolent expressions. In particular, I feel obliged to **Brentano** <note>Franz Brentano (1838–1917), a philosopher and psychologist known for his work on "intentionality" and his critique of Fechner's psychophysical methods.</note> and **Hering** for the fact that, in the correspondence I conducted with them regarding their objections, they met my endeavor to keep the discussion within the limits of purely scientific controversy in the most friendly manner.

But how do I now position myself against this entire flood of objections, which in part seem factually devastating to the doctrine I represent? — I do not believe that things are as bad for the doctrine as they might seem at first glance. Indeed, to counter the decisiveness of my opponents with a decisiveness of my own, however modest, I maintain this doctrine in all fundamental points, after considering all objections known to me, insofar as I see it as the most probable,

<summary>Fechner acknowledges the "flood" of sharp criticisms from prominent scientists like Hering and Delboeuf, who claim his laws are mathematically and logically flawed. Despite these attacks, Fechner politely thanks his critics for their civil discourse while remaining firm in his belief that his psychophysical system remains the most probable explanation of the facts.</summary>

<keywords>Ewald Hering, Joseph Delboeuf, Franz Brentano, Fechner's Law, measurement formula, teleology, scientific controversy, psychophysics, mathematical psychology</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical theories against his critics. He transitions from naming specific opponents like Delboeuf to explaining his confidence in his own system despite the ongoing controversy.</meta>

...that has been established so far, and at the same time I hold that this relationship will not change in the future. The great difficulties of the doctrine prevent me from expressing myself even more decisively; but I find these difficulties even greater in every alternative interpretation of the fundamental points than in my own. My confidence in my own view has grown because of this; this does not prevent me from acknowledging the cleverness of some objections—though many others could have been spared for both the authors and myself with a bit of reflection—and from making concessions in certain specific regards.

But let us assume for a moment that my doctrine—against which almost all the conflict has been directed as the starting point—were truly disposed of by the objections raised against it. What would be the consequence? The opponents would then fight each other all the more, since their own doctrines contradict one another almost more than they do mine, from which almost all of them have only deviated more or less in different directions. One should read the fourth section. Thus, <term>Psychophysics</term> <note>The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations, founded by Fechner.</note> would run the risk of simply sharing in the discord of philosophy, just after having boasted of being an exact science. Now, it would be wonderful if this new conflict became unnecessary because the first one proved to be superfluous; that is, if it could be shown that there is no sufficient reason to abandon the fundamental views upon which the initial structure of the doctrine was built.

This work is written with this goal in mind, and in this spirit, I take up the dispute with my opponents. However, I refrain from returning to the conflict regarding the most general points of the doctrine of the relationship between <term>body and soul</term> <note>The "mind-body problem," or the question of how physical matter relates to mental experience.</note>, where I find myself at odds with this or that philosophical school, and mostly with the prevailing view. People are inclined to refuse to admit <term>animation</term> <note>original: "Beseelung"; the presence of a soul or consciousness. Fechner famously advocated for "panpsychism," the idea that all things, including plants and the Earth, have a soul.</note> beyond humans and animals. From a certain point onward, people detach mental activities from their physical foundation. They believe the soul resides in a single, simple point within the brain. I hold a different view on all these points; but I believe I have already said enough about this in several chapters of my *Elements* <note>Refers to his seminal 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics).</note> and my philosophical writings—enough to prove how far my views differ from materialistic and irreligious [views]—

<summary>Fechner argues that his psychophysical system remains the most stable foundation for the science, suggesting that his critics' alternative theories conflict with each other more than with his own. He also distances his work from purely materialistic views while maintaining his unique stance on the soul's presence in nature.</summary>

<keywords>Psychophysics, body and soul, animation, brain, Elements of Psychophysics, philosophy of science</keywords>

...to deviate. The scope and formation of <term>psychophysics</term> <note>The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations.</note> is by no means independent of the views on these points; on the contrary. ## II. Psychophysical laws and formulas established by me, which will be the main focus of this defense, alongside the primary objections raised against them.

1) The **Law of the Threshold** (referred to less aptly in **Elements** <note>Fechner refers to his 1860 book *Elemente der Psychophysik*.</note> I. 238 as the **Fact of the Threshold**) states that a sensation, as well as a difference between sensations, does not first become imperceptible to consciousness when the stimulus or stimulus-difference upon which they depend has dropped to a zero-value of effect, but rather vanishes for consciousness at a finite value. This finite value of the stimulus or stimulus-difference, which must be exceeded for a sensation or a discrimination of sensations to take place, I call the **stimulus threshold** and the **difference threshold**, respectively. However, since according to the laws that follow, it is not so much a matter of a ge-

<summary>Fechner lists the specific critics he is responding to and introduces his "Law of the Threshold," which argues that stimuli must reach a certain minimum intensity to be consciously perceived.</summary>
<keywords>Aubert, Delboeuf, Brentano, Hering, Law of the Threshold, stimulus threshold, difference threshold, Elements of Psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s systematic definitions of the fundamental laws of psychophysics. It completes his explanation of the "Threshold," specifically distinguishing between absolute and relative differences, and introduces his two most famous formulations: Weber's Law and the Measurement Law of Sensation.</meta>

...certain absolute difference between stimuli. Rather, a relative difference—one standing in a certain relationship to the stimuli—or, to put it another way, a certain ratio of stimuli must be exceeded for a difference between the stimuli to be noticed. Because of this, instead of a **difference threshold**, one can also speak of a **ratio threshold** of stimuli. Now, where the stimulus, the relative stimulus difference, or the stimulus ratio (in the aforementioned sense) is at, above, or below the threshold, one also says that the corresponding simple sensation or sensation of difference is at, above, or below the threshold.

2) **Weber's Law** (represented in the *Elements* <note>original: *Elem.*, referring to Fechner’s 1860 foundational work *Elemente der Psychophysik* (*Elements of Psychophysics*).</note> Vol. I, p. 134 ff., and for smaller differences in Vol. II, p. 9 ff. by the **fundamental formula**), named so by me because it was first expressed with a certain degree of generality by E. H. Weber <note>Ernst Heinrich Weber (1795–1878), a German physician whose studies on touch led to the realization that we perceive relative rather than absolute changes in physical weight.</note>. It refers to the dependency of the differences, changes, or increases in a sensation upon those of the stimulus that triggers it. It states that the differences, changes, or increases in sensation remain equal not when the absolute, but when the relative differences, changes, or increases in the stimulus remain equal. By relative increase, we understand the absolute increase divided by the magnitude of the stimulus to which the increase is added. Mathematically, the following is connected with the previous law and can therefore be expressed equivalently as **Weber's Law**: The difference between two stimuli appears equal in sensation if the ratio of the stimuli remains the same, whether they both increase or decrease in absolute value, provided this occurs in the same ratio.

3) **Measurement Law of Sensation** (represented in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 12 ff. by the **measurement formula**), which can be derived mathematically from Weber's Law and the Law of the Threshold. It refers to the dependency of the magnitude of sensation itself on the magnitude of the stimulus and states: that the magnitude of sensation grows proportionally to the logarithm of the stimulus divided by its threshold value; or, if one takes the threshold value as the unit of stimulus, simply proportional to the logarithm of the stimulus upon which the sensation depends. If the stimulus falls below the threshold value, the sensation (according to the measurement formula) takes on negative values, which I interpret as unconscious.

<note>Terminology: Weber's Law, Fundamental Formula, Measurement Law of Sensation, Measurement Formula, Stimulus, Sensation, Threshold Value, E. H. Weber, Difference Threshold, Ratio Threshold</note>

<summary>This page defines the mathematical foundations of psychophysics, specifically Weber's Law regarding relative stimulus changes and Fechner's Measurement Law, which uses logarithms to describe the relationship between physical intensity and mental perception.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, Measurement Law of Sensation, stimulus, sensation, threshold, logarithm, E. H. Weber, psychophysics, fundamental formula</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic definitions of psychophysical laws, specifically expanding on the mathematical relationships between stimulus and sensation introduced on the previous page. It transitions from Weber's Law to Fechner's specific formulas for measuring differences in sensation.</meta>

4) **Law of Measurement for the Difference in Sensation** (found in *Elements* <note>original: *Elem.*, referring to Fechner’s 1860 foundational work *Elemente der Psychophysik* (*Elements of Psychophysics*).</note> Vol. II, p. 89 ff., represented by the **difference formula**), states, as a direct consequence of the previous law, that the measure of the difference between two sensations is proportional to the difference between the logarithmic measures of both sensations. Consequently—since the difference between the logarithms of two quantities is equal to the logarithm of the quotient of those two quantities—it is proportional to the logarithm of the quotient of the two stimuli upon which the sensations depend.

Now, it should be noted, and is discussed in more detail in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 82, that a difference between two sensations can exist without it actually being perceived as a difference. This occurs, for example, if the sensations are experienced by different people, or if they occur in the same person at such different times that the first is forgotten when the second occurs, or if attention is directed to only one of the two, or finally, if the difference between the two stimuli is too small. In order to actually emerge as a **perceived difference** or as a **sensation of difference** (which I also call a **contrast sensation**), special conditions must be met. These conditions need not be met for a mere difference between two sensations (referred to briefly as a **difference in sensation**), which provides the basis for the following law and the formula that expresses it.

5) **Law of Measurement for the Sensation of Difference or Contrast** (found in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 96 ff., represented by the **formula for the measurement of difference**), states that the magnitude or strength of the sensation of difference—insofar as it depends on the size relationship of the stimuli, with all other factors being equal—is proportional to the logarithm of a quotient. The numerator of this quotient is represented by the ratio of the two stimulus magnitudes being distinguished, while the denominator is represented by the <term>threshold value</term> <note>The point at which a stimulus is just strong enough to be noticed.</note> of this ratio; that is, the value at which the perception of the difference disappears and above which it becomes noticeable. In short, this is represented by the **ratio threshold**.

Generally, let the magnitude of a sensation be called $\gamma$, the stimulus magnitude on which it depends be called $\beta$, and the threshold value of the stimulus (the point at which the sensation first begins to become noticeable) be called $b$. Let $K$ and $k$ be constants, which depend on the magnitude of the sensation...

<summary>Fechner explains that a mathematical difference between sensations is not the same as a "perceived" difference, which requires exceeding a specific ratio threshold. He introduces the logarithmic formulas used to measure these "contrast sensations."</summary>
<keywords>Law of Measurement, difference formula, perceived difference, sensation of difference, contrast sensation, ratio threshold, stimulus magnitude, Psychophysics</keywords>

and are independent of the stimulus. Where it is necessary to distinguish between multiple sensations and stimuli, this shall be done using added primes <note>original: *Strichelchen*, refers to the use of tick marks or prime symbols like $\beta'$ or $\beta_1$.</note>. Furthermore, let the ratio of two stimuli be $\frac{\beta}{\beta_1} = \varphi$, the threshold value <term>threshold value (Schwellenwerth)<gloss>the minimum intensity a stimulus must reach to be perceived</gloss></term> of this stimulus ratio be $v$, and the magnitude of the sensation of difference be $u$; then the preceding laws are expressed in the following formulas:

1) **Threshold formulas**:
->b = Const. ; v = Const.<-

2) **Fundamental formula**, for expressing Weber’s Law for differences in sensation, changes, or small increases:
->$\partial \gamma = \frac{K \partial \beta}{\beta}$<-
according to one form of the law, where $\partial \gamma$ and $\partial \beta$ represent very small changes; or generally, for arbitrary magnitudes of sensation and stimulus:
->$\gamma - \gamma_1 = f \left( \frac{\beta}{\beta_1} \right)$<-
where $f$ signifies the general function symbol, according to the other form of expression.

3) **Measurement formula**, for the measure of the magnitude of sensation $\gamma$:
->$\gamma = k*) \log \frac{\beta}{b} = k (\log \beta - \log b)$<-
or, if one sets $k = 1$ and $b = 1$—which can occur under certain conditions—simply:
->$\gamma = \log \beta$.<-

4) **Difference formula**, for the measure of the magnitude of a difference in sensation:
->$\gamma - \gamma_1 = k \left( \log \frac{\beta}{b} - \log \frac{\beta_1}{b} \right) = k \log \frac{\beta}{\beta_1} = k (\log \beta - \log \beta_1)$<-

*) The constant $k$ coincides with the constant $K$ of the fundamental formula if one employs natural logarithms; however, when using common logarithms, $k = \frac{K}{M}$, where $M$ denotes the modulus of the common logarithmic system. From the fact that the logarithm of a proper fraction is negative, negative values of $\gamma$ follow for values of $\beta$ that are smaller than the threshold value <note>Fechner used these negative values to mathematically represent "unconscious" or "subliminal" sensations—states where the stimulus is physically present but the mind does not consciously perceive it.</note>.

## 5) **Difference Measurement Formula**, for the measure of a sensation of difference or contrast *u*

-> $u = k \log \frac{\varphi}{v} = k \log \frac{\beta}{v \beta_{\prime}}$ <-

Regarding the derivation and mutual relationship of the previous laws and formulas, the following additional remarks may be made concerning the explanations given in the *Elements* <note>original: *Elementen*; this refers to Gustav Fechner's seminal 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics).</note>. **Weber's Law** is expressed in relation to **differences in sensation**, but the experiments upon which Weber’s Law is based directly concern only **perceived differences** or **sensations of difference** in the sense of the distinction made above (p. 9). However, one is justified in assuming that differences in sensation remain equal if the perceived differences remain equal, provided everything else—besides the stimulus differences—that helps determine the sensation of difference remains the same; this is a point to be observed in experiments establishing Weber’s Law. Without this most natural assumption (to which we will return in Section 5 regarding a specific objection), it would be impossible to arrive at a measurement law for sensation from experimental data at all. Thus, while **Weber's Law** in relation to differences in sensation (expressed through the **fundamental formula**) constitutes the **mathematical basis** for deriving the other formulas along with the **threshold law**, in its relation to sensations of difference, it serves as the **experimental basis**. This is because one must first infer its validity in the former sense from its empirical validity in the latter.

Depending on one relationship or the other, one can proceed from it—by involving the **threshold laws**—to the **measurement formula** and the associated **difference formula**, as well as to the **difference measurement formula**, as follows.

The **fundamental formula** $d\gamma = K \frac{d\beta}{\beta}$ can be written as an expression for **Weber's Law**, regardless of which of the two relationships is used, in this way:

-> $d\gamma' - d\gamma_{\prime} = K \left( \frac{d\beta'}{\beta'} - \frac{d\beta_{\prime}}{\beta_{\prime}} \right)$ <-

since any quantity, including $d\gamma$, can mathematically be replaced by a difference between two quantities of the same kind.

Using natural logarithms, this yields:

-> $\gamma' - \gamma_{\prime} = K \log \frac{\beta'}{\beta_{\prime}} + \text{Constant}$ <-

One can, however, determine the Constant differently for two different cases. First, by assuming according to the simple **threshold law** that $\gamma_{\prime} = 0$ when the stimulus $\beta$ has the specific finite value $b$ <note>Fechner uses $b$ to denote the "threshold" or the point where sensation begins.</note>, which gives the measurement formula:

-> $\gamma' = K \log \frac{\beta'}{b}$ <-

<summary>Fechner explains the transition from experimental observations of perceived differences to his mathematical measurement formulas, asserting that Weber's Law serves as both an experimental and mathematical foundation. He demonstrates how the fundamental formula can be integrated to derive the measurement formula using the concept of the threshold.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s mathematical exposition of psychophysics. On the previous page, he established that Weber’s Law serves as both a mathematical basis for sensation measurement and an experimental basis for sensations of difference. Here, he refines the "Difference Measurement Formula" and introduces the crucial distinction between "outer" and "inner" psychophysics.</meta>

yields, and, insofar as hereafter $\gamma, = K \log \frac{\beta}{b}$ <note>$\gamma$ (gamma) represents the magnitude of sensation, while $\beta$ (beta) represents the intensity of the physical stimulus. $K$ is a constant of proportionality.</note> also holds, it yields as the difference formula $\gamma' - \gamma, = K \log \frac{\beta'}{\beta,}$, which applies regardless of a **comparison** of both sensations for their actual difference; secondly, in the case where one **compares** both sensations according to their magnitude—thereby allowing a new act of consciousness to occur—one assumes, according to the law of the difference or ratio threshold, that $\gamma' - \gamma, = 0$ when $\frac{\beta'}{\beta,} = v$ (where $v$ has a certain finite value), which yields the <term>Difference Measurement Formula</term> <note>Unterschiedsmassformel: A formula used to calculate the perceived difference between two stimuli.</note>:

->$\gamma' - \gamma, = K \log \frac{\frac{\beta'}{\beta,}}{v}$<-

By transitioning from natural logarithms to common logarithms, $K$ is finally transformed into $k$ (according to the note on p. 10).

Although the preceding laws and formulas are expressed for the relationship between sensation and stimulus—and thus for **outer psychophysics** <note>Outer psychophysics (äussere Psychophysik) investigates the relationship between the physical stimulus and the resulting mental sensation.</note>—it must be explicitly noted that they prove true for this relationship only with greater or lesser **approximation**, primarily within the limits of ordinary sensory use, aside from the everywhere unmistakable **threshold law**. Hypothetically, however, I regard them as strictly valid for the relationship between sensation and <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>Psychophysische Thätigkeit: For Fechner, this refers to the internal physiological/neural processes that directly underlie sensation.</note>—so that the latter can be substituted for the stimulus in the preceding laws and formulas—and thus as fundamental for **inner psychophysics** <note>Inner psychophysics (innere Psychophysik) investigates the relationship between internal physiological activity (brain/nerve processes) and mental sensation.</note>. In the latter, the threshold value related to **psychophysical activity** is to be regarded as an absolute constant for a given type of sensation; in outer psychophysics, however, the threshold value related to the **stimulus** is only constant insofar as **irritability** <note>Reizbarkeit: Also translated as sensitivity or excitability; the capacity of the organism to respond to a stimulus.</note> remains constant. That is, as long as a stimulus of equal strength always triggers the same psychophysical activity. Conversely, as the irritability sinks or becomes exhausted—meaning the stimulus triggers an ever-decreasing psychophysical activity—the threshold value of the stimulus rises, since the stimulus itself must increase more and more to bring the psychophysical activity to its constant threshold value. This allows the measurement formula to also be used for assessing changes in **irritability**. There are various reasons that can cause a deviation from the established laws in experiments within the field of outer psychophysics, of which...

<summary>Fechner derives the Difference Measurement Formula and explains that while psychophysical laws are only approximations in "outer" psychophysics (stimulus-to-sensation), they are strictly valid in "inner" psychophysics (neural activity-to-sensation).</summary>
<keywords>difference measurement formula, outer psychophysics, inner psychophysics, threshold law, irritability, psychophysical activity, stimulus, sensation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of psychophysical laws. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing factors ("aside from...") that might limit the application of these formulas. ...will be enough opportunity to speak, fall away entirely for inner psychophysics <note>Inner psychophysics (innere Psychophysik) refers to the direct relationship between the soul/consciousness and the body's internal activities, such as neural firing.</note>.

Insofar as I will henceforth make a distinction between the **experimental** and **fundamental** validity of laws and formulas—a distinction that is unfortunately often neglected—it is to be understood that validity in the first sense refers to demonstrability through observations or experiments in outer psychophysics <note>Outer psychophysics (äussere Psychophysik) studies the relationship between external physical stimuli (like light or sound) and the resulting sensation.</note>, while in the second sense it refers to correctness for inner psychophysics.

The measure of sensation is not to be confused with a measure of **sensitivity**. According to the discussions in the *Elements* <note>original: "Elementen"; referring to Fechner's 1860 work, "Elements of Psychophysics" (Elemente der Psychophysik).</note> (Vol. I, p. 45 ff.), the measure of sensitivity for a given type of stimulus is the reciprocal value <note>The mathematical inverse (1/x).</note> of the stimulus that appears equally noticeable to the sensation. The **measure of absolute difference sensitivity** is the absolute stimulus difference that appears equally noticeable; the **measure of relative difference sensitivity** is, depending on how one wishes to define it, the reciprocal value of the relative difference or of the ratio of the stimuli whose difference appears equally noticeable.

Insofar as Weber's Law is confirmed, absolute sensitivity decreases more and more as the absolute stimulus difference grows, while the relative sensitivity remains the same.

The following should be highlighted as the main objections that have been raised against the establishment of the previous laws and formulas:

1) That these laws and formulas do not agree with the facts, whether because they are incorrectly derived from them (Section V), or because experiments show **deviations** from them rather than confirmations (Sections VI, XVI). Weber's Law in particular has faced challenges in these respects; but if this law fails, then the laws derived from it also fail.

2) That, insofar as one can still speak of an experimental confirmation of the relevant laws—that is, for outer psychophysics—they are nonetheless invalidly transferred into inner psychophysics, as a logarithmic dependence of sensation on the psychophysical activity immediately underlying it, such as exists according to the <term>Measurement Formula</term> <note>Massformel: The formula $S = k \log I$, where sensation ($S$) is proportional to the logarithm of stimulus intensity ($I$).</note> for sensation regarding the stimulus, is a priori unaccept- <meta>The text on this page ends with the hyphenated "unannehm-," likely completing as "unannehmbar" (unacceptable) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between experimental validity (outer psychophysics) and fundamental validity (inner psychophysics) while addressing major criticisms of Weber’s Law and the logarithmic measurement of sensation.</summary>
<keywords>inner psychophysics, outer psychophysics, Weber's Law, sensitivity, absolute difference sensitivity, relative difference sensitivity, measurement formula, experimental validity</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of objections raised by critics against Fechner’s psychophysical laws. The previous page ended while defining the measure of relative difference sensitivity.</meta>

...lly noticeable. Instead of, as we do, thinking of the sensation as logarithmically dependent on psychophysical activity, and this activity as dependent on the stimulus in simple proportion, one should instead assume the reverse (VIII).

3) That our laws and formulas include conceptual and mathematical inconsistencies <note>original: "Untriftigkeiten"; referring to logical or mathematical flaws in the derivation of the laws.</note>, and specifically that the negative sensation values—to which our measurement formula leads for stimulus values below the threshold <note>The "threshold" (Schwelle) is the minimum intensity a stimulus must reach to be consciously perceived. Mathematically, Fechner's formula resulted in negative numbers for stimuli below this point.</note>—are inadmissible (X).

4) That a clear understanding of the relationships of the external world and a rational teleology <note>Teleology is the study of purpose or design in nature; critics argued that Fechner's mathematical laws did not align with a world designed for specific purposes.</note> are incompatible with our laws (VII. VIII.).

5) That accordingly, the formulas I established for the mathematical expression of psychophysical laws must either be abandoned, or at least modified, or if they are to be kept in their current form, they must be interpreted in a fundamentally different way than I have done (IV).

The opponents are partly divided across these objections and partly agree on them, though they do not (with the possible exception of *Plateau* <note>Joseph Plateau (1801–1883), a Belgian physicist who proposed a power law for sensation as an alternative to Fechner's log law.</note> and *Brentano* <note>Franz Brentano (1838–1917), a philosopher and psychologist known for his work on intentionality.</note>) agree on the positive alternative views they have put forward.

### III. Literature regarding the objections and opposing views to be primarily considered in the following sections.

Whenever a page reference is added to an author's name in the future, it refers to the treatise or publication described below. For *Hering* <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist who was one of Fechner's most significant scientific critics.</note> in particular, where none of his papers are specifically named, the reference is to the first-listed, most important treatise.

A nuisance when citing treatises from the *Vienna Session Reports* <note>original: "Wiener Sitzungsberichte"; the official proceedings of the Imperial Academy of Sciences in Vienna.</note> is that the offprints <term>offprints (Separatabzüge): separate copies of an article originally published as part of a larger journal or book, often distributed by the author.</term> of these papers, which authors send to their colleagues, are all paginated starting from page 1. Consequently, their page numbers generally do not match the volumes of the *Session Reports* in which they originally appeared—a nuisance that, in my opinion, should be avoided. I will cite according to the offprints,

## IV. A Systematic Presentation of the Opponents (Helmholtz, Aubert, Mach, Bernstein, Plateau, Brentano, Hering, Langer).

Having provided a summary of the main objections at the end of Section 2 against the laws and formulas defended here, I will now present a chronological overview of the opponents from whom these objections originated. As briefly as possible, I will assign to each author their specific objections as well as their positive views, providing my own responses as I find necessary; where further elaboration is required, I will refer to subsequent sections. The formulas which the opponents propose as alternatives to mine are listed in brackets.

Unless explicitly stated otherwise, the measure of sensation is denoted throughout by γ <note>the Greek letter gamma</note>, and the measure of stimulus is denoted by β <note>the Greek letter beta</note>; the letters $c, k, K$ represent constants that are independent of the stimulus. In general, the letter designations used by the authors in their original formulas have been changed in several instances (with notice provided) to maintain consistency here.

### Helmholtz and Aubert.

Helmholtz and Aubert assert that the experimental deviations from <term>Weber’s Law</term><note>Weber's Law states that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is proportional to the magnitude of the stimuli. For example, it's easier to notice a small change in a quiet sound than the same change in a loud one.</note> in the field of light sensation, according to their experiments, go considerably further than I had reason to assume based on all previous trials. However, this does not prevent Helmholtz from recognizing the approximate character of the law—which is all that matters in <term>outer psychophysics</term><note>Fechner distinguished between "outer" psychophysics (the relationship between physical stimuli and sensation) and "inner" psychophysics (the relationship between neural activity and sensation).</note>—for the purpose of drawing conclusions for inner psychophysics; whereas Aubert considers the law as generally not to—

<summary>This page concludes the bibliography of Fechner's critics and begins a systematic rebuttal of their arguments, specifically addressing the experimental deviations from Weber’s Law noted by Helmholtz and Aubert.</summary>

<keywords>psychophysics, Weber's Law, Helmholtz, Aubert, Ewald Hering, stimulus and sensation, outer vs inner psychophysics, light sensation, color sense</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic presentation of psychophysical opponents, specifically moving into the mathematical refinements proposed by Helmholtz and Aubert to address the shortcomings of Weber's Law at extreme stimulus intensities.</meta>

...aptly explained. In the meantime, I have previously shown at the place cited above (p. 7) that Aubert's own experiments prove the approximation of the law <note>meaning Weber's Law</note> even beyond the limits of ordinary ocular use, and I will return to this with more specific details in the 16th section. The following interjection provides an account of the formulas established by Helmholtz and Aubert.

Helmholtz first provides (p. 313) a formula to cover the lower experimental deviation from Weber's Law—that is, the deviation occurring toward the smaller stimulus values—which agrees with the formula I myself established for this purpose (*Elements* II. 195). However, in order to simultaneously cover the upper deviation, which occurs toward the larger stimulus values, he proposes the following as a differential formula (using only different letters):

->$\partial \gamma = \frac{c \partial \beta}{(a + \beta) (A + \beta)}$ (1)<-

in which $c, a, A$ are constants independent of the stimulus; $a$, in particular, is a small value that measures the internal excitation independent of the stimulus <note>This "internal excitation" or "intrinsic light" refers to the sensation we experience even in total darkness due to the activity of our own nervous system</note>, and it becomes negligible compared to significant values of $\beta$. $A$ is a very large value, against which values of $\beta$ that are not very large (and even more so $a$) vanish. In cases where $a$ is negligible compared to $\beta$, while $\beta$ is negligible compared to $A$, this formula transforms into our simple fundamental formula, with $\frac{c}{A}$ representing our $K$ <note>Fechner is showing how his own simple law is a "middle case" of Helmholtz's more complex one</note>. If one considers only the lower deviation—that is, if one neglects $\beta$ in relation to $A$—one arrives at our above-mentioned formula in *Elements* II. 195, which represents this deviation.

Through integration, one obtains the following measurement formula from (1):

->$\gamma = \frac{c}{A - a} \log. \text{nat.} \frac{a + \beta}{A + \beta} + \text{Const.}$<-

At extraordinarily large values of $\beta$, where not only $a$ but also $A$ becomes negligible by comparison, the "Constant" <note>original: "Const."</note> appears as the maximum of sensation $\gamma$; accordingly, if this maximum is called $G$, then:

->$G - \gamma = \frac{c}{A - a} \log. \text{nat.} \frac{A + \beta}{a + \beta}$<-

Furthermore, let $g$ be the value of $\gamma$ that occurs without any external stimulus, purely by virtue of the internal excitation $a$; then one has, as I note in passing:

->$\text{Const.} = g - \frac{c}{A - a} \log. \text{nat.} \frac{a}{A}$<-

which gives:

->$g - \gamma = \frac{c}{A - a} \cdot \log. \text{nat.} \frac{a}{A} \cdot \frac{a + \beta}{A + \beta}$<-

according to which the expression for the difference in sensation between maximum and minimum is found to be:

<meta>Fechner, On Matters of Psychophysics.</meta>
<sig>2</sig>

<summary>Fechner examines Helmholtz's mathematical modifications to Weber's Law, specifically how Helmholtz uses constants to account for sensory noise at low intensities and sensory saturation at high intensities.</summary>
<keywords>Hermann von Helmholtz, Hermann Aubert, Weber's Law, stimulus intensity, internal excitation, sensory saturation, differential formula, measurement formula, intrinsic light</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical critique of Weber’s Law, specifically evaluating how Hermann von Helmholtz and Hermann Aubert attempted to modify the law to account for the human eye's sensitivity at very high and very low light intensities.</meta>

-> $G - g = \frac{c}{A - a} \log. *nat.* \frac{A}{a}$ <-
<note>This formula describes the relationship between a sensation (G) and the physical stimulus intensity, where "log. nat." refers to the natural logarithm.</note>

In the coefficient $\frac{c}{A - a}$, however, $a$ can be neglected in comparison to $A$.

The maximum value of the <term>relative difference sensitivity</term> <note>The ability of an observer to detect the smallest possible change in light intensity relative to the existing light level</note> is found according to the formulas above at $\beta = \sqrt{A a}$—that is, at the <term>geometric mean</term> between $a$ and $A$. This is found by taking the ratio of $\partial \gamma$ to $\frac{\partial \beta}{\beta}$, which according to equation (1) <note>from the previous page</note> is $= \frac{c \beta}{(a + \beta) (A + \beta)}$, and seeking the maximum value through standard mathematical methods.

**Aubert** has attempted on page 68 to represent his experimental results empirically within certain limits by assuming that the values of $\frac{\partial \beta}{\beta}$ (which Aubert designates as $\frac{I'}{I}$), which are just barely noticeable, stand in inverse proportion to the logarithms of $\beta$. This assumes that the logarithm of $\beta$ at a certain low value of $\beta$ is set to 1, as is the corresponding value of $\frac{\partial \beta}{\beta} = 1$. Starting from this low value of $\beta$, he provides two tables based on two series of experiments comparing observation and calculation. These range from the baseline intensity up to 100 times that value, with the remark that the differences between observation and calculation do not exceed the limits of expected observational error. 

However, one can see in this comparison that as $\beta$ increases, the differences move from negative to positive in an almost regular progression. At the highest $\beta$ considered, instead of the value $\frac{\partial \beta}{\beta} = \frac{1}{3}$ required by the calculation, values from $\frac{1}{3.48}$ to $\frac{1}{3.74}$ actually appear. Finally, it must be remembered that at the weak starting value of $\beta$ which Aubert assumes as his unit, it would be impossible to neglect the <term>internal brightness of the eye-black</term> <note>original: *Augenschwarz*; this refers to the "intrinsic light" or the dim visual sensation present even in total darkness</note> in the way he does, if one wished to arrive at a rational formula. Aubert himself admits (p. 56) that his assumption is no longer sufficient for very low values of $\beta$, and therefore he explicitly presents it only as an empirically useful formula for the specific, limited section of his experiments.

To formulate his assumption (something Aubert did not attempt himself), one would have to set:

-> $\frac{\partial \beta}{\beta} = \frac{Const.}{\log. \beta}$ <-

and, by replacing the constant with the small, itself constant, just-noticeable sensation difference $\partial \gamma$ (multiplied by a constant $c$), as dependent on $\partial \beta, \beta$, and $\log \beta$,

->$\partial \gamma = \frac{1}{c} \text{ log. } \beta \frac{\partial \beta}{\beta}$<-

from this, by setting $\frac{1}{c} = K$

->$\gamma - \gamma_{\prime} = k \left[ (\text{log. } \beta)^2 - (\text{log. } \beta_{\prime})^2 \right].$<-

If the <term>threshold principle</term> <note>Schwellenprincip: the concept that a stimulus must reach a certain minimum intensity—the "threshold"—before it can be consciously perceived</note> is applied, according to which $\gamma_{\prime} = 0$ when $\beta_{\prime}$ equals the finite threshold value $b$, one finally obtains the <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Massformel: the mathematical equation used to calculate the magnitude of a sensation based on the physical intensity of the stimulus</note>

->$\gamma = k \left[ (\text{log. } \beta)^2 - (\text{log. } b)^2 \right]$<-

which differs from our measurement formula (p. 10) only in that $\log \beta$ and $\log b$ are raised to the power of two. — The empirical maximum of the <term>relative difference sensitivity</term> <note>The ability to detect the smallest proportional change between two stimuli</note> is revealed by Aubert's formula just as little as by ours.

->Mach.<-

**Mach** <note>Ernst Mach (1838–1916), an Austrian physicist and philosopher who made significant contributions to the study of shock waves and sensory perception</note> allows my fundamental formulas for <term>external psychophysics</term> <note>The study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the resulting sensations</note> in the realm of intensive sensations to stand essentially as valid, and has even gone into a more detailed discussion of them in a special small pamphlet (Lectures on Psychophysics. <note>original: "Vorträge über Psychophysik"</note> Vienna, Summer, 1863). However, in his second point, he contests the transferability I assumed of these formulas into <term>internal psychophysics</term> <note>The study of the relationship between the mind and the brain/nervous system</note> based on a theoretical assumption that I cannot find binding after the discussions under section VIII.

Furthermore, in his first treatise, based on his own experiments, he finds <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus</note> not applicable to the extensive <term>sensations of time</term>. However, according to the compilation of his and **Vierordt's** <note>Karl von Vierordt (1818–1884), a German physiologist who studied the perception of time and the circulatory system</note> experiments in the 16th section, it [the law] is indeed subject to a lower experimental deviation regarding time sensations—just as it likely is with all sensations—but higher up it applies with sufficient approximation that one can hardly avoid attributing a fundamental significance to the law in this field as well. — I gladly concur with the formulas for <term>contrast sensations</term> proposed by the author in point no. 2, which are to be discussed in the 11th section, insofar as they prove that no experimental disturbances for the provability of Weber’s Law are to be feared from the effects of contrast.

<meta>This page continues the author's comparison of his psychophysical measurement formula with those of his contemporaries. Having previously discussed Aubert and Mach, he now moves to the theories of Julius Bernstein.</meta>

### Bernstein.

**Bernstein** <note>Julius Bernstein (1839–1911), a German physiologist who pioneered the "membrane theory" of nerve conduction</note> indeed accepts the form of my <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Massformel: the mathematical equation expressing the relationship between stimulus intensity and sensation magnitude</note>, but gives it a completely different interpretation. Instead of setting the **sensation** *γ* in logarithmic dependence on the strength of the stimulus or the **psychophysical activity** *β*, he sets it simply proportional to the number of **brain ganglion cells** through which the excitation—conceived as a **vibration process**—propagates from the entry of the stimulus into the brain; or, assuming a uniform distribution of these cells, simply proportional to the size of the space *S* in which these cells are contained, multiplied by the density *α* with which the space is filled by the cells. In doing so, he assumes that beyond a certain space around the entry point of the excitation, no further progression of the excitation takes place—the excitation weakening more and more through spreading and resistance—and thus regards the space *S*, the so-called **irradiation space**, as a limited one. However, according to his deduction, *α S* depends on the magnitude of the stimulus *β* with which the excitation enters the brain, and the **threshold value** *b* (in the sense used by that author) at which the excitation terminates at the boundary of the irradiation space, according to the same formula as sensation depends on *β* and *b* in our version; this provides the basis for the identical form of the measurement formula.

One thus has, in the sense of the author:

->*γ* = *α S* = 1/*k* natural log. *β*/*b*<-
<note>original: "log. nat." (logarithmus naturalis)</note>

where *k* is a constant that depends on the specific resistance that the ganglion cells oppose to the propagation of the excitation, and which varies according to the different sensitivities of individuals to stimuli.

The derivation of Bernstein’s measurement formula rests on many assumptions that are by no means evident; however, since these cannot be discussed briefly, I refer the reader from here to Section 15, which specifically relates to this. For my part, I am unable to share the author's view primarily for the following two reasons: first, because I could not concede a measurement of sensation based on the spreading of the excitation process (upon which the sensation depends) without including the strength of the excitation at every point in the measurement; second, because I find the finite limitation of the irra-

<summary>The author critiques Julius Bernstein’s interpretation of the psychophysical measurement formula, which replaces logarithmic stimulus intensity with the physical spread of excitation through brain cells (the irradiation space).</summary>
<keywords>Julius Bernstein, measurement formula, sensation, psychophysical activity, ganglion cells, irradiation space, threshold value, excitation propagation</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Julius Bernstein's theories regarding the "irradiation space" in the brain. The previous page ended with a mathematical formula expressing sensation as proportional to the log of the stimulus divided by a threshold value.</meta>

[irra]diation space through the author's deduction cannot be found sufficiently justified. However, more detail on this will be provided in the 15th section and in some remarks at the end of the 18th.

### Plateau.

**Plateau** <note>Joseph Plateau (1801–1883), a Belgian physicist known for his research on visual perception and capillary action</note>, without asserting more specific objections against me, says (p. 471): "Fechner’s formula leads to the conclusion that when the general illumination varies, the differences in sensation remain constant. It seemed more natural to me, in order to explain the constancy of the general impression of a **copperplate engraving** <note>A common type of printed image in the 19th century; the example is used to show how we perceive the relative brightness of an image despite changes in lighting</note>, to assume *a priori* the constancy of these *ratios* and not the *differences* between the sensations;" and in this regard, he asserts more specifically the following fact: "Everyone knows that a copperplate engraving remains pretty much the same whether one views it in daylight, in gaslight, or even in sunshine. These so varied illuminations produce no significant change in the relationships between the light and dark parts."

By means of an analysis, which I will omit here, Plateau then arrives at the following <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Massformel: the mathematical rule for calculating the magnitude of a sensation</note> corresponding to his assumption:
-> γ = k β^p <-
wherein **k** and **p** are constants, of which **p**—according to Plateau’s remark—can be smaller than 1, and must unquestionably be assumed to be smaller if it is to be explained that the sensation increases in a slower ratio than the stimulus. From the previous formula, it follows (setting kp = K):
-> ∂γ = K β^{p-1} ∂β <-
which would replace our <term>fundamental formula</term> <note>Fundamentalformel: the basic differential equation expressing how a small change in stimulus leads to a small change in sensation</note>, and:
-> ∂γ / γ = p ∂β / β <-

**Helmholtz** <note>Hermann von Helmholtz (1821–1894), a massive figure in 19th-century science who made fundamental contributions to physiology and physics</note> has interpreted the fact of the fairly constant impression of a copperplate engraving under different degrees of illumination more in my sense than in Plateau’s; however, nothing can be decided through subjective views and authorities; but there exists an objective means of decision in experiments, such as those that serve to test **Weber’s Law** <note>The principle that the just-noticeable difference between two stimuli is proportional to the magnitude of the stimuli</note>. Plateau also realized this very well, and, undoubtedly distrusting the methods applied thus far or their results, himself specified a new testing procedure (to be briefly described below), which can indeed be regarded as an <meta>catchword: izbare</meta> [estimable] enrichment of psychophysical methods.

<summary>This page discusses Joseph Plateau's "Power Law" alternative to Fechner's logarithmic formula, focusing on the example of a copperplate engraving's appearance under changing light. Fechner notes that while Helmholtz supports his own view, the dispute must be settled by objective experimentation rather than scientific authority.</summary>
<keywords>Plateau, Fechner, Helmholtz, copperplate engraving, sensation, stimulus, measurement formula, Weber's Law, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Joseph Plateau’s theories on sensation. It explains how he deferred the experimental validation of his formula to Delboeuf due to his own failing eyesight and eventually conceded that his formula was incorrect.</meta>

... [Plateau] has also preliminarily tested its practical feasibility and usefulness and found it proven; but since he (in consideration of his eye ailment) could not carry out the testing of the law itself, he deferred to a test to be undertaken first by Delboeuf using this method, after which he concludes his treatise on the matter with the following words: "When the results are published, it will be known which of the three formulas—that of Fechner, that of Delboeuf [of which more is found under the heading **Delboeuf**], or mine—deserves the preference."

In the method proposed by Plateau and later executed by Delboeuf, it is essential to graduate three adjacent light surfaces *A*, *B*, and *C* in their physical brightness against one another so that, according to the judgment of sensation, the <term>brightness difference</term> <note>Helligkeitsunterschied: the perceived gap or "distance" in intensity between two stimuli as experienced by the observer</note> between *C* and *B* appears exactly as large as that between *A* and *B*. This is to be carried out through a larger scale of light intensities; after which calculation does its part to test whether the ratios of these intensities sufficiently fit the formula under examination. Plateau had already convinced himself through several test subjects that the judgment of the point where the equality of the two differences between *A* and *B*, and *B* and *C* occurs for perception is not subject to too much uncertainty, and this is confirmed by Delboeuf’s experiments. Indeed, even <term>stellar magnitude estimations</term> <note>Sterngrössenschätzungen: the system used by astronomers to classify stars by their brightness; Fechner argues this relies on the same psychological principle of comparing brightness intervals</note> basically fall under this principle.

Well then, the experiments according to Plateau’s method have since been conducted and published by Delboeuf. They decide against Plateau’s formula in favor of **Weber’s Law**; and if Delboeuf, in his first treatise (*Study* <note>original: "Étude"</note>), still believed he could advocate for a modification of Weber’s Law based on these experiments—which indeed fits his experiments just as well, yet no better than this law itself—he nevertheless accepts the pure Weber’s Law in his later treatises, though he certainly gives it a different fundamental interpretation than I do.

Finally, Plateau himself, in a report on Delboeuf’s *Study*, retracted his formula based on these experiments with the following words: "As for my formula, by the fact alone that it differs from that of Mr. Delboeuf, it is evident that it is inaccurate." <note>original: "Quant à ma formule par cela seul qu’elle diffère de celle de M. Delboeuf il est évident, qu’elle est inexacte."</note> *) In the meantime, Plateau’s conception remains of interest,

---

*) I am quoting here from a communication of this passage sent to me, as I have not read Plateau's report myself.

<summary>This page details the experimental testing of Plateau's sensation formula by Delboeuf, which ultimately led Plateau to retract his own formula in favor of Weber's Law. The text explains the method of comparing perceived brightness intervals as a way to mathematically verify psychological laws.</summary>

<keywords>Plateau, Delboeuf, Weber's Law, brightness difference, light intensity, stellar magnitudes, psychophysics, experimental method</keywords>
