<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Joseph Plateau’s proposed formula for the relationship between stimulus and sensation. Having discussed the experimental methods of Plateau and Delboeuf on the previous page, Fechner now addresses other scholars who support Plateau and defends his own theories against Plateau’s recent criticisms regarding "subjective colors" like afterimages.</meta>

...insofar as others (**Brentano**, **Ueberhorst**) have arrived at perspectives from different directions that lead back to Plateau’s formula, as will be discussed under the heading "Brentano."

Incidentally, one may note that Plateau’s formula contains no <term>threshold value</term> <note>Schwellenwerth: the minimum amount of stimulus needed for a person to perceive a sensation. Fechner’s own law includes this "starting point," whereas Plateau’s power law often does not.</note>, which means that everything in Section IX that supports the fundamental necessity of a threshold also speaks against Plateau’s formula.

As for the objections that Plateau has recently raised against my views on subjective colors in his treatise *Sur les couleurs accidentelles ou subjectives* <note>original: "On accidental or subjective colors"</note> (Brussels, Hayez 1875), their full consideration does not belong here. I will only briefly mention that I could not yield to them based on the following viewpoints: I believe that in <term>afterimage</term> <note>Nachbild: the ghost-like image that stays in your vision after looking at a bright object.</note> experiments, one must take into account not only the "light-dust" and other bright phenomena that happen to mix into the <term>retinal black</term> <note>Augenschwarz: the "inner black" or grayish-darkness we see when our eyes are closed; Fechner argues this is a baseline of activity in the visual system, not a total absence of sensation.</note>, but mainly the **inner light** (the inner <term>psychophysical excitation</term> <note>psychophysische Erregung: the physical energy or activity in the nervous system that directly creates a conscious experience.</note>) which is represented by the visual phenomenon of the retinal black itself. I believe that for internal reasons—especially in the area surrounding afterimages of bright objects on a dark background—this inner light can increase its brightness far above its average value, though by virtue of an organic arrangement, it cannot decrease below a certain limit. Furthermore, in the same sense that one can speak of a "decomposition" of external light into colors, this inner light is no less decomposable, and this decomposability must be taken into account everywhere in experiments on subjective <term>complementary colors</term> <note>Complementärfarben: pairs of colors that, when combined, cancel each other out to produce white or gray; in afterimages, staring at red often produces a green "subjective" color.</note>. Indeed, I already expressed myself essentially in this sense in my treatise on subjective complementary colors in *Poggendorff's Annalen* Vol. XLIV, p. 517 ff. However, I spoke there too one-sidedly, pointing to the more accidental bright phenomena that can permeate the retinal black as proof that an inner subjective light development can exist independently of external light stimuli. This, of course, is not sufficient to explain many phenomena, and against that, I could certainly find a justified opposition. Nevertheless, I have explained myself clearly enough regarding the light-value I attribute to the retinal black itself, both in the *Elements of Psychophysics* and in the treatise on <term>contrast sensation</term> <note>Contrastempfindung: the way the appearance of a color or brightness changes depending on the colors surrounding it.</note> contained in the *Reports of the Saxon Society* (1860). — It should be evident that the experiment conducted with red and green paper, which Plateau mentions on page 10, could only mean something if the red and green were of a simple, <term>homogeneous</term> <note>In this context, light of a single wavelength, rather than a mixture of different colors.</note> nature; but even in that case, it would not be provable if one may assume that every objectively simple color ray internally triggers a composition of color rays—a view that **Helmholtz** expresses with reference to the **Youngian hypothesis**, though I support such a view in a different sense in Section 14. In any case, there are reasons for this.

<summary>Fechner defends his psychophysical law against Joseph Plateau, arguing that Plateau’s formula fails by ignoring the sensory threshold. He also discusses "retinal black" (Augenschwarz) as a baseline of internal neural activity that influences how we perceive afterimages and subjective colors.</summary>
<keywords>threshold value, afterimages, retinal black, subjective colors, psychophysical excitation, Plateau, Helmholtz, complementary colors, sensory contrast</keywords>

# Brentano.

**Brentano** raises (p. 90) an objection—which I will consider under section **V**—against my method of justifying **Weber’s Law** <note>Weber's Law: A principle of psychology stating that the smallest change in a stimulus that can be perceived is a constant proportion of the original stimulus.</note>, an objection in which he concurs with other opponents. Based on a view of the facts from which I derive this law—a view that differs from my own—he replaces it with the following:

While according to Weber’s Law, every increase in **sensation** remains equally noticeable if the increase in the **stimulus** of that sensation maintains the same ratio to the stimulus itself, according to Brentano, every increase in sensation is equally noticeable if it maintains the same ratio to the intensity of the **sensation** to which it is added; and the relative increase in sensation remains the same if the relative increase in the physical stimulus remains the same.

**Ueberhorst** arrives at essentially the same conclusion in his work: *Die Entstehung der Gesichtswahrnehmung* <note>original: "The Origin of Visual Perception." Göttingen 1876</note> based on purely psychological discussions. On the assumption that sensations are simply proportional to nerve excitation, he puts forward the proposition: "Similar sensations that are just barely distinguishable always differ from one another by an equal fraction of their own strength."

Brentano did not himself express his law in mathematical symbols; however, this has been done by me in the following insertion, and through correspondence with him, I have ensured that the sense in which he understands his law has been correctly captured. It turns out that this formula, and therefore the author's law, leads entirely back to the Plateau formula and Plateau’s law. Consequently, everything that has been said against the latter also applies to Brentano’s version, without me needing to return to it specifically.

Let *γ* <note>gamma</note> be the sensation, *β* <note>beta</note> be the stimulus, and *p* be a constant; thus, the author's fundamental law is:

->∂γ / γ = p · ∂β / β<-

which leads to the <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Massformel: The mathematical equation used to calculate the magnitude of a sensation based on the stimulus intensity.</note>

->log γ = p log β + Const.<-

If we now set *γ* = 1 at *β* = *B*, whereby log. *γ* = 0, the Constant = -*p* log. *B* results, thus generally:

<summary>Fechner discusses how Brentano and Ueberhorst proposed an alternative to Weber's Law where the noticeability of a change depends on the ratio of the sensation itself, rather than the stimulus. Fechner demonstrates mathematically that this proposal is identical to Plateau's "power law," which he has already critiqued.</summary>

<keywords>Brentano, Weber's Law, Ueberhorst, Psychophysics, Plateau's Formula, sensation, stimulus, measurement formula</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and conceptual critique of Brentano's proposed psychophysical law. Fechner is discussing how Brentano derives his "power law" formula from empirical observations and how it differs from Fechner's own logarithmic law.</meta>

->log. γ = p log. $\frac{β}{B}$<- <note>In these equations, γ represents the intensity of the sensation, β is the physical stimulus, B is the threshold of the stimulus, and p is a constant power.</note>

and from this

->γ = ($\frac{β}{B}$)$^p$ = k $β^p$<-

when ($\frac{1}{B}$)$^p$ = k is established.

To justify his law, **Brentano** does not cite the same facts as **Plateau**, but rather relies on facts that are otherwise generally interpreted in favor of **Weber’s** law, such as: "That the increase of an inch <note>original: "Zoll," a historical unit of length approximately equal to an English inch</note> by a line <note>original: "Linie," a historical unit usually equal to 1/12th of an inch</note> is far more noticeable than the increase of a foot by that same amount." Admittedly, I must confess that his method of deriving his law from such facts has not become completely clear to me, neither through his writing nor through my correspondence with him. It would seem to me, based on this and other remarks of his\*), that the perspective from which physical and psychic <note>In this context, "psychic" refers to internal mental or sensory experiences.</note> magnitudes are to be distinguished on the one hand, and placed into a functional relationship with one another in <term>psychophysics: the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations or perceptions they evoke</term> on the other, was not clearly before him. Let some remarks on this be inserted here.

In my opinion, the distinction we must make in this regard—and which we silently make everywhere even outside of psychophysics—is this: physical magnitudes are measurable either directly through external means, or where difficulties of direct measurement exist, they are at least in principle reducible to a scale obtainable in such a way. Psychic magnitudes are not directly measurable in this way, but are only comparable internally according to equality and inequality, or greater and lesser inequality. Nonetheless, they are subject to measurement psychophysically as a function of the physical magnitudes upon which they depend, based on laws that exist for this dependency; such a measurement, while rarely feasible experimentally, exists in principle everywhere.

To illustrate the distinction to be made with at least one example, let the following serve:

According to an interesting observation by **Hering**, two points placed on paper at a certain horizontal distance from each other appear more distant if one places a series of points between them than if one leaves the space between them empty. Anyone can immediately convince themselves of this by placing a horizontal distance that is empty and one interspersed with points—both set to the same width using a pair of compasses—one beneath the other. Insofar as they are of equal

---

<note>*) For instance, when he claims on p. 94 that my method measures a physical phenomenon rather than a psychic one.</note>

He uses an example from Hering about the perception of distance to illustrate how subjective perception can differ from physical measurement.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical methods against the criticisms of Franz Brentano. Fechner is explaining how physical measurements (like the distance between points) can be used to mathematically model internal mental experiences, even when those experiences don't perfectly mirror physical reality.</meta>

...correspond to the width of the compass, they are **physically** equal, whereas they are **psychically** <note>In this context, "psychically" (psychisch) refers to the subjective, mental perception of a stimulus rather than its objective physical properties.</note> unequal.

Now, one could place the psychic impression of magnitude into a functional relationship with the physical measure by enlarging the empty distance or reducing the one filled with **dots** until both appeared equal to the eye. This would yield a functional relationship between psychic equality—which can be judged psychically—and physical inequality—which can be measured externally. Depending on the greater or lesser absolute length of the lines, or the greater or lesser number of dots, one would undoubtedly find different ratios in this regard. Thus, a **psychophysical** <term>psychophysics: the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations or perceptions they evoke</term> investigation can generally be directed toward determining under which variations and ratios of **physical** (i.e., **externally** measurable) values an equality of the **psychic** values dependent on them is maintained or restored (as is done when testing **Weber’s Law** <note>Weber's Law states that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note>). While this does not yet directly provide a **psychic measure**, it does provide a **functional relationship** between the two, from which such a measure is fundamentally derivable.

The author <note>Referring to Franz Brentano, Fechner's contemporary and critic.</note> additionally makes the following general **counter-remarks** against my **psychophysical doctrine of measurement**:

> "First—he says (p. 90)—the possibility of measuring intensities according to the method specified by **Fechner** is limited entirely to those phenomena produced by external stimulation of the **sensory organs**. For all psychic phenomena rooted in physical processes within the organism, we still lack a measure of intensity." — However, we do not thereby lack a principle of measuring psychic magnitudes according to their functional dependence on physical magnitudes—a principle derivable from experiments in the field of **outer psychophysics**. This is psychologically most important and does not preclude the possibility of an **inner psychophysics** <note>Fechner distinguished between "outer" psychophysics (stimulus to sensation) and "inner" psychophysics (the relationship between the soul/mind and the nervous system).</note>, which the author seems to dispute.

> "But more still! — the author adds (p. 91) — Sensations themselves do not depend solely on the strength of the external stimulus; they also depend on psychic conditions, such as the degree of **attention**. It will therefore be necessary to eliminate this influence—perhaps by assuming the case of the highest and fullest attention. But then, if no other inconveniences arise, at least a new and significant limitation results."

But it is incorrect to say that the strength with which a sensory phenomenon appears still depends on the degree of **attention**

...depends, provided the phenomenon does not escape attention altogether. Enough has been said about this in the *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's own 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik*, Volume II, pages 452 and following.</note> II. 452 ff., and anyone can find immediately that a white or gray paper does not appear brighter, nor a tone louder, regardless of whether one directs a more or less intensive attention toward it. But why does it disappear entirely from consciousness if one does not direct any attention to it at all? Well, my explanation for this can be found in Section 42 of the *Elements*, and I will briefly return to it under point IX. As for how my opponents find an explanation for this, they may see to it themselves.

## Delboeuf.

Delboeuf <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896) was a Belgian psychologist and philosopher who conducted early experiments in psychophysics.</note> takes a peculiar and wavering position against my doctrine in his three treatises designated above on page 15—to be distinguished briefly as *Ét.*, *Théor.*, and *Rev.* <note>Shortened titles for Delboeuf's works: *Étude psychophysique* (1873), *Théorie générale de la sensibilité* (1876), and articles in the *Revue philosophique*.</note>.\*) Based on the statement on page 5 above, one might look for one of the most decided opponents to it in him. Nevertheless, his *experiments* (in the *Ét.*) are only a very welcome confirmation of **Weber’s Law**, and indeed in an approximation that goes much further than according to **Aubert’s experiments** <note>Hermann Aubert (1826–1892) was a physiologist who found that Weber’s Law failed at very low or very high levels of light intensity.</note>, as one can convince oneself from Section 17, which discusses his experiments. Furthermore, Delboeuf already recognizes the approximate character of Weber's Law in his first treatise (*Ét.* pages 22, 46) and only believes he must modify it slightly—partly for theoretical reasons, partly to cover the experimental lower deviation from Weber's Law that remains in his experiments as it does everywhere—whereas in his later treatises he drops this modification,\*\*) and even [accepts] the same as a consequence—

---

\*) Besides these treatises, which alone concern us here, Delboeuf—an excellent experimental and philosophical researcher in Belgium—has also published investigations on the treatment of logical-mathematical problems, as well as on optical illusions. His most recently published work is titled: *Algorithmic Logic: Essay on a system of signs applied to logic with an introduction where general questions relative to the use of notations in the sciences are treated* <note>original: "Logique algorithmique. Essai sur un système de signes appliqué à la logique avec une introduction ou sont traitées les questions générales relatives à l'emploi des notations dans les sciences."</note>. Brussels, Muquart, 1877.

\*\*) While Delboeuf in his first treatise (*Ét.*) could reproach my formula for Weber's Law for its lower deviation <note>A "lower deviation" refers to experimental results that don't match the mathematical prediction at the lowest levels of stimulus intensity.</note> from the result of the experiments, which the modification he adopted there [attempted to address]...

<keywords>Gustav Fechner, attention, consciousness, Joseph Delboeuf, Weber's Law, psychophysics, sensation intensity, experimental psychology</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s detailed comparison between his own psychophysical laws and the theories of Joseph Delboeuf. Fechner is addressing how Delboeuf’s mathematical formulas align with his own, while noting their differing interpretations of physiological activity and the "threshold" of sensation.</meta>

admits based on a law of measurement established by him, the formula of which agrees in form entirely with my logarithmic measurement formula. However, the <term>threshold value</term> <note>The point at which a stimulus is just strong enough to be perceived.</note> I introduced there is replaced by a value understood differently, which certainly makes it fundamentally different in application (see below). His theoretical objections, maintained even in his more recent treatises, mainly concern my establishment and interpretation of negative sensation values and the threshold value intrinsically linked with them, which I shall address under sections IX and X. Since, in the meantime, Hering's <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918) was a physiologist and Fechner’s primary scientific rival; he argued that the mind's response to stimuli was more complex than a simple mathematical ratio.</note> objections against **Weber's Law** and the logarithmic measurement formula derived from it affect Delboeuf just as much as me, Delboeuf actually shifts from being an opponent in the *Étude* to an ally in the *Revue*. He supports me insofar as he—without abandoning his own objections—opposes Hering's objections in this latter regard, albeit generally from different viewpoints than those I will present.

To briefly describe Delboeuf's own positive view, which he has maintained in his latest treatises (*Théor.* and *Rev.*) as distinct from both mine and his own earlier view (in the *Ét.*): according to him, the <term>activity</term> <note>original: "Thätigkeit"; here referring to the physiological energy or firing of the sensory nerves.</note> in which the sensory nerves are engaged—imagined as an oscillatory process and whose magnitude he denotes as $p$—strives to reach an equilibrium with the externally stimulating activity, the <term>stimulus</term> <note>original: "Reiz"</note>, whose magnitude he denotes as $p'$. Sensation occurs as long as this equilibrium is not reached: positive sensation (such as warmth or light) occurs as long as the stimulus predominates, and negative sensation (such as cold or darkness) occurs in the opposite case. In every instance, the sensation diminishes through the effect of that striving while the stimulus is kept constant, until equilibrium is reached, after which the sensation remains silent so long as the stimulus does not change.

---

<note>
If he does not present a modification of the law, it is only because he failed to notice that, for the representation of this deviation, I account for the small value $c$ (which he includes as an addition to the stimulus $\beta$) as the value of the <term>brightness of the eye-black</term> <note>original: "Helligkeit des Augenschwarz." This refers to the "intrinsic light" of the retina—the fact that we do not see absolute black, but rather a dark gray, even in a room with no light.</note>. Delboeuf, of course, does not understand $c$ in this way, though this is irrelevant for the calculation.
</note>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of Joseph Delboeuf’s theories. Having previously established that Delboeuf is now an ally against Hering's objections, Fechner here begins to dissect the mechanics of Delboeuf’s "fundamental formula" and how it describes the physiological response of nerves to external stimuli.</meta>

Accordingly, Delboeuf's current fundamental formula is:

->$\gamma = \log \frac{p'}{p}$<-

where $\gamma$ is the measure of the sensation, $p'$ is that of the external stimulus, and $p$ is that of the internal activity (*Théor.* <note>Delboeuf's *Théorie générale de la sensibilité* (1876).</note> p. 26, 30).

However, sensation does not depend on a static moment according to Delboeuf; rather—if I correctly understand the author based on the passages I have gathered from his recent treatises—the situation is as follows:

The particles of the nerves have a naturally assigned position (*Rev.* <note>Delboeuf's articles in the *Revue Philosophique* (1877–1878).</note> p. 246) and exist in a naturally assigned <term>state of vibration</term> <note>original: "Schwingungszustand"; the rhythmic movement of nerve particles.</note> around this position (*Théor.* p. 25–26, *Rev.* p. 245). The external stimulus is also generally regarded as being in a state of vibration (at least explicitly according to *Rev.* p. 246). Under the influence of the force <note>original: "force"</note> *) of the external stimulus, denoted by $p'$, the internal state of vibration changes and the position of the particles changes. The alteration of the internal state of vibration continues according to a law formulated by the author (*Théor.* p. 27) **) until its force, denoted by $p$, has become equal to the $p'$ of the external state of vibration; meanwhile, the position of the particles changes—against a resistance they encounter upon leaving their natural position—until the resistance, which grows with the deviation, has come into <term>equilibrium</term> with the altering force. In the *Théorie*, the author refers to the change in the internal state of vibration; in the *Revue* (p. 246), he refers to the change in position. In either case, the state of the nerve under the influence of a stimulus with which it has reached equilibrium is comparable to a <term>state of tension</term> <note>original: "Spannungszustand".</note>. Sensation, however, is proportional to the **work** <note>original: "Arbeit" or "travail".</note> $T$ by which this change of the internal state of vibration and the...

---

*) Insofar as I have been able to follow the author, the *force* brought into play here is characterized by him only through the effects he attributes to it and through comparisons. I do not find it stated that he means the <term>kinetic energy</term> <note>original: "lebendige Kraft"; literally "living force," a historical term for $mv^2$, the precursor to the modern formula for kinetic energy.</note> of vibrations; yet there is likely nothing else to be considered.

**) If $p'$ is constant, and $p$ as a weaker force was initially $= p^0$, then the time $t$ in which $p^0$ has risen to $p$ will be $t = \frac{1}{k} \log \frac{p' - p^0}{p' - p}$ where $k$ is a constant.

<summary>Fechner explains Delboeuf's mathematical formula for sensation, describing it as a result of nerve particles shifting from their natural state of vibration into a new state of equilibrium and tension caused by external stimuli.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical derivation of Delboeuf’s "fundamental formula," specifically focusing on the concept of "work" (T). Fechner is explaining how Delboeuf uses an analogy from thermodynamics—the expansion of gas—to describe the internal physiological work performed by the nervous system when responding to a stimulus.</meta>

...the change in the position of the particles is accomplished (*Théor.* p. 47, *Rev.* p. 246), and this work **T** is (*Théor.* p. 47) comparable to that with which the change of the pressure **p** and the volume **v** of a mass of gas enclosed in a cylinder with a movable piston takes place at a constant temperature, when the pressure **p** is transformed into pressure **p'** and **v** into **v'**, whereby in this process:

->**$T = c \log. \frac{v}{v'} = c \log. \frac{p'}{p}$**<-

where **c** is a constant. *)

Without the influence of a stimulus **p'**, the sentient being exists (according to *Théor.* p. 31) in a state of <term>natural equilibrium</term> <note>original: "Gleichgewichtszustande (équilibre naturel)"</note>, comparable to that in which a violin string finds itself when it is stretched but left to its own devices. However, this state of the string must be clearly distinguished from the state of tension and equilibrium <note>original: "Zustande der Spannung und des Gleichgewichts (équilibre de tension)"</note> in which the string finds itself when it is held away from its natural position by an external force; it is with this latter state that the condition of the sentient being is comparable when **p**, through the effect of **p'**, has reached equilibrium with it. Now, there exists a maximum and minimum of **p** which cannot be exceeded in either direction without the organ suffering, just as a string breaks if it is stretched too strongly by being pulled from its natural position. The state of natural equilibrium lies in the middle between these two limits. Sensory sensation is (according to *Théor.* 31, 34, 43 ff.) accompanied either by a feeling of fatigue, exhaustion, or pain, or a feeling of well-being and pleasure,

---

*) Namely: "Let **p** be the pressure and **v** the volume (expressed by the height of the gas when it is enclosed in a cylinder), then one has [according to <term>Boyle-Mariotte's Law</term> <note>A physics law stating that the pressure and volume of a gas are inversely proportional at a constant temperature.</note>]: $pv = \text{Const.}$ Let **T** be the <term>work</term> <note>In physics, 'work' (T) is the energy transferred to or from an object via the application of force along a displacement.</note>, then it will be:

->$\partial T = - p \partial v = - c \frac{\partial v}{v}$<-

from which one derives:

->$T = - c \int_v^{v'} \frac{\partial v}{v} = c \log. \frac{v}{v'}$<-

and, by replacing **v** with its function in relation to **p**:

->$T = c \log. \frac{p'}{p}$<-

<meta>This page continues the critique of Joseph Delboeuf’s psychophysical theories. The previous page discussed Delboeuf's use of a "natural equilibrium" state (comparable to a violin string) and how internal "work" (T) changes when a stimulus (p') acts upon an internal state (p). We are now moving into how these changes relate to the experience of sensation and pain.</meta>

...depending on whether one approaches one of these two limits or the <term>natural state</term> <note>original: "natürlichen Zustande"</note>. This feeling always blends (according to *Theory* p. 32) with the sensation and is usually quite masked by it; however, if the former feeling becomes too strong, it in turn masks the sensation; the sensation then disappears, so to speak, and gives way to pain. The author also develops formulas for this feeling based on theoretical views (p. 32 ff.), though I do not wish to go into those here.

Since Delboeuf’s fundamental view and formula are essentially built upon the fact that <term>sensation</term> <note>original: "Empfindung"</note> diminishes under a constant <term>stimulus</term> <note>original: "Reiz"</note>, and that this can even lead to the vanishing of the sensation \*) , it naturally explains or rather covers this fact excellently; however, it certainly encounters other difficulties because of this.

An application of Delboeuf’s view and formula to <term>inner psychophysics</term> <note>Fechner’s term for the study of the immediate relationship between the mind and the internal activity of the nervous system, as opposed to "outer psychophysics" which looks at external stimuli</note> seems excluded in principle from the start, since all sensation is supposed to depend on changes of an internal *p* through an external *p'*; Delboeuf himself has not entered into such an application. However, there may be no essential difficulty in this regard, insofar as one could also speak of external excitations of the nervous system within our own body—namely, originating from other organic systems—and could make the subjective sensations that arise without the presence of objective stimuli dependent upon them. On the other hand, Delboeuf’s formula lacks the <term>threshold</term> <note>original: "Schwellenwerth"; the minimum intensity a stimulus must reach to be consciously perceived</note>, regarding which one may compare the remarks under Section IX. Furthermore, it brings sensations of temperature under such a common point of view with sensations of light and sound that the following distinction, which must be made between them, does not receive its due. Between the sensation of cold and warmth, there is a <term>zero-state</term> <note>original: "Nullzustand"</note> of temperature sensation; there is no such zero-state between the sensation of black and white in light sensation, nor between the sensation of silence and the...

---

\*) According to our view, this fact depends on the source of the <term>psychophysical activity</term>—which can be triggered by a continuous stimulus—becoming more and more exhausted, so that under certain circumstances (for it is not the case universally, e.g., in light sensation) it can be depressed to the <term>threshold</term> <note>original: "Schwellenwerth"</note> and even below it.

<summary>Fechner critiques Delboeuf’s formula for failing to account for the sensory threshold and for ignoring the unique "zero-point" found in temperature sensations but absent in light and sound. He also explains the transition from sensation to pain as a masking effect of internal tension.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Joseph Delboeuf’s psychophysical theories, specifically his attempt to create a universal formula for sensation. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing how sensation relates to internal states; this page begins with the conclusion of that thought regarding the "zero-state" of sound.</meta>

...of noise a <term>zero-state</term> <note>A point where no sensation is perceived despite the presence of a potential stimulus.</note> of sound sensation. I believe that the relationships of dependency of temperature sensation on external temperature are, in fact, too different from the relationships of dependency of light and sound sensation on external light and sound stimuli to force the same formula upon them.

However, the most important objection seems to me to lie in the following. Grey always provides us with a positive sensation of light, whether we transition to it from a prolonged observation of white or black; and specifically, setting aside the fact that in the first case the eye is more fatigued than in the second, it is the same positive sensation of light. According to the consequence of Delboeuf’s view and formula, however, grey would provide us in the first case with a sensation of the opposite character than in the second; in the first case a negative sensation, and in the second a positive one. For in order to have the sensation of white, the *p* <note>Delboeuf uses *p* to represent the internal state or "tension" of the sensory organ.</note> of the eye must have approached the *p'* <note>Delboeuf uses *p'* to represent the external stimulus.</note> of the white, the more so the longer the white was observed; so that it finally must have risen above the *p'* of the subsequently observed grey, which is the condition for a negative sensation of grey. In the transition from black to grey, everything is the opposite. Now, one will admittedly feel in the transition from white to grey that the grey has a "minus" of brightness compared to the white, and in the opposite direction of transition, that it has a "plus" of brightness compared to the black; but that is precisely just a matter of the sensation of a *difference*. The sensation of brightness of the grey itself remains the same, just as the number 2 remains the same number whether one transitions to it from 1 or from 3, even if it is found to be a "plus" compared to 1 and a "minus" compared to 3. Now Delboeuf might counter that he generally only posits <term>contrast-sensations</term>; however, I believe that one must distinguish between the inherent intensity of a sensation and the relationship of the plus and minus of its intensity to other sensations. For between what is the relationship of plus and minus to take place if one denies the sensations a strength in themselves? There would be nothing there for it to relate to. In fact, it seems to me that Delboeuf confuses or mixes up that which is merely a matter of <term>difference-sensation</term> <note>original: "Unterschiedsempfindung"</note> with that which is a matter of <term>absolute sensation</term>. For the former, I have the <term>formula for the measurement of difference</term> <note>original: "Unterschiedsmassformel"</note>, for

<summary>Fechner argues that Delboeuf’s psychophysical formula fails to distinguish between the absolute intensity of a sensation (like the color grey) and the relative sensation of change or contrast. He posits that while our perception of a stimulus depends on what we saw previously, the sensation itself still possesses an inherent, positive value.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner's critique of Joseph Delboeuf’s "Theory of Sensibility." Fechner is specifically challenging Delboeuf's idea that sensation only occurs during a state of change or transition toward equilibrium, rather than as a steady state.</meta>

### Fechner, On Matters of Psychophysics. 3

...this is the simple <term>measurement formula</term> <note>A mathematical rule used to calculate the relationship between physical stimulus and mental sensation.</note>. Delboeuf uses only one formula for both.

The following is related to what was just said: Since there is no level poorer in light than the blackest black to which one could transition from that point, the sensation of pure black—where it exists—can, according to Delboeuf, only have a negative character. This would be based on its relationship to a sensation of greater brightness that had previously acted upon the eye. However, this does not do justice to the very weak, but nonetheless positive, value of light sensation that "black" holds at the lower end of the scale of light sensations. In reality, black transitions continuously through shades of grey all the way to white.

I also find a difficulty in the fact that I cannot quite imagine how, according to Delboeuf, a *lasting* sensation of black can exist at all; and yet, the sensation of black is indeed a sensation distinct from "nothing." Suppose it is a pitch-black night around me; I close my eyes as well; there is no light stimulus from the outside world. Yet, according to the logic on page 31, I can still make the sensation of black dependent on the fact that the internal *p* <note>Delboeuf uses *p* to represent the internal "tension" or state of the sensory organ.</note> of my visual apparatus differed from the *p'* <note>Delboeuf uses *p'* to represent the external stimulus or the state of the surrounding environment.</note> in its surroundings, and that the sensation of black occurred in the transition from *p* to equilibrium with *p'*. But surely it cannot take forever for this equilibrium to be reached; why then do I continue to see black? Why does the sensation of it not at least diminish, since according to his theory, the sensation should cease once equilibrium is reached and should decrease during the transition toward it? Delboeuf admittedly cites examples where a continuous, uniform noise eventually ceases to be felt; but if his explanation for that were sufficient, it would have to apply to light just as well as to sound.

Indeed, how is it that we can perceive brightness for an entire day? If I understand him correctly based on his *Theory* <note>original: "Théor."; referring to Delboeuf’s 1876 work, *Théorie générale de la sensibilité*.</note> page 39, he explains it as follows: There is objectively brighter and darker material around us; let us say, for the sake of simplicity, white and black. If the eye remained completely immobile in the same relationship to these, and if the external conditions of brightness did not change, the sensation of both white and black would gradually cease as the *p* of every part of the visual apparatus <note>The text "A ----- s" likely refers to *Auges* (eye) or *Apparates* (apparatus).</note>

<summary>Fechner argues against Delboeuf’s theory that sensation requires a transition toward equilibrium, noting that we can perceive the sensation of "black" even in total darkness for long periods without the sensation fading to nothing.</summary>
<keywords>psychophysics, Joseph Delboeuf, sensation of black, equilibrium, light sensation, measurement formula, visual apparatus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Joseph Delboeuf’s "Theory of Sensibility." Fechner is debating Delboeuf’s mathematical models for how we perceive light, specifically the transition between internal states of the eye and external light stimuli.</meta>

...equilibrated with the *p'* of the external light falling upon it. However, the eye constantly shifts back and forth from white to black and vice versa; or rather, black and white externally alternate in their relationship to the same spots on the <term>retina</term> <note>The light-sensitive layer at the back of the eye.</note>. Consequently, a lasting equilibrium is never achieved, and the light sensation at a single spot fluctuates between a positive and negative character. But if this were possible, then during the transition between the positive and negative states, we would repeatedly see nothing—or almost nothing—at various points on the retina; yet we certainly see something with every part of the retina throughout the entire day. (Compare also Section XII on this point.)

Finally, I find that Delboeuf only considers the contrast between a current sensation and the *preceding* sensation as an influence on the value of that sensation. In my view, the contrast that occurs, for example, between *neighboring* areas of white and black, demands just as much consideration.

These are difficulties that—regardless of Delboeuf’s theoretical assumptions, in which I simply find nothing binding—prevent me from agreeing with his <term>measurement formula</term> <note>A mathematical equation intended to calculate the intensity of a sensation based on the strength of a stimulus.</note> and the way he applies it.

Since Delboeuf discussed his valuable experiments (which will be addressed in Section 17) in relation to his earlier views in his *Étude* <note>original: "Étude"; referring to Delboeuf's 1873 work *Étude sur la psychophysique* (Study on Psychophysics).</note>, it is unavoidable that we also refer to them. Therefore, the following points should be briefly mentioned.

In the *Étude*, Delboeuf also assumes that every sense organ is already internally excited, even apart from any external stimulus. However, he allows this internal excitation—the magnitude of which he labels *c*—to play a completely different role than *p* plays in his current view. According to him, this internal excitation *c* does not provide a sensation in itself; it merely offers "a living and sensitive background" <note>original: "un fond vivant et sensible"</note> for the emergence of a sensation. Accordingly, he does not wish for *c* in visual sensation to be identified with the brightness of <term>eye-black</term> <note>The greyish-black "internal light" one perceives in total darkness, caused by the spontaneous firing of neurons in the visual system.</note>—which he ignores entirely—as I do. Furthermore, while the *p* in his newer view is highly variable along with the external stimulus and can rise until it is equivalent to it, Delboeuf instead introduced *c* into his formulas as a constant that always remains small, though he admitted that this constancy was not fully maintained during his experiments.

In order to correspond as closely as possible to observations while avoiding the <term>threshold</term> <note>The minimum amount of stimulus required to produce a sensation.</note> and negative sensation values, Delboeuf proposes the following (*Étude* pages 34, 35):

### Hering.

Hering's <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918) was a physiologist and Fechner's primary intellectual rival, known for his "opponent-process" theory of color vision.</note> objections are so multifaceted that, following the very general summary of them on page 5, I will waive a more specific presentation of them here, in order to address them more closely only in the following sections—and his name will be found in and across almost every one of them. In general, he has attempted to fundamentally reshape the entire <term>psychophysical theory of sensation</term> <note>The study of the quantitative relationship between physical stimuli and the mental sensations they produce.</note>—so far especially regarding the field of light sensations, though with a promised expansion—and has thereby also come into conflict with my views from a positive standpoint. In his 5th and 6th communication, he proposes a **chemical** theory of light sensations; and although, according to the discussions in Section XIII, such a theory is actually quite compatible with the <term>oscillation view</term> <note>Fechner's belief that sensations are caused by physical "oscillations" or vibrations in the nervous system.</note> I maintain, it is not so in the way the author conceives it. However, the contradiction of his views against mine is primarily asserted in the way he conceives (in his 4th communication) of the relationship between the sensations of black and white, insofar as the sensation of black is, according to him, just as positive as that of white, and the various degrees of brightness sensation can be represented by the combination of different degrees of the sensation of black and white.

To the extent that Hering still considers the concept of intensity applicable to light sensation and other sensations, he sets it—in agreement with Mach <note>Ernst Mach (1838–1916), a physicist and philosopher who argued that sensations are the fundamental building blocks of science.</note> and many others—simply proportional to the underlying psychophysical activity, instead of in <term>logarithmic dependence</term> <note>Fechner's Law states that the intensity of a sensation increases as the logarithm of the stimulus, meaning the physical world must increase exponentially for us to perceive a steady increase in sensation.</note> on it, as I do.

I will address Hering's objections, which are directed specifically against my views and formulas, in most of the following sections (V, VI, VII, VIII); his views on the sensations of black and white in Section XIV; his ge-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ewald Hering’s theories, specifically focusing on his "chemical theory" of vision. Fechner contrasts Hering’s approach with his own and provides direct quotations from Hering's fifth communication to explain the concepts of assimilation and dissimilation.</meta>

weight experiments under XVIII. The most general perspectives of **Hering’s chemical theory** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918) proposed that vision is not just a physical response to light intensity, but a metabolic process involving the constant breaking down and rebuilding of chemicals in the eye.</note> can be found in his own words in the following insertion; more specific details are in the appendix to XIV, whereas in XIII and XIV, I give voice to my own view, which differs significantly from his. To my knowledge, Hering has not yet established his own psychophysical formulas, unless one wishes to count his ideal formulas for the composition of the sensation of *gray* from those of *black* and *white* as such.

> "To the two qualities of sensation which we designate as white or light and black or dark, there correspond (according to Hering’s 5th Communication, p. 9) two different qualities of chemical activity in the <term>visual substance</term> <note>Hering’s term for the hypothetical chemical matter in the retina or nervous system that reacts to light.</note>; and to the different proportions of clarity or intensity with which those two sensations emerge in the individual transitions between pure white and pure black, or the proportions in which they appear mixed, there correspond the same proportions of the intensities of those two psychophysical processes." . . .

> "We must assume a substance in the nervous visual apparatus which undergoes a change under the influence of incoming light, and this change—regardless of how it might be characterized physically—is nevertheless, as nerve physiology must assume, simultaneously a chemical process. Once the influence of light has ceased, the altered (more or less 'fatigued') substance returns sooner or later to its original state. This return can again be nothing other than a chemical change in the opposite sense. If one wishes to interpret the chemical change of the excitable substance occurring under the direct influence of light as a partial consumption, then one must designate the return to the former condition as a restitution; if one regards the former as a process of splitting, then one must see the latter as a synthetic process. The latter process, through which the living organic substance replaces the loss suffered through excitation or activity, is also commonly referred to as **assimilation** <note>In modern terms, this is "anabolism"—the metabolic process of building up complex molecules from simpler ones.</note>, and I wish to retain this expression. During excitation or activity, every living or excitable organic substance, according to general assumption, now forms certain chemical products. I will analogously designate the emergence of these products as the process of **dissimilation** <note>In modern terms, "catabolism"—the metabolic process of breaking down molecules to release energy.</note>." . . .

> "The sensation of white or light now corresponds to dissimilation, while the sensation of black or dark corresponds to the assimilation of the visual substance. . . . What comes to our consciousness as a visual sensation is the psychic expression or the conscious correlate of the <term>metabolism</term> <note>original: *Stoffwechsel*</note> of the visual substance."

For the further development of these propositions, one should refer to Hering’s 5th and 6th communications themselves and the appendix to Section XIV.

<summary>Fechner introduces Ewald Hering’s chemical theory of vision, which argues that sensations of light and dark are the mental results of metabolic changes (assimilation and dissimilation) in the visual system.</summary>
<keywords>Ewald Hering, chemical theory of vision, visual substance, assimilation, dissimilation, metabolism, light sensation, psychophysical processes</keywords>

### Langer.

Langer repeatedly aligns himself with the objections raised by <term>Helmholtz</term>, <term>Aubert</term>, and <term>Hering</term> <note>Hermann von Helmholtz, Hermann Aubert, and Ewald Hering were the leading physiologists and physicists of the 19th century who often debated Fechner’s psychophysical methods.</note>, developing these objections further in several respects and adding his own—particularly against my conception of **negative sensation values** and the **threshold**. While he still allows for a <term>logarithmic measurement formula</term> <note>The Weber-Fechner law, which states that the intensity of a sensation is proportional to the logarithm of the stimulus intensity.</note> to remain valid, he structures it in a significantly different way than I have.

Reserving a detailed examination of those objections by Langer that more or less coincide with other opponents for later sections (V, VI, X), I will for now focus only on those points that are unique to him.

1) As a fundamental objection, he raises the following against the **negative sensation values**—which derive from my measurement formula—for **stimulus values below the threshold**:

> »Negative sensation values must, under all circumstances, be such that when additively combined with equally large positive values, they yield the value 0; this alone corresponds to the concept of the opposition between the positive and the negative. — If one imagined a finite stimulus that evoked sensation $\gamma$, and another stimulus smaller than the threshold value that evoked sensation $-\gamma$, then the simultaneous action of both stimuli would have to evoke sensation 0 if the relationship were to hold. In every single instance of opposition, this way of looking at things applies, because it constitutes the definition of the opposition of positive and negative magnitudes.«

Now, it is true that if I let the two stimuli $\beta = nb$ and $\beta = \frac{b}{n}$ (where $n$ is arbitrary and $b$ is the threshold value) act individually, they yield the sensations $+\gamma$ and $-\gamma$ according to my measurement formula $\gamma = k \log \frac{\beta}{b}$. However, if I let them act together as $nb + \frac{b}{n}$, they yield, instead of sensation 0, the sensation $k \log (n + \frac{1}{n})$, which is greater than either of the two sensations $k \log n$ and $k \log \frac{1}{n}$ on its own. But is it not a strange requirement that they must yield zero in order to justify the establishment of negative sensation values in contrast to positive ones

<note>Vocabulary: Langer, Helmholtz, Aubert, Hering, measurement formula, sensation values, threshold, stimulus values, threshold value, opposition.</note>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the researcher Langer, specifically addressing his mathematical objections to how sensation and stimulus are related below the threshold of consciousness. ...to find [it] justified as a function of $\beta$ <note>$\beta$ (beta) represents the intensity of a physical stimulus, such as light or sound.</note>. If the correct definition of the opposition between positive and negative quantities (of equal absolute value) is that both added together yield zero, it still does not follow that, in a case of functional dependence on other values, the **function of the sum** of these values would also equal zero. For example:

The <term>trigonometric functions</term> <note>Mathematical functions relating the angles of a triangle to its sides, such as sine and cosine.</note> cos. 0° and cos. 180° stand in mathematical opposition as $+1$ and $-1$, in that they yield 0 when added. Now, if one adds the angle 0° to 180° and takes the cosine of the result, one does not get 0, as **Langer** demands, but $-1$. Thus, one also cannot demand that for

->$+\gamma = k \log n$ and $-\gamma = k \log \frac{1}{n}$<-

to stand in mathematical opposition, the stimulus $nb$ combined with the stimulus $\frac{b}{n}$ must yield a sensation of zero <note>Fechner is arguing that the relationship between stimulus and sensation is logarithmic, meaning they don't add up in a simple linear way.</note>.

2) **Langer** expresses (p. 58) **Weber's Law**—which is expressed by our fundamental formula (p. 10)

->$\partial\gamma = K \frac{\partial\beta}{\beta}$<-

only with different symbols and in a different arrangement, as follows:

->$u = k \beta$<-

where $u$ represents the <term>just noticeable stimulus difference</term> <note>The smallest change in a stimulus that a person can detect.</note> $\partial\beta$, and $k$ represents the constant value $\partial\gamma$ of our formula (assuming a constant ratio $\frac{u}{\beta}$), and finds the law inadequate. First, he argues that the value $\frac{u}{\beta}$, according to experiments—particularly those of **Aubert**—attains a <term>minimal value</term> <note>Hermann Aubert found that Weber's Law, which suggests a constant ratio, actually fails at very high or very low intensities.</note> (and thus the relative differential sensitivity attains a maximal value) as $\beta$ increases. Secondly,

> »because the law contradicts the fact of the threshold, since the just noticeable stimulus difference must merge into the stimulus threshold when $\beta$ is the smaller of the two stimuli forming the just noticeable stimulus difference $u$.«

As for the first point, **Weber's Law**, established as fundamental for <term>inner psychophysics</term> <note>The study of the direct relationship between the soul/sensation and the brain/nervous system, rather than the external physical stimulus.</note>, certainly does not cover the occurrence of that minimal value; however, before one uses this occurrence itself as a fundamental argument against **Weber's Law**, one must first investigate whether it does not belong to the expe-

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal of Langer’s mathematical and conceptual critiques of Weber’s Law. ...experimental deviations which the fundamental law cannot cover at all. One can certainly argue against the view I advocated in the 6th section—that such deviations belong to the law. If **Langer** himself merely intended to establish an *experimentally* applicable formula (a point about which one remains in doubt), it would have required much more than simply including that specific circumstance in his formula to justify it *experimentally*. By the same token, however, it would require much more than what is found in **Langer** to use the exclusion of that empirical circumstance from **Weber's Law**—which is established as a fundamental law—as a valid argument against the law itself.

Regarding the second point, the *fact* of the *threshold* <note>The "threshold" (Schwelle) is the point at which a stimulus—like a sound or a weight—is just strong enough to be perceived by the mind.</note> is only contradicted by **Weber's Law** if one uses a conception of the *threshold* that differs essentially from ours. This is connected to **Langer's** objections against our *negative sensation values* <note>Fechner used negative numbers to mathematically represent "unconscious" sensations—stimuli that are physically present but too weak to be consciously felt.</note>, about which I will say a few words in the following insertion.

**Langer's** requirement that when $\beta = 0$ <note>$\beta$ (beta) represents the intensity of the physical stimulus.</note>, the value $u$ should equal the *stimulus threshold*, stems from the fact that he assumes the *zero-value* of *sensation* occurs at the *zero-value* of $\beta$ (the physical stimulus). In contrast, we assume the zero-point of sensation occurs at a finite, positive value of $\beta$ <note>In other words, Fechner believes you need a certain amount of physical energy before you feel anything at all; Langer suggests sensation starts growing the moment any energy is present.</note>. Consequently, in line with his objection mentioned above, he does not recognize any *negative sensation values*, whereas in my view, *negative sensation values* are inextricably linked with a *stimulus threshold*. What **Langer** calls the *stimulus threshold* (which we denote as $b$) does indeed separate *conscious* from *unconscious* sensations according to him as well; however, for him, *unconscious* sensations are merely very small sensations. The distinction he makes between his *conscious* and *unconscious* sensations (p. 54) has not become quite clear to me; one should read **Langer** himself on this point. For further remarks on this, see Section 10.

3) On p. 21, **Langer** presents a consideration from which he concludes: if "just noticeable" difference sensations were truly to correspond to equal sensations of difference, one would have to assume that:

> "the uncertainty with which one remembers a past magnitude of sensation—the amount by which one can deviate from the true sensation when judging it through memory—is completely independent of the magnitude of the sensations,"

which, while possible to assume hypothetically for the sake of the theory, is anything but probable. — Now, however, I am of the opinion that since difference sensations <term>Unterschiedsempfindungen</term> <note>The mental perception of the difference between two stimuli, such as realizing one weight is slightly heavier than another.</note>, like simple sensations, appear psychophysically as a function of stimulus differences and stimuli, the discussion

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal of Langer’s critique. While the previous page addressed the mathematical definition of the "threshold" where physical stimuli become conscious sensations, Fechner now turns to the role of memory and specific experimental data from his weight-lifting studies.</meta>

...not at all about the influence of memory in question being kept within the realm of pure sensation, as **Langer** does, but rather must be conducted with regard to the stimuli; and I present the following brief consideration in opposition to it.

If one accepts the law that <term>stimulus differences<note>The physical change in a stimulus (like adding weight) required for a person to notice a difference.</note></term> at various levels of the <term>scale of stimuli<note>The range of physical intensities, from very weak to very strong.</note></term> are estimated as equal by sensation if they have an equal ratio to their stimuli, then the <term>average errors<note>The typical degree of inaccuracy in a person's judgment during a test.</note></term>—which one commits under otherwise identical circumstances (while avoiding or eliminating constant errors) when estimating two stimuli as equal at different levels of the stimulus scale—must also have an equal ratio to their stimuli (upon which the <term>method of average error<note>A psychological testing method where a subject tries to adjust one stimulus until it matches a "standard" stimulus; the inaccuracy of their match reveals their sensitivity.</note></term> is based). This will also apply to those estimation errors committed due to faulty memory, provided that—apart from the altered magnitude of the stimuli—the other conditions on which the durability of memory depends are kept equal: namely, that attention is always kept at the same level (or at the same average level across many trials for each degree of stimulus), and the time interval between the stimulus impacts is always kept equal, or at least equal on average. Under this condition—which is essential and one I have always maintained—errors in estimation due to faulty memory cannot cause a disruption of the above law, because they themselves follow the law.

4) A few more minor points: How **Langer** (p. 4) can say, 
> "that I have relegated the deviations of the numbers from **Weber’s Law** in my [weight] experiments to the category of observation errors" 
is incomprehensible to me, since that indeed did not occur to me, a fact which the discussion of these experiments in the *Elements* <note>Fechner’s foundational work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics).</note> can leave no doubt about. — **Langer** is no less mistaken when he says (p. 35): 
> "by **Fechner**, the validity of his psychophysical law has also been declared for <term>extensive sensations<note>Sensations involving spatial or temporal extension, such as the perceived length of a line, as opposed to "intensive" sensations like the loudness of a sound or the pressure of a weight.</note></term> in the same manner as for all other stimuli"
, since the exact opposite follows from *Elements* II. 336 (cf. also the following Section VII). — Finally, I do not know what flaw in my weight experiments **Langer** (p. 84) refers to when he claims that **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering, a contemporary physiologist and a frequent scientific rival of Fechner.</note> is supposed to have avoided it.
