2) that for $\beta = 0$, the value $u$ becomes equal to the <term>threshold value<note>The "Schwellenwert" or point at which a stimulus is strong enough to cross into conscious perception.</note></term> $b$ (in Langer’s sense), and he posits as the simplest expression of both conditions (p. 58)
-> $u = k\beta^2 + b$ <-
where $k$ is a constant; however, as the simplest possible assumption for the dependence of the psychological change on the physical change (p. 60): "that the change in <term>sensation differences<note>The internal, mental perception of how much a stimulus has changed.</note></term> is directly proportional in magnitude to the magnitude of the stimuli that form the just noticeable stimulus interval." On the basis of these prerequisites, he arrives at the following <term>measurement formula<note>A mathematical equation used to calculate the intensity of a mental sensation based on the physical intensity of a stimulus.</note></term> (p. 62) through a detailed analysis:
-> $\gamma = \frac{l}{2k} \text{ natural log. } \frac{k\beta^2 + b}{b}$ <note>original: "log. nat." (logarithmus naturalis). Fechner is discussing a natural logarithm formula.</note> <-
with which the following <term>fundamental formula<note>The basic differential equation from which the measurement formula is derived.</note></term> is connected:
-> $\partial\gamma = \frac{l\beta\partial\beta}{k\beta^2 + b}$ <-
In this, $b$ signifies the constant stimulus threshold in Langer's sense, while $l$ and $k$ are other constants.
These formulas naturally represent the author's theoretical assumptions along with the experimental fact of that "minimum," insofar as they are based upon it, without providing proof that those assumptions are tenable, or that this fact exists for <term>inner psychophysics<note>The study of the relationship between the soul/mind and the internal workings of the body (nerves/brain).</note></term>—for which a fundamental formula must apply—just as it does for <term>outer psychophysics<note>The study of the relationship between the soul/mind and external physical stimuli.</note></term>. They have not been numerically compared with actual observations by the author.
In any case, no one will find it *probable* from the outset that such complicated formulas possess a fundamental significance for the relationship between psychological and physical values.
In addition to the measurement formula above, Langer develops (p. 65)—by means of a corresponding determination of the <term>constant of integration<note>A constant added to the end of an integral to represent values that are lost during differentiation; here used to set the "zero point" of sensation.</note></term>—another formula for the case where one only wishes to consider conscious sensations in his sense, where the stimulus exceeds the Langerian threshold value $b$, according to which one has:
-> $\gamma = \frac{l}{2k} \text{ natural log. } \left( \frac{k\beta^2 + b}{kb^2 + b} \right)$ <-
For $\beta < 0$ <note>This likely refers to stimuli below the threshold of detection, though mathematically $\beta$ represents stimulus intensity.</note>, this formula yields negative values of $\gamma$, which are therefore, according to Langer, unusable.

<summary>Fechner analyzes Langer’s proposed mathematical formulas for stimulus thresholds and sensation, criticizing them for being overly complex and lacking empirical comparison to real-world observations. He highlights that Langer's models result in "unusable" negative values for sensations below a certain threshold.</summary>
<keywords>Langer, stimulus threshold, measurement formula, fundamental formula, inner psychophysics, outer psychophysics, natural logarithm, mathematical modeling</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Fechner's specific mathematical critique of Langer’s formulas to a broader defense of his psychophysical system. He begins addressing major criticisms from contemporary scholars—Brentano, Hering, and Langer—who challenged the logical foundations of Weber's Law, specifically the assumption that all "just noticeable differences" are psychologically equal.</meta>

## V. Objection that Weber’s Law was incorrectly inferred from the experiments (Brentano, Hering, Langer).

My entire <term>psychophysical system<note>The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the mental sensations they produce.</note></term> would collapse along with the <term>threshold law<note>The principle that a stimulus must reach a certain minimum intensity (the threshold) before it can be perceived at all.</note></term> and <term>Weber’s Law<note>The rule stating that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note></term>, insofar as the other main laws of the system rely upon them. Now, while there is hardly anything to object to regarding the former law from an experimental standpoint, much more has been raised against the latter. Thus, a fundamental question for the stability of the whole system is whether the objections against this law are justified.

According to the previous formulation of Weber’s Law, it is based on the idea that the **sensation differences** (or increases) corresponding to given **stimulus differences** (or increases) remain equal to one another if the stimulus differences (or increases) maintain the same ratio to the stimuli between which the difference exists. In short, it holds if the <term>relative stimulus difference<note>The ratio of the change in a stimulus to the original stimulus (e.g., adding 1 gram to 10 grams is a 1/10 ratio).</note></term> remains the same, or if the stimuli maintain the same ratio to each other, regardless of the absolute magnitude of the stimulus differences and the stimuli themselves.

However, some object (**Brentano**, **Hering**, **Langer**) that the facts from which the law is supposed to be derived—which generally relate to small differences or increases in sensation—say nothing about such a law. They merely state that the difference in sensation remains "just noticeable" whenever the relative stimulus difference remains constant. But the idea that "just noticeable" sensation differences are always equal to one another across different levels of stimulus intensity is a completely arbitrary assumption. Yet, the <term>fundamental formula<note>The basic mathematical expression representing Weber's Law.</note></term> as an expression of Weber’s Law, and consequently the <term>measurement formula<note>The equation derived from the fundamental formula used to calculate the magnitude of a mental sensation.</note></term>, both rely on this very assumption.

To this, I have the following to reply:

If, during experiments with just noticeable differences, one does not specifically ensure that they appear as equal as possible to the senses, then it is naturally to be expected that they will not be equal; but it seemed self-evident to me that one would pay attention to this. In the meantime, I have addressed this even more explicitly than in my *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's 1860 book, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics), the founding text of the field.</note>, where I only [discussed] the methods of measurement...

<summary>Fechner defends the logical basis of Weber's Law against critics like Brentano and Hering, who argued that "just noticeable" sensations are not necessarily equal units of measurement. Fechner maintains that the perceived equality of these differences is a necessary and observable foundation for his mathematical formulas.</summary>

<keywords>Weber's Law, threshold law, psychophysical system, stimulus difference, sensation difference, Franz Brentano, Ewald Hering, J.J. Langer, fundamental formula, measurement formula, just noticeable difference</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense against the criticisms of Ewald Hering regarding the "Method of Just Noticeable Differences." Fechner is addressing the subjective nature of these measurements and whether a human observer can truly judge when two small differences in sensation are "equal."</meta>

...briefly mentioned, expressed in my lectures on these methods with the following sentence:

> "Experience teaches that in the experiments one can, so to speak, come to an understanding with oneself regarding the sensation of a small but still surely perceived difference; that one can reproduce this sensation nearly exactly—if not absolutely—across various trials; and that one can obtain a reliable average result by multiplying the number of trials."

Now, I cannot guarantee that all observers proceed in the experiments as it seems to me one should proceed, if the method is not to become altogether illusory. **Hering**, of course, assures me in the correspondence carried out between us that he and the students he enlisted for the experiments do not proceed in this way, and that a judgment of the equality of small perceived differences at different <term>stimulus levels<note>The various intensities of physical energy, such as light or weight, used in the experiments.</note></term> is not possible at all. I, however, believe and find for myself that it is indeed possible within certain limits. Furthermore, everyone must concede that the inequality of sensation differences, even at different stimulus levels, cannot exceed certain limits without being recognized as a difference. 

But no matter; it is of little consequence. For, even if in my opinion one must truly try to keep the deviation from the <term>zero-sensation<note>original: "Nullempfindung." The point at which an observer perceives no difference at all between two stimuli.</note></term> of a difference as equal as possible when applying the method, it can easily be shown that an inequality in this deviation cannot significantly disturb the law to be inferred from the experiments, as long as the deviation itself remains very small *), as is inherent in the concept of the **method of just noticeable differences**. For then the deviation can be twice as large at one time as at another, and this will mean no more nor less for the conclusions one wishes to draw from the experiments regarding the validity of the law in my formulation than a double accidental observation error means in experiments in other fields; and one never gets away without observation errors. And that is actually—

---

*) By **very small**, I mean so small that the increase in stimulus difference associated with the deviation of the sensation of difference from the zero-point has a small ratio to the stimulus difference itself, which corresponds to the exact zero-sensation of the difference.

<summary>Fechner defends his experimental method by arguing that while subjective judgments of "just noticeable differences" may vary, these variations function like standard observation errors and do not invalidate the underlying psychophysical laws.</summary>
<keywords>Ewald Hering, stimulus levels, zero-sensation, sensation differences, method of just noticeable differences, observation error, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to Ewald Hering. He is arguing that even if a human observer cannot perfectly identify the exact moment a difference becomes "just noticeable," the resulting "errors" are statistically insignificant when compared to the larger ratios being measured.</meta>

...the central point of the method. Indeed, let us assume, for example, that in weight experiments a difference of 5 grams disappears at one point, and another time, with heavier weights, a difference of 10 grams disappears—under circumstances kept as similar as possible—**exactly** for the sensation; one will nevertheless, in the method of just noticeable differences, have to increase the physical difference slightly in order to find it **surely** noticeable, so as neither to remain in the realm of the unnoticeable, nor to stumble into the <term>method of right and wrong cases<note>Now commonly known in psychology as the "Method of Constant Stimuli." In this approach, a subject is presented with various stimuli and must judge whether they are "greater than," "less than," or "equal to" a standard.</note></term>, which must be handled differently. 

Whether this error is 0.1 grams in one case due to an unsuccessful estimation of equality, or 0.2 or 0.3 grams in the other, one will only be committing an error of the order of common observational errors. This is because, for the stated purpose of these experiments, one does not draw conclusions from the ratio of 0.1 to 0.3, but from the ratio of 5.1 to 10.3 grams. Furthermore, one may assume that the estimation errors of the just noticeable difference itself are subject to <term>Weber’s Law<note>The principle that the smallest change in a stimulus that can be perceived is a constant proportion of the original stimulus (e.g., adding 1 ounce to 10 ounces is as noticeable as adding 1 pound to 10 pounds).</note></term>. If one could, of course, hit the threshold of difference exactly in all experiments, it would be all the better; however, one does approach this ideal case in the method of just noticeable differences.

Despite all this, this method is not the most precise, but only the most convenient, and it is inferior to other methods in the former regard; however, this does not mean that all results obtained with it are to be rejected, a point I have already addressed in the *Elements* <note>Fechner refers here to his foundational 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics).</note>. Weber’s Law, however, by no means rests solely on the method of just noticeable differences; rather, in the realm of light sensations, it can primarily appeal to <term>star magnitude estimates<note>The classification system used by astronomers to rank the brightness of stars. Fechner used this as "real-world" proof that our senses perceive logarithmic increases in light as linear steps in brightness.</note></term>. Yet the star magnitudes designated with whole numbers are so far from deviating from one another merely by just noticeable differences that astronomers even insert tenths of magnitudes between them. <term>Plateau’s method<note>A technique developed by Joseph Plateau where an observer adjusts a gray color until it appears to be exactly halfway between a white and a black sample.</note></term> returns to an analogous principle. In more general terms, however, the law is supported by the results of the <term>method of average error<note>An experimental setup where the participant themselves adjusts a stimulus (like the length of a line) until it appears equal to a standard.</note></term> and the method of right and wrong cases, which—as far as comparisons have been possible—agree with those of the method of just noticeable differences.

<summary>Fechner defends the "Method of Just Noticeable Differences" by arguing that small human errors in judgment do not invalidate the broader ratios that prove Weber's Law. He highlights that his theories are supported not just by his own experiments, but also by astronomical data on star brightness and other psychophysical methods.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, just noticeable difference, star magnitudes, method of average error, method of right and wrong cases, Joseph Plateau, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of the methods used to prove Weber's Law. He is transitioning from a technical discussion of experimental error to a deeper philosophical and psychological objection raised by his contemporaries regarding the nature of sensation itself.</meta>

...have shown unanimously, and under the measures suitable for eliminating constant errors, are much freer from subjective estimation errors than the <term>method of just noticeable differences<note>A psychophysical procedure where a participant adjusts a stimulus until they can just barely perceive a change.</note></term>. The objections against the derivation of <term>Weber's Law<note>The principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of that stimulus.</note></term> from the results of the method of just noticeable differences, should they be tenable at all, would thus remain fruitless as long as they cannot be extended to the other methods.

Now, an objection can indeed be raised against all methods, including the method of just noticeable differences, from a perspective that *Brentano* (p. 88) has in mind<note>Franz Brentano (1838–1917) was a philosopher and psychologist who famously critiqued Fechner’s mathematical approach to the soul.</note> when he admits that just noticeable increases in sensation are equally noticeable, but not for that reason that they are equal—a perspective which *Hering* <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918) was Fechner's primary scientific rival, who argued for a more physiological rather than mathematical understanding of perception.</note> emphasizes with particular weight in his correspondence with me, and which *Langer* (p. 23 ff.) also comes to discuss (regarding the power of memory).

Let this objection be illustrated here by weight experiments.

One lifts a weight; it arouses a certain sensation. After some time, one lifts a heavier weight; it arouses a stronger sensation. These sensations are given; the difference between these sensations is given—it is quite definite and fixed, regardless of how it appears to me when I judge it by calling upon memory (which is not at all certain) after comparing the second lifted weight with the first. Depending on the time interval between the lifts, the attention I have applied, the way the weights are attached, my subjective moods—generally according to the most diverse incidental circumstances—I may find the difference to be varying, non-existent, distinct, or indistinct, while it nevertheless remains one and the same in its specific magnitude. In any case, the actual difference in sensations (*sensation-difference* according to my terminology) must first reach a certain magnitude to be recognized as existing at all (as a perceived difference or *sensation of difference* according to my terminology), while much smaller differences already truly exist.

---

<note>*) That this is the case, I recognize more clearly from my correspondence with him than from his work itself.</note>

<summary>Fechner addresses a major psychological critique: the distinction between the objective internal difference between two sensations and our subjective ability to perceive that difference. He argues that even if our perception is unreliable due to memory or mood, the underlying sensations maintain a fixed relationship.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, just noticeable differences, Franz Brentano, Ewald Hering, sensation-difference, sensation of difference, weight experiments, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal against his critics, specifically addressing the philosophical gap between what a person "perceives" as a difference and what the "actual" mathematical difference in sensation might be. He is defending the logical foundations of his psychophysical laws.</meta>

Now people ask—or at least, I can only interpret the somewhat less clearly stated objection in this way: how can you claim that the **real** differences between two sensations—your "sensation differences"—are equal to one another, when the **apparent** differences—your "perceived differences"—are equal to one another, while the latter can have a completely different magnitude even for the same real differences? Your entire psychophysical structure of formulas, however, rests upon this not only unproven, but downright false assumption.

To this, I respond: I assume—and I believe it to be a fairly self-evident assumption—that the magnitude which the difference *appears* to have during the experiment depends on two things: first, the magnitude of the **real** sensation difference; second, on secondary circumstances <note>original: "Nebenumstände." Fechner refers to extraneous variables like fatigue, attention, or the order in which weights are lifted.</note>, such as those mentioned above. To this, I add a second assumption—again, the most natural one that can be made—that if, in experiments with variable <term>stimulus magnitudes<note>The physical intensity of a stimulus, such as the actual weight in grams or the brightness of a light.</note></term>, the secondary circumstances are kept equal, the consistency of the apparent sensation difference can only depend on a consistency of the true one. In short: the real sensation difference remains constant when the apparent or perceived one remains constant and when the secondary circumstances remain constant. In fact, I do not know what more probable or alternative assumption I should make at all.

The task of every good series of experiments in this field, however, is to keep the secondary conditions—which can influence the modification of the perceived difference—as constant as possible throughout the entire series of experiments with varying stimulus ratios; or, to compensate for unavoidable inequalities by counterbalancing them in an equal number of trials, thereby eliminating <term>constant errors<note>Systematic errors that consistently skew results in one direction, such as a person always perceiving the second weight lifted as heavier simply because of the order.</note></term>. One can see for oneself from my *Elements* <note>Referring to his 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics).</note> Vol. I, p. 99, that I, for my part, spared no care in this regard during the weight experiments; meanwhile, with other observers, at least the **self-evident** precautions in this regard can naturally be assumed to have been taken. However, if not all observers have observed **everything** required in this regard, and specifically if constant errors depending on a one-sided direction of the experiments were neither sufficiently avoided everywhere, nor...

<summary>Fechner defends the logical link between perceived sensation and real sensation, arguing that if experimental conditions are controlled, a constant perceived difference must reflect a constant real difference. He emphasizes the importance of eliminating systematic errors to maintain the integrity of psychophysical formulas.</summary>

<keywords>Gustav Theodor Fechner, Psychophysics, sensation differences, stimulus magnitudes, constant errors, experimental methodology, Elements of Psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical methods against Ewald Hering. On the previous page, Fechner argued that if "secondary circumstances" (extraneous variables) are kept constant, then the perceived difference in sensation must accurately reflect the real difference in sensation.</meta>

...nor have the experiments been conducted in a suitable manner to eliminate them; thus, I believe that this has contributed much more to the empirical deviations from the law than to its approximate confirmation, which is nonetheless generally pervasive in experimental results.

The question remains whether any new objections persist against this. **Naturally, one cannot assert that the altered magnitude of the main weight is itself a secondary circumstance—one that would alter the judgment of the sensation difference—when the very task at hand is to investigate the dependence of the sensation difference on those altered stimulus conditions.** **Hering** **presupposes that the actual sensation of weight increases proportionally with the weight; thus, an increase of 1 added to weight 1 would increase the weight sensation of 1 by 1, and an increase of 1 added to weight 2 would increase the sensation of 2 again by 1.** <note>Hering’s theory of proportionality suggests a linear relationship: if you double the physical weight, you double the mental sensation. Fechner, conversely, argues for a logarithmic relationship (Weber's Law), where it takes more and more physical weight to produce the same "step" in sensation.</note> In experiments involving small weight increases while varying the <term>main weight<note>The "standard" or "reference" weight to which a smaller "increment" weight is added to see if the subject can tell the difference.</note></term>, this law is not confirmed—not even according to Hering’s own experiments (see XVIII); and if one were to assume that this failure depends on the magnitude of the weight altering the judgment of the sensation difference, one would naturally ask why this is not also the case with large increases from 1 to 1, or from 1 to 2. How, then, could Hering’s law even stand? In short, one would fall into an internal contradiction.

Meanwhile, an objection that Hering raised—partly in his writing and partly in correspondence—takes a different turn: that with changes to the main weight in weight experiments, or light intensity in light experiments—essentially everywhere—changes in "secondary circumstances" are summoned up. These circumstances must truly be counted as such, and they prevent the requirement of "constant secondary circumstances" from being met despite all care taken. This would explain the deviation of experimental results from the law of <term>proportionality<note>The idea that sensation and stimulus grow at the same rate.</note></term> just as much as it would justify the apparent approximation of **Weber's Law**.

In this regard, Hering first notes (p. 28) regarding **light experiments**—following several considerations that do not immediately apply here—that firstly, the **pupil** contracts as the **illumination** of visible things increases and dilates as it decreases; as a result, it admits relatively less light...

<summary>Fechner addresses Ewald Hering’s critique that physical changes in a stimulus (like weight or light) automatically trigger physiological changes, such as pupil dilation, which invalidate the "constant conditions" required for psychophysical laws.</summary>

<keywords>Ewald Hering, Weber's Law, sensation of weight, light experiments, pupil, secondary circumstances, proportionality, stimulus magnitude</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical laws against critics like Aubert and Hering. On the previous page, Fechner addressed the role of "secondary circumstances" in experiments, specifically how the magnitude of weights might influence a subject's judgment. Here, he shifts the focus to light sensations and the physical adaptation of the eye.</meta>

...or allows more light to enter; and secondly, that the nervous apparatus of the eye adapts to the altered illumination—that is, its sensitivity decreases under increased lighting. This fact was proven long ago by striking evidence, investigated in detail recently by Aubert, and finally subjected to a physiological explanation by him. He concludes (p. 29) with the following remark:

> "Fechner, when discussing the validity of his law for light sensations, took the necessary consideration of neither the adaptation of the pupil nor the retina."

I cannot concede this last point in any way. Rather, it seems to me that neither Aubert nor Hering have taken necessary consideration of what I explained in the 12th section of my *Elements* <note>Referring to his 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (*Elements of Psychophysics*), the founding text of the field.</note> (Vol. I, p. 300) regarding adaptation—even if I did not use that exact name—based on both others' and my own experiments. From that explanation, it emerges that in experiments on **differential sensitivity** <term>Unterschiedsempfindlichkeit<note>The ability to detect the smallest possible difference between two stimuli, such as two slightly different brightnesses. This is the basis for Weber's Law.</note></term>, of the kind used to establish Weber's Law, so-called adaptation only matters as a disturbance when the external light is so weak that the internal brightness of the **eye-black** <term>Augenschwarz<note>Also known as "intrinsic light" or "retinal gray." It refers to the sensation of faint light or grayness that the eye perceives even in total darkness, caused by the spontaneous activity of the retina.</note></term> becomes a significant factor in proportion to it.\*) This is what leads to the so-called "lower deviation" from Weber’s Law and is partly responsible for it. The same applies to changes in the size of the pupil. The common principle here is that both components being compared—the difference of which is being observed—are affected in the same proportion by the strengthening or weakening influence of adaptation or pupil changes (whether internal or external).

It is quite different with experiments on **absolute sensitivity**. Here, adaptation plays a major role, but it seems to me that when raising this objection, the two types of sensitivity were not clearly distinguished at all.

Moreover, if adaptation at light intensities that are not too weak had the great influence on experiments regarding **differential sensitivity** that Aubert and Hering attribute to it, then Aubert, in his own experiments which he considers authoritative...

---

\*) Aubert’s experiments on adaptation were indeed conducted with very weak light influences.

<summary>Fechner defends his psychophysical laws against the charge that he ignored how the eye adapts to light, arguing that such adaptation only significantly skews results at very low light levels where the "intrinsic light" of the eye interferes.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, adaptation, retina, pupil, differential sensitivity, absolute sensitivity, eye-black, Augenschwarz, Aubert, Hering</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal of his critics, specifically Hermann Aubert and Ewald Hering. On the previous page, Fechner discussed how the eye adapts to light and argued that his "Elements of Psychophysics" already accounted for these changes. He now transitions from discussing light sensitivity to discussing the perception of weight, quoting Hering’s physiological explanations of how we "feel" a load.</meta>

Fechner, In the Matter of Psychophysics. 4

...sought to account for and eliminate the influence of adaptation as much as possible, for which a statement can be found on page 68 of his publication; indeed, he himself could not assign to the values in his experimental tables the significance he does, if he did not present them as being as free as possible from disturbance through adaptation. Why then is there—though certainly not emphasized by Aubert, yet follows from his experiments—this great approximation of the results to **Weber's Law** <term>Webersche Gesetz<note>A fundamental principle in psychophysics stating that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is proportional to the original intensity of the stimulus.</note></term>, which I mention in Section 16, provided one does not follow his experiments down to intensities too far below the usual use of the eye? Including those extremely low intensities, of course, makes the law appear quite faulty.

Regarding the weight experiments, the following remarks from a letter from **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a prominent physiologist and Fechner’s chief intellectual rival, who preferred physiological explanations over Fechner's mathematical ones.</note> to me may find a place here in literal reproduction.

1) > "When we lift two different weights one after the other for the purpose of distinguishing between them, we give our muscles the same **volitional impulse** <term>Willensimpuls<note>The conscious mental signal sent to the muscles to initiate movement.</note></term> for both lifts, for we do not know in advance which weight would require the greater muscular effort. The lighter weight now rises more rapidly than the heavier one, and we distinguish quite well the faster movement of the lifted arm from the slower one. For every movement of the arm gives us a whole series of so-called **sensations of movement** <term>Bewegungsempfindungen<note>A 19th-century term for kinesthesia—the sense that allows us to perceive the location and movement of our body parts.</note></term>, which follow one another more quickly with the lighter weight than with the heavier. We have a **control-sense** <term>Controlsinn</term> for every muscle motion. For the muscles of the limbs, this control is mediated by the sensations of movement which arise in the sensory nerves of the skin, especially in the regions of the joints, as well as certain deeper-seated parts."

2) > "We feel the effort that we need to hold the weight at all, for first we must hold it before we can lift it. We feel, furthermore, the muscular effort which is necessary for the lifting itself, and finally that which we must expend to maintain the balance of the whole body during lifting, or when sitting, at least of the upper body."

3) > "In lifting weights, sensations of pressure and tension arise at the spots on the skin with which the load is held..."

<summary>Fechner argues that Aubert's experimental data actually supports Weber's Law under normal conditions. He then shares a letter from Ewald Hering, who explains that our perception of weight depends on the speed of movement and internal sensations in the joints and skin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s presentation of a letter from his rival, Ewald Hering. Hering is critiquing Fechner’s mathematical approach to "intensity," arguing that what Fechner calls a single "intensity" of weight is actually a messy, shifting collection of different physical feelings in the muscles and skin.</meta>

...[the sensation] occurs and at which points it effectively takes hold. These sensations are all the stronger and more extensive the greater the weight is. They change very significantly during the act of lifting, and the nature of their change is different with heavy weights than with lighter ones."

> "This is only a fleeting sketch of the manifold sensations that come into consideration here. It depends entirely on the size of the weights, the manner of lifting, the body position, etc., whether we turn our attention more to one group of sensations or another during the lift. In any case, the entire **sensation complex** <term>Empfindungscomplex<note>Hering’s term for the total "package" of feelings—pressure on the skin, tension in the tendons, and muscle effort—that occur all at once.</note></term> always becomes more multifaceted and extensive the greater the weight lifted is."

> "You proceed somewhat summarily by simply grouping this entire vast diversity of sensations under the concept of **intensity** and designating the highly varied changes of the sensation complex in composition, chronological sequence, spatial distribution, etc., simply as changes in intensity."

> "Since, as I mentioned, new sensations are always added when the weight becomes heavier, and the total sensation becomes increasingly complicated and takes hold of ever new areas of skin and muscle, it seems to me from the outset extremely probable that, so to speak, the clarity of the sensation and its reliable comprehension suffer more and more, and that ever larger differences in the sensation complex and correspondingly larger differences in weight are necessary to obtain the same degree of **noticeability** <term>Merklichkeit<note>The threshold or "just-noticeable difference" (JND) at which a person can tell two weights apart.</note></term> of the difference and the same relative number of correct decisions. This explains why 'more intense' weight sensations also require larger 'differences in intensity' if the noticeability of the difference is to remain the same."

Against this, the following.

At any rate, my own weight experiments cited in the *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's seminal 1860 work, *Elements of Psychophysics*.</note> are not affected by a portion of the secondary circumstances asserted above. I did not lift the heavier weight more slowly than the lighter one; rather, I kept the time and height of the lifting equal according to a strictly defined **measure of time and space** <term>Zeit- und Raummass<note>Fechner used a metronome and a physical scale to ensure his movements were identical every time, aiming for scientific consistency.</note></term>. Nor did I first [hold] the weights for a period of time...

<summary>Ewald Hering argues that perceiving weight is a complex physical experience involving various muscles and skin areas, rather than a simple measurement of "intensity." Fechner begins his rebuttal by defending his experimental rigor, specifically his use of standardized timing and movement.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner's rebuttal to Ewald Hering’s criticisms. In the previous page, Hering argued that "weight" isn't a single sensation but a complex "package" of muscle and skin feelings that changes as weights get heavier. Here, Fechner defends his experimental rigor and introduces a new chapter on why experimental results often deviate from the mathematical ideal of Weber's Law.</meta>

held, before I lifted them; instead, each was lifted directly from the **floor** at the precise moment determined by the counter <note>likely a metronome or similar timing device used to ensure consistent movements</note>. (**Regarding this, one should see my Elements.** <note>Fechner refers to his 1860 foundational work, *Elements of Psychophysics*.</note>) What **Hering** otherwise remarks generally concerns a "composition of sensations" that increase along with the weight, because the bodily changes upon which they depend also increase. If, therefore, **Weber's Law** <term>Weber's Law<note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two weights is a constant ratio of the starting weight.</note></term> possesses a certain generality, no disturbance of the law will arise from this complication; rather, the law will simply extend to the entire complication. This does not yet constitute a distracting side-circumstance for establishing the law in general; it only hinders the specialization of the law for individual sensory fields. However, I admit that with all of this—which in my opinion cannot be coordinated as indiscriminately as **Hering** has done—it remains conceivable that side-circumstances really do change as the primary weight increases, even when one uses the greatest care to prevent such changes. Thus, my methods may be better suited to explain experimental deviations from the law than to provide an approximate confirmation of it through trials.

Now, **Hering** certainly believes he can prove the invalidity of **Weber's Law** through direct experiments with weights; however, I will address this specifically in Section 18.

---

### VI. Objections regarding the experimental deviations from Weber's Law (Aubert, Delboeuf, Hering, Langer).

Insofar as the laws in Section 2 are claimed to be strict and fundamental only for the dependence of sensation on **psychophysical activity** <term>psychophysical activity<note>Fechner’s term for the internal physical process (like nerve firing) that directly causes a mental sensation.</note></term>, they can only be confirmed by experiments on the dependence of sensation on the **external stimulus** to the extent that one can assume:

1) that the **external stimulus** triggers a psychophysical activity proportional to it;
2) that no factors independent of the **external stimulus effect**

<summary>Fechner defends his experimental methods against Hering’s critique of "sensation complexes" and begins analyzing why real-world experiments often show deviations from the ideal mathematical predictions of Weber’s Law.</summary>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on why experimental data might not perfectly match Weber’s Law. On the previous page, he addressed Ewald Hering’s critique regarding the complexity of sensations. Here, he begins to explain the "upper" and "lower" limits of the law, where biological factors interfere with mathematical proportionality.</meta>

...already internally, a certain psychophysical arousal exists of the same nature as that triggered by the stimulus, which should actually be attributed to it.

In fact, however, obvious disturbances to the experimental verifiability of *Weber’s Law* <term>Weber's Law<note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note></term> occur for both reasons.

When the stimulus becomes so strong that the sensory organ is destroyed, or suffers near that point, the proportionality between stimulus and psychophysical arousal can no longer be expected; indeed, since the source of psychophysical power required for a sensation to occur is indisputably just as limited in humans as the source of living force <term>living force<note>In 19th-century science, "lebendige Kraft" (vis viva) referred to what we now call kinetic energy, though here Fechner applies it to the biological energy required for muscle movement.</note></term> required for muscle movement, it should not be surprising if, even before the sensory organ suffers damage, the psychophysical activity triggered by increasing levels of stimulus falls short of being proportional to that stimulus. *) On the other hand, various facts prove that the *second cause* of deviation is also present. In every sensory field, hallucinations can arise depending on internal arousal; it is at least very probable that this is merely the amplification of an internal arousal already present everywhere in a weak degree (below the threshold). The experience of "seeing black" <note>original: "Schwarzsehen"</note> in the eye when closed or in total nocturnal darkness (which is quite different from simply not seeing one's finger) proves that a normally existing internal arousal already exists for the eye; more detailed discussions on this can be found in *Elements*, Volume I, page 165 and following. It also belongs to this point that the arm raised with a weight already has a "weight-moment" <term>weight-moment<note>The inherent physical weight and resistance of the limb itself, which must be factored into any measurement of external weights.</note></term> quite apart from the weight being held.

The first reason for deviation must become more noticeable in experiments with external stimuli the higher one rises with the application of the stimulus, which I briefly call the *upper deviation from the law*; the *second* becomes more noticeable the lower one descends, which I call the *lower deviation*. Meanwhile, at moderate levels of external stimulus, the weak internal arousal can be viewed as almost negligible compared to the external one.

---

*) In the *Elements*, I only pointed out the first point (the suffering of the organ), but I asserted the second—which likely deserves even more important consideration—in the treatise against **Aubert** (1860).

<summary>Fechner explains that Weber’s Law deviates at extremes: an "upper deviation" occurs when the sense organ is overwhelmed or depleted of energy, and a "lower deviation" occurs when internal bodily sensations (like the weight of one's own arm or the eye's intrinsic light) interfere with small stimuli.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, psychophysical arousal, upper deviation, lower deviation, sensory organs, internal stimulus, Hermann Aubert, intrinsic light</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner’s analysis of Weber’s Law. He is explaining that while the law states a constant ratio for sensation, in real-world experiments, our sensitivity is actually best in the "middle" range of stimuli, tapering off at the extreme highs and lows due to biological and physical factors.</meta>

By virtue of the lower deviation, the just noticeable relative stimulus difference—which is equally noticeable in the sense of the previous section—increases more the closer one approaches the lower limit of stimulus action, where only internal arousal remains. Conversely, by virtue of the upper deviation, this difference increases the higher one rises with the stimulus effect. This leads to the fact that in the middle ranges, there is a minimum of the just noticeable relative stimulus difference, and thus, according to the explanation given on page 13, a maximum of **relative differential sensitivity** <term>differential sensitivity<note>The ability of a person to detect the smallest possible change in a stimulus, such as a slight increase in weight or brightness.</note></term> occurs. Around this middle range, the approximation to *Weber’s Law* is at its most precise.

Undeniably, it is in keeping with the **tendency toward purposiveness** <note>original: "Zweckmässigkeitstendenz"; this refers to the biological principle that organisms are designed to function most efficiently in the environments they encounter most often.</note> observed elsewhere in the organism that this closest approximation to the law—and the greatest relative sensitivity—is found within the limits of ordinary sensory use. If *Weber’s Law* for **outer psychophysics** <term>outer psychophysics<note>The study of the relationship between physical stimuli (like light or sound) and the resulting mental sensation.</note></term> were to exist without these reasons for deviation, there would be no minimum for the just noticeable relative stimulus difference; instead, it would remain constant throughout the entire scale of stimuli. However, one must view this experimental minimum as a necessary consequence of the directions taken by the lower and upper deviations.

Beyond the causes of these deviations named here, which seem quite clear to me, there may possibly be others that are simply less certain. The stimulus might first have to overcome a certain **inertia of the nerve molecules** before it triggers a vibration in them; thus, the **threshold** <term>threshold<note>The "Schwelle" or point at which a physical cause is strong enough to produce a perceptible effect.</note></term>, which already asserts itself in the physical realm between many causes and effects, could play a role here. Furthermore, in Section VIII (page 77), a circumstance will be considered that might contribute to either the lower or upper deviation, or perhaps both.

Finally, deviations that are not essential in themselves but are grounded only in external experimental conditions can occur. These may depend on the fact that the stimuli being compared are not presented in the same spatial or temporal position, or that the comparison is always made in a one-sided direction from larger to smaller or vice versa. These circumstances are the basis for so-called **constant errors**, which are either compensated for by reversing the experimental positions and directions in repeated trials—

<meta>catchword: com- (likely "compensiert", meaning compensated or balanced out)</meta>

<summary>Fechner explains that our senses are most accurate in the "middle" range of intensity, which he attributes to biological efficiency. He also introduces the idea that nerves might have a physical "inertia" and discusses how experimental biases, or "constant errors," can skew data.</summary>

Everything Hering charges against the hypothesis of Weber’s Law in this regard applies much more strongly to his own hypothesis—the simple **proportionality of sensation** <note>Hering argued for a linear relationship (1:1) between stimulus and sensation, whereas Fechner argued for a logarithmic relationship.</note> to the stimulus or to psychophysical activity. Indeed, I believe that no simple hypothesis can be formulated at all that would not be affected by this to an even greater degree. Hering himself asserts numerous reasons that could impair the pure outcome of experiments (cf. p. 49); but he has not proven—and it would likely be difficult for him to prove—that these factors produce an approximation of the very Weber's Law he disputes, rather than...

Now, however, the very fact that Weber's Law emerges again and again as an approximation under such varying and seemingly incomparable circumstances—in areas as diverse as those found in Section 16—seems to me, if anything, to suggest that it represents the common fundamental core of truth beneath and between all deviations from it. I confess I do not understand how this reason *for* the law could be used as an argument *against* it.

If the objections against Weber's Law based on deviations were to gain any significance at all, it would have to be proven that the causes of the lower and upper deviations I have asserted are not permissible, or cannot have the effect I attribute to them. Furthermore, it would have to be shown that the significant approximation to Weber's Law, which has been demonstrated for a considerable interval of intensities across a majority of domains, allows for a more probable interpretation than that it is an approximation of a law that is merely disturbed by the conditions of **outer psychophysics** <term>outer psychophysics<note>The study of the relationship between physical stimuli (the "outer" world) and the resulting mental sensation.</note></term>...

Perhaps one might remark that, since we possess no direct means of observation and only more or less uncertain means of inference for recognizing and tracing the relationship between psychic and psycho-physical activity, whereas we do have means of observation for tracing the relationship between external stimulus and sensation, all practical interest depends on the latter alone; and it might be best not to concern oneself at all with the former relationship, and thus with an **inner psychophysics** <note>Fechner defines "inner psychophysics" as the study of the relationship between the soul (the mental) and the body's internal activities, such as neural impulses. And why should one not allow such formulas to stand, insofar as they achieve their purpose and as long as they are not intended to replace fundamental formulas for inner psychophysics or be unclearly lumped together with them according to their significance.

However, less weight should be placed on such empirical formulas because they must necessarily turn out differently according to the differences in **experimental subjects**, experimental circumstances, and sensory domains. In any case, the practical interest that one might attribute to outer psychophysics and the formulas appropriately adapted for it recedes far behind the deeper scientific interest—already touched upon in the introduction—which inner psychophysics gains for psychology, physiology, and philosophy by offering fundamental determinations for the relationship between **body and soul**; and the experiments of outer psychophysics can only serve this interest by establishing **probabilistic inferences** <note>original: "Wahrscheinlichkeitsschlüsse." Fechner admits that because we cannot see the brain's internal activity directly, we must use the data from external experiments to make educated guesses about what is happening inside.</note> for the establishment of these determinations, although it is indeed to be regretted that the certainty of such inferences is limited by experimental deviations of the kind discussed.

## VII. Teleological Objection and Remarks on the Extensive Sensations (Hering).

Hering argues in several places (p. 12, 13, 23, 24, 30) that a nearly decisive point against the fundamental psychophysical laws I support is that, according to them, the psychic perception of the relationship between two physical magnitudes—whether they be **extensive** <note>In this context, "extensive" refers to spatial dimensions like length or area.</note> or **intensive** <note>"Intensive" refers to the strength of a sensation, such as the brightness of light or the loudness of a sound.</note> magnitudes $\beta$ and $\beta'$—would have to change if both were increased or decreased proportionally to their size (and thus in the same ratio). He claims this is not only empirically incorrect but also **teleologically** <note>A "teleological" argument is one based on the idea of purpose or design. Hering is arguing that our senses must have been designed to perceive the world accurately, otherwise we would be constantly confused.</note> inadmissible, because if it were the case, confusion would necessarily enter our perception of the relationships of the external world.

> "How—he says, among other things (p. 22)—shall the soul be able to correctly perceive the ratios of the external world if no proportionality exists between the things and events of the external world and those of our internal world; if the measure which the soul applies to external things, namely the magnitude or strength of its sensations, does not fit these external things at all?"

Regarding the **extensive sensations**, I cannot accept from the outset an objection drawn from them, based on remarks to be made later. However, Hering also extends the objection to **intensive sensations**; and against this, I first offer the following.

Even the measure of sensation evoked by a single stimulus $\beta$ is not actually determined solely by a relationship to the stimulus itself, but by its relationship to its **threshold value** $b$ <note>The "threshold" is the minimum level of physical stimulus required for a person to detect a sensation.</note>; in short, according to my derivation, it is: \*)
$= k \log \frac{\beta}{b}$. Instead of the threshold value $b$, however, any other stimulus value $\beta_1$ can enter into the formula, following the method of the derivation itself. In short, the impression that the stimulus ratio $\frac{\beta}{\beta_1}$ makes on the...

---

\*) As a side note to this, I by no means added the logarithmic law of measurement as a "hypothesis" to the Weber's Law <note>Weber's Law states that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> I adopted, as Hering says (p. 19), but rather derived it as a mathematical consequence of Weber's Law and the Law of the Threshold.

<summary>Fechner addresses Ewald Hering's objection that psychophysical laws should show direct proportionality between stimulus and sensation to avoid "perceptual confusion." Fechner clarifies that his logarithmic law is a mathematical derivation based on thresholds, not a mere hypothesis.</summary>

sensation makes, is measured by $k \log \frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime}$ <note>This is a variation of Fechner's Law. It expresses the sensory difference between two stimuli ($\beta$ and $\beta_\prime$) as the logarithm of their ratio. $k$ is a constant specific to the sense being measured.</note>. Now, whether both stimuli increase or decrease by the ratio $m$, the impression is measured accordingly by $k \log \frac{m\beta}{m\beta_\prime}$, which is equal to $k \log \frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime}$; it therefore remains **unchanged**. I should think that Hering's arguments have been addressed simply enough in this regard, at least from one side. To prevent certain confusions or misunderstandings that could easily arise from this, however, I am adding a few supplementary remarks in an insertion at the end of this section.

It remains true, of course, that according to the laws and formulas I have established, neither individual sensations nor changes in sensation are simply proportional to the stimuli and changes in stimuli; only the impression made by the ratios of the stimuli when perceived together remains the same when the stimuli change in the same ratio—two things which must, in fact, be kept distinct.*) But regardless of whether the laws I have proposed hold true or not, with or without them, there is **factually no proportionality** between stimuli and sensations, or between stimulus changes and sensation changes, and teleology <note>The study of purpose or design in nature; here, the idea that our senses are "designed" to help us navigate the world accurately.</note> manages to deal with that; so why accuse our **laws** of making it impossible for teleology to function? Would that be the case if they feigned a proportionality that does not actually exist?

When I bring a light into a dark room, what an enormous increase in brightness for my eye! If I add a second one, thus doubling the physical brightness, the brightness for my eye does not double; instead, it increases only a relatively small amount. This agrees with our laws; but even if it did not, it would agree just as little with proportionality. A light in a mirror appears almost as bright to me as the light in front of the mirror, even though—

One should look at Aubert's <note>Hermann Aubert (1826–1892), a physiologist known for his work on psychophysics and the "Aubert-Hering law" of brightness.</note> experiments in the field of light, with which some believe they can overturn <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant proportion of the original stimulus.</note>; one should see whether they do not even more severely overturn the proportionality required by teleology <note>The philosophical idea that our senses are "designed" with a specific purpose, such as giving us a perfectly accurate 1:1 representation of the world.</note>.

This is, of course, so obvious that **Hering** himself explicitly does not extend his teleological objection to light intensities; rather, after some preliminary discussions in this regard (*p. 26*), he remarks: "it appears from the outset quite immaterial whether we can perceive the quantities of light reflected from different things and under different illumination in their correct proportions—that is, according to the law of proportionality between stimulus and sensation," whereas "proportionality between the actual spatial dimensions and the spatial dimensions of sensation, as well as between actual weights and the magnitudes of weight sensations, seems indispensable if we are to perceive the external world correctly and master it through our movements."

Very well then, our laws would at least be safe from the teleological objection regarding light sensations, and should be no less safe regarding sensations of sound. But now, look at **Hering’s** own weight experiments, which I share in Section 18, and see if they fit the law of proportionality any better than the light experiments. Increases in weight, which according to that law should be equal, relate to one another in one instance as 12 : 28, and in another even as 0.7 : 25.5. What does it mean, then, to assert a teleological demand against one law in favor of another, when the latter factually does not agree with it either?

To a millionaire, the increase of his fortune by 100 <term>Thalers</term> <note>An old German silver coin; Fechner is using an economic analogy to explain how our minds perceive "value" or "intensity" differently as the total amount grows.</note> is worth no more than an increase of 1 Thaler to a poor man. It does not "weigh" more for him, one might simply say. Even if one might argue whether this relationship between the "stimulus" of money and its estimation is teleologically appropriate or not—though I believe one would more easily decide for the former than the latter—it nevertheless exists in the world. Why, then, should there not be something corresponding between the external stimulus, be it light or weight, and its estimation through sensation? As great a friend of teleology as one may be—and I count myself...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical theories against contemporary critics, specifically addressing the application of Weber's Law to spatial perception versus intensity.</meta>

against [the claim] that “the apparent length of a line grows proportionally with its actual length, and that the <term>magnitude of sensation</term><note>German: *Empfindungsgrösse*; the internal, psychological strength of a feeling or perception.</note> increases proportionally with the <term>magnitude of the stimulus</term><note>German: *Reizgrösse*; the physical energy or dimension of the object being perceived.</note>,” so that a line twice as long also appears twice as long; which indeed contradicts the laws I have established just as much as it corresponds to the law of proportionality advocated by **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist who was often Fechner’s scientific rival.</note>. However, this could very well be because it is quite a different matter to seek a law for the case where a stimulus intensifies upon the *same nerve point* than for the case where it spreads across a *multitude* of such points. Only through an analogy can one infer from one case to the other, without any guarantee that the analogy holds for the essentially different conditions. Therefore, after having already acknowledged in the *Elements* (II. p. 336)—for the very reasons Hering asserted—that the applicability of **Weber’s Law** to the domain of extensive sensations is precarious, I must declare myself against the idea that an invalidity of the law in this domain (which I have admitted as very possible) should be used as an objection against my advocacy of it in another, essentially different domain. *) **Delboeuf** <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896), a Belgian philosopher and mathematician.</note> (*Revue Philosophique* p. 250) even sees a “regrettable factual and linguistic confusion” in speaking of sensations of time and extension and using the expression “extensive sensation” to designate them. However, since I use this expression myself, I would only agree with him if explicit explanations (such as in *Elements* I. 15) did not prevent such confusion. The motive for grouping what I call extensive sensations together with intensive sensations under a common expression, distinguishing them only by the adjective, is that even if there is no common unit of measurement, there are common **principles of measurement** and methods for them. Consequently, it is often necessary to be able to refer to both together in brief. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "precarious" nature of applying Weber’s Law—which usually describes how we perceive changes in weight or light—to the perception of physical space and the size of the retina.</meta>

...investigate in this regard. However, the reason I found the applicability of **Weber’s Law** to <term>extensive sensations</term><note>Sensations related to space and volume, such as the length of a line or the size of an object, rather than the "strength" of a feeling.</note> to be merely precarious, without reaching a final decision, despite that apparent contradiction, is as follows.

There is much to be said (according to *Elements* II. 336) for the assumption that, if the number of <term>sensory circles</term><note>German: *Empfindungskreise*; the minimal physical areas on the skin or retina that the nervous system perceives as a single "point."</note> in extensive sensations is to represent the strength of the stimulus in <term>intensive sensations</term><note>Sensations of degree or strength, such as how loud a sound is or how bright a light appears.</note>, a smaller and larger stimulus cannot be represented by a smaller and larger part of the <term>retina’s</term><note>The light-sensitive tissue at the back of the eye.</note> extent, but only by a smaller and larger *entire* retina. This is something we cannot create in our experiments, though nature has produced such variations in the eyes of different animals. It would still be quite compatible with this that every part of the retina appears large according to the actual ratio of the sensory circles within it to the total number of circles. Indeed, truly insoluble contradictions would arise if the appearance of size in the individual parts of the retina did not, in sum, reflect the appearance of size of the entire retina. Naturally, every fraction of an intensive <term>light stimulus</term><note>The physical energy of light hitting the eye before it is processed into a mental sensation.</note> contributes, in proportion to the whole intensity, to determining the magnitude of the total sensation—or how else would one conceive of it?—without the total sensation itself standing in a simple relationship to the total intensity. The only difference from the case of extensive sensations is that the parts of the intensive stimulus do not provide separately perceivable sensations in the same way that those of the extensive stimulus do.

Thus, I imagine that two animals, whose retinas relate to each other in size as *a* to *a'*, have visual fields that appear large to them in the ratio of the logarithm of *a* to the logarithm of *a'* (log *a* to log *a'*), whereas every fraction of their retina appears large to them according to its ratio to their entire retina. However, I admit the hypothetical nature of this way of thinking. What holds true for extensive visual sensations would also be transferable to extensive tactile sensations.

When **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a significant physiologist and contemporary rival to Fechner’s theories.</note> (p. 22) says: "I had supposedly made the <term>auxiliary hypothesis</term><note>An additional assumption used to protect a theory from being proven wrong by a specific contradiction.</note> for the eye, that for it, by virtue of a special arrangement, the ver—

---

—one just as much as on that of the others, and would have had to speak of it in a coherent <term>Psychophysics</term><note>The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the mental sensations they produce.</note>.

<summary>Fechner explores a theoretical reason why Weber's Law might not apply to spatial perception as simply as it does to intensity, suggesting that spatial perception depends on the total number of sensory points (retina size) rather than just the stimulus size.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his application of Weber’s Law to spatial perception, specifically addressing potential contradictions found in eye-measurement experiments and responding to criticisms from the physiologist Ewald Hering.</meta>

...confusion, which the <term>psychophysical law</term><note>Fechner’s mathematical formula (S = k log R) describing the relationship between the physical intensity of a stimulus and the mental perception of it.</note> would have to cause in vision, would be prevented," one will easily note against this that my hypothesis does not speak of a "special arrangement" of the eye, but rather of the possibility that **Weber's Law** <note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> is to be understood in a special way in relation to the eye.

Initially, of course, my own and <term>Volkmann's</term><note>Alfred Wilhelm Volkmann (1801–1877), a German physiologist who collaborated closely with Fechner on vision research.</note> <term>eye-measurement experiments</term><note>Experiments designed to test how accurately a person can judge the length of lines or distances using only their sight.</note> (*Elements* I. 211 ff.) may seem to contradict the previous view, insofar as Weber's Law is verified there not across whole <term>retinas</term><note>The light-sensitive inner surface of the eye.</note>, but on linear segments on the retina. This verification is factual, is very decisive, and cannot be dismissed by <term>Hering's objections</term><note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a major rival to Fechner and Helmholtz, who argued for a different physiological basis for perception.</note>. However, to resolve the contradiction that also seems to exist for us here, a twofold interpretation is possible.

On one hand, it can be thought that in these experiments involving small differences between the lines being compared, we do indeed compare both lines with each other—thanks to a corresponding direction of attention—rather than with the whole retina; meanwhile, without an occasion for such a comparison, we estimate every part of the retina as a fraction of the entire retina. Secondly, however, it is possible that these experiments—as already recalled in *Elements* I. 234 and II. 336—relate rather to the **muscular or movement sensation** called upon in the process as a direct <term>extensive sensation of measure</term><note>A feeling of "how much" space or distance is covered, derived here from the physical movement of the eye muscles tracking the lines.</note>. The latter is more probable because the verification of Weber's Law, which is so clear in the eye-measurement experiments, is not found in corresponding **tactile experiments** on the skin that I conducted, where the sense of movement is not called upon (*Elements* II. 353 ff.).

**Additional Remark to p. 58.** When considering the circumstances involved here, one must be careful not to confuse the **impression of size**—which the ratio of two compared stimuli $\beta, \beta_\prime$ makes on us—with the ratio of sensations (which does not fall under observation at all) that two stimuli awaken when they are **not** compared with each other. The measure of the first impression is $k \log \frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime}$, while the measure of the latter ratio (if one wishes to concern oneself with it) is $\frac{\log \frac{\beta}{b}}{\log \frac{\beta_\prime}{b}}$, which does not coincide with the former. Hering's objection would only apply to the latter impression, whereas the comparison of given impressions does not happen according to it.

<summary>Fechner explains that Weber’s Law applies to spatial vision through the "muscle sense" of moving the eye, rather than just the physical layout of the retina. He also provides a mathematical distinction to refute Hering's criticisms regarding how we compare two different stimuli.</summary>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s technical defense of the logarithmic relationship between stimulus and sensation. He is addressing the internal consistency of his mathematical model, specifically responding to potential objections regarding how we compare sensations in our minds versus how we perceive differences between physical stimuli.</meta>

One might perhaps counter-remark: "I can first consider two stimuli on their own, imprint their **magnitude-impression** upon myself, and then compare the sensations they have awakened in me individually; and yet their magnitude-ratio will appear the same to me as if I performed the comparison on the stimuli themselves." But comparing sensations in the soul—even those reproduced in memory—without simultaneously reproducing the corresponding <term>psychophysical activities</term><note>The physical processes in the brain or nervous system that Fechner believed were the direct counterpart to mental experiences.</note> is not possible. Thus, the first case is always re-established, though it is not exact due to the insufficiently precise reproduction of the original ratio through memory; hence, errors of estimation in memory are possible.

In addition, consider the following, where for the sake of a easier overview I set the <term>measure of sensation</term><note>Represented here by the Greek letter gamma ($\gamma$).</note> $\gamma$ simply $= \log \beta$, by imagining the constants $k$ and $b$ are determined such that they become $= 1$, although in principle nothing would prevent leaving them as arbitrary values.

One can note as a peculiarity in mathematics that the difference between the logarithms of two quantities $\beta$ and $\beta_{,}$ coincides with the logarithm of the ratio of both. There is no mathematical and therefore no logical contradiction in this, and so one cannot find a contradiction in the fact that the same is found in the logarithmic measurement-ratios of sensation. The difference between the logarithmic measures of the sensation of two stimuli $= \log \beta - \log \beta_{,}$ coincides with the measure of the sensation of the ratio of both stimuli $= \log \frac{\beta}{\beta_{,}}$.

Nor is there any real contradiction or conflict in this. For just as one must not confuse the ratio of the measures of two sensations $\frac{\log \beta}{\log \beta_{,}}$ with the measure of the sensation afforded by the ratio of the two stimuli being compared (i.e., <note>original: "d. i." (das ist)</note> with $\log \frac{\beta}{\beta_{,}}$), one must also not confuse the measure of the difference of both sensations (which coincides with the latter) with the measure of the sensation afforded by the difference of both stimuli. The latter is rather $\log \frac{\beta}{v\beta_{,}}$, if $v$ denotes the <term>ratio threshold</term><note>The minimum ratio between two stimuli required for a human to perceive a difference between them.</note>. Thus, the sensation afforded by the ratio of two stimuli by no means coincides with the sensation afforded by the difference of two stimuli; if that were the case, it would indeed constitute a contradiction. However, the absolute difference as well as the absolute ratio of two sensations is not a matter of observation at all.

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical laws against critics. Having previously discussed the relationship between physical stimuli and our sensations, he now moves to the "A Priori Objection"—a theoretical argument from other scientists that challenges his logarithmic law by suggesting that internal brain activity should have a direct, simple one-to-one relationship with sensation.</meta>

### VIII. A Priori Objection (Mach, Hering, Classen, Ueberhorst, Bernstein).

Deeper than the question belonging to <term>outer psychophysics</term><note>The study of the relationship between an external physical stimulus (like light or sound) and the resulting mental sensation.</note> discussed in the previous section—namely, whether sensation is proportional to the **stimulus**—lies the question belonging to <term>inner psychophysics</term><note>The study of the relationship between internal physiological activity (nerve impulses or brain states) and mental sensation.</note>: whether sensation is proportional to **psychophysical activity**. Since experiments cannot teach us anything directly about this, and conclusions drawn from experiments do not seem decisive, an "a priori" perspective (reasoning based on theory rather than direct observation) is asserted for this latter proportionality. This view has seemed obvious to many from the very start. Let us not withdraw from considering it.

Sensation depends on the stimulus only indirectly, but on the psychophysical activity **directly**; experimental disturbances of regularity—which involve the transfer of the stimulus effect onto the psychophysical system in outer psychophysics—fall away regarding the direct dependence between sensation and psychophysical activity. Inner psychophysics leaves such things behind. Now, it is thought to be most natural, indeed to some extent self-evident, that a simple proportionality exists between a cause and the effect that depends on it without any intermediate influence. Therefore, it is argued that proportionality must exist between the psychophysical activity and the sensation directly dependent on it, as well as between differences in psychophysical activity and the corresponding sensations of difference. 

However, facts from outer psychophysics seem to support <term>Weber’s Law</term><note>The principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus.</note> and the resulting logarithmic <term>measurement law</term><note>Fechner’s Formula ($S = k \log I$), which states that sensation increases as the logarithm of the stimulus intensity.</note>, which do not align with this simple proportionality. Consequently, critics argue that even if we concede an experimental approximation of these laws, they should only be claimed for outer psychophysics (the relationship between the stimulus and the psychophysical activity). They argue these laws should not be transferred into inner psychophysics, as we have done, nor translated into laws for the relationship between psychophysical activity and sensation.

Thus says **Mach** (no. 2. p. 11): 

> "The final nerve excitation and the sensation, which invariably go hand in hand, can probably not be anything other than proportional to one another."

->Fechner, In the Matter of Psychophysics. 5<-

<summary>Fechner addresses a major theoretical objection from contemporary scientists like Ernst Mach, who argued that while stimuli might relate to sensation logarithmically, the internal brain activity must relate to sensation in a direct, proportional way.</summary>
<keywords>inner psychophysics, outer psychophysics, Weber's Law, Ernst Mach, proportionality, psychophysical activity, nerve excitation, measurement law</keywords>
