<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of the "A Priori Objection." On the previous page, he established that critics believe there must be a simple, direct (linear) proportionality between internal brain activity and the sensation we feel. Here, he quotes specific scientists who hold this view—Hering, Classen, Ueberhorst, and Bernstein—before beginning his rebuttal by distinguishing between "sequential cause and effect" and "simultaneous states."</meta>

Hering (p. 21): "A proportionality between cause and effect, the acting force and the result, is understandable to us from the very beginning; however, a more complicated law of relations between the two is particularly difficult to grasp when, as in this case, the actor and the result depend on each other directly and not through intermediate links. Such a direct dependence, however, exists according to our assumption between psychic and psychophysical processes; for we designate as the latter exclusively those bodily processes with which the psychic event is immediately given. This all holds true even if one sets body and soul against each other as two different entities; how much more so, then, if, as Fechner assumes, the actor and the result—the psychophysical and the psychic process—are fundamentally one and the same, merely two sides or ways of appearing of one and the same essence."

Classen (*On the Physiology of the Sense of Sight* p. 17): "If sensation were nothing but a function of nerve substance, it would be incomprehensible how an increase in the stimulus should not constantly evoke a corresponding increase in sensation."

Ueberhorst (*The Origin of Visual Perception*. Göttingen 1876 p. 90): "I cannot give in to the belief that, as Fechner once said, the proportionality between nerve excitation and sensation 'might well be conceivable given the essential difference between the physical and psychic realms.'"

: Bernstein postulates (according to p. 20) not a proportionality of sensation with the **magnitude**, but with the spread—which he assumes to be limited—of psychophysical activity. Regarding his views relevant here, compare some remarks specifically relating to this further on, pp. 78, 79.

Now, the **principle of proportionality between cause and effect** may indeed be valid insofar as the effect is the **immediate consequence of the cause**, and is not disturbed by resistances or secondary conditions which intervene in the effect; and I myself build upon this by setting the magnitude of the <term>psychophysical movement</term><note>The physical activity in the brain or nerves that corresponds to a mental experience.</note> proportional to the magnitude of the stimulus, setting aside such disturbances. But we are not dealing with sequential dependence regarding the relationship between sensation and psychophysical activity. When the activity belonging to a sensation is present, the sensation is simultaneously present, and vice versa. It is rather a relationship of **simultaneous** or **mutual dependence** that is at issue here, and the opponents themselves generally grasp it as such. Now, however, one commits a grave error and a consequential confusion if one takes what applies to the former—sequential dependence—and applies it to the latter—simultaneous or mutual dependence—as self-evidently trans- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "über-" likely begins "übertragen" (transferred/applied).</note>

<summary>Fechner cites various scholars (Hering, Classen, Ueberhorst) who argue that internal brain activity and mental sensation must be linearly proportional. Fechner begins his rebuttal by arguing that "cause and effect" rules for sequential events do not necessarily apply to the simultaneous relationship between mind and body.</summary>

<keywords>proportionality, cause and effect, Ewald Hering, Classen, Ueberhorst, Bernstein, sequential dependence, simultaneous dependence, inner psychophysics, nerve excitation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to the "A Priori Objection." On the previous page, he quoted critics like Hering and Classen who argued that if the soul and body are two sides of the same thing, their changes must be directly proportional (a 1:1 linear relationship). Here, Fechner begins his counter-argument by using examples from physics and geometry to show that two things can be "simultaneously dependent" without being "proportional."</meta>

claims; since rather what applies to the former does **not** apply to the latter. And I assert this for my view against my opponents—not in an <note>original: "aprioristisch"</note> <term>a priori</term> <note>Refers to reasoning based on theoretical deduction rather than observation</note> manner, but after a survey of the field of experience.

Indeed, as far as I look around, I find that qualities or changes which are simultaneously dependent on one another proceed proportionally only in the single case that they are of the same nature; I shall illustrate this with the following examples that occur to me.

When a planet orbits the sun, the distance of the planet from the sun, its speed, the space traversed, and the time elapsed (calculated from any given point) change in <term>simultaneous dependence</term> <note>A relationship where two variables change at the same time according to a fixed rule, but not necessarily at the same rate</note> on one another, yet none of these changes is proportional to the others. — The physical brightness of an illuminated surface and its distance from the light source, the length of a pendulum and the duration of its swing, stand in simultaneous dependence on one another, but instead of a simple relationship, a squared relationship exists between them. — The organs and limbs of an embryo grow in simultaneous dependence on one another, but anything but proportionally to one another.

We have the most general model for such dependence in the relationship of the length of a curve to the length of the <term>abscissa</term> <note>the horizontal or 'x' coordinate on a graph</note> starting from the same point. In the **only** case where the curve **is** just such a straight line as the abscissa—or more generally, where the curve and abscissa are curves of the same type—they both proceed proportionally to one another. However, the psychic sensation and the corresponding physical process are as dissimilar as possible, to the greatest extent that dissimilarity can exist between things or processes, which, to be sure, are all ultimately characterizable only through qualities that fall within our consciousness. The <term>dualistic view</term> <note>The philosophical belief that mind and matter are two fundamentally different substances</note> establishes an essential dissimilarity in principle; according to my view, the difference depends on the fundamentally different—respectively internal and external—viewpoint of observation. Accordingly, soul and body relate to one another like the internal and external modes of appearance of the same essence. Hering himself mentions this view of mine as if it naturally demanded proportionality; but on the contrary, it provides a new confirmation of non-proportionality. For if one takes

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that "simultaneous dependence" does not require "proportionality." On the previous page, he used examples like planetary orbits and growing embryos to show that two things can change together according to a rule without changing at the same rate. He now moves into geometric and mechanical examples.</meta>

For example, if one takes a circle and places an eye in the center (or at any point within it) looking in all directions, and places another such eye at some point outside the circle, then as they follow the circumference, generally speaking, both will see the same segments of the circle's circumference. However, they will see them from different <term>visual angles</term> <note>The angle formed at the eye by the lines coming from the edges of an object; it determines how large the object appears to the observer.</note>. Thus, the segments will appear to be of different sizes, and these apparent sizes will by no means change proportionally to one another. — The same applies to the appearance of the Copernican and Ptolemaic world systems, depending on whether one adopts a <term>heliocentric</term> <note>Sun-centered</note> or <term>geocentric</term> <note>Earth-centered</note> viewpoint.

I cannot say for certain whether further searching might reveal exceptions to the principle stated here—the non-proportionality between heterogeneous determinations or changes that are simultaneously dependent on one another. Regardless, these would only be exceptions and could not establish an opposing principle. Perhaps one could claim as an exception that in <term>uniform motion</term> <note>Motion at a constant speed in a straight line.</note>, space and time are proportional to each other; but we only measure time itself through uniform motion. How can we escape this <term>circular reasoning</term>? <note>original: "Cirkel"; a logical fallacy where the proof assumes the truth of the conclusion.</note> I confess I have not found a fully sufficient explanation for this; however, if I am not mistaken, the following explanation—which I mention here in passing—is adequate. If two points with unequal speeds (that is, points that cover different distances in the same amount of time) move such that both points, in <term>corresponding time intervals</term> <note>Time periods taken between the same starting and ending moments.</note>, always cover distances that maintain the same proportion to one another—no matter how large or small those intervals are—then both speeds are constant, and thus the motion of both is uniform. The speeds of both points must be assumed to be unequal; otherwise, the previous explanation would not apply specifically to uniform motion, but to equal motions in general. Thus, the fact that time and space are proportional in uniform motion translates to this: in successive corresponding time intervals, unequal but proportional distances are covered by two points. Fundamentally, this reduces to a proportionality between *distances* (spaces), not between space and time, except insofar as the latter is itself measured by distance.

On the other hand, one might claim as an exception that the length of a <term>string</term> <note>Such as a musical or vibrating string.</note> and the duration of its <term>vibration</term> <note>The time it takes for a string to complete one back-and-forth movement.</note> are proportional to each other. But one could just as easily say that the length of a string and the *number* of vibrations in a given time are inversely proportional to each other; thus, an ambiguity exists here. However, the second way of expressing it seems preferable, insofar as one can directly observe the number of vibrations for two strings of unequal length over corresponding times, whereas the duration of the vibrations must first be derived from this or...

It is easy to see that, depending on the **diversity** of the field into which the **simultaneous dependence** <note>The concept that two different things—like a physical stimulus and a mental sensation—change at the exact same time according to a specific rule.</note> falls, very different quantitative relationships can exist. Since it is impossible to determine this relationship between **body and soul** from first principles <note>original: "a priori"; reasoning based on theoretical deduction rather than observation.</note>, there is also no theoretical obstacle to it being exactly what experience has indicated most strongly until now. And to this, the **following** may be added.

In general, fundamental laws possess a certain character of simplicity, or at least we assume that to be the case. Now, the simplest law of dependence between physical and mental changes would indeed be that of simple proportionality; however, if previous considerations and experience speak just as little in its favor, then at least **Weber's Law**—which sets mental changes proportional to the **relative** physical changes—is the simplest that can be conceived. It is certainly simpler than all the modifications that researchers have tried to substitute for it based on their experiments.

What these experiments might initially point toward would, of course, be a law that incorporates the empirical fact that as a stimulus grows continuously, the "just noticeable" relative stimulus difference reaches a minimum at a certain stimulus value and then rises again beyond that. We saw on page 40 that **Langer** <note>Likely Karl Langer (1819–1887), an Austrian anatomist who explored the mathematical limits of human perception.</note> actually based a law on this; however, it is considerably more complicated than ours and therefore has a lower probability, from a theoretical standpoint, of being considered a fundamental law.

From another perspective, there is the observed fact that the sensation itself cannot be increased beyond a certain limit as the stimulus grows. **Wundt** <note>Wilhelm Wundt (1832–1920), often called the "father of experimental psychology."</note> is quite right in this regard (in his *Principles of Physiological Psychology*, pages 282–283 and 293) when he speaks not only of a **stimulus threshold** <term>Reizschwelle</term> <note>The minimum amount of energy required for a stimulus to be detected by the senses.</note> but also of an insurmountable **stimulus ceiling** <term>Reizhöhe</term> <note>The maximum point of intensity beyond which an increase in stimulus produces no further increase in sensation.</note>. However, he himself does not translate this stimulus ceiling into a correspondingly understood "height" of <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>The internal physical process (like nerve firing) that directly correlates with a mental sensation.</note> as such, which does not...

...surpassable <term>maximum of sensation</term> <note>The highest intensity a sensation can reach before the sensory organs or nerves are exhausted and can no longer respond</note>, stating in agreement with my own view: 

> "The supply of force within the nervous elements is limited; at a certain intensity, the stimulus will therefore trigger all available forces, so that beyond this point the nervous process can no longer be increased," 

with which I entirely agree.*) If the nervous process (the <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>The underlying physical activity in the brain or nerves that corresponds directly to a conscious feeling</note>) could be increased beyond this point, the sensation would also continue to grow. In fact, it would be very strange to establish a fundamental law for the dependence of mental activity on physical activity that allowed the former to grow with the latter only up to certain limits, but then caused it to decrease beyond them; and for this, I claim an inherent improbability based on first principles <note>original: "aprioristische Unwahrscheinlichkeit"</note>.

The view of a simple proportionality between physical and mental changes is particularly significant because it eliminates the transferability of the <term>law of the threshold</term> <note>The concept that a stimulus must reach a minimum intensity to be consciously felt</note> from outer to <term>inner psychophysics</term> <note>Outer psychophysics studies the relationship between external stimuli and sensation; inner psychophysics looks at the relationship between internal nervous activity and sensation</note>. For according to the law of proportionality, even the weakest psychophysical process and the slightest difference between two such processes must call into consciousness a noticeable absolute sensation or a sensation of difference, however weak; whereas the law of the threshold requires a value of psychophysical activity—either simple or a difference—that exceeds zero by a finite degree. Below this point, psychophysical activities and changes can still occur without making the mental phenomena perceptible in consciousness, which they are only able to provide once the threshold is crossed. With the inner threshold, the entire psychophysical representation of the

*) I do not wish to agree in the same way when <term>Wundt</term> <note>Wilhelm Wundt (1832–1920), a foundational figure in experimental psychology</note> says on p. 287 of his work: "We can undoubtedly say of two qualitatively identical sensations that their intensity is equal if they correspond either to the threshold of sensation or the peak of sensation [which correspond respectively to the stimulus threshold and the stimulus peak]." Sensations corresponding to the threshold of sensation are undeniably equal, insofar as they are simply equal to zero; but at the peak of sensation, depending on the state of the individual and the resulting conditions of the lower and upper deviations from <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that we perceive the relative change in a stimulus rather than the absolute change</note>, very different degrees of sensory intensity can, in my opinion, correspond.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the threshold of sensation is essential for understanding the transition between conscious and unconscious states. The author is defending his specific mathematical model of "inner psychophysics" against critics who favor a simpler proportional model.</meta>

...relationship between sleep and waking, conscious and unconscious mental life, and thus the entire development of <term>inner psychophysics</term> <note>Inner psychophysics explores the direct relationship between internal brain activity and conscious experience, as opposed to outer psychophysics, which looks at external stimuli like light or sound.</note> in general—which I discussed in the second part of the *Elements* <note>Referring to his 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics)</note>—without my knowing what could be substituted to represent these corresponding relationships. Thus, the question of whether the <term>law of the threshold</term> <note>The principle that a physical stimulus must reach a minimum intensity to trigger a conscious sensation</note> can be transferred into inner psychophysics seems to me a "life-or-death" question, not only for my specific execution of inner psychophysics but for the very possibility of such a field existing at all. In the way I have conceived and presented this doctrine, the law of the threshold plays a much more important role than <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>A law stating that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is proportional to the original intensity of the stimulus</note>; if the latter were to fall, nothing in my presentation of inner psychophysics would change; however, if the law of the threshold were to fall, the entire presentation would simply have to be deleted. It is the pivot point for all the levers at play within it.

Of course, this *need* for an internal threshold within inner psychophysics is not enough to prove its existence as a fact. Very well; in the following section, I will address the reasons that support it as an actual fact.

While I assume that <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>The physical process in the brain or nerves that corresponds to a sensation</note> or excitation is directly proportional to the stimulus (so long as no causes for deviation exist), yet believe that <term>sensation</term> depends <term>logarithmically</term> <note>A mathematical relationship where the perceived intensity grows much more slowly than the physical intensity; for example, if a light becomes 10 times brighter, we might only perceive it as twice as bright</note> on the stimulus, my opponents take a different view. They assume that sensation is directly proportional to psychophysical activity. They try to explain the experimental evidence for the laws I have established by claiming that those very laws—which I hold apply to the relationship between psychophysical activity and sensation—instead apply to the relationship between the stimulus and the psychophysical activity itself. Now, according to the opponents' own principles, it would seem obvious that—if there were no obstacles or limiting conditions in the transfer of the stimulus to the nerves, and no resistance within the nerves or the brain—the physical effect in the sensory organs would be directly proportional to the physical cause in the stimulus, rather than having a logarithmic relationship. However, my opponents now try to identify causes that modify this proportionality so significantly that their interpretation is supported over mine. However, I cannot [find/see] in what they assert in this regard...

<summary>Fechner argues that the "law of the threshold" is the foundational pillar of inner psychophysics, more essential even than Weber's Law. He also challenges critics who suggest that the logarithmic nature of perception resides in the physical nerves rather than in the relationship between the physical and the mental.</summary>

<keywords>inner psychophysics, law of the threshold, Weber's Law, sensation, stimulus, psychophysical activity, logarithmic dependency, consciousness vs. unconsciousness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of "inner psychophysics." He is addressing specific criticisms from other scientists—namely Hering, Mach, and Langer—who argue that the physiological response of the body (like the eye's pupil) accounts for the logarithmic relationship between stimulus and sensation, rather than a fundamental psychological law.</meta>

...is made, one finds only partly vague, partly unfounded possibilities compared to the causes of deviation that I asserted in Section 6.

Earlier (p. 48, 54), I already considered several statements by **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist who frequently disagreed with Fechner’s mathematical approach to psychology.</note> in this regard. **Mach** <note>Ernst Mach (1838–1916), a physicist and philosopher who contributed significantly to the study of shock waves and perception.</note>, like **Hering**, points to the change in pupil width during a change in light stimulus and a "law of contrast" he established to argue that <term>psychophysical arousal</term> <note>The physical process in the nervous system that corresponds to a conscious sensation.</note> cannot be proportional to the strength of the stimulus due to these compensating causes. And indeed, an empirical deviation from the pure **measurement laws** <note>Referring to the mathematical formulas Fechner developed to measure sensation.</note> for <term>outer psychophysics</term> <note>The study of the relationship between external physical stimuli and sensation.</note> must be based on this; however, the basic psychophysical laws are not based on experiments regarding the measurement law itself, but rather on **Weber’s Law**; and since in experiments regarding Weber’s Law—according to the remark made earlier (p. 48) and to be supplemented in Section 11 (on contrast sensation)—the effect of both stimuli is modified in the same proportion by those causes, nothing can be calculated here in favor of the opponents.

**Langer** <note>Probably referencing Carl Langer, an anatomist, or perhaps a contemporary critic of Fechner’s mathematical psychology.</note> says (p. 68):

> "The assumption of **Fechner** [of the proportionality between stimulus and psychophysical activity] is extremely hypothetical and has little probability in the face of empirical facts; rather, the assumption is far more justified that from larger stimuli, not proportional amounts are transformed into psychophysical activity, but rather smaller ones—specifically because of the reflex movements occurring simultaneously with the stimuli, which protect the organism against stronger stimuli. In the eye, for example, this effect is evident in the narrowing of the pupil, whereby part of the strong stimuli is blocked out; in the case of other strong stimuli in other sensory areas, something analogous likely occurs; in any case, the probability that it happens cannot be dismissed."

Again, the change in pupil width—which means nothing at all here—serves as the primary example, and something analogous to it is suspected in other sensory domains, which, if it occurs, might likely mean just as little.

Certainly, if I stated in **Elements Vol. II, p. 429** <note>Fechner’s 1860 book, *Elemente der Psychophysik*.</note> that "given the essential difference between the physical and mental domains, a dependency between mental and physical activity in the sense of the **fundamental formula** <note>The mathematical statement that a change in sensation is proportional to the relative change in stimulus.</note> and **measurement formula** <note>The result of integrating the fundamental formula, often known as the Fechner-Weber Law: S = k log R.</note> is quite conceivable, whereas such a dependency between two bodily activities—represented on one hand by the stimulus effect and on the other by psychophysical activity—in the sense of physiological and physical...

<summary>Fechner defends his psychophysical laws against critics like Mach and Hering, arguing that physiological defenses (like pupil contraction) do not invalidate his mathematical formulas. He maintains that while physical stimulus and brain activity might not be perfectly proportional, the underlying relationship between brain activity and sensation remains consistent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "measurement formula" (his mathematical law for sensation). Having previously mentioned critics like Hering and Mach, he is now specifically addressing whether the logarithmic relationship he observed in psychology might actually be rooted in pure physiology—specifically in the way nerves and muscles react to physical stimuli.</meta>

laws are not thinkable"—I must retract this latter statement; rather, within the field of <term>nerve physics</term> <note>The study of the physical and electrical properties of the nervous system.</note>, there exist dependency relationships between bodily activities which at least approximately coincide with the logarithmic <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Fechner’s Law ($S = k \log I$), which proposes that sensation increases according to the logarithm of the stimulus.</note>, and the measurement formula is indeed only approximately confirmed in <term>outer psychophysics</term> <note>The study of the relationship between external physical stimuli and the resulting sensation.</note>. Why then, one might ask, should the stimulus not also trigger <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>The internal physiological process (like nerve firing) that directly correlates with conscious experience.</note> according to this relationship—which deviates entirely from simple proportionality—and then the mental activity simply proceed proportionally to the psychophysical activity thus triggered?

That would be quite fine, if only the cases to be asserted in this regard agreed in their conditions with our case of the transfer from stimulus to nerve; however, this is so little the case that one can consider them favorable rather than contradictory to our view.

The following is a case belonging here.

Let a muscle of length *l*, fastened at the top in a vertical position, be <term>tetanized</term> <note>Subjected to rapid, repeated stimulation resulting in a sustained muscle contraction.</note> by an electric stimulus of strength *q*. Under the constant influence of the stimulus, it contracts by a certain amount, which shall be designated as the lift height *h*. The contraction only begins to become noticeable at a finite stimulus value *s*, which thus appears as the <term>threshold value</term> for it. Now, through an ingenious combination of experiments and conclusions, **Preyer** <note>William Thierry Preyer (1841–1897), a physiologist who was a pioneer in child psychology and studied muscle physiology.</note> has found the following law—designated by him as the **myophysical law**—for the dependence of the relative lift height $\frac{h}{l}$ on the strength of the stimulus *q* in relation to the threshold value *s*:

->$\frac{h}{l} = k \log \frac{q}{s}$<-

where *k* is a constant.

This formula corresponds completely to our measurement formula, except that it exists between purely physical quantities.

Indisputably, this formula can only be an approximation,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "measurement formula" (Fechner's Law) and its application to physiology. Having previously introduced an experiment involving muscle contraction (tetanization) and the variables h (lift height) and l (muscle length), Fechner now addresses objections and explores whether the logarithmic nature of the law resides in the nerve or the muscle.</meta>

which becomes invalid at high <term>stimulus values</term> <note>The physical intensity of the power applied to the nerve.</note>, since according to it *h* <note>The "Hubhöhe" or the height to which a muscle contracts/lifts a weight.</note> would eventually have to become larger than *l* <note>The total length of the muscle.</note> as the stimulus intensity increases, whereas the maximum of *h*—which is not even achievable in finite experiments—can only be smaller than *l*. Many objections have also been raised against the way the formula is grounded, and a discussion has arisen which I have not followed in its entirety. But regardless, even if we allow the assumption—which appears unfavorable to us but is likely correct anyway—that there exists at least an *approximate* validity of the formula within certain limits, the following must be taken into consideration.

The stimulus first acts on the nerve, and the nerve acts on the muscle. Now the question arises: does the excitation of the nerve by the stimulus follow a logarithmic relationship, with the height of the lift being proportional to that nerve excitation—or is it the other way around? The former would be in the sense of my opponents, the latter in my own sense. But the former is impossible; for as the height of the lift increases in proportion to the stimulus, a growing resistance develops against further lifting, which brings it to a halt at a certain limit. Thus, the magnitude of the lift *cannot* grow proportionally with the nerve excitation. On the other hand, there is in principle nothing preventing us from thinking that the <term>nerve excitation</term> <note>The internal physiological activity or "inner psychophysical" energy within the nerve.</note> grows in proportion to the stimulus effect, as long as the source of the "living force" of nerve activity does not approach exhaustion, or as long as no resistance similar to that of muscle contraction can be demonstrated—and certainly nothing of the sort has been demonstrated so far.

One can also support the previous indirect conclusion with a much more direct one. According to a communication made to me by a knowledgeable authority, it makes no difference in comparative experiments regarding the contraction ratios of a muscle under the influence of a stimulus whether the muscle is stimulated from the nerve through its irritation, or *directly* after poisoning with <term>Curare</term> <note>A plant-derived poison used in 19th-century physiology to paralyze the connection between nerve and muscle, allowing researchers to stimulate the muscle in isolation.</note>, by which the nerve becomes inactive. Now, if the excitation of the nerve were not proportional to the stimulus, the ratios of the effect on the muscle could not remain the same in both cases.

Insofar as the <term>myophysical law</term> <note>A proposed law describing the physical relationship between stimulus and muscle response.</note> really agrees with the <term>psychophysical measurement law</term> <note>Fechner’s Law, which states that the intensity of a sensation is proportional to the logarithm of the stimulus.</note>, one will, according to our...

<summary>Fechner argues that the logarithmic relationship observed in muscle movement is caused by the physical limits of the muscle itself, not the nerve. He uses evidence from Curare experiments to suggest that nerve excitation remains proportional to the stimulus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "measurement formula" (Fechner's Law) and the debate over whether logarithmic relationships exist within the nerve itself or in the resulting muscle performance. Fechner is currently arguing against opponents who claim that if sensation were proportional to nerve excitation, muscle output would not follow the same pattern.</meta>

...assumption, we can say that the sensation triggered by the stimulus in the nerve corresponds in magnitude to the performance that the nerve triggers in the muscle. According to the opposing assumption—which holds that sensation is simply proportional to the excitation in the nerve—the increase in muscle performance would lag far behind the increase in sensation.

One can apply this to the following remark by **Hering** (p. 8) against the logarithmic dependence of weight sensation on actual weights:

> "If one allows someone very practiced in throwing to cast a number of balls of the same size but very different weights toward a target, he will hit the latter just as well with the lighter balls as with the heavier ones. Before each throw, he weighs the ball in his hand and calculates the magnitude of the *voluntary expenditure of force* accordingly. However, the thrower is generally unable to say how heavy the ball is in grams or loths <note>A "loth" is an old German unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 10 to 16 grams depending on the region.</note>. But since he nonetheless throws every single ball with the force corresponding to its weight, it follows that he has perceived its weight correctly; he has, as one is accustomed to saying, 'had the weight in his feeling.'"

**Hering** finds this to be entirely in favor of the assumption that the intensity of weight sensation grows proportionally to the weight, even though he concedes that there is no rigorous proof in this, since it might also be possible with a different relationship between sensation and stimulus to learn through experience which measure of voluntary force is required for each specific weight. And indeed, an unpracticed child might fail entirely at the correct estimation. But one now sees from the previous consideration that, under the assumption of **Preyer’s Law** <note>Named for William Thierry Preyer; a physiological principle relating the intensity of a stimulus to the muscle's physical response.</note>, the sensation and the performance of the muscle involved in the throw proceed proportionally, without the logarithmic dependence of sensation on weight being contradicted. Just as **Delboeuf** (Review p. 233) briefly remarks, "that if one assumes **one** logarithmic law, nothing prevents us from admitting **two**," <note>original: "que, si l'on admet une loi logarithmique, rien n'empêche d'en admettre deux"</note> and in doing so he acknowledges the ingenuity of Hering’s objection, as do I.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Fechner’s Law by examining physiological experiments. While the previous page addressed Hering’s skepticism regarding the relationship between sensation and stimulus, this page presents evidence from electrophysiology—specifically the work of Emil du Bois-Reymond and others—to show that electrical changes in the optic nerve follow a logarithmic pattern when exposed to light.</meta>

If one employs measures such as those used by **Dubois** <note>Emil du Bois-Reymond (1818–1896) was a German physiologist and the founder of modern electrophysiology.</note> to demonstrate natural muscle or nerve currents, connecting them to a **multiplier** <term>multiplier: an early sensitive galvanometer used to measure very small electrical currents by using a coil of wire</term> such that one wire communicates with the cornea and the other with the cross-section of the optic nerve, then this current produces a deflection on the multiplier. When read via a mirror device, this deflection reached approximately 600 degrees on the scale during the experiments. After observing that the deflection remains constant in the dark, one allows the light of a gas flame to enter. At that moment, the deflection changes.

> “First, an increase is shown, then a decrease; when the light is removed, another increase in the electromotive force [indicated by the deflection] occurs. Occasionally, as a result of the nerve dying, one sees only a slight increase and then a decrease, but the rise upon removing the light is always constant. The magnitude of the change in electromotive force [i.e., the deflection] caused by the influence of light is about 3 percent of the total value.”

> “The change is not proportional to the amount of light when using lights of different intensities, but rather to the logarithm of the quotient, and thus agrees with **Fechner’s** psychophysical law.”

> “The change originates essentially from the retina, since, if this organ is removed while the other structures of the eye remain, no sensitivity to light exists, although an electromotive force is still present.”

> “This change can be traced as far as the **optic thalami** <note>original: *Sehhügel*. The thalami are brain structures that serve as a relay station for sensory information.</note>.”

The communication from which the preceding was extracted is only a preliminary one; I do not know whether a more complete presentation of the investigation has appeared since then; the previous one, however, leaves several points unclear. Does the first deflection—calculated in the positive direction from the original deviation—pass immediately into the negative after the admission of light, or does the first positive increase only gradually diminish without returning to the first deviation? And is the first positive...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether physiological data supports Fechner’s psychophysical law. page 77

Be that as it may, these experiments appear in any case to provide direct proof that between the light stimulus and its effect on the nerve, there exists not a simple proportional relationship, but rather a *logarithmic relationship*; this is, in fact, more in line with the opposing view than my own.

But if we look closer, an analogous consideration applies to this case as it did to the previous one. According to our current knowledge, the natural nerve current—without the introduction of a stimulus when the circuit is closed—stems from the fact that the inherently electrically distinct <term>nerve molecules: hypothetical microscopic units of nerves proposed by 19th-century physiologists to explain electrical activity</term> are positioned in a way suitable for the generation of a current. If the current increases or decreases upon the introduction of a stimulus—the latter of which manifests in other experiments as the so-called *negative fluctuation* <term>negative fluctuation: a 19th-century term for the decrease in electrical current in a nerve or muscle during activity, known today as part of the action potential</term>—this can only arise from the nerve molecules shifting into a position suitable for the strengthening or weakening of the current. It may very well be that there is a resistance against this change of position (in which they are held by natural conditions) that grows with the magnitude of the change, corresponding to how it was in the previous case regarding the change in the position of the muscle molecules upon which contraction depends; and here again, a *logarithmic relationship* asserts itself. 

But sensation likely depends on vibrations that arise simultaneously with the change in the position of the molecules\*), and it is by no means self-evident that their <term>living force: a historical term (vis viva) for kinetic energy</term>, or whatever measure for <term>psychophysical activity: the interaction between physical stimuli and mental sensation</term> one might assume, changes proportionally with the change in the position of the molecules—although it may be that a certain influence occurs that contributes to the deviations appearing in external psychophysics. In any case, according to the previous remarks, the experiments of *Dewar and McKendrick* <note>Sir James Dewar (1842–1923) and John Gray McKendrick (1841–1926) were British scientists who pioneered the study of the electrical response of the retina to light.</note> can decide nothing at all regarding our question.

Now one could say that the *Du Bois hypothesis* <note>Referring to Emil du Bois-Reymond (1818–1896), who proposed that nerves and muscles are composed of "electromotive molecules."</note> regarding the electri—

\*) The *negative fluctuation*, which according to other experiments by physiologists arises through a <term>tetanizing nerve stimulus: a rapid, repetitive electrical stimulation that causes a muscle to stay in a state of constant contraction</term>, is also demonstrably accompanied by vibrations of the molecules.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical mechanisms within nerves. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the "position" of nerve components. The current page begins by completing the phrase "physiological nature" (physiologischen Beschaffenheit) of nerve molecules.</meta>

...logical nature of the **nerve molecules** <term>nerve molecules: hypothetical microscopic units that 19th-century physiologists believed arranged themselves to carry electrical currents</term>, upon which we have based our reasoning here, is indeed a hypothesis. But any other hypothesis one might attempt to establish instead must achieve the same for the explanation of the facts and, consequently, undeniably for the clarification of our case.

In general, however, it is clear from the outset that static or equilibrium phenomena—such as the magnitude of a **muscle contraction** or the **negative current fluctuation** <term>negative current fluctuation: the historical term for the reduction in a nerve's resting electrical current during activity; a precursor to the modern understanding of the action potential</term> occurring under the influence of a continuous external stimulus—do not seem very suitable for being thought of as linked to an ongoing **psychic activity**. Furthermore, regarding the idea that **vibrations**—under which form we most likely have to conceptualize **psychophysical activity**—depend in a **logarithmic ratio** on other vibrations (and the **light stimulus** is indeed oscillatory), no example is known to me even now.

If one nevertheless wished to propose the hypothesis that **sensation** depends more on the **change in position** of the molecules than on vibrations, the question arises: what could be achieved with such a hypothesis? According to the vibration hypothesis, we can relate the different qualities of sensation to the different periodicity and form of the vibrations; but with what could we relate them according to the positional change hypothesis? Under the former, the internal condition of sensation is entirely adequate to the external stimulus; under the latter, it is entirely inadequate.

The fact that the light stimulus fails to have an effect in the previous experiments when the **retina** is removed aligns with the fact that the light stimulus—even in a living creature—produces no sensation when acting directly on the nerve without the mediation of the specific end organs that constitute the retina. This is proven by the **blind spot** at the entry point of the **optic nerve**, where these end organs are absent. Undeniably, therefore, the vibrations of external light first awaken specific (chemical) processes of an oscillatory nature (see Section XIII) within the end organs, from which vibrations propagate through the nerve; but nothing prevents us from thinking of the internal vibrations awakened thereby as proportional to the external vibrations, even according to this conception.

**Bernstein** <note>original: "Bernstein" refers to Julius Bernstein (1839–1917), a pioneer in neurophysiology who later formulated the membrane theory of nerve conduction.</note> assumes that the excitation dependent on the stimulus propagates undiminished through the sensory nerve, although...

It also highlights the necessity of the retina’s specialized structures for translating light into nerve signals, citing the optic nerve's "blind spot" as evidence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of physiological explanations for the Weber-Fechner Law. The previous page ended with a question regarding how sensation might relate to the change in position of molecules. This page begins by addressing the counter-argument that brain resistance might explain the logarithmic relationship between stimulus and sensation.</meta>

...that however the **ganglion cells** <term>ganglion cells: historically, this term referred generally to nerve cells (neurons) within the brain and nervous system</term> of the brain offer a resistance to the propagation [of nerve activity], by virtue of which each cell reduces the **kinetic energy** <term>kinetic energy: the original German uses 'lebendige Kraft' (vis viva), a 19th-century term for kinetic energy, often used in this context to describe the intensity of nerve activity</term> of the excitation entering it by a part proportional to the degree of this excitation. — So be it; but this cannot damage the proportionality between the total excitation propagated through the brain and the stimulus; a double stimulus will carry a double excitation upon entering the brain with double the weakening as it progresses through it; a triple stimulus will carry triple; this does not change the proportionality.

Bernstein <note>Julius Bernstein (1839–1917), a prominent German physiologist who worked on the electrical nature of nerve impulses</note> further deepens his view (no. 2 p. 177) to the effect that, fundamentally, it is the **annihilation** of the kinetic energy of the excitation through the resistance of the ganglion cells of the brain upon which sensation depends. Although I do not actually subscribe to this interpretation, let us allow it to stand here for now. But now, the consumption of the total kinetic energy (which enters the brain in proportion to the stimulus) would be determined in its magnitude by the magnitude of the kinetic energy itself. Thus, the logarithmic dependence of the **psychophysical process** <term>psychophysical process: the internal physical activity (like nerve impulses) that directly corresponds to a conscious sensation</term> on the stimulus sought by my opponents would exist just as little if one seeks the psychophysical process in the *consumption* of kinetic energy as if one seeks it in the energy *itself*; in short, the view of my opponents is again not helped by this. Furthermore, Bernstein arrives at his view—which deviates from ours in a peculiar way (p. 20)—only by regarding the **number of cells** performing the consumption of kinetic energy (which he views as limited) as the measure of sensation, rather than the total magnitude of the consumption itself by the cells. (Further details on Bernstein’s view under section XV.)

I suspect that in the field of modern **experimental physiology** there is still much available that would be debatable regarding the question discussed in this section, but I confess that this field has long been somewhat foreign to me, and for the reasons stated in the preface, I have no longer been able to study it extensively or deeply enough to be properly oriented within it. In the meantime, any consideration of it should always lead back to the previous points of view. At least, this seems to be the case with the following investigations, which I will mention briefly here, without, admittedly, having [integrated] them thoroughly enough—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between stimulus and sensation, moving from Julius Bernstein’s physiological theories to Sigmund Exner’s experimental findings regarding the time required for perception. The previous page ended abruptly while discussing the logarithmic dependence of psychophysical processes.</meta>

...to be able to see, in order to be completely certain of its correct utilization.

Exner <note>Sigmund Exner (1846–1926) was an Austrian physiologist who conducted pioneering research on reaction times and the physiology of the senses.</note> draws the following result, among others (p. 24), from an extensive investigation:

> “If the intensities of illumination of an object increase in a geometric progression [and thus the logarithms of the intensities in an arithmetic progression], then the times necessary for the perception of that object decrease in an arithmetic progression.”

He claims the validity of this law for light intensities that do not exceed a certain degree (roughly the intensity of a white paper illuminated by direct sunlight); and notes on page 22 that the very first portion of incoming light demonstrably exerts an effect on the retina.

Now, let γ <note>gamma, representing the strength of sensation</note> be the strength of sensation, β <note>beta, representing stimulus intensity</note> the intensity of the light stimulus relative to the threshold value <term>threshold value: the point at which a stimulus is just strong enough to be perceived by the senses</term> as a unit, *t* the time elapsed from the start of the light's action, and *k* a constant. In that case, the following equation corresponds in the simplest way to the empirical law stated above, though it is certainly not generally proven by it, since all changes of *t* and β in the specified sense are always observed only for the same specific value of γ:

-> γ = *kt* log β <-

Specifically, when the sensation γ just reaches the threshold—that is, begins to become noticeable—it is to be regarded as equal, regardless of the values *t* and β may have at that moment; however, according to the author's law, experience shows that to produce this equality, *t* must change in inverse proportion to the logarithm of β.

One must not count on the indefinite validity of the previous formula beyond the threshold value of γ, according to which γ would have to grow indefinitely with *t*, just as Exner claims no such validity for his law. For one thing, the formula does not take into account the weakening of sensitivity <note>this refers to "sensory adaptation," where the eye or ear becomes less sensitive to a stimulus the longer it persists</note> that increases with the duration of the stimulus; secondly, the question arises whether the proportionality of γ with *t* does not hold only for such small values of *t* as those considered in the relevant experiments...

\*) In his treatise “On the time necessary for a visual perception,” 58th volume of the Vienna Academy Reports <note>original: "Wien. Sitzungsber."</note>, 1868.

<summary>The author analyzes Sigmund Exner’s findings on the relationship between light intensity and the time required for perception, proposing a mathematical formula (γ = kt log β) to describe how sensation relates to stimulus duration and intensity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s mathematical and physiological defense of his "measurement formula" (the Weber-Fechner Law). He is comparing his theory with the experimental results of other scientists—specifically Baxt and Exner—concerning the time it takes for a stimulus to produce a physical or sensory response.</meta>

...is exactly correct. Suppose, for example, that instead of *t* <note>the time elapsed since the stimulus began</note> one actually had to use $1 - e^{-t}$, where *e* represents the base of natural logarithms <note>approximately 2.718, a constant used to describe growth or decay</note>. In that case, $1 - e^{-t}$ converts simply to *t* for very small values of *t*. For large values, it reaches a constant value of 1, which cannot be exceeded. In general, the formula is $e^{-t} = 1 - \frac{t}{1} + \frac{t^2}{2} \dots$ in which the higher powers are negligible for small values of *t*, whereas for large values of *t*, one has $e^{-t} = \frac{1}{e^t} = 0$. According to this, our pure measurement formula would result for the final state achieved.

The fact that $\gamma$ <note>the strength of sensation</note> in the previous formula depends on the logarithmic ratio of $\beta$ <note>the intensity of the stimulus</note> certainly corresponds to our measurement formula. However, it initially leaves the alternative we have been considering undecided: it could be that sensation ($\gamma$) depends in simple proportion on internal psychophysical activity <note>the actual physical firing of nerves</note>, while that activity itself depends logarithmically on the stimulus. One might think, after considering further experiments by <term>Baxt</term> <note>N. Baxt was a researcher who worked in Helmholtz's laboratory, focusing on the speed of nerve impulses</note>, that a decision should really be made in this sense. 

These experiments concern the interval of time that passes from the moment a frog's leg is immersed in diluted sulfuric acid to the moment the muscle contraction, triggered by the reflex stimulus of the acid, occurs. It was found that the intervals increase in a **geometric progression** <note>a sequence where each term is multiplied by a constant, like 2, 4, 8, 16</note>, while the percentage of acid concentration falls according to an **arithmetic progression** <note>a sequence where each term has a constant added, like 2, 4, 6, 8</note>. Thus, we see here the same law for the onset of muscle contraction that <term>Exner</term> <note>Sigmund Exner, an Austrian physiologist</note> found for the onset of sensation. 

And so one might say: Regardless of sensation—which does not appear at all in Baxt’s experiments—neural excitation must first be increased to a certain degree by the ultimate effect of the stimulus before the muscle contraction begins; and it is this neural excitation which depends logarithmically on the stimulus. But neural excitation must first be transferred to the muscle to trigger the contraction. If the consideration made regarding <term>Preyer’s</term> <note>Wilhelm Thierry Preyer (1841–1897), a physiologist known for studying developmental psychology and muscle response</note> experiments is correct, it could also be applied here to suggest that the muscle contraction depends on the neural excitation in a logarithmic ratio (just as sensation does), while the neural excitation itself depends on the stimulus in simple proportion.

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of experimental data (Baxt and Exner) regarding the timing of sensations to a theoretical investigation of the "threshold." ## IX. Inquiry into the Applicability of the Law of the Threshold to Internal Psychophysics.

That the law of the simple stimulus or sensation threshold, as well as that of the difference threshold, exists for *external* psychophysics <note>The study of the relationship between physical stimuli (like light or sound) and the sensations they produce</note> is supported by obvious facts. Every stimulus, as well as the difference between stimuli, actually requires a certain finite magnitude—one exceeding zero—in order to be perceived, or to make an impression on consciousness that reports its existence. Furthermore, a stimulus that we initially perceived can become imperceptible to us if we remain exposed to it for a long period, because its threshold value increases for us <note>This refers to sensory adaptation, where we stop noticing a constant sound or smell</note>. But the question arises whether the same law can be applied to *internal* psychophysics <note>The relationship between internal physiological activity (nerve firing) and conscious sensation</note>: whether a finite degree of psychophysical activity (that is, one exceeding zero) is also necessary for the corresponding sensory quality to be felt by consciousness, and whether a difference between the degrees of this activity is required for a difference to be recognized.

The importance of this question, and its connection to the question of whether a simple proportionality exists between sensation and psychophysical activity, was discussed in the previous section (p. 70 ff.). Those who declare themselves in favor of proportionality <note>The idea that sensation grows at the exact same rate as the physical activity in the nerves</note> simultaneously declare themselves against the assumption of an *internal* threshold, whereas the assumption of such a threshold aligns with our own view.

Now, one might argue from the outset against our assumption that causes can be found for the *external* threshold which do not exist for the assumption of an *internal* one. Thus, it is quite conceivable that a very weak *external* stimulus might not be able to penetrate to the interior of the psychophysical system at all, due to external obstacles opposing its entry or due to interrupted nerve conduction; or perhaps a kind of inertia in the triggering of psychophysical activity by the stimulus—analogous to starting a carriage on an uneven road—requires a certain finite magnitude of stimulus to be overcome.*)

---

*) Among others, <term>Delboeuf</term> <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896), a Belgian philosopher and scientist who proposed alternative mathematical laws for sensation</note>, whose measurement formula includes no threshold value, raises this latter reason, which I already mentioned in the *Elements* <note>Fechner’s 1860 foundational work, *Elements of Psychophysics* (*Elemente der Psychophysik*)</note> II. 431, as—

<summary>Fechner discusses whether the "threshold"—the minimum intensity required for a stimulus to be felt—applies not just to external objects but also to the internal activity of the mind and nerves. He argues against the idea of simple proportionality between nerve activity and sensation, suggesting a minimum "internal" energy is required for consciousness.</summary>

<keywords>internal psychophysics, external psychophysics, stimulus threshold, difference threshold, Joseph Delboeuf, consciousness, proportionality, nerve conduction</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "law of the threshold." Having established the difference between internal and external psychophysics on the previous page, Fechner now addresses whether sensory thresholds are caused by external physical obstacles or by an inherent property of how our consciousness processes internal neural activity.</meta>

If such causes really exist, they will contribute to the lower experimental deviation from Weber’s Law <note>The principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus</note>, which would only serve to explain this deviation. However, the ability to explain the threshold through such causes is not sufficient in every case, insofar as it fails even with the simplest facts concerning the **difference threshold** <note>The minimum amount by which stimulus intensity must be changed in order to produce a noticeable variation in sensory experience</note>. 

If, for example, in light experiments I see two surfaces side by side, which I know for certain have a physical difference in brightness based on their lighting conditions, and yet even with the closest attention I am unable to recognize any difference in brightness; or if during the day I am equally unable, despite the most careful attention, to distinguish a star from the surrounding sky, even though the star adds a considerable degree of physical brightness to the spot where it stands—in such cases, I can no longer claim that the light stimulus failed to reach the interior due to external obstacles, or failed to trigger psychophysical activity <note>The physical activity in the brain or nervous system that corresponds to a sensation</note>. And why then, if even the weakest psychophysical excitation could be felt in consciousness, is it that with strong stimuli, even a very significant excess of one over the other remains imperceptible if it does not exceed a certain limit?

On this point, consider the following citation from *Elements* I, p. 243 <note>Refers to Fechner’s seminal 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik*</note>:

> "One places two lamps next to each other and an object that casts a shadow in front of them. Each of the two lamps casts a shadow that is illuminated only by the other lamp, while the surrounding background is illuminated by both lamps. If one now turns the wick of one lamp lower and lower, or moves it further and further from the shadow-casting object, one sees the shadow it casts become weaker and weaker, and finally this shadow disappears, as if absorbed by the general illumination of the background, despite both light sources still being present. I was quite astonished when I first noticed seeing two lights cast **only one shadow**. Both lamps are clearly burning, yet only **one** shadow is there. In a word, if the difference between the illumination of one shadow and the surrounding space falls below a certain limit, the difference disappears totally for sensation and can no longer be perceived at all."

<summary>Fechner argues that the sensory threshold is not merely a result of external physical interference, but a fundamental property of consciousness, illustrated by the experiment where a faint second shadow becomes invisible despite its physical presence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of thresholds within "inner psychophysics." Here, he moves to a more technical discussion of the "difference threshold" (the smallest change we can notice) versus the "sensation threshold" (the point where we feel anything at all), using experiments on light and shadow to prove his points.</meta>

->»This experiment is particularly striking because one observes the components simultaneously, and can direct the eye sharply, calmly, and steadily toward the boundary line between them while causing their difference to disappear; thus, there can be no talk of either forgetting the previous impression or overlooking the difference.«<-

Undoubtedly, the **difference threshold** <term>the minimum amount of change in a stimulus required for a person to detect a difference</term>, which must be crossed for a difference to become noticeable, is not to be confused with the simple **sensation threshold** <term>the minimum intensity of a stimulus required to produce any sensation at all</term>; but it is not clear what principle could argue against one threshold without also arguing against the other. Should the reason for the internal difference threshold lie in the fact that the conduction <note>referring to the transmission of signals through the nervous system</note> is lacking between two points that do not differ strongly enough in their psychophysical excitation <term>the physical activity in the brain or nervous system that corresponds to a sensation</term>? If so, this would not only be highly improbable for two neighboring points, but it also does not align with the fact that we can make a very noticeable difference in light between two illuminated surfaces disappear by increasing the illumination of **both**—specifically, by increasing the brighter one in a smaller proportion than the less bright one. One can easily be convinced of this through shadow experiments used to test Weber's Law <note>original: "Weberschen Gesetzes"; the principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus</note>, despite the fact that the resistance to conduction between the two should be more easily overcome by the mutual strengthening of the stimuli. Or, conversely, should the reason for the lack of differentiation lie in the fact that the psychophysical activities of both points equalize when the proportional difference of the stimuli is too small? But since no internal equalization occurs with a large proportional difference, one might expect a closer approximation to equalization with a smaller difference, but not a total equalization; furthermore, both illuminated surfaces can be separated by a dark interval of space or time without the difference threshold ceasing to exist.

Undoubtedly, within a psychophysical system, facts must be understood and judged in connection with one another; therefore, if one must grant the difference threshold to internal psychophysics, one cannot deny it the simple sensation threshold without binding reasons. Or if, as some wish—though I do not agree with them—all sensations are to be understood in principle as sensations of difference...

should be (compare Section XII), it would already be sufficient to have proven that the **difference threshold** <term>the minimum change in a stimulus required for a person to detect a difference</term> is adequately grounded for the internal realm.

Furthermore, there is no lack of facts that can be related to the simple **sensation threshold** <term>the lowest level of stimulus intensity that can be perceived by the senses</term> in our sense, without the above objections being applicable; however, these facts do not possess such a simple character as the previous ones regarding the difference threshold, and thus they may be more easily subject to alternative interpretations from various hypothetical points of view.

This includes the well-known fact that we can subsequently bring a word to our consciousness—even if we missed it while distracted—by properly directing our **attention** toward it. Undeniably, this cannot be explained by saying the word failed to reach the "seat of psychophysical activity" <note>Fechner refers here to the specific part of the nervous system or brain where physical stimuli are converted into mental experiences</note>; rather, it is explained by the fact that through the addition of the psychophysical activity of attention, the psychophysical excitation caused by the word receives a boost, thereby being lifted above the **threshold of general consciousness**. For this to happen, however, an **inner threshold** must exist. I have considered other facts of a similar character in *Elements of Psychophysics*, Volume II, page 432.

Other factual relationships are connected to these types of facts, and I do not know how a "psychophysical representation" <note>The way the brain physically maps or "represents" a thought or sensation</note> could even be conceived without the assumption of an inner threshold; furthermore, my opponents, with their differing views, have never dared to address these facts and have simply ignored my presentation of them. I count it as a great advantage for the **threshold principle** that one can venture to address them with its help. Regarding the more detailed discussions on this in Chapter 42 of my **Elements**, I will only state the following briefly.

When we perceive a sensory phenomenon or visualize one in our imagination, the intensity of our conscious activity is determined, on the one hand, by the degree of **attention** with which we perceive the sensory or memory phenomenon, and on the other hand, by the vividness or strength with which the phenomenon itself appears to us. We can view a dim image with intense **attention**, or listen to a faint sound with intense **attention**,

Accordingly, for example, a gray or white does not appear brighter to us, nor a sound louder, when we direct increased **attention** <note>original: "Aufmerksamkeit"</note> toward it; we simply feel our attention is strengthened. But strangely, while the degree of attention thus appears to have no influence on the strength of the phenomenon, the phenomenon itself vanishes entirely from consciousness if attention is not directed toward it at all. One who is completely immersed in study does not, so to speak, see or hear what is happening around them; one can be so absorbed in listening to a concert that everything visible in the surroundings vanishes from our consciousness, and so on.

How is this relationship explained? In my view, as follows: attention is a general psychic phenomenon—that is, one that comes into play in every sensory as well as higher mental domain—and for which, if one demands a well-developed **psychophysics** <term>the scientific study of the relation between stimulus and sensation</term>, a correspondingly general psychophysical activity must be assumed to underlie it. This general activity, however, is co-determined*) by activities of a special nature, upon which the special psychic phenomena depend, and the **total consciousness** <note>original: "Totalbewusstsein"</note>, which comes into play during the perception of a particular phenomenon, is determined by the combination of both. Now, total consciousness—and with it the entire psychophysical activity underlying it—has its **threshold** <term>the point at which a stimulus or mental process becomes strong enough to be consciously perceived</term>, and if this is nowhere exceeded, dreamless sleep takes place; however, the particular activity on which the special phenomenon depends has a threshold distinguishable from it (**special threshold**). Now, no matter how strongly attention may be directed toward a domain, if this special threshold is not reached, then we perceive...

In any case, it remains favorable for the fundamental validity of the law of the threshold, apart from the facts speaking in its favor,

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical laws. that generally no finite measure for finite sensations could be derived from **Weber’s Law** <term>the principle that the ratio of a stimulus increment to the background stimulus is constant for a just-noticeable difference</term> alone without including the **threshold principle** <term>the rule that a stimulus must reach a minimum intensity to be perceived</term>, as was shown in *Elements of Psychophysics* II. 34.

But when **Langer** (p. 66) says: "that the fact of the threshold could be derived from any arbitrary curve as a psychophysical law, because it only requires that the curve should intersect the abscissa axis <note>the horizontal axis (x-axis) in a graph, here representing stimulus intensity</note> at a certain finite value, the threshold; a requirement that could be fulfilled by all possible curves"; I then ask him whether it could be derived, for example, from the curve *γ* = *kβ* or = *kβ*² etc., as a psychophysical law. He will say: "Oh yes, I only need to shift the starting point of the abscissa *β*." I reply: "Oh no, in a curve acting as a psychophysical law, it is not at all arbitrary where I place the starting point of the abscissa; rather, it is a **peculiarity** of the psychophysical curve—demanded by specific facts and flowing again from the logarithmic measurement formula—that the starting point of the abscissa must be placed at a point that lies at a finite distance from the point where it intersects the curve." We are not dealing here with an abstract mathematical curve, but with one that must express empirical relationships between the ordinate *γ* (in its specific meaning as sensation) and the abscissa *β* (in its specific meaning as stimulus). In short, I find here in Langer the same lack of cogency of which I shall have to remind **Delboeuf** <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896), a Belgian philosopher who proposed a different mathematical model for sensation</note> on page 95.
