## X. Objections against negative sensation values. Further remarks on the threshold (Delboeuf, Langer, Preyer).

If the magnitude of the stimulus *β*, or the psychophysical activity triggered by the stimulus, falls below the threshold value *b*, then according to the **measurement formula** <term>Fechner's formula $S = k \log(R/R_0)$</term>, the sensation *γ* takes on negative values (p. 8, 10). And even regardless of the validity of our measurement formula, one will find oneself led everywhere to the assumption of negative sensation values below the threshold if one merely accepts the threshold law itself, which could exist even apart from the Weberian and logarithmic measurement laws; for while the logarithmic measurement law requires the threshold law for its derivation (*Elem. II. 34*), the reverse is not true. And like simple sensation, the **differential sensation** <term>the perception of the difference between two stimuli</term> and indeed every mental activity bound to psychophysical activity for which a threshold exists...

<summary>Fechner defends his mathematical model of the threshold against Langer’s claim that it is arbitrary, arguing that the relationship between stimulus and sensation is rooted in empirical fact. He begins a new section addressing the concept of "negative sensation values" that occur when a stimulus is below the threshold of perception.</summary>

<keywords>Weber's Law, threshold principle, psychophysics, Langer, Delboeuf, Preyer, measurement formula, negative sensation values, stimulus, mental activity</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s mathematical and philosophical justification of the "threshold." He is explaining why "negative sensations"—sensations that exist below the level of conscious awareness—are a necessary part of his psychophysical system.</meta>

exists, they assume negative values below the threshold. In short, the concept of the **psychophysical threshold** <term>the point at which a physical stimulus becomes strong enough to be consciously felt</term> and the concept of **negative psychical values** below that threshold are, at least as I understand them both, interdependent.

Indeed, if we look at the **sensation** <term>represented by the Greek letter gamma, *γ*</term>, it only gains noticeable values for consciousness once a certain **stimulus value** <term>represented by the letter *b*</term> (or the psychophysical activity triggered by it) is exceeded. If the sensation increases in functional dependence on the degree to which it exceeds this point, then it is self-evident that as long as *b* is not reached, something is missing in the formation of the sensation. 

Just as one describes a wave sinking below a certain level using negative height values (while using positive values for those that rise above it), one must naturally also apply a negative sign to that which is still "missing" from reaching the point where sensation first becomes noticeable. This "missing" amount (which should not be confused with the amount of physical stimulus still required) must be marked with a negative sign if we mark what exceeds it with a positive sign, provided that a functional dependence of sensation on the stimulus is to be maintained across the entire **stimulus scale** <term>the range of physical intensities</term>. Thus, the task is to place the measure of this negative distance from the zero-point—the "sunkenness" beneath it—into a shared functional relationship with the measure of positive elevation above it. Assuming the validity of **Weber’s Law**, this is achieved through the **measurement formula** <note>Fechner’s famous logarithmic formula, S = k log R, which relates sensation to stimulus</note>.

We do not have a direct measure in our consciousness for what is still "missing" for a sensation to arise. However, calculating this measure through the measurement formula is the logical consequence of the mathematical and experimental reasons upon which we base the measurement of sensations that positively exceed the **threshold value** (the zero-value of sensation). It must be understood according to this logic. Thus, when correctly grasped and understood, the inclusion of negative sensation values...

---

*) It would be different if the strength of a sensation were compared to the height of a wave measured from the seabed rather than from the surface level; in that case, there would be no threshold, no sinking below the ground, and consequently no negative sensations.

<summary>Fechner argues that "negative sensations" are a mathematical necessity; just as a wave can sink below sea level, a mental state can exist in a "deficit" state below the threshold of conscious perception.</summary>
<keywords>psychophysical threshold, negative sensations, stimulus scale, Weber's Law, measurement formula, consciousness, mathematical modeling</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s mathematical and philosophical justification for "negative sensations." He is arguing that even though we cannot "feel" a negative sensation, it is a necessary mathematical value to describe how close a physical stimulus is to reaching the threshold of conscious awareness.</meta>

along with the positive ones in the same formula, are by no means irrelevant to our experience. For even if negative **sensation** <term>the mental response to a stimulus</term> as such does not enter our experience—and one cannot in any way state, through the direct testimony of consciousness, exactly how much is "missing" for a sensation to arise—we can still determine it through the formula's relationship between negative sensation and **stimulus values** <term>original: *Reizwerthe*; the measurable physical intensity of a trigger, like light or sound</term>. Specifically, we can determine how much stimulus value is still lacking to produce a positive, which is to say a real, sensation. It is important not to confuse these two things: the missing mental sensation and the missing physical stimulus.

The purpose of **Psychophysics** <term>the scientific study of the relationship between stimulus and sensation</term> is, after all, to understand the functional relationship between physical and mental activity. Part of this involves knowing whether more or less is missing from the physical conditions required for a sensation to exist. Or, to put it another way, we need to know how much of this condition (represented by the symbol $\beta$) is present, and what level (represented by $b$) must be reached so that the requirement for an actual sensation is fulfilled. 

If a single formula is to fit both the case where this condition is not reached and the case where it is exceeded, then the sensation naturally takes on negative values in the first case and positive values in the second. The measurement of the sensation in that first, "sub-threshold" case is not simply a measurement of the incomplete physical condition ($\beta$) itself; rather, it exists in the same functional dependence upon it as a positive sensation does upon a condition that has been exceeded.

One must not overlook the fact that negative sensation values—so long as they do not sink all the way to negative infinity ($-\infty$) when the stimulus ($\beta$) is at zero—always remain attached to a positive element. This element does not concern the sensation itself, but rather the stimulus or the psychophysical activity. This is what distinguishes the existence of a "negative sensation" from the total absence of any cause for sensation whatsoever. 

The fact that something is missing for a positive sensation to exist depends functionally, as just noted, on the fact that something is missing for the fulfillment of the physical condition at which sensation begins to become positive. However, an incomplete condition is nevertheless already present; it only requires a "supplement" or addition to break through into a positive sensation. Furthermore, this incomplete state is capable of providing such a supplement to other causes of sensation that might arise from elsewhere. Even without being completed into a conscious sensation, it remains physi- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "physiologisch" (physiologically) or "physikalisch" (physically).</note>

<summary>Fechner explains that "negative sensations" are not just empty numbers, but mathematical representations of how much physical energy is currently present below the threshold of conscious awareness. This "hidden" energy is significant because it can combine with other stimuli to eventually break through into consciousness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that "negative sensations" are not merely mathematical abstractions, but represent real physical activities occurring below the threshold of consciousness. He transition into explaining how this provides a bridge for "causal continuity" in our mental lives.</meta>

...is able to produce successes in the psychophysical system which are of the same nature as those produced by the complete conditions of conscious sensations. The important role that psychophysical activities play beneath the **threshold** <term>the point at which a physical stimulus becomes strong enough to be consciously felt</term> in so-called unconscious life depends on these relationships. In regard to this, I have called the sensations functionally belonging to them **unconscious sensations**. This name, however, is of little consequence, so long as one is prepared to defend the stated factual meaning of negative sensations—or, more generally, negative phenomena of consciousness—below the threshold.

It is only on the basis of this perspective—that in the so-called unconscious states of mental life, a remnant of the physical (psychophysical) conditions of consciousness persists below the threshold without being sufficient for consciousness itself—that a clear distinction between unconscious and conscious states seems at all possible. Furthermore, this allows us to understand the maintenance of a **causal connection** <term>original: *causalen Zusammenhang*; the chain of cause-and-effect that keeps our identity and thoughts consistent</term> between conscious states through unconscious ones. In this respect, psychology would be unable to manage without **Psychophysics** <term>the study of the relationship between the mind and the physical world</term>. Indeed, even if conscious mental life is temporarily interrupted by states of vanished consciousness (states of sleep) *), the physical conditions of that life continue to play out below the threshold to maintain the causal connection. Upon awakening, this play simply rises back into consciousness, just as it sank below the threshold when falling asleep. The same principle that applies to the whole of conscious life can also apply to specific parts of it, as proven by the facts of **memory** <term>Fechner suggests that memories "exist" below the threshold until they are called back into consciousness</term>. Any other way of imagining what unconscious states are—on the one hand compared to conscious ones, and on the other to "un-conscious" ones (where psychophysical movement ceases entirely)—seems to me factually unclear. 

However, to avoid getting entangled in conceptual contradictions, when using the expression "**unconscious sensation**," one must not confuse the mental lack of sensation with its incomplete physical condition. One could, by definition, designate the latter rather than the former as an **unconscious sensation** in order to have, so to speak, a palpable idea of it; however, doing so would move us away from the conceptual context and the...

---

*) Some, of course, believe—though no certain proof or counter-proof can be provided—that there is no sleep without dreams (which we simply cannot always remember). But even if it were so, these almost always confused dream-images would be poorly suited to maintain the causal connection between rational waking states. Moreover, one can become so aware of the gradual fading of consciousness as sleep approaches that one can believe in its total disappearance during full sleep.

He posits that psychophysics is essential for psychology because it explains how the mind "remains" even when we are not conscious.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "threshold of consciousness." He explains why "negative sensations"—though not consciously felt—must be mathematically distinguished from a total lack of sensation (zero) to understand the connection between the mind and the physical world.</meta>

Psychophysics to emerge from the necessary distinction between the psychic and the physical sides of life.

Furthermore, nothing prevents us from thinking of the physical activity that is unconscious for humans as contributing positively to the more general consciousness of a **World-Soul** <term>original: *Weltgeist*; the idea that the universe itself possesses a form of spirit or consciousness</term>, which exists at a deeper **threshold** <term>the boundary between being unconscious and becoming aware</term> than human consciousness. Aside from my works on natural philosophy, I even delve into such ideas toward the end of my *Elements* in Chapter 45. However, **Psychophysics** <term>the study of how physical stimuli relate to mental perceptions</term> must not begin with such far-reaching ideas. Instead, it must start with the facts of human experience and the rules that govern human consciousness. From there, we can find a solid basis to draw conclusions that go beyond and above the human level. For a human, consciousness may still be lacking in a certain state because the threshold of human consciousness—due to its physical conditions—has not yet been crossed, even though the deeper threshold of a more general consciousness (which we assume through logical deduction) has been surpassed. In any case, we are speaking here of consciousness only in the sense that it can be attributed to human experience.

One might argue: since even I admit that a "negative sensation" is indistinguishable from a "zero sensation" for the consciousness—given that sensation is itself a phenomenon of consciousness—then both must be designated equally with zero; in both cases, nothing is felt. This argument is untenable because the measurement of a sensation is given as a **function** <term>a relationship where one value changes based on another</term> of the measurement of the **stimulus** <term>original: *Reiz*; a physical energy like light or sound that triggers a sense</term>. Regarding the relationship between sensation and stimulus, the two cases differ quite significantly. Thus, a negative sensation, although immediately equivalent to a zero sensation for the conscious mind, has a different meaning for the **inner life of the soul** <term>original: *Seelenleben*</term> than a zero sensation does. It is, therefore, desirable for this difference to be reflected in the mathematical expression of the sensation itself, in a way that connects with the measurement of actual, positive sensations.

The zero value and the negative value of the **radius vector** <term>in mathematics, a line connecting a fixed center point to a point on a curve</term> of a curve as a function of an angle are similarly equivalent, in that one can find a noticeable value in reality for the one no more than for the other; and yet, they must be distinguished. In the same sense, the zero value and the negative value of a sensation must be distinguished; any mathematical objection against the latter distinction would likewise apply to the former. With regard to this application of the

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s mathematical justification for negative sensation values. He uses analogies from physics (sunlight) and everyday life (building a house) to explain why a "negative" value represents a state where conditions are building up toward a threshold, even if no sensation is yet felt.</meta>

...of the negative sign in mathematics for an analogous case, one can also place **negative sensation** <note>In Fechner's mathematical model, "negative" sensation refers to physical stimuli that are below the threshold of being felt</note> under the concept of the **imaginary** <note>original: *Imaginären*; here used to mean a mathematical abstraction that does not yet manifest as a conscious reality</note>, just as is done with the negative **radius vector** <term>radius vector: in geometry, a line segment representing the distance and direction of a point from the origin; Fechner uses this to show how math treats values that exist in a "negative" direction</term>.

It would be just as invalid to raise an objection from the previous point of view as to say: according to my theory, with negative sensations, an "incomplete condition" for sensation exists; but one might think an incomplete sensation should naturally correspond to such a state, whereas I claim an imaginary, non-existent sensation corresponds to it—one where something is still missing for its realization. Meanwhile, the following simple example should defeat this objection along with the previous one.

Physical **daylight brightness** (*H*) can be viewed as being measured as a function of **solar altitude** (*h*) through the formula **sin *h*** <note>original: *sin h*; the sine of the angle of the sun above the horizon</note>. Suppose there were no atmosphere present—which, in reality, reflects rays upward even when the sun sinks below the horizon—then brightness would only begin when *h* exceeds the zero value. All depths of the sun below the horizon would result in an equally absolute darkness. However, the values of *H* would still change depending on the different values of the negative altitude ($-h$), appearing with different mathematical values of **sin $-h = -\sin h$** <note>the sine of a negative angle is the negative of the sine</note>. And who would not say that when the sun moves from its lowest point toward the horizon, an "incomplete condition" for daylight is already developing, even though not the slightest bit of light is yet present, because something is still lacking for its realization?

When an iron stove is heated, every degree of heating contributes something toward bringing its plates to a visible glow; yet the glowing only begins when a certain degree of heat is reached, and only then does it grow in intensity with the degree of heating. Here too, while the condition for glowing is incomplete, absolutely nothing of the glow itself is yet perceptible.

One might collect money to build a house, but only begins the actual construction once a certain sum is gathered. One can pile up countless examples of this kind, which prove that an incomplete condition for a thing is by no means sufficient to bring about or carry along any part of the actual thing itself; rather, it corresponds to the opposite...

<summary>Fechner argues that "negative sensations" are mathematical representations of physical conditions building up toward a threshold, using analogies like the sun below the horizon or a stove heating up before it glows.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of "negative sensations." The preceding is essentially the same explanation, only in slightly different phrasing, that I gave in my *Elements* <note>original: *Elementen der Psychophysik* (1860), Fechner's foundational work</note> (particularly in the 18th section) regarding **negative sensation values**, and I still find no reason to retract any of it today. However, many have been unable to reconcile themselves with this explanation; some have raised mathematical objections, others have found fault with the experimental foundation of the **threshold law** <term>threshold law: the principle that a stimulus must reach a certain intensity before it is consciously felt</term>, and still others have ignored this foundation entirely. Consequently, they have sought to replace my **measurement formula** <term>measurement formula: the mathematical equation (Fechner's Law) used to calculate the intensity of a sensation based on the stimulus</term> with other formulas that contain no threshold value and thus yield no negative sensations. This has been done by **Plateau**, **Brentano**, **Delboeuf**, and **Langer**\*\*); in particular, it is **Delboeuf** and **Langer** who have spoken most explicitly about the difficulties in the relationships I have described. Here is the essence of their critiques, along with my reply.

Regarding the conceptual and mathematical aspects, **Delboeuf** says (*Ét.* p. 17): "According to Fechner, a negative sensation is a very weak sensation of which one has no consciousness." It never occurred to me, of course, to give such an explanation, as for me it contains a **contradiction in terms** <note>original Latin: *contradictio in adjecto*</note>. — **Delboeuf** further states (*Ét.* p. 17): "We could reject negative sensations a priori <note>from theoretical deduction rather than observation</note> because sensations are necessarily *something*, and because the expression 'negative sensation' is nonsense." Of course it is nonsense if one understands it as such; but I do not believe it is nonsense when one views it in

*) In a treatise which I can no longer cite, Volkmann argued very convincingly against **Darwin** that a rudiment—for example, a wing—that occurred by chance in the course of a creature's development could not yet grant that creature any advantage for its preservation or victory in the **struggle for existence** <note>original: *Kampfe um das Dasein*</note> against other creatures. Therefore, the progress toward a complete wing in the further course of generations could not have happened by virtue of an advantage that only arrived with the completed wing.

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner's defense of his psychophysical theories against his critic, Joseph Delboeuf. On the previous page, Fechner argued that "negative sensations" are not mathematical absurdities but represent physical stimuli that exist below the threshold of conscious awareness.</meta>

...one understands it in the sense mentioned above. One should not cling to words, but rather to the factual explanation of those words, which is necessary everywhere in this new field to avoid being misunderstood. — Delboeuf considers it (in *Ét.* p. 18) <note>original: *Étude psychophysique* (1873) by Joseph Delboeuf</note> almost self-evident that the sensation corresponding to a zero-value of the stimulus should be designated as zero, since there is indeed absolutely no sensation present there. In contrast, my **measurement formula** <term>measurement formula: the mathematical equation (Fechner's Law) used to calculate the intensity of a sensation based on the stimulus</term> links the zero-value of sensation to a specific finite value of the stimulus, while for a zero-value of the stimulus, it results in a sensation of negative infinity ($-\infty$). I believe, however, that I have sufficiently responded to this through the entirety of the discussions conducted from page 90 onwards. These arguments show that the sensation value corresponding to a zero-value of the stimulus—where there is absolutely no occasion for sensation—is functionally quite distinct from the zero-value of sensation at a finite stimulus value <term>finite stimulus value: a physical stimulus that is present but has not yet reached the "threshold" of awareness</term>. — Delboeuf asserts that the arbitrariness with which one can shift the starting point of an abscissa <note>the horizontal axis on a graph</note> or a thermometer scale applies against the assumption of a sensation's zero-point at a specific stimulus value. Yet, *in fact*, the same arbitrariness does not exist for the magnitudes of sensation as it does for arbitrary mathematical quantities or physical scales, because sensation truly only begins at a *certain* stimulus value—that is, it only exceeds the zero-value at that point. Generally speaking, one can no more establish or refute a formula for psychophysical relationships through pure mathematics than one could for any other real-world relationships—unless one could demonstrate contradictions or impossibilities within it, which Delboeuf has not done.

Meanwhile, Delboeuf considers his objections against our negative sensation values to be so fundamental and decisive that he seeks to avoid them in his formulas at any cost—even by sacrificing the **threshold** <term>threshold: the "limen," or the point where physical stimulus becomes conscious feeling</term>. Consequently, in his first treatise (*Étud.*), he proposes formulas that lead to no negative sensation values at all. In his second and third works (*Théor.* and *Rev.*) <note>Delboeuf's *Théorie générale de la sensibilité* and articles in the *Revue philosophique*</note>, he does indeed posit negative sensation values as well as positive ones, but in an entirely different sense than I do. In those, he (in *Théor.* p. 30) defines them not as imaginary or absent sensations, but as sensations of a negative character compared to those of a positive character—such as cold compared to...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal of his critics regarding the mathematical and psychological interpretation of sensations below the threshold of consciousness. He specifically addresses the objections of Langer, who disputes Fechner's concept of "negative sensation values."</meta>

...heat, black against white, silence versus noise, and brings both types of sensations under the same formula; about which and against which I believe I have already expressed myself sufficiently on page 31 ff.

Following this, we turn to *Langer's* objections.
I believe I have shown simply enough on page 37 that the fundamental mathematical contradiction which *Langer* (p. 51) raises against my **negative sensation values** <term>negative sensation values: Fechner's mathematical way of representing stimuli that are present physically but too weak to be felt consciously</term> is, in fact, a fundamental mathematical oversight. Regarding his own views, which are consistent with this contradiction, he posits—as noted earlier on pages 38 and 39—a threshold in his own sense and unconscious, but not negative or imaginary, sensations below that threshold. Rather, to him, sensations below the threshold are merely "small sensations which are too small to enter directly into consciousness," and are "always positive, in that stimulus and sensation become zero at the same time." Furthermore, "to the threshold value itself corresponds a finite positive sensation, which, following *Wundt* <note>Wilhelm Wundt (1832–1920), a foundational figure in modern psychology</note>, one can call the **sensation threshold** <term>sensation threshold: the point at which a stimulus becomes strong enough to just barely be perceived</term>, and which indicates that sensations must have crossed this threshold if they are to enter directly into consciousness—that is, if it is to be possible to immediately relate their existence to other sensations through intellectual activity." — Now, while these definitions may fit *Langer's* way of understanding threshold, consciousness, and the unconscious, I would not know why I should give up my own way of conceiving these same concepts—to which his definitions do not fit—since I find his opposing view linked to a mathematical invalidity. I am unable to derive immediate clarity from his definitions, nor do I know how to translate them into something factually clear to me; indeed, I cannot succeed with any conception that speaks of the existence of positive sensations below the threshold.

In fact, when I have my attention fixed on a field of sensation and can perceive absolutely nothing of a sensation (or a perceived difference), despite the fact that a physical cause for it exists—which is the factual case of the threshold in my sense—I do not understand how one can say that a small sensation (whether simple or a difference) is nevertheless present, which one simply cannot compare with others...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Langer's definitions of the threshold. On the previous page, Fechner began discussing Langer's claim that sensations below the threshold are merely "small" but still positive, rather than being zero or negative.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<sig>Fechner, *In the Matter of Psychophysics*. 7</sig>

...[beings/concepts]; <note>original: "kann;" — this concludes the sentence from the previous page regarding Langer's definitions</note> these seem to me to be completely mystical concepts. Unquestionably, there are cases where one can no longer remember a psychic value clearly enough to compare it with others, even though it was present; however, these are cases where the attention was not directed toward the first value or where it has been forgotten. In contrast, the fact of the **threshold** <term>threshold (Schwelle): the point at which a stimulus becomes strong enough to just barely be perceived by the senses</term> in our sense exists when one directs sharp attention to the relevant area, asks oneself directly whether anything of the kind belonging to that area is felt, and must conclude that one recognizes nothing of it. **My formulas take this conception of the threshold—and the absence of a sensation when the stimulus is below that threshold—directly as their underlying definition; they are declared valid specifically for this purpose. Therefore, if one speaks of "sensation" even in cases where nothing is felt despite directed attention, then my formulas naturally do not apply to such a sensation. If my opponents were to find formulas that fit both their own definition and experience—as Langer, at least, has attempted—they would not yet contradict mine; they would simply need to be interpreted consistently according to that different definition.** However, for me, the clear concept of the existence or absence of a sensation under conditions most suitable for observation is entirely lost if one does not grasp it in the aforementioned way. This leads easily to a confusion or an unclear blurring of the psychic sensation with its physical condition <note>Fechner refers here to the physical stimulus or the physiological process in the nerves</note>, which, as stated, can already be partially present when nothing of the sensation itself is yet there in the sense described above. And such a confusion seems to me to occur here and there.

Moreover, the following incidental remarks may perhaps contribute somewhat to a more definite conceptual clarification of the relationships involved here.

Take note for which case and from what viewpoint I provide and claim my definition of the absence of sensation as **fundamental**. I may or may not have sensations in a given area without later knowing for certain whether I had them or not, because my attention was not sufficiently engaged by them to leave clear traces behind. Such sensations and non-sensations can provide as little basis for fundamental determinations in **psychophysics** <term>psychophysics: the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations/perceptions they evoke</term> as the existence or non-existence of a star in a given location in **astronomy** outside the...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal of his critics, specifically addressing the empirical requirements for observing a sensation. It follows the discussion on page 97 regarding the "threshold" as a definitive boundary between perception and non-perception.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>

...time of its direct observation. However, if the goal is to establish the existence or non-existence and the conditions of the existence of a sensation in a given field and under given circumstances—in order to learn the dependence of the sensation on these circumstances—then it is also necessary to direct one's attention there under these circumstances. I then say that I have or do not have the sensation under these circumstances depending on whether I notice something of it or not; and I conclude that even when I do not particularly direct my attention to it, the sensation will follow the lawful dependency relationships that I was thus able to observe, so long as the **degree** of attention does not show itself to have an influence on it, which is itself a matter of observation (see pp. 26, 27). Naturally, I can find no reason to speak of the existence of a sensation under given circumstances when attention is *not* directed toward it, if I notice nothing of it when attention *is* directed—or even concentrated—upon it. Thus, if the stimulus is too small, I cannot admit the existence of a sensation under such circumstances at all.

By establishing the concepts for the most fundamental observation conditions of **psychophysics** <term>psychophysics: the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations they evoke</term> in this way, **speculations** about what **consciousness** is in general fall away entirely, and everything is kept within what can be internally demonstrated, as far as anything can be internally demonstrated with words. In short: I **call an unconscious sensation** one for whose existence something is still lacking, while an incomplete physical condition for it is present. <note>Fechner is arguing that an "unconscious sensation" is not a hidden feeling, but a physiological state that has not yet reached the intensity required to become a conscious sensation.</note> Whether something of a sensation is present or not here, I consider determined by the previous explanations. Even in the decisive observations regarding **difference sensations**, the question of whether such a sensation is present or not under given circumstances is to be answered by current observation under those circumstances with **attention** directed toward it; with this, the **fact** of its existence or non-existence is directly determined. Meanwhile, the observations themselves teach us that a lawful behavior of difference sensations—as determined by the **difference measurement formula** <term>difference measurement formula (Unterschiedsmassformel): Fechner’s mathematical formula used to calculate how much a stimulus must change for a person to notice a difference</term> and providing a basis for concluding the lawful behavior of sensation differences—only takes place insofar as the variable influence exerted on the fact of the **difference sensation** by the uncertainty of **memory** and the spatial and temporal positioning of the components of the difference is reduced and eliminated as much as possible through averaging a majority of trials and counterbalancing trials with opposite spatial and temporal positions, as discussed in Section 5. Consequently, general speculations about the hindering influence of memory on the establishment of our laws, which some have felt the need to inject here, become irrelevant.

After all this, I suspect that various conceptual or seemingly factual difficulties will still be raised, the nature of which I cannot foresee in every case. Against some that occur to me as possible, I note the following in advance.

One must not object to my explanations that I myself

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 98 regarding the "threshold" of perception. Fechner is clarifying the distinction between having a sensation and noticing a difference between two sensations, arguing that "unconscious" sensations are effectively non-existent for the purposes of measurement until they cross the threshold of awareness.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<sig>7 *</sig>

I concede that one sensation could be greater than another without me noticing it, if the difference is too small; I thus concede that something can exist within sensations that I do not notice. But it is one thing to notice a difference between two sensations and another to have two different sensations. I can notice that I have the one, and likewise that I have the other; but to compare them and to *perceive* their difference, a new act of consciousness must occur, which is subject to its own specific psycho-physical <term>psycho-physical: relating to the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations</term> conditions. Just as the psycho-physical activity must exceed a certain relationship to its threshold value <term>threshold value (Schwellenwerth): the minimum level of intensity required for a stimulus to be perceived</term> for me to have a simple sensation, the ratio of two psycho-physical activities must exceed a certain relationship to the ratio threshold so that I can notice a difference between two sensations I have already noticed.

Now one might say: Well, if I can have a difference between two sensations within me without being able to notice it, then I must also be able to have a simple sensation within me without being able to notice it. — But if I—to avoid speaking of something as existing for which there is absolutely no means of knowing it exists, and thus falling into the incomprehensible—may say that I have no differential sensation <note>original: *Unterschiedsempfindung*</note> when I can notice absolutely nothing of it, despite the presence of a stimulus difference, then I must also be allowed to say that I have no simple sensation when I can notice absolutely nothing of it, despite the presence of a simple stimulus. A difference between sensations is certainly present in the first case, but in the sense of the formula for measuring differences <note>original: *Unterschiedsmassformel*</note> it exists as a negative value below the threshold; and so, in the second case, in the sense of the measurement formula <note>original: *Massformel*</note>, a sensation is present, but likewise only as a negative value below the threshold. In its actual realization, something is missing rather than something positive being present. I believe that all of this is merely logical and consistent with the established fundamental definitions.

In every empirical science, it is necessary to start from simple facts of direct observation as fundamentals, to label these clearly and firmly, and to base conclusions on their combination. Fundamental psycho-physical facts of direct observation are: 1) that upon reflection on a given sensory field, I can *notice* nothing of a sensation if the stimulus is too small, and *notice* nothing of a differential sensation if the stimulus difference is too small; 2) that beyond the point (the threshold point <term>threshold point (Schwellenpunct): the specific mathematical point where a sensation becomes perceptible</term>) where the sensation or differential sensation begins to become noticeable, they grow along with the stimulus or stimulus difference. To briefly designate the first fact, I say, as already mentioned, that I *do not have* a simple or differential sensation if I can notice absolutely nothing of it by reflecting on the sensory field in question; however, the combination of the first fact with the second allows me to speak *by way of conclusion* of a difference in sensations existing, of which I notice nothing as a differential sensation, without

<summary>Fechner explains the difference between possessing a sensation and being conscious of a difference between two sensations. He argues that sensations below the threshold of awareness are mathematically "negative" and logically non-existent for empirical study until they become perceptible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 99, where Fechner concludes a point about the logic of "unnoticed" sensations. He is refuting the idea that something can be a "sensation" if it cannot be consciously perceived, defending his mathematical model of the threshold.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

...that the same consequence leads to speaking of sensations as existing when nothing of them can be noticed. For once we arrive at simple sensations, we are dealing with something elementary, a final point within the realm of consciousness.

If one wishes to say: In those cases where Fechner speaks of sensations below the threshold <term>threshold (Schwelle): the boundary between unconscious and conscious perception</term>, of negative sensations, or of unconscious sensations—all of which synonymously denote distances from the point where a sensation begins to become perceptible—there are already small positive sensations present which simply cannot be noticed; then I cannot prevent this. However, it seems to me as idle as it is conceptually unclear, and it changes nothing in the factual circumstances as I have envisioned them.

Langer <note>Christian Gottfried Langer (1835–1902), a contemporary critic of Fechner’s work</note> certainly asserts that, in the method of right and wrong cases <term>method of right and wrong cases: a scientific procedure where a subject compares two stimuli and the frequency of correct guesses is measured to determine sensitivity</term>, small positive differential sensations <note>original: *Unterschiedsempfindungen*</note> below the threshold do in fact exert an influence, stating (p. 52) in a somewhat difficult construction: This method is based on the fact,
> “that the sensation of a small difference in stimulus <term>stimulus difference (Reizunterschied): the physical difference between two things, such as weight or light</term>, which does not directly enter consciousness, is neither zero nor negative, but has a certain small positive value; this value is reinforced by the influences present during the observation of this small stimulus difference across many observations, and its existence is thereby proven in the clearest possible way.” —

But how so? According to my explanation in *Elements* I. 247 <note>original: *Elem. I. 247* — refers to Fechner's foundational work, *Elements of Psychophysics*</note>, which I find in no way invalidated by Langer’s “clear proof,” the influences of observation in that method can reinforce a stimulus difference below the threshold—which as such would not be *felt* and would therefore not yield even the smallest positive differential sensation on its own. That is to say, the corresponding difference in **psycho-physical activities** <term>psycho-physical activities: the underlying physiological or neural processes that correlate with mental sensations</term> can be so increased that the threshold is crossed, and it is thus *felt*. This happens more easily and more often the greater the difference is, because it then requires a smaller reinforcing increase to exceed the threshold. This is the foundation upon which the method rests. Langer, of course, relates the influences during observation *directly* to the reinforcement of a small sensation that he already presupposes to be present; but he does so wrongly, if a psychophysics is to be valid. For insofar as the differential sensation is hereafter a function of the relative stimulus difference

<summary>Fechner defends his concept of the sensory threshold against the critic Langer, arguing that "unconscious sensations" are not small positive values, but rather physical activities that haven't yet crossed into awareness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 100, where the author (likely Gustav Fechner) refutes the claims of critics like Langer and Delboeuf. The discussion focuses on whether a sensation can be said to "exist" if it falls below the threshold of conscious perception.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>

...or stimulus ratio <term>stimulus ratio (Reizverhältniss): the mathematical relationship between a physical stimulus and the sensation it produces</term>, the change in sensation can only be made dependent on changes in stimulus influences or psychophysical activities; but that a small sensation below the threshold <term>threshold (Schwelle): the boundary between unconscious and conscious perception</term> already exists without the reinforcing influence of observation is merely Langer’s <note>Christian Gottfried Langer, a critic of Fechner's psychophysical laws</note> assumption, not a proof for this assumption.

Aside from the objections explicitly raised by Delboeuf <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896), a Belgian philosopher and psychologist who engaged deeply with Fechner’s work</note> and Langer, one might appeal to the following case against me.

It may be that one is so desensitized to the general daylight noise <note>original: *Tagesgeräusch*; refers to the constant background hum of daily activity that we often tune out</note> that even with the most intense attention one thinks they hear nothing—that one says to oneself: it is perfectly still—and yet, when the daylight noise (that is, its physical condition) ceases, one perceives it as an *increased* silence. It may be that one has become so accustomed to air filled with odorous substances that even when directing attention to it, one thinks they smell nothing, and yet, when one enters air free of those odorous substances, one senses it as *increased* purity. Therefore, one might say, I must have had a hearing sensation in the first case and a smelling sensation in the second.

But if—to stay with the first example—I find no determination of my consciousness within myself that I recognize as a hearing sensation during a weak or seemingly absent daylight noise, then according to my definition, I have just as little reason in the first case as in the second to speak of the existence of such a sensation. I designate both the first and second states as a state of silence—that is, the absence of hearing sensation. If I nevertheless perceive a difference between both states, it is not a difference between positive sensations (which, rather, are not there), but a difference between states of the general consciousness <term>general consciousness (Allgemeinbewusstsein): the background state of the mind's awareness before it focuses on a specific sense</term> related to the field of hearing, which *are* there. In the same way, namely, as I can have a differential sensation <note>original: *Unterschiedsempfindung*</note> when the general consciousness is occupied with a given field (i.e., the direction of attention toward it) in the case of various stimuli *above* the threshold—where one of the two positive sensations exceeds the zero-point or threshold-point <term>threshold-point (Schwellenpunct): the exact mathematical value where a stimulus becomes perceptible</term> *more* than the other—it is only the difference ratio of the...

<summary>The author argues that background stimuli (like daylight noise) do not count as "sensations" if they are below the threshold of awareness, even if their absence causes a perceived change in the mind's general state. He maintains a strict definition of sensation as something that must be consciously detectable.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of sensations below the threshold of consciousness, specifically responding to the idea that we can perceive "increased silence" or "increased purity," which critics use to argue that unconscious sensations must exist.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>

stimuli are large enough for it—can I, with **two stimuli below the threshold in my general consciousness related to the relevant field, have the sensation of difference** that, to reach the **zero point** <term>zero point (Nullpunct): the state where a physical stimulus is just strong enough to produce the very first flicker of conscious sensation</term> of sensation in that field, more is missing in the effect of one than the other? In the realm of sound, this appears as **greater silence** (**not as a weaker sound**); the **difference ratio** of the stimuli below the threshold must simply be large enough for it. Indeed, the expression **greater silence** would have no meaning in our case if one did not want to understand silence in our sense. For a **Nothing** cannot even be greater than another, whereas something can be "more missing" in one instance than another—just as with assets when there are larger debts, or in reaching a point when one is further back from it.

One might object that even in the case where our **attention** is not directed toward the realm of sound, the **transition to greater silence** is felt, and indeed **attention** can be directed toward the realm of sound by it. But every stimulus in a given area attracts the psychophysical activity underlying attention to a certain degree; it distributes itself in a certain dependence on the distribution of stimuli, and must only exceed a certain threshold in any given area to be perceived as a direction toward this area. However, **attention is attracted even more strongly by stimulus differences and contrasts**, so this can also occur through the transition into **greater silence**.

If, conversely, as assumed until now, a longer **silence** (which had existed for sensation under the influence of a continuous, uniform, very weak daylight noise <note>original: *Tagesgeräusch*; background environmental noise</note>) was followed by a proportionally stronger one that would have also been below the threshold without the preceding silence, it could be that **sensitivity** <term>sensitivity (Reizbarkeit): the capacity of a sensory organ or the mind to respond to stimuli; here, Fechner suggests silence "rests" the sense, making it more sensitive later</term> is sufficiently increased by the preceding silence that not only a diminished **silence** is felt, but the noise is now actually perceived as **noise**.

Let us further note that the case under consideration by no means falls outside the scope of our formulas; rather, without it, a case provided for in these formulas would remain unsupported by experience.

In fact, if we consider the relevant **difference measurement formula** on p. 11, it states that for the perception of the difference between two stimuli $\beta, \beta_\prime$, the ratio of the stimuli whose differentiation is in question—that is $\frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime}$, or $\varphi$ for short—must reach a certain finite value, the **ratio threshold** <term>ratio threshold (Verhältnisschwelle): the minimum ratio between two stimuli required for their difference to be perceived, central to Weber's Law</term>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of stimuli falling below the "threshold of consciousness." Fechner is explaining how we can perceive a difference between two silent states (two "nothings") if the mathematical ratio between their physical intensities is large enough, even if neither is strong enough to be heard individually.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

exceeds *v* <note>original: "v" – Fechner uses 'v' to represent the "ratio threshold" or the minimum proportional difference required for a change to be noticed</note>. Even if $\beta$ and $\beta_\prime$ are both very small and therefore both below the <term>threshold</term> <note>the "Schwelle," or the point where physical energy becomes conscious sensation</note>, generally speaking, $\beta$ can be so small in proportion to $\beta_\prime$ that $\frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime} > v$; the only reason the practical range for this is not large in reality is because every sensory field is, so to speak, already "charged" with a certain <term>psychophysical activity</term> even without an external <term>stimulus</term>. This internal activity, even if it is below the threshold on its own, must be added to the external stimulus. Consequently, an infinite $\frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime}$ cannot occur for us, because $\beta_\prime$ can never truly be zero for us <note>because of the body's own internal "noise" or baseline activity</note>. However, in the event that the proportional difference between two stimuli that are below their own threshold is not large enough, it remains just as imperceptible as a difference between two stimuli above their threshold that is not large enough.

Thus, while it is admittedly impossible to have a conscious measure of how much is "missing" to produce a single <term>sensation</term> from a single <term>stimulus</term> below the threshold, the contrast between two successive stimuli below the threshold in a given sensory field can indeed bring to our consciousness that one is "more missing" than the other, provided the contrast is large enough. The fact that we can distinguish "greater silence" in the realm of sound from "greater purity of air" in the realm of smell is because we feel perfectly well which field our **attention** is directed toward, even if a <term>sensation</term> is more or less missing within that field.

In my **Elements** <note>referring to his seminal 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics)</note>, the question of how to interpret the case where $\beta, \beta_\prime$ are both below the threshold and yet $\frac{\beta}{\beta_\prime}$ is above the <term>ratio threshold</term> <note>the "Verhältnissschwelle," the minimum ratio between two stimuli needed to perceive a difference</note> caused me a certain amount of difficulty, which I believe I have solved here more convincingly than I did there (**Elem. II. 101**).

Based on the above, I cannot agree with the view of **Preyer**, when he says (in his treatise "**On the Limits of Tone Perception**" p. 67): "I claim, on the contrary [i.e., against Fechner], that just as the psychophysical activity of seeing is normally above the threshold even without an external light stimulus, so too is that of hearing without external sound; and that the sensation of silence is precisely what corresponds perfectly to the sensation of so-called 'eye-black' <note>original: "Augenschwarz" – the gray/black one sees in total darkness, often called 'eigengrau'</note> when the eye is closed tight against light or in the dark. Just as..."

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of other physiologists, specifically William Preyer, regarding the nature of "silence" versus "darkness." On the previous page, Fechner argued that even when external sounds are below the threshold of hearing, the body's internal activity prevents a true mathematical zero in sensation. Here, he examines whether silence is a sensation in its own right or merely the absence of one.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

"...just as the black in painting is an indispensable sensation, so the pause in music is an indispensable sensation." — To support this, Preyer <note>William Thierry Preyer (1841–1897), a physiologist and pioneer in developmental psychology</note> asserts that external and internal auditory stimuli are always present. — Certainly, though I simply mean that these stimuli can exist just as well below the <term>threshold</term> <note>the point where physical energy becomes strong enough to be consciously felt</note> as above it; indeed, the very essence of the law of the threshold is that the former can be the case. — Furthermore, he claims: "that the sensation of soundlessness [*silence*] is distinct from simply not hearing [for example, with one's finger]." But in my view, this is only because we remain conscious of directing our attention toward the auditory realm, even when the act of hearing itself is absent. — Furthermore, he claims: "that silence can be perceived as *greater* or *lesser*." — But surely only as a *greater* or *lesser* *degree of silence*, as discussed previously; and by *silence*, one understands the absence of auditory sensation.

I cannot find the analogy between the sensation of silence and that of <term>eye-black</term> <note>original: "Augenschwarz" – the subjective sensation of "gray" or "black" seen by the eye in total darkness, caused by the retina's own activity</note> to be entirely accurate. If I direct my attention to the auditory realm, I hear nothing if my hearing apparatus is healthy and all external noise is absent—that is exactly what I call *silence*; however, if I direct my attention to the visual field, I see *black*, even in the most normal state and when all external light is absent. *Silence* can be permeated by faint noise, just as the eye-black can be permeated by <term>light-dust</term> <note>original: "Lichtstaub" – the tiny, dancing sparks or "visual noise" seen when the eyes are closed</note>; but to the extent that this occurs, it is simply no longer complete *silence*. To be sure, a *pause* is required in music just as *black* is in painting; but only because in both cases <term>contrast</term> is required, into which *silence* can certainly enter just as well as *black*.

A case may arise where a stimulus exceeds its threshold—yielding a positive sensation according to our <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Fechner’s mathematical formula (S = k log R) relating the intensity of a stimulus to the strength of the sensation</note>—but exceeds it by such a small ratio that no difference from a <term>zero-sensation</term> should be noticeable; and yet, a positive sensation must necessarily exist in excess of a zero-sensation. Do we then feel something, or do we feel nothing of it? How is this conflict to be resolved or this contradiction to be interpreted? In my opinion, it is thus: based on previous discussions, simply perceiving the existence of a sensation is not the same as perceiving its difference from a simultaneous or preceding sensation, whether that sensation be zero or non-zero. Now, every doubt depends...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion of thresholds and sensations. On the previous page, he addressed William Preyer’s arguments about whether "silence" is a sensation or merely the absence of one. Here, he explores the psychological "gray area" that occurs when a stimulus is just barely strong enough to be detected, leading to a state of uncertainty in the observer.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>

...whether something is there or not there, regardless of the fact that certain determining grounds incline us toward one side and others toward the other; and such a conflict exists in the case where the <term>stimulus threshold</term> <note>the absolute minimum intensity required for a stimulus to be perceived</note> is crossed, but the <term>difference threshold</term> <note>the minimum change in intensity required to notice a difference</note>—or the <term>proportional threshold</term> relative to that stimulus threshold—is not. In this state, our consciousness is determined differently from one side than from the other, and depending on one's mood and other contributing factors, it may follow one direction or the other. Thus, one can view the interval where the stimulus threshold has been crossed, but the proportional threshold relative to it has not, as the interval where one is unsure, when questioned, whether one has a sensation at all or does not have one—a case that occurs often enough during <term>self-observation</term> <note>the practice of introspection, central to Fechner’s experimental method</note>, whereas one is certain of the existence of the sensation once that interval is exceeded.

If the doubt as to whether we have a certain sensation or not were limited to the simple case where the stimulus was *exactly* on the threshold, then—leaving aside the influence of imaginary factors, which must also be considered—such a case could only occur infinitely rarely, i.e., as good as never, because the exact point of the threshold can only be hit infinitely rarely. Furthermore, an infinitely small crossing of the stimulus threshold could only awaken an infinitely small sensation, of which one could not really speak; yet according to the <term>measurement formula</term> <note>Fechner’s famous logarithmic law (S = k log R) which describes the relationship between physical stimulus and mental sensation</note>, it should already be possible to speak of it. However, in conflict with the statement of the <term>difference measurement formula</term>, which applies to a different determination of consciousness, an uncertainty occurs as to which way the scale tips, even for somewhat larger crossings.

I believe I have explained these relationships more convincingly here than in the *Elements* Vol. II, p. 87 f. <note>Refers to Fechner’s 1860 foundational work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (*Elements of Psychophysics*)</note>.

One can still speak of a threshold in a special sense, which I did not consider in the *Elements*, though it does not contradict other explanations therein.

Suppose a violin or flute is played so loudly that it is clearly heard when played by itself. But suppose it were played in the midst of a massive crowd’s tumult,

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the mathematics and psychology of sensory perception. Having previously discussed the "stimulus threshold" (the point where a sensation begins) and the "difference threshold" (the point where a change is noticed), he now introduces the "mixture threshold" to explain how individual sounds or sensations can contribute to a larger experience even when they aren't consciously recognized on their own.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

...it can happen that its sound, despite the closest attention, completely dissolves indistinguishably into the general noise of a crowd, and one perceives nothing of the existence of the flute or violin tone. However, it would suffice to strengthen the tone as required against the rest of the noise to hear it distinguishably after all; in this respect, there exists a threshold similar to the <term>difference threshold</term> <note>The minimum amount by which a stimulus intensity must be changed in order to produce a noticeable variation in sensory experience.</note>, only that here we are not dealing with similar stimuli presented side-by-side or one after another, but rather with mixtures of different stimuli. Therefore, one could speak here of a **mixture threshold** <note>original: "Mischungsschwelle"</note>. 

It is indisputable that the tone of the instrument, even if not recognizable as such within the great noise, still contributes something to the strengthening and <term>nuancing</term> <note>original: "Nüancirung"; the subtle shading or coloring of a sensory experience.</note> of the noise above the threshold; indeed, even stimuli that are themselves below the threshold can contribute to the strengthening and nuancing of a sensation by being added to stimuli that are above the threshold. Regarding such sensations—which are not distinguishable on their own but contribute to a collective sensation—I say that they dissolve unconsciously into a more general <term>phenomenon of consciousness</term> <note>The overall state of what we are currently aware of.</note>. 

Indisputably, in every waking state of consciousness, a multitude of such unconsciously dissolving sensations from various sensory domains play a part. As is easy to see, however, the fact of the mixture threshold does not abolish the general concept of the threshold—along with its dependent <term>negative values of consciousness</term> <note>A mathematical concept in Fechner's work where sensations below the threshold of awareness are assigned negative numbers to describe their "potential" to become conscious.</note>—but rather generalizes it.

The difference threshold cannot be reduced to the idea that both intensities of sensation whose distinction is in question simply blur into a mixture of sensations (both being below the mixture threshold). For the difference threshold exists for sensations that are both clearly present—that is, they exceed the mixture threshold. And even if we cannot observe any waking state in which every external or internal <term>stimulus effect</term> <note>The physiological or psychological impact of a stimulus.</note> that causes a sensation is not accompanied by incidental stimulus effects, they all nonetheless fall below the threshold simultaneously in dreamless sleep—if such a thing even exists.

<summary>Fechner introduces the "mixture threshold" to explain how individual stimuli, like a single instrument in a crowd, can influence our overall experience even when they aren't consciously distinguishable. He argues that these "unconscious" sensations contribute to the nuances of our waking mind but disappear entirely during dreamless sleep.</summary>

<keywords>difference threshold, mixture threshold, unconscious sensation, psychophysics, stimulus effect, consciousness, sensory perception, Gustav Fechner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion of sensory thresholds, specifically moving from the "mixture threshold" mentioned on the previous page to the phenomenon of contrast. Having established how sensations can blend or emerge from a background, Fechner now addresses how the proximity of different stimuli (like black and white) changes our perception of their intensity—a key factor in testing the validity of Weber’s Law.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>

## XI. Remarks on Contrast Perception.
### (**Hering**, **Mach.**)
<note>Ewald Hering and Ernst Mach were influential contemporaries of Fechner; Hering is famous for his color vision theories, and Mach for his work on physics and the "Mach bands" optical illusion.</note>

It is not my intention to treat the doctrine of contrast sensations here in a general manner, but only to discuss a few main points of it, which might appear partly as difficulties against the experimental confirmation of **Weber's Law** <term>Weber's Law: The principle that the minimum change in a stimulus required for us to notice a difference is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</term>, and partly are to be touched upon elsewhere from one point of view or another.

In a treatise in the *Reports of the Saxon Society* from the year 1860, p. 71 ff. "On Contrast Perception"*) I have attempted to demonstrate, among other points not to be considered here, the following—whereby for the sake of brevity I shall call the psychophysical activity upon which light sensation depends **inner light**, and the boundary common to two contrasting surfaces the **boundary line**: 

1) That the so-called **elevation** <note>original: "Hebung"; the perceived enhancement or "lifting" of a sensation's intensity.</note> of impressions through contrast—by virtue of which white appears brighter next to black, and black appears blacker next to white, than next to a similar neighboring surface or next to gray—is not a purely psychological phenomenon. Instead, it stems at least partially (if not entirely, cf. p. 128 of the treatise) from the fact that, through the proximity of the contrasting surfaces (by virtue of an interaction between the parts of the **retina** or the **central organ** <note>The brain or central nervous system processing the visual signal.</note>), the inner light is strengthened on the white and weakened on the black, compared to the case where the neighboring surface were equally illuminated. Thus, the intensity of the inner light on each part of the retina depends not merely on the intensity of the external light falling upon that part itself, but also on the intensity of the light on the adjacent and even more distant parts. This I summarize for brevity—under an expression that should not be misunderstood after the previous explanation—by saying that the manner in which the inner light is distributed is co-determined by the distribution of the external light on the retina. 

2) That the elevation through contrast, by virtue of its long-range effect, does indeed extend over larger areas from the common boundary line toward both sides...

---

*) Cf. also my earlier treatise in *Poggendorff's Annalen* <note>A prominent 19th-century scientific journal.</note> Vol. L. 1840. p. 193 ff.

<summary>Fechner explains that visual contrast (making white look whiter next to black) is a physiological process where the "inner light" of the eye is physically altered by neighboring stimuli, rather than just a mental illusion. He argues that the intensity of sensation on one part of the retina is influenced by the light falling on surrounding parts.</summary>

<keywords>contrast perception, Weber's Law, inner light, retina, visual interaction, Ewald Hering, Ernst Mach, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s itemized list from page 107 regarding the nature of contrast. He is elaborating on his theory that contrast is not just a "trick of the mind" but a physiological (psychophysical) change in what he calls "inner light."</meta>

...extends, but is reinforced near the boundary line into so-called **edge-glows** <term>Randscheinen: today known as "Mach bands," these are illusory light or dark stripes that appear where areas of different brightness meet.</term>, which dissipate into the background with rapidly decreasing clarity from the boundary line onwards. Under certain circumstances they do not appear at all, while under others they are extraordinarily distinct; I have investigated the primary conditions for this in some detail. — 3) That in addition to the sensory sensation—which is reinforced on one side and weakened on the other, and which depends on the psychophysical strengthening and weakening of the components of contrast (in short, the "elevation phenomenon" <note>original: "Hebungsphenomen"</note> caused by the modification of the inner light)—there is a peculiar **mental affection** of a higher character. This depends on the difference between the impressions and is, in a narrow sense, **contrast perception**. Through this, the soul is more occupied and the attention more stimulated than by a spatial continuity of either white or black, despite the fact that a continuous white surface triggers a greater total sum of sensory sensation than when part of that white is replaced by black.

**Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist who often challenged Fechner and Helmholtz, particularly regarding color perception.</note> has, independently of me, conducted and published experiments (in his 2nd and 3rd Communications, 1873) which lead him to the same result regarding the first point—though from a different perspective—namely, the dependence of the inner light distribution on the external light distribution. Furthermore, **Mach** <note>Ernst Mach (1838–1916), for whom "Mach bands" are named.</note> has developed formulas for this dependence in his second paper which, as far as can be tracked through observation, agree well with experience.

Now, it is not unimportant that, according to these formulas, the experimental verifiability of Weber's Law is not significantly altered by this dependence of inner light on external light distribution. If we set aside the inner light of the **retinal black** <term>Augenschwarz: the "intrinsic light" of the eye; the sensation of gray or black we see even in a completely dark room.</term>—the existence of which must **everywhere** be accounted for to explain experimental downward deviations from Weber's Law—then the multiplication by a factor of *m* of the external light on **both** components being observed also results in the multiplication by *m* of the inner light on **both**, regardless of how the other distribution conditions of the external light are constituted. This follows generally from the universal formulas that Mach has established for the dependence of inner on external light distribution, including the edge-glows. It can be expressed more simply in agreement with Mach's simple formula...

<summary>Fechner explains how "edge-glows" (Mach bands) and contrast perception affect our inner sensory experience, arguing that while contrast changes our perception, it does not invalidate the mathematical consistency of Weber’s Law.</summary>
<keywords>edge-glows, Mach bands, contrast perception, inner light, retinal black, Weber's Law, Ewald Hering, Ernst Mach</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner's mathematical derivation of his contrast theory. On the previous page, he established that contrast involves a physiological modification of "inner light" and cited Hering's agreement on this point. Here, he begins to express these relationships through algebraic formulas to show how they align with Weber's Law.</meta>

(p. 3) ...show as follows for the case where one does not take into account the influence that a greater proximity of the contrasting parts has on reinforcing the effect; whereby I additionally, for now, set aside the internal light of the **eye-black** <note>original: "Augenschwarz"; this refers to the "dark light" or baseline neural activity of the retina even when no external light is present.</note>, which Mach <note>Ernst Mach (1838–1916), a physicist who studied how our eyes perceive edges and boundaries.</note> also neglected.

Let the retina <term>Netzhaut: the light-sensitive layer at the back of the eye.</term>, with total area $F$, be divided into the areas $f'$, $f''$, $f'''$ ..., upon which the respective external light intensities $i'$, $i''$, $i'''$ ... fall. Let the intensities of the **inner light** on these same areas, which depend on the magnitude and distribution of the external light, be respectively $I'$, $I''$, $I'''$ ...; let $W$ be the pupil width, $k$ a constant, and for brevity, let the total **sum of light** <term>Lichtsumme: the total amount of light energy falling across the entire surface of the retina.</term> falling on the retina be defined as $f' i' + f'' i'' + f''' i''' ... = \Sigma if$. Thus, each $I$ is, on one hand, simply proportional to the corresponding external $i$, and on the other hand, proportional to $i$ divided by the total sum of external light; therefore:

-> $I' = k W i' \frac{i'}{\Sigma if} ; \quad I'' = k W i'' \frac{i''}{\Sigma if}$ etc. <-

From this it follows immediately—as is required for the possibility of experimentally confirming Weber’s Law <term>Weber’s Law: a fundamental principle in psychology stating that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus.</term> for the areas $f'$ and $f''$—that the ratio $\frac{I''}{I'}$ remains constant when both $i''$ and $i'$ are multiplied by the same factor $m$. This is because while $W$ and $\Sigma if$ change as a result, they do so in the same way for both components of the contrast observed simultaneously.

The same still holds true when the distance between the contrasting parts is included in the more general formulas. However, it may be of interest to show that even the previous simplest formula is generally sufficient for the most basic cases of contrast that can be observed.

Thus, the elevating effect of contrast <note>The "elevation phenomenon" refers to how the presence of a dark area makes an adjacent light area appear even brighter than it actually is.</note> in such a case is easily derived as follows.

Let us imagine the entire surface of the retina divided into two equal parts, each set to a value of $1$. If both are initially illuminated uniformly with the intensity $i$, so that no contrast exists between them, then the total light sum $\Sigma if$ will be $2i$. Consequently, according to the formula, each half will have an inner intensity $I$ of:

-> $I = k W \frac{i}{2}$ <-

<summary>Fechner presents a mathematical model for how external light is transformed into "inner light" (perception) within the retina. If we now keep *i* for one half of the field of vision and illuminate the other with *m* times *i*, thereby introducing a **contrast**, then—although nothing has changed in the external illumination of the first half—the **inner intensity** will nevertheless become different; specifically, according to the formula, it will change into:

->*I* = $\frac{k W' i}{1 + m}$<-

However, aside from the change in **pupil width** <term>Pupillenweite: the diameter of the pupil, which regulates how much light enters the eye.</term> from *W* to *W'*, the second *I* will be larger or smaller than the first, depending on whether *m* is smaller or larger than 1. That is to say, depending on whether one strengthens or weakens the illumination of the second half, the inner light of the first will decrease or increase. Indeed, with an ever-increasing strengthening of *m*, it would decrease indefinitely, were it not for the existence of the internal brightness of the **eye-black** <note>original: "Augenschwarz"; this refers to the "dark light" or baseline neural activity of the retina even when no external light is present.</note>—which is disregarded here, but shall be considered below—setting a limit that cannot be crossed in this way. — But moving on.

A **moonlight spot** or **spot of light** from street lighting, falling upon the gray wall of an otherwise completely dark room through a gap in the window curtain or shutter, appears as if glowing—indeed, brighter than the entire wall in full daylight—despite the fact that one would be unable to read any writing held within that spot. The planet **Jupiter** appears incomparably brighter at night than the **sky-blue** during the day, in which it nevertheless vanishes. Now, to explain this, one might point out that the pupil dilates in the dark; however, its range of change is confined within much too narrow limits to suffice as an explanation. Furthermore: that the eye becomes more sensitive to light in a dark room—meaning that in a rested eye, the same external **light stimulus** <term>Lichtreiz: any external light that triggers a physiological response in the eye.</term> draws forth a stronger internal light than in a fatigued eye. However, even before the eye has rested in the dark—immediately upon stepping from a lit room into a dark one—the **phenomenon** <note>original: "Phänomen"; here referring to the subjective perception of heightened brightness.</note> on the wall asserts itself with the described character. Therefore, both circumstances can only act as contributing factors, not as the primary cause of the phenomenon.
