The second value is thus smaller not only because of the smaller pupil width, but also because the denominator *f'* has changed to F = *f' + f'' + f'''* ...; indeed, it is smaller in proportion to how small a fraction *f'* is of the total retinal surface. This immediately explains why the brightness of a star, such as Jupiter, appears so great at night, as it approximately corresponds to the first case. However, one should not assume that the brightness appears proportional to the value I'; rather, the sensation of brightness must be thought of as dependent on I' according to our **measurement law** <note>original: "Massgesetz"; this refers to Fechner’s formula (often called the Weber-Fechner Law) which states that sensation increases proportionally to the logarithm of the stimulus.</note>. In fact, **Weber's Law** <term>Webersche Gesetz: a psychological principle stating that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant proportion of the original stimulus.</term>, upon which the measurement law depends, can itself be supported by observations of **stellar brightness** <note>original: "Sternhelligkeiten".</note>.

The *i'* of a wall fully illuminated by daylight is, of course, much larger than that of a **moonlit spot** <note>original: "Mondfleck".</note> on a dark wall, and through this, the disadvantage that I' suffers in the first case compared to the second can, under certain circumstances, be compensated and outweighed—for instance, if the moonlight, instead of being concentrated on a relatively small spot, illuminates the entire room uniformly. But as long as the area *f* of the moonlit spot remains small compared to F, one can see that its I can easily maintain a significant superiority over the I of a room completely brightened by daylight.

Strictly speaking, of course, the formula becomes more complicated—without thereby overturning the explanation of the previous facts—if the internal light is to be included, which belongs to the eye even without external light stimulation in the so-called **eye-black** <note>original: "Augenschwarz"; the subjective sensation of "blackness" or the baseline neural activity in total darkness.</note>. It is probable that the distribution of this naturally occurring internal light—let us call it briefly the **natural eye-light** <note>original: "natürliche Augenlicht".</note>, which is distributed almost uniformly in the closed, rested eye—changes when the eye is opened to external light and its contrasts, in connection with the distribution of the inner light produced by external excitation

It has neither been claimed by me, nor does it follow from any of my fundamental views, that the intensity of the internal light originating from an inner source in closed eyes cannot rise above that of the natural eye-light after a preceding contrast excitation; rather, the opposite is true. Yet, such a claim or consequence has occasionally been attributed to me, making it easy to use facts that speak against it—and which were long known to me—against my position; however, I will not go into further detail on this here.

(p. 73) Since white reflects 57 times as much light as black, we have for the <term>intensity</term> <note>Intensität: refers here to the psychological magnitude of the light sensation.</note> *I'* of the writing in the <term>moonlight spot</term> <note>Mondscheinfleck: likely referring to a specific experimental setup where a spot of light, mimicking moonlight, is cast upon a surface to test the limits of legibility.</note>:

->$I' = \frac{k W (\alpha + \alpha')^2}{\sum if}$<-

for the *I''* of the surroundings of the writing:

->$I'' = \frac{k W (\alpha + 29 \alpha')^2}{\sum if}$<-

therefore:

->$\frac{I''}{I'} = \frac{(\alpha + 29 \alpha')^2}{(\alpha + \alpha')^2}$<-

As $\alpha'$ <note>$\alpha'$ represents the light reflected from the external object, while $\alpha$ represents the "natural light of the eye."</note> becomes smaller in relation to $\alpha$—that is, as the light reflected from the black ink of the writing vanishes compared to the <term>natural light of the eye</term>, a condition approached under weak external lighting—then even $29 \alpha'$ will recede compared to $\alpha$. In any case, $\alpha$ will be factored into the calculation, and *I''* will approach equality with *I'*. Consequently, the difference between *I''* and *I'* will become indistinct. Conversely, if $\alpha'$ becomes so strong through bright illumination that $\alpha$ vanishes by comparison, the ratio $\frac{I''}{I'}$ will rise to a maximum value; without the factors causing upper-level deviations, this ratio would remain constant despite changing external illumination.

## XII. The Difference-View of Sensations
->(Schneider, Delboeuf).<-

Many are inclined to explain all sensations in general as **difference-sensations** <note>Differenzempfindungen: the theory that we do not perceive absolute stimuli, but only the change or difference between a current stimulus and a previous or surrounding one.</note> in the sense that we can only experience a sensation through its distinction from one (or several) other sensations—whether stronger, weaker, or of a different kind—which existed previously or exist simultaneously. This naturally requires that some kind of differences occur between the <term>psychophysical processes</term> <note>Psychophysische Vorgänge: the physical activities in the brain or nervous system that correspond to mental experiences.</note> upon which the sensations rest.

This view, briefly called the **difference-view**, has recently been [advanced] by **Schneider** <note>G. H. Schneider, a contemporary of Fechner who wrote on the origin of musical and sensory perception.</note> in a small pamphlet ("The Distinction, Analysis, Origin...

<summary>Fechner provides a mathematical model showing how the eye's intrinsic light limits our ability to see contrast in low light, then introduces the "Difference-View," which argues that all perception is relative rather than absolute.</summary>
<keywords>psychophysics, light intensity, natural light of the eye, contrast ratio, difference-sensations, Schneider, Delboeuf, perception theory</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Difference-View" of sensation, transitioning from mathematical formulas of retinal light into a critique of contemporary psychological theories that claim sensation only arises from changes or differences in stimuli.</meta>

...and development of the same, etc. [Zürich, Schmidt, 1877 a)] <note>The author is likely referring to a work by the philosopher and psychologist Adolf Horwicz, who published *Psychologische Analysen auf physiologischer Grundlage* around this time.</note> forcefully advocated and further developed. He says, among other things (p. 3): "Sensations are not caused by individual <term>nerve excitations</term> <note>Nervenerregungen: the physiological firing or activation of nerves in response to a stimulus.</note> as such, but by the differences between nerve excitations among themselves and their relationship to the state of rest." — (p. 5): "Only differences in external states can act as stimuli." — (p. 6): "In the case of entirely continuous and extremely slow nerve stimulation, no sensation arises."

Joseph Delboeuf <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896) was a Belgian psychologist who worked on psychophysics and the relationship between physical stimuli and mental perception.</note> also advocates a certain **difference-view** in his *Theory*, which was accounted for on page 28 ff., and draws from it, among other things (p. 39), the following conclusion: "Analogy leads to the belief that an eye without eyelids and iris (through whose movements the incident light intensity could change), surrounded by a surface of uniform and constant brightness, would have no <term>sensation of light</term> <note>Lichtempfindung: the mental experience of seeing light, as opposed to the physical light itself.</note> because there would be no possible contrast. Furthermore, if the eye were immobile, so that it could not direct its gaze from one point to another, the surface could be differently bright and colored in different places, and yet the sensation would be null <insert>should probably mean: gradually fade</insert>, because the various points of the <term>retina</term> <note>Netzhaut: the light-sensitive inner lining of the back of the eyeball.</note> would each accommodate themselves in their own way to the type of light by which they are struck. The eye would find itself in the same relation to this luminous surface as the whole body in relation to an unevenly heated bath."

Now, it may well be—and to me it seems predominantly probable (cf. Section XX)—that no sensation can truly be attached to a *uniform* state of motion that includes no differences. However, insofar as <term>states of vibration</term> <note>Schwingungszustände: referring to the wave-like nature of light or nerve impulses.</note>—such as those that likely underlie our sensations—already include continuous changes, I confess that I do not see why such states must, so to speak, *be different* once again in order to produce sensation. This does not preclude the fact that, if they truly are different, the difference is also perceived; nor does it preclude that, in the contrast between <term>stimuli</term> <note>Reiz: any external factor that excites a sensory organ.</note>, every sensation appears relatively stronger or weaker than if one or the other stimulus acted continuously, as discussed in Section 11. Admittedly, since we never have sensations of a certain type or intensity without preceding or accompanying ones of another type or intensity, no strict experimental proof can be provided that sensation would still exist at all if these did not precede or accompany it. I simply find neither a theoretical nor an empirical reason to prevent such an assumption, and therefore believe—even though the opposite view—

<summary>The author examines the "Difference-View" held by psychologists like Horwicz and Delboeuf, who argue that sensation requires contrast or change. While the author admits that perfectly uniform states might not be perceivable, he argues that the inherent vibrations of stimuli might be sufficient to trigger sensation without needing external contrast.</summary>
<keywords>Difference-View, nerve excitation, Delboeuf, light sensation, retina, vibration states, stimulus, psychology, sensation, adaptation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the "Difference-View" of sensation. While the previous page discussed the theories of Delboeuf and Horwicz—who argued that sensation only arises from change or contrast—this page presents the author's own experimental evidence from 1860 to suggest that sensation can persist even under constant, uniform stimulation.</meta>

...acceptance, however, can only be based on a belief—that if a child were to wake for the first time in a completely uniform brightness, while keeping all other <term>sensory stimuli</term> <note>Sinnesreize: external physical factors, like light or sound, that trigger a response in the nervous system.</note> away as much as possible (though they certainly cannot be entirely excluded), it would nonetheless perceive the brightness of the light; and that even if, during a prolonged, uniform stimulus, the sensation can be **pushed down** below the <term>threshold</term> <note>Schwelle: the point at which a stimulus is strong enough to be consciously perceived.</note> due to a decrease in <term>irritability</term> <note>Reizbarkeit: the physiological capacity of a nerve or organ to respond to a stimulus.</note>—whereupon the sensation vanishes—it can nonetheless, as far as experiments allow for a probable conclusion, remain at a degree above the threshold.

In this regard, I recall the following experiment, which I reported in the treatise "On Contrast Sensation" in the *Reports of the Saxon Society*, 1860, p. 114.

> "In the square opening (6 <term>Paris inches</term> <note>par. Zoll: a historical unit of length, approximately 2.7 centimeters per inch.</note> wide) of the shutter of an otherwise dark room, I placed a colored glass pane and gazed steadily through it at the sandy courtyard in front of the house, which was half in the house's shadow and half illuminated by the sun. I continued this for 1/4 of an hour using a dark violet glass, which showed a vast predominance of red over blue in the <term>prismatic spectrum</term> <note>Prismatisches Spectrum: the array of colors produced when light is dispersed through a prism.</note>, and for 5 minutes using a deep red glass. In the first moment, the color of the bright part of the courtyard certainly appeared most vivid, but it did not seem to diminish very strongly, and (with both the one and the other glass) it did not seem to diminish at all beyond a certain limit, so that a longer continuation of the experiment seemed useless."

Now, one can naturally say: if the courtyard had been **completely** uniformly intense violet or red, and the experiment had been continued **even** longer, the <term>color sensation</term> <note>Farbenempfindung: the mental experience of a color as interpreted by the brain.</note> would eventually have been completely extinguished. However, if one considers the experiment not proving enough for my view because it was not possible to **completely** establish these conditions, then that **assertion** is even less proving for the opposite view.

When using a blue glass, which showed almost only blue with very little red in the spectrum, the color impression vanished into a kind of whitish glow almost immediately after putting the glass on; and even then, this was still very much the case when I used a double or even triple layer of glass instead of a single one. In my opinion, this means: here we have, in contrast to the first case—where the color sensation, weakened by long exposure to the objective color, remained permanently above the threshold—a second case, where it sank below the threshold and remained there. But why did this not also happen in the first case, if the opposite view were correct?

To be sure, experiments like the following can seem to speak very much in favor of the supporters of the **difference-view**.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument against the theory that sensation only exists through change or contrast. Aubert says (p. 61): "I have convinced myself that the judgment of pure gray is very uncertain, and that a nuance toward blue, red, or yellow is not recognized or is incorrectly determined if one does not have a gray of equal brightness next to it for comparison." <note>Hermann Aubert (1826–1892) was a prominent physiologist who studied how we perceive light and color.</note>

And Ulrici*) (*Body and Soul*, p. 294): "The reddish color of a barrel of water in which perhaps only 1/10 <term>grain</term> <note>Gran: a historical unit of weight; one grain is roughly 65 milligrams.</note> of <term>carmine</term> <note>Carmin: a deep-red pigment. Ulrici is illustrating that very dilute colors are invisible without a clear comparison.</note> has been dissolved, we are unable to perceive; after mixing in a somewhat larger amount of carmine, we are indeed able to recognize it, but only if we have other, uncolored water next to it and compare the former with the latter."

But these facts prove only: **firstly**, that small <term>stimulus differences</term> <note>Reizdifferenzen: the physical difference between two inputs, such as two different weights or two shades of light.</note> are not recognized on their own at all if they fall below the <term>difference threshold</term> <note>Unterschiedsschwelle: the minimum amount by which a stimulus must change in order for a person to notice a difference.</note>; **secondly**, that the difference between stimuli, according to the laws of contrast, brings about a relative strengthening or weakening of the components of that difference compared to a case where no difference existed (a topic dealt with in the previous section).

According to **Schneider**, the <term>sensation of rest</term> <note>Ruheempfindung: the feeling of stillness or lack of activity in a sensory organ.</note> also only arises through the difference—the contrast of weak excitations against strong ones. (See his short work *On the Sensation of Rest*, Zurich, Schmidt, 1876, p. 12.) I do not wish to argue against this. Without comparing the state of rest with either an excited state or a state even freer from excitation, we do not have a sensation of rest, but simply no sensation at all in that particular sensory field. Thus, the sensation of rest is indeed a matter of <term>contrast sensation</term>, to which the points made in Section 10, page 103 f., can be applied. However, the fact that the sensation of rest can only be perceived through contrast does not force us to assume that *no sensation at all* can be perceived without contrast.

### XIII. The Oscillatory and Chemical Hypotheses (Hering).

I will not return here to the very general views on the nature of psycho-physical activity or movement that I established in Chapter 42 of my *Elements* and for which I still find no reason to abandon, as the following contains no occasion to venture into such generalities. Rather, the concern here shall only be the question of whether one should adhere to one or the other of the two most prominent hypotheses regarding the nature of psychophysical activity—not in the world at large, but specifically in humans and animals.

**For my part, I consider it overwhelmingly probable—and have discussed the reasons for this in detail in *Elements* II. 282, finding a fairly broad agreement on this point—that at least the clearly oscillatory stimuli of light and sound trigger corresponding oscillatory movements within our nervous system. The quality of sensation depends on the simple or complex periods of these oscillations, while the quantitative side of sensation depends on the <term>living force</term> <note>lebendige Kraft: an older term for kinetic energy; Fechner suggests that the intensity of a feeling relates to the physical energy of the neural vibrations.</note> or, more generally, a function of the velocity or change in velocity with which the active particles oscillate (Section XX). Meanwhile, recently and from various sides—with particular emphasis and extensive development by Hering in particular—the view has gained ground that it is chemical processes which are triggered in our nervous system by light stimuli, and likely by stimuli in general. According to this view, the quality and quantity of sensation depend on the nature and intensity of these processes. This view has also received confirmation of seemingly compelling force regarding the sensation of light through recent findings concerning <term>visual purple</term> <note>Sehpurpur: now known as rhodopsin, a light-sensitive pigment in the retina that bleaches when exposed to light, starting a chemical reaction that allows us to see.</note>.** *)

---

*) To be sure, there are also some counter-arguments that still require clarification before one can venture too far into the chemical theory from this angle. Aside from the fact that yellow <term>sodium light</term> <note>Natronlicht: the bright yellow light produced by sodium vapor, often used in historical experiments because of its specific wavelength.</note> is perceived but is unable to bleach the visual purple, another point is that according to recent investigations by **Kühne (Investigations from the Physiological Institute at Heidelberg 1877 p. 17 ff.)** <note>Wilhelm Kühne (1837–1900) was a pioneering physiologist who conducted famous experiments on how the retina captures "optograms"—images "printed" on the visual purple of the eye.</note> the cones in the retina lack [visual purple]... <note>The original German text "ent-" is cut off, likely meant to be *entbehren* (to lack).</note>

<summary>Fechner discusses two competing theories of neural activity: the oscillatory hypothesis (nerves vibrate like strings) and the chemical hypothesis (stimuli trigger chemical reactions). He notes that the discovery of "visual purple" (rhodopsin) has recently lent strong support to the chemical view favored by Ewald Hering.</summary>

<keywords>psychophysics, Ewald Hering, Wilhelm Kühne, oscillatory hypothesis, chemical hypothesis, visual purple, rhodopsin, kinetic energy, sensory quality</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner's reconciliation of Hering’s chemical theory of sensation with his own oscillation-based model. He argues that even if neural activity is chemical, it must be understood through the physics of vibration to be mathematically useful.</meta>

Now, however, I already unhesitatingly conceded the possibility of the chemical hypothesis in *Elements* II. 283, while simultaneously pointing out that it in no way contradicts the oscillation hypothesis* ; indeed, in my view, it must be translated into those very terms according to the following considerations, in order to provide a useful starting point for <term>psychophysics</term> <note>The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations.</note>. This is necessary, firstly, because it maintains a traceable relationship between the stimulus and the psychophysical activity, and secondly, because oscillations are subject to clearer <term>quantitative measurements</term> and clearer mental conceptions in general than chemical processes. Let us examine the points under consideration here more closely.

A chemical process generally relies on the fact that previously separate particles combine, or previously combined ones separate, or particles exchange between compounds, as certain particles enter each other's <term>sphere of attraction</term> <note>The physical range within which particles exert a force on one another.</note> while others exit it.** In every case, this requires a movement of the particles toward or away from each other, and it is quite conceivable from the outset that this movement—if it is triggered and sustained by an oscillating stimulus—might itself take on an oscillatory character instead of occurring in a uniform progression. This does not prevent the approach or distancing of the particles from ultimately gaining the upper hand, thus resulting in a permanent connection or separation. Furthermore, it is nothing new that chemical processes are accompanied by oscillation phenomena, even when they are not awakened by oscillatory stimuli. When fire burns in a stove, the chemical process is accompanied by a strong development of heat and light—that is, by a vigorous oscillatory process...

...lacking. "However, the sensory epithelium in the human <term>yellow spot</term> <note>The macula lutea, the area of the retina responsible for sharp, detailed central vision.</note> consists predominantly—and in the <term>central fovea</term> <note>The small pit in the retina that provides the clearest vision.</note> at the place of clearest vision, which certainly also possesses color sensitivity—exclusively of <term>cones</term> <note>Photoreceptor cells in the eye that respond to light and allow us to see color.</note>."

---

*) <note>original: "Selbst, wenn man die durch Licht- und Schallreiz erweckten Veränderungen als chemische fassen will..."</note> "Even if one wishes to grasp the changes awakened by light and sound stimuli as chemical—which they well might be, or at least with which they might be associated—these will ultimately have to be reduced to changes in <term>molecular conditions</term>, which, insofar as they are stimulated and sustained by vibrations, can hardly be thought of as anything other than vibrational movements."

**) I am only able to trace <term>Hering's assimilation and dissimilation process</term> <note>Ewald Hering's theory that the visual system works through the buildup (assimilation) and breakdown (dissimilation) of chemical substances.</note> back to these viewpoints as well.

<summary>Fechner argues that the chemical and oscillation hypotheses are compatible, suggesting that chemical reactions in the nerves are essentially vibrational movements that can be measured mathematically. He references the anatomy of the human eye to support his claims about sensory processing.</summary>
<keywords>oscillation hypothesis, chemical hypothesis, psychophysics, molecular movement, Ewald Hering, retinal cones, macula lutea, fovea centralis, assimilation, dissimilation</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that sensory perception is better explained by vibrations (oscillations) than by purely chemical reactions. He acknowledges that while chemical changes occur in the eye, the transmission of that signal to the brain and the nature of sustained sensation point toward an oscillatory model.</meta>

...glides along; less lively chemical processes are undeniably accompanied only by less lively oscillatory processes or changes to those already present.

Now, the question certainly arises from the outset whether sensation is functionally dependent on the chemical process itself—which consists of the approach or removal of particles relative to one another (that is, the positions around which they vibrate)—or rather on the accompanying oscillations. I say: the latter is to be preferred. First, for the two general reasons stated above on page 118; second, because in some sensory perceptions, such as sound, the mechanical affection of the nerves through vibrations is clearly evident, while a chemical process in that case can only be thought of as a vague possibility; third, because the chemical process that the sensation of light triggers on the <term>retina</term> <note>The light-sensitive layer at the back of the eyeball.</note> can only be related to the <term>visual substance</term> <note>The hypothetical matter in the eye (like "visual purple") that reacts chemically to light.</note> of the retina itself, and it remains entirely obscure how its effect should extend to the brain other than through the accompanying oscillations. Even if one includes the retina itself as a carrier of sensation within the entire sensory organ (which aligns more with my own view than with the prevailing one), this can only be the case insofar as its <term>psychophysical processes</term> <note>The physical activities in the body that directly correspond to mental experiences.</note> are in continuity with those of the brain, if our consciousness is to be affected by them at all. The fact that vibrations propagate more slowly through the nerves than light vibrations do through the <term>aether</term> <note>A now-obsolete scientific concept of a medium that fills all space, once thought to carry light waves.</note> outside does not affect their period or amplitude, and thus does not affect the qualities of sensation dependent upon them. And this does not change even if one believes the propagated vibrations can be categorized as electrical rather than light vibrations.

The question remains: how is one to conceive of the occurrence of lasting sensations through a chemical process at all? I can look into the bright sky for a very long time: the sensation of brightness gradually weakens, but it does not disappear. It seems impossible to reconcile this with a chemical process proceeding continuously in the same direction—be it a process through which particles separate or one through which they combine; for the separation or connection must eventually be completed; and as long as the light-

<summary>Fechner argues that sensation must be fundamentally based on oscillations rather than chemical processes, noting that chemical reactions in the retina would be unable to explain how signals travel to the brain or why sensations can persist over long periods.</summary>

<keywords>psychophysics, retina, oscillations, chemical process, sensation, aether, neural transmission, visual substance</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on whether sensory perception is driven by chemical reactions in the body or by physical vibrations (oscillations). He responds to potential criticisms regarding the "exhaustion" of chemical materials in the eye and body, arguing that biological systems are naturally periodic and restorative.</meta>

...sensation, even if not linked to the chemical process itself, but rather to the oscillations carried by it, the **oscillation hypothesis** is affected by this difficulty in the same way as the **chemical hypothesis**; whereas this difficulty naturally disappears for the former if one does not consider the oscillations to be mediated by the chemical process at all. However, even if one allows for this mediation—which should certainly be recognized as probable in the case of light sensation and possible in others—the difficulty does not appear to be decisive. For one, one can imagine it to be just like an ever-burning fire, where new fuel is constantly supplied to the chemical process while the burnt material falls aside as ash. Correspondingly, new material can be constantly supplied to the site of the chemical process through **circulatory pathways**, and the altered material can be carried away. Furthermore, one can also imagine that just as photographic changes caused by light outside the eye occur only gradually, the same may be the case with the photographic changes of the <term>visual purple</term> <note>Also known as rhodopsin, this is a light-sensitive pigment in the retina that bleaches when exposed to light and regenerates in the dark.</note>. And it is very possible that both circumstances come into play together.

I have developed my own ideas here in opposition to the very different ones set forth by **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918) was a physiologist who proposed that vision was based on "opponent processes" (chemical buildup and breakdown) rather than the physical vibrations Fechner favored.</note> in his 5th and 6th communications, of which the most general viewpoints were shared on page 36, and some specifics shall be shared in the appendix to the following section, without entering into a critical comparison between the two here. Rather, I only wanted to show in a general sense that the involvement of the chemical process in the sensory process can be conceived in ways other than Hering’s view, and to state the reasons why I prefer my own way of understanding it.

Although, following the discussions in the 42nd section of my *Elements* <note>Referring to his seminal 1860 work, *Elements of Psychophysics* (Elemente der Psychophysik).</note>, I am far from restricting the **psychophysical process** in principle to **vibrations**, **oscillations**, or **periodic movements**, I nevertheless hardly doubt that the psychic processes within us are indeed generally linked to such things. After all, our organism is overall a thoroughly oscillatory or periodic being, as seen in sleeping and waking, the pulse, breathing, the need for food and drink, in gait, swallowing, sobbing, laughing, and the pleasure of rhythm...

<summary>Fechner argues that sensory perception is rooted in physical oscillations rather than just chemical changes, suggesting that the body’s natural circulatory and periodic systems prevent the "burning out" of sensory substances like visual purple.</summary>
<keywords>oscillation hypothesis, chemical hypothesis, visual purple, Ewald Hering, psychophysical process, periodicity, sensation of light, rhodopsin</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of the "oscillation hypothesis." Having addressed the chemical hypothesis in the previous page, he now speculates on how physical vibrations (oscillations) within the body’s molecules and nerves could account for various sensations like pressure, temperature, taste, and smell, while comparing his views to those of the Belgian mathematician Joseph Delboeuf.</meta>

in **Music**, and finally evidenced by the oscillatory **movements** of **muscle molecules** during **muscle contraction**, which are well-known to **physiologists**: And though some **sensory stimuli** do not betray their oscillatory nature in themselves, it is easy to imagine that they act by modifying **vibrational movements** already present in the **body**, which on their own do not cross the **threshold** <note>The "threshold" (Schwelle) is a central concept in Fechner's psychophysics, referring to the minimum intensity a stimulus must reach to be consciously perceived.</note>. Thus, lifted **weights** act upon the oscillatory **movements** of the **muscle molecules** just mentioned; these movements, however, undoubtedly come about under the **influence** of an equally oscillatory **activity** of the **nerves**, with which **sensation** is connected. The **sensation** of **pressure** on the **skin** could arise from the fact that the oscillatory **activity** of the **nerves** at the **periphery** <note>The "periphery" refers to the sensory organs and nerves far from the brain, such as those in the skin.</note> is inhibited and, in an antagonistic way, is reinforced—effectively dammed up—in the **brain**; the **sensation of temperature** through an **equalizing movement** between the differing **intensities** of peripheral and central **vibrations**, a view which **Delboeuf’s** **way of thinking** <note>Joseph Delboeuf (1831–1896) was a Belgian scholar who proposed that sensation depends on the tension or difference between external stimuli and internal states.</note> could fit, without necessarily fitting the remaining **sensations**. The **oscillations** in **sensations of taste** and **smell** could be **companions** of chemical **processes** in a similar **way** as they are in **sensations of light**. **Of course**, I offer all of this only as entirely hypothetical.

The **view** indicated here regarding **temperature sensations** would simultaneously explain: 1) that two **modifications** of this **sensation** exist, depending on whether the **intensity** of the **vibrations** predominates on the central or peripheral side, thus indeed agreeing entirely with **Delboeuf’s** **conception**; 2) that **sensations of light** and **sensations of sound**, insofar as they do not rest on such a **difference**, also cannot—as **Delboeuf** does—be brought under the same **point of view** as **temperature sensations** or be covered by the same **formula**. This, moreover, according to a **remark** already made earlier (**Page 31**), is prevented without any **hypothesis** by the fact that **sensations of light** and **sound** **ascend** to ever higher **values** from the bottom up with the **strengthening** of the **stimulus**, without an **indifference point** <note>An "indifference point" (Indifferenzpunct) is a neutral state, such as a temperature that feels neither hot nor cold.</note> in between; whereas with the **strengthening** of the external **heat stimulus**, the **sensation of cold** decreases from the bottom up and passes through an **indifference point** into a growing **sensation of warmth**.

<meta>This page transitions from Fechner’s discussion of the "oscillation hypothesis" to a specific critique of his rival, Ewald Hering. ### XIV. On the Sensation Relationships of Black and White, with an Appendix on Hering’s Color Theory.

Apart from the fact that Hering does not accept **Weber’s Law** <note>Weber’s Law states that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is proportional to the magnitude of the stimuli. Fechner used this as a mathematical foundation for measuring the mind.</note>—which I champion—as applying to either external or internal **psychophysics** <note>Psychophysics is the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and the sensations they produce.</note>, and thereby undermines the very foundation of my work, a major point of contention exists. He holds fundamentally different views regarding the intensity of sensations in general, though he has currently only detailed these views in relation to the sensation of light. Since this disagreement is most evident when considering the relationship between **Black** and **White** and their transitions through **Gray**, I must first describe Hering’s views on the matter. I will do so using his own words as much as possible before explaining my own position.

Hering’s views on this subject are found in his *4th Communication* <note>original: "4. Mittheil." This refers to one of Hering's published reports to the Vienna Academy of Sciences titled "Zur Lehre vom Lichtsinne" (On the Theory of the Light Sense).</note>, and all page references in this section refer exclusively to that work unless another report is mentioned. His explanation of his chemical theory in the *5th Communication* (which I mentioned on page 36) and his theory of color sense in the *6th Communication* serve as supplements. I provide the main points of the latter as an appendix at the end of this section, along with a hypothetical view of my own.

Hering believes it is incorrect to view the scale of light sensations—from **Black** through **Gray** to **White**—as a purely quantitative progression of brightness. Instead, he argues these sensations differ *qualitatively*, just like color sensations do, and should be classified under the same general category. **Black** transitions through **Gray** into **White** just as **Blue** passes through **Violet** into **Red**, or **Red** through **Orange** into **Yellow**, and so on.

Accordingly, the author translates or identifies sensations of "lighter" and "darker" as being "more whitish" (containing more white) or "more blackish" (containing more black). He refers to the scale of transitional sensations from the deepest black to the purest or brightest white as the **black-white sensation series**.

<summary>Fechner introduces a fundamental disagreement with Ewald Hering regarding light perception, specifically Hering's rejection of Weber's Law and his argument that the black-to-white spectrum is a qualitative "series" rather than a quantitative increase in intensity.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Fechner, Ewald Hering, psychophysics, Weber's Law, light sensation, color theory, black-white sensation series, qualitative vs quantitative</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Ewald Hering’s "4th Communication," specifically examining his proposal for how black, white, and gray sensations should be categorized and measured. In the meantime, however, he still leaves room in a certain way for the **quantitative determinability** <note>The ability to measure a sensation using numerical values.</note> of the sensations in this series, noting (on p. 4): "One must either completely drop the term **'intensity'** [when applied to the scale of transitional sensations between black and white] and say that in the sensation-series in question, the sensation changes its quality step by step—viewing the entire scale in the same way as the **color scale** which leads from one saturated color, e.g., red, to another, e.g., yellow; or one must assume **two intensity scales** in the black-white sensation-series, one corresponding to white or light, the other to black or dark."

"If two intensity series are to be assumed simultaneously, this means (according to p. 5) nothing other than that all transitions from white to black can be viewed as **mixtures** of those two sensations which emerge most purely at the two ends of the series"; and the terms intensity, strength, and intensive magnitude can only be applied to the sensation-series under discussion on the condition "that one grants two intensities to each individual member of the series, and specifies the **ratio** in which the intensities of the black and white sensations stand to each other; here, one distinguishes black and white as relatively simple sensations from the transitions between the two as mixed sensations."

An absolutely purest, deepest black and an absolutely purest, brightest white are ideally conceivable, even if they cannot be demonstrated in reality (p. 7, 8); in any case, every stage of **gray** must theoretically be represented by mixtures of this ideal black and white, and there must be a gray that is equally distant from both, containing an equal amount of each. By using letter designations and expressions in the form of **formulas** (p. 9 ff.), the author seeks to better explain this ideal conception, without, however, indicating a way to base actual **measurements** <note>original: "Massbestimmungen"</note> upon it.

"The actual sensation of black (p. 13, 14) only comes about under the influence of an external **light stimulus** <note>original: "Lichtreiz"</note>, just as the white sensation usually does through objective..."

But regardless of how the feasibility of the analogy between the black-white and colored sensation series holds up within the field of light itself, the following further difficulties arise when looking at other sensory fields.

**Hering** believes he can translate the **scale** of intensities of **light sensations** into a **scale** of changing qualities and thus eliminate the concept of intensities from it. But a corresponding **elimination** or **translation** of the concept of intensity would not work for **sensations of sound**, **taste**, or **weight**\*) etc.; and it would indeed be strange if, for light sensations, there were not just such a dependence of strength on the strength of the stimuli as for other sensations, but rather, exceptionally for them, only a different **quality** of sensation depended on different strengths of the stimuli, while the concept of strength or intensity were to be dropped entirely. One closes one's eyes before a very strong light, just as one plugs one's ears against a very strong sound; if black were as positively stimulating as white, one would merely be falling from one excess of excitation into another.

If the strong impression that black truly makes in contrast with white were to stem from the fact that black itself acts just as positively as white, then it would remain inexplicable why the impression on the soul is, in a certain sense, decidedly stronger—and less tedious—when one places a black spot or any black lines on a white background, than when one has a purely white or purely black background before them. If black possessed an equally positive character as white, then the "sum of the positive" would not be changed by placing a black spot on the white ground compared to a purely white ground. In my view, however, the sum of the sensory sensations of brightness has actually decreased; yet, through contrast, the higher (discriminatory) <term>mental activity<note>Original: *Seelenthätigkeit*. This refers to the active processing of the mind—or "soul"—as it distinguishes and interprets sensory data.</note></term> is positively stimulated in such a way that we find ourselves, on the whole, more intensely occupied. Even Hering will not deny this influence of contrast, yet he would then be unable to attribute it to a positive character of black in itself; but if that is the case, what remains to allow us to speak of such a character at all?

### Appendix on Hering’s Color Theory and an Original Hypothesis.

Color sensations are treated by Hering in his "6th Communication" as generally analogous to the sensations of white and black, and in connection with them. The author’s most fundamental definitions follow here, though for a more precise knowledge of his views, one cannot be spared from looking up his own closer explanations and further elaborations.

Analogous series to the black-white sensations are formed by the blue-red, blue-green, yellow-red, and yellow-green color sensations. Regarding the first, for example, one must assume—just as <term>ideally<note>Original: *idealiter*. This suggests a theoretical or perfect state used for the sake of an argument, even if it is rarely found in pure form in nature.</note></term> as a pure black and pure white—a purely blue and purely red sensation, with transitions between them in which one recognizes more or less blue or red, such as...

<summary>The author argues that the psychological impact of black comes from mental engagement with contrast rather than black being a "positive" stimulus. He then introduces Hering's theory that colors like red, green, blue, and yellow function in opposing pairs similar to black and white.</summary>
<keywords>contrast, Ewald Hering, color theory, mental activity, sensations of brightness, blue-red, opponent-process theory</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s presentation of Ewald Hering’s color theory, specifically detailing the four primary colors and the concept of "opponent colors" that cannot be perceived simultaneously.</meta>

...one recognizes more or less black or white in the gradations of gray. The same applies to the other series.

Apart from black and white, there are generally only four simple <term>color sensations<note>Original: *Farbenempfindungen*. Hering’s theory focuses on the psychological experience of color rather than the physical properties of light.</note></term> or <term>primary colors<note>Original: *Grundfarben*.</note></term>: green, red, blue, and yellow. These are characterized by the fact that they "can occur, as it were, without the aftertaste of another color, or, if they have such a recognizable quality, they can only lean into one, never into two others at once." Thus, "yellow can lean toward red or green, but not toward blue; blue only toward either red or green; red only toward either yellow or blue... All other colors we can call **compound** or **mixed colors** insofar as **two** colors can always be recognized in them simultaneously. **But no more than two simple colors can be sensed within any compound color**"; and "**on the one hand**, **red** and **green**, and **on the other**, **yellow** and **blue** are never simultaneously perceivable in a single color. Why this is so cannot be understood from the outset; but it is so... An answer to this is currently impossible... For the fact cannot be logically grounded, and for now it is quite conceivable that we may once again succeed in establishing conditions under which, for example, a sensation mixed from red and green arises, or that there are beings who have such a sensation more often." (*)—For the sake of brevity, the author calls these mutually exclusive primary colors "**opponent colors**" <note>Original: *Gegenfarben*. This is a foundational concept in modern color science, known as the Hering Opponent Process Theory.</note>. —

In other words, if the black-white sensation mixed with the color sensation becomes **whitish** or **blackish** without changing its proportion to the color itself.«

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Ewald Hering’s "opponent process" theory of color vision. The text transitions from describing color nuances to defining the specific chemical mechanisms Hering proposed to explain how the eye perceives six primary sensations through three types of visual matter.</meta>

...chemical theory, the most general features of which were shared on page 36, through the following main propositions:

> »The six basic sensations of the visual substance are arranged into three pairs: Black and White, Blue and Yellow, Green and Red.«

> »To each of these three pairs corresponds a process of **dissimilation** and **assimilation** [designated by the author as *D* and *A* respectively] of a particular quality, so that the visual substance is capable of chemical change or <term>metabolism<note>Original: *Stoffwechsel*. Hering proposed that vision was the result of the constant breaking down (dissimilation) and building up (assimilation) of chemical substances in the retina.</note></term> in three different ways.«

Accordingly, the author distinguishes, with certain reservations, »three different components of the visual substance,« which he designates as the black-white sensing, the blue-yellow sensing, and the red-green sensing substances.

For the black-white substance, the author assumes that its dissimilation *D* corresponds to white, and its assimilation *A* corresponds to black. For the blue-yellow and red-green substances, he leaves it undecided for now which color is the *D*-color and which is the *A*-color.

> »The three substances do not compose the visual substance in equal parts; rather, the black-white substance is much more abundant in the visual organ than the other two, and even these two are not equal to one another.«

> »All rays of the visible spectrum have a dissimilating effect on the black-white substance, but the various rays to different degrees. In contrast, only certain rays have a dissimilating effect on the blue-yellow or green-red substance, certain others an assimilating effect, and certain rays none at all.«

> »Mixed light appears colorless when it creates an equally strong <term>moment<note>In 19th-century scientific German, *Moment* refers to a constituent factor or a force acting in a specific direction.</note></term> of dissimilation and assimilation for both the blue-yellow and the red-green substance, because then both moments cancel each other out, and the effect on the black-white substance emerges purely.« Etc.

To attach some remarks to the previous theory: Hering’s light theory—which is quite ingeniously executed and more **psycho-chemical** than **psycho-physical**—would naturally take on a completely different significance if there were a more direct empirical foundation for the assumption of the three different visual substances and the two types of chemical processes intervening therein—

<meta>This page continues the critique of Ewald Hering’s physiological optics, specifically focusing on the logical consistency of his "chemical theory" regarding how opposing visual processes (Assimilation and Dissimilation) interact to produce color sensations.</meta>

...position in <term>physiological optics<note>The study of how the physical properties of light are processed by the biological systems of the eye and brain.</note></term> if there were no more to it than the phenomena for whose explanation it was assumed, and if a connection between the external and internal conditions of sensation could be consistently followed thereafter. But apart from the incongruencies already noted on page 124, a quite fundamental one seems to me to lie in the following point.

Black and White as *A* and *D* of the same visual substance together give the sensation of black-white or gray. According to this, Blue and Yellow, and likewise Red and Green—conceived by the author as *A* and *D* (or *D* and *A*) of the same visual substance, but distinct from the black-white one—should give a blue-yellow and red-green sensation, which would be distinct from white or gray. Instead, they give (according to their relationship as <term>complementary colors<note>Pairs of colors that, when combined, cancel each other out to produce a neutral color like white or gray.</note></term>) also a gray or white sensation.

The author himself finds it (as noted on page 129) mysterious that neither a blue-yellow nor a red-green sensation exists. Instead, the gray or white sensation exists in its place. Based on the above, the author seems to seek the explanation in the idea that when *A* and *D* in the blue-yellow or red-green substance mutually cancel each other out, only the colorless sensation of the always-mixed black-white substance remains. But since the mutual cancellation of *A* and *D* in the black-white substance results not in a *disappearance* of the sensation of Black and White, but in a *middle result* of both—Gray—it should, by natural consistency, be the case for the blue-yellow and red-green substances as well. Insofar as this is not the case, something in the basic assumptions about the role that *A* and *D* are supposed to play seems to me to be incorrect. In this instance, when acting together with equal strength, they cause a *disappearance*, *instead* of a *middle result* of their *special effects*.

But let us admit that the **oscillation theory** <note>The "oscillation theory" refers to the wave-based theory of light (associated with Young and Helmholtz), which competed with Hering's chemical "opponent-process" theory.</note>, in its current development, also still presents difficulties, for the solution of which it is just as dependent on <term>auxiliary hypotheses<note>Original: *Hülfshypothesen*. Secondary assumptions added to a theory to explain away observations that seem to contradict the primary theory.</note></term> as the chemical theory; it only seems to me that such hypotheses can be developed more consistently with themselves and the facts. <meta>This page continues the author's response to the challenges of color theory. Having previously discussed the shortcomings of Ewald Hering’s "opponent process" model, the author now proposes his own hypothesis—originally presented in his 1860 work, *Elements of Psychophysics*—to explain how the physical vibrations of light are translated into the complex sensations of color within the human nervous system.</meta>

I do not believe I am yet fully equal to these difficulties, but I do believe that a path toward a common solution lies in the application of a hypothesis that I already put forward in *Elements of Psychophysics*, Vol. II, pp. 301 ff., and which I wish to reproduce here with an attempt to apply it to the points mentioned above.

According to this hypothesis, every <term>optic nerve fiber<note>The individual nerve cells that carry visual information from the retina to the brain.</note></term>, under the influence of even the simplest <term>color stimulus<note>The physical light entering the eye.</note></term>, undergoes a composition of vibrations; therefore, there are no "simple" subjective colors in the same sense that "simple" objective colors can be produced through prismatic decomposition. Instead, the simplest objective color merely evokes the *relatively simplest subjective color mixture*—that is, a composition of vibrations of varying durations—and the quality of the sensation associated with it depends on the *mode of composition* of this mixture.*) Presumably, the vibration corresponding to the objective oscillation predominates in strength within the mixture, while the others diminish in this regard to the extent that they deviate in duration or vibration frequency. However, because the excitability of the <term>retina<note>The light-sensitive layer at the back of the eyeball.</note></term> is restricted within certain limits of vibration frequency, an exception must occur for those objective rays falling beyond the boundaries of the visible spectrum. ### XV. Bernstein's Psychophysical Fundamental Law

*Bernstein* <note>Julius Bernstein (1839–1917) was a pioneering German physiologist who developed the first membrane theory of nerve conduction.</note> has, as previously noted (p. 20), published two treatises on the <term>psychophysical fundamental law<note>The mathematical relationship between physical stimulus and mental sensation, often associated with the Weber-Fechner Law, which suggests sensation grows logarithmically relative to the stimulus.</note></term>. The first, hereafter briefly designated as I, appeared in *Reichert and Du Bois-Reymond's Archives* <note>Original: "Reichert-Duboisschen Arch." This was a leading medical and physiological journal of the 19th century.</note> 1868, p. 388 ff.; the second, designated as II, in his work: *“Investigations on the Process of Excitation in the Nervous and Muscle Systems”* 1871, p. 166 ff. The second treatise, while referencing the first, contains only a few further elaborations and formal changes, without significantly altering the principle or result of the observation. In the first treatise, the author limits himself to considering the propagation of <term>excitation<note>In this context, the physiological activity or "signal" traveling through the nervous system.</note></term> from its point of entry into the brain in only *one dimension*; in the second, he tracks it through a circular space in *two dimensions*, with the remark (II. 178) that tracking it through three dimensions would lead to essentially the same result.

As already briefly mentioned in the 4th section, the author's intention is to establish—instead of the logarithmic dependence of the intensity of sensation on the intensity of the internal excitation that I have assumed—a simple *proportionality* of the intensity of sensation. This proportionality is not with the intensity itself, but with the spatial *spread* (which the author considers limited) of the internal excitation from its point of entry into the brain. He views this *spread* as being measured by the number of central elements, or <term>ganglion cells<note>Large nerve cells in the brain or nervous system that process incoming signals.</note></term>, through which it occurs. At the same time, however, he aims to establish that this spread stands in a corresponding logarithmic dependence on the strength of the stimulus, just as I posit regarding the relationship between sensation and stimulus.

The entire circular space through which the excitation spreads from the entry point into the brain until its disappearance—

<summary>The author introduces Julius Bernstein’s alternative take on the psychophysical law, which argues that sensation is proportional to the physical spread of nerve excitation across ganglion cells rather than the intensity of the excitation itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of Julius Bernstein’s physiological theories, specifically moving from the dimensions of nervous propagation to the specific mechanics of how "excitation" (neural signals) behaves within the brain's "irradiation space."</meta>

...is able to propagate its intensity, measured in the specified manner, is called by him the **circle of irradiation**, for which we can more generally substitute **irradiation space**, if the intention is to include the possibility of spreading in all directions into a space of three dimensions.

The author assumes the intensity of the <term>excitation<note>The physiological response or signal triggered by a stimulus.</note></term> upon entering the brain to be proportional to the intensity of the external stimulus, or identifies it as such when deriving his formulas. This is based on his discussion of his own and others' experiments (using electrical stimuli), which suggest that the excitation—propagating through the nerve in the form of a wave (**stimulus wave**)—suffers no weakening during this propagation. By contrast, according to the author, such weakening begins upon entry into the <term>ganglion cells<note>In 19th-century physiology, this term often referred generally to nerve cell bodies within the central nervous system.</note></term>.

The strength of the excitation at every point of the **circle of irradiation** is regarded by the author as being measured by the <term>kinetic energy<note>Original: "lebendige Kraft." This is a historical term for kinetic energy ($mv^2$), commonly used in 19th-century physics and physiology.</note></term> of a <term>vibratory process<note>Original: "Schwingungsvorgang." Bernstein and his contemporaries often conceptualized nerve conduction as a mechanical or etheric vibration.</note></term> propagating through it. However, for the author, when estimating sensation based on the size of the circle of irradiation or the number of ganglion cells contained within it, this strength only comes into consideration insofar as a greater intensity upon entering the brain—and consequently at every point of the circle of irradiation—results in a greater expansion of that circle.

The author explains that the excitation does not spread beyond certain limits in the brain, but rather, as it spreads, it weakens more and more until it finally terminates at a certain boundary of the circle of irradiation. He attributes this not only to the weakening that must naturally accompany distribution over a larger space (which he nevertheless takes into account), but also to a peculiar resistance proportional to the intensity of the excitation, which every central element exerts on the passing excitation, in connection with the fact of the **threshold**, as indicated below.*)

---

*) To avoid confusion between two equally possible ways of conceptualizing this, it should be noted that in the case of a momentary stimulus, one can only speak of a **successive** passage through the central elements with decreasing intensity of excitation. Meanwhile, in the case of a steadily acting stimulus, the full intensity at the entry into the first central element exists simultaneously with the vanishing intensity (or the intensity reduced to the <term>threshold value<note>Original: "Schwellenwerth." The minimum level of intensity required for a stimulus to be perceived or for a physiological effect to occur.</note></term>) in the last element reached by the expansion. That sensation is actually *proportional* to a physical circumstance, rather than *logarithmically* dependent on one, and that it is a *spatial expansion* rather than an *intensity* to which it is proportional, are really only *assumptions of the author* <note>The "author" (German: *Verfasser*) refers to Julius Bernstein, whose 1871 work on the "nerve-excitation" Fechner is discussing here.</note>, which he considers a priori more probable than my own based on the perspectives given below; meanwhile, the *author’s deductions*, while drawing on the assumption of a limited extension of the *irradiation space*, aim to show how the *logarithmic law of measurement* <note>This refers to the Weber-Fechner Law, which states that the intensity of a sensation increases as the logarithm of the stimulus.</note> regarding the stimulus emerges from it. In substituting infinitely small elements in infinite proximity for the actually finite, yet very small and closely situated excited *elements of the brain* <note>Bernstein and Fechner are conceptualizing the brain's "elements" (cells or molecules) as discrete points that can be treated as a continuum for the purposes of calculus.</note> to obtain a *differential and integral equation*, I merely follow the author, and there should be no significant objection to this.

According to the degree to which the excitation progresses from its point of entry, it will fill a *linear, circular, or spherical space s*, depending on the assumption; and according to the growth of *s*, the *intensity of the excitation y* at the boundary of this space will be weakened. However, the *expansion* itself cannot thrive beyond a certain size *S*, where *y* has fallen to the *threshold value b* <note>The "threshold" is the point at which a stimulus is just strong enough to produce a sensation; here, it marks the physical limit of how far an "excitation wave" can travel before it becomes too weak to be felt.</note>—in short, not beyond the so-called *irradiation space*.

According to the *fundamental assumption* now, the *sensation* $\gamma$ is to be regarded as measured by the formula

->$\gamma = \alpha S$ (1)<-

in which $\alpha$ signifies the *density of the central mass* <note>The "central mass" refers to the neural tissue of the brain or spinal cord.</note> through which the excitation spreads—that is, the number of central elements in a unit of spatial extension.

->natural log $b = -k \alpha S +$ Const.<-

->$k \alpha S =$ natural log $\frac{\beta}{b}$ (4)<-

Let us now consider what the Author <note>Julius Bernstein</note> (Vol. I, p. 391) puts forward in favor of his basic assumptions, and what might be countered.

> "That measure—he says (I. 391)—with which we measure the intensity of any force, is space. We measure the force of attraction by the distance of a fall in one second. We measure heat by the expansion a heated body undergoes. The strength of an electric current by the deflection of a magnetic needle from its resting position. The stronger the incoming excitation is, the deeper it penetrates into the sensing center, the more central mass enters a state corresponding to the process of sensation."

But it is not correct that we fundamentally measure the intensity of a force—whether conceived as impact force, accelerating, moving, or kinetic force—merely through the space traveled by the body driven by that force. Rather, we measure it with regard to the time in which the space is covered; that is, as a function of the velocity or change in velocity produced by the force. Accordingly, no one can find it believable that if sensation depends at all on an excitation process in the **ganglion cells** <note>ganglion cells: nerve cells in the central nervous system that process information</note>, it would, regarding the strength of sensation, depend merely on the space or the number of cells through which it spreads in dependence on its... <note>The sentence continues on the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critical examination of Julius Bernstein’s theory regarding how nerve impulses spread through the brain. This happens despite the theoretical assumption that if resistance were proportional to velocity, the body should continue indefinitely with ever-decreasing speed; just as, at the beginning of its movement, it requires a certain finite velocity, and as long as the impact cannot produce this, the body remains unmoved.

Meanwhile, this analogy seems to me to speak against rather than for the Author <note>The "Author" refers to Julius Bernstein</note>. If I am not mistaken, the example asserted here behaves as follows:

Every impact exerted on the body from the start, without yet being strong enough to set it sliding on the plane, is nonetheless not without effect upon it. Instead, it evokes vibrations of its particles \*), until the effect of the impact reaches a magnitude sufficient to carry the particles of the body resting on the plane past the immediately adjacent particles of the surface. Otherwise, the force of the impact is exhausted in the production of vibrations; and likewise, the end of the sliding will depend on the fact that the sliding velocity of the body's particles—through the continuous partial conversion into vibrational motion which occurs during friction—finally no longer suffices to cause that "crossing over" of particles. Now, if the propagation of excitation in the brain were comparable to the sliding of a body on a plane, one could indeed take that finite velocity of the particles, below which that crossing over can no longer occur, as the threshold value of the sliding velocity, and apply it to the propagation of excitation in the brain. But since Bernstein himself this—

---

\*) As is well known, heat—that is, a vibrational process of the particles—is produced by every impact.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Julius Bernstein’s theory of nerve impulse propagation. On the previous page, Fechner argued that a physical body experiencing friction doesn't just "stop" instantly at a threshold unless specific conditions are met; here, he applies that logic to wave mechanics and heat, ultimately proposing an alternative mathematical relationship between stimulus and brain activity.</meta>

If one conceives of propagation as the transmission of a wave process, it seems to me that any analogy is lacking that would suggest such a process—possessing a finite value of its kinetic energy <term>kinetic energy: Fechner uses the older term "lebendige Kraft" (vis viva), which in modern physics corresponds to $mv^2$, or twice the kinetic energy</term> at any point in space (that is, at the material point located there)—could simply break off without some part of it passing on to the next particle. The Author <note>Julius Bernstein</note> certainly speaks of the extinguishing of kinetic energy through its conversion into potential energy <term>potential energy: "Spannkraft," literally "tension force," referring to energy stored in a state of tension or position</term>; yet I am not aware that one speaks of such an "extinguishing" in the sense the Author intends when describing the propagation of vibrations. 

For when a sound wave is hindered in its propagation by the friction of air particles, this can, in my opinion—as with all friction—only occur through the conversion of larger air vibrations into smaller heat vibrations. This process manifests throughout the entire course of propagation and leads not to an abrupt end, but only to an indefinitely continuing (asymptotic) weakening of the vibrations upon which the sound is based. Consequently, the signal eventually falls below the threshold of audibility, though this has nothing to do with the "threshold" described by the Author. Meanwhile, I admit possible doubts regarding the adequacy of this interpretation of the process.

If we discard the Author’s view of a **limited** irradiation space <term>irradiation space: the area within the brain where a nerve stimulus spreads out from its point of entry</term> but maintain the assumption that the resistance to the progression of excitation is proportional to the excitation itself, one can indeed use Formula (3) (which does not yet incorporate the former assumption) to derive a formula for the relationship between: the original stimulus $\beta$; the intensity of excitation $y$ at a distance $r$ from the entry of the stimulus into the brain; and the expansion space $S_y$ (conceived as linear, circular, or spherical) filled to a given density $a$ up to the distance $r$. This formula is of interest due to its analogy with our own measurement formula for sensation, even if it no longer signifies anything regarding the relationship between stimulus and sensation. Specifically, one finds:

-> $a S_y = \frac{1}{k} \text{ natural log } \frac{\beta}{y}$ <-

and, if $e$ is the base of natural logarithms:

-> $y = \beta e^{- a k S_y}$ <-

I would also like to make a remark regarding the following statements by the Author <note>Julius Bernstein (1839–1917), a pioneer in electrophysiology</note> (Vol. II, p. 177), with which he seeks, to some extent, to deepen his view.

> »We attribute the property of destroying the kinetic energy <term>kinetic energy: "lebendige Kraft" (vis viva), the energy of motion</term> of the stimulus wave primarily to the central element of the ganglion cell <term>ganglion cell: a type of nerve cell body</term> known to us, and we assert that the very essence of sensation <term>sensation: "Empfindung," the mental experience of a physical stimulus</term> is contained within this property of destroying the kinetic energy of the stimulus wave. If the excitation were to pass through the ganglion cell as it passes through the nerve fiber, without losing intensity, then no sensation would come about. Only the resistance opposed to the excitation calls forth the phenomenon of sensation. From this it will also become clear that we estimate the magnitude of sensation according to the number of ganglion spheres <note>Bernstein uses "Ganglienkugeln" to describe the spherical cell bodies in the nervous system</note> whose cooperation was necessary to destroy the kinetic energy of an excitation.« (cf. also Vol. II, p. 202.)

Now, in cases where—as here—the reduction of the excitation at every point is proportional to the excitation itself, it may be a matter of indifference whether one makes sensation functionally dependent on the reduction or on the persistence of the kinetic energy of the excitation. However, I would not know what would grant the Author's **assertion** in the first sense <note>Bernstein's idea that sensation is energy loss</note> preference over the assumption in the second sense <note>Fechner's idea that sensation is the energy itself</note>. Broadly speaking, it seems preferable to me to think of a positive **sensation** as being dependent on a positive process rather than on the weakening of one, and thus far I know of no reason in experience that would prevent us from doing the former.

<meta>This page transitions from a specific critique of Julius Bernstein’s neurological theories to a broader review of the evidence for Weber’s Law. While the previous page focused on the biological "destruction" of energy in nerve cells, Fechner now begins Section XVI to summarize experimental results that support or challenge his psychophysical theories.</meta>

## XVI. Summary of previous, and especially more recent, experimental results concerning Weber’s Law.

If the validity of Weber’s Law <term>Weber's Law: the principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus</term> and the laws derived from it can be defended for a certain range of psychophysical relationships, it does not necessarily follow that it is valid for the entire range of relationships that psychophysics must consider; and it may well be that in the *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's seminal 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (*Elements of Psychophysics*)</note> I harbored expectations that went too far in this regard. Every field requires specific investigation in this respect; and naturally, significantly different laws can apply to significantly different conditions. Let us now survey what currently exists from an experimental standpoint for evaluation regarding Weber’s Law; not forgetting that not every experimental deviation from it argues against its fundamental validity—that is, its validity for *internal psychophysics* <term>internal psychophysics: the study of the relationship between the soul/mind and the internal physical activities of the brain and nervous system</term>; rather, we will find such validity more probable the more approximately, within wider limits, and the more decisively in the middle ranges of sensory use the law is confirmed. Regarding some of the more important experimental studies that require a more detailed discussion, I will only briefly state the result here, in order to return to them in the following sections; for many, the information provided here will suffice.

Previously, **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist and Fechner's primary intellectual rival</note> (p. 23 ff.) and **Langer** <note>Ludwig Langer, a contemporary researcher</note> (p. 82 ff.) have given a brief overview with the same intent: to weigh the validity of Weber’s Law against existing experience. And according to them, one could not really speak of an experimental confirmation of the law, except perhaps in the area of sound intensity through the previously uncontrolled experiments of **Volkmann** <note>Alfred Wilhelm Volkmann (1801–1877), a physiologist and close collaborator of Fechner</note>, and in the area of visual estimation of distance <note>original: "Augenmass"</note> through **Volkmann’s** and my own experiments. If a confirmation truly occurred even in these areas alone, it would already be significant; it would create difficulties for opposing theories and diminish their reach. However, upon closer inspection, the situation may present itself quite differently than it has been portrayed by the opponents. One simply must not demand the impossible—that is, a pure confirmation of the

<summary>Fechner begins a summary of experimental evidence for Weber's Law, acknowledging that while its application might not be universal across all senses, its validity in specific areas like sound and vision still presents a significant challenge to his critics.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, Psychophysics, Internal Psychophysics, Ewald Hering, Alfred Volkmann, sound intensity, visual estimation</keywords>
