<meta>This page continues the discussion on Weber's Law, transitioning from theoretical defenses to a systematic review of experimental data across different sensory modalities. Fechner is addressing why experimental deviations do not necessarily invalidate the law's fundamental psychophysical status.</meta>

[...of] Weber’s Law against the reasons for experimental deviation; an impossibility that no law proposed by opponents could satisfy.

As the most general result, I find that, insofar as it concerns the discrimination of mere **stimulus intensities** <term>stimulus intensities: the physical strength of a sensation, such as the brightness of a light or the loudness of a sound</term> without change of color or pitch or, more generally, of the quality of sensation, the law can demonstrate approximate confirmation in the fields of **light**, **sound**, **taste**, and **weights**, which establishes the probability of its fundamental validity. On the other hand, the discrimination of color nuances and musical pitches, regardless of intensity, eludes the law. — Furthermore, in the field of extensive sensations <note>extensive sensations: sensations related to physical space or duration, such as the size of an object or the length of a time interval</note>, there are approximate confirmations of the law for **visual estimation** <term>visual estimation (Augenmass): the ability to judge distances or sizes by eye</term>, and even extending into **time estimation**. For the former, however, this is likely only insofar as it rests on a feeling of movement in the eye muscles; meanwhile, a direct estimation of spatial magnitudes, whether with the retina or the skin, does not follow the law. — Finally, it remains doubtful whether the **total intensity** of a tonal impression (which depends simultaneously on vibration frequency and amplitude) as well as the **total appearance** of the visual field might follow Weber's Law.

In general, in the following sections, I will use ε <note>original: "ε" (the Greek letter epsilon)</note> to designate the relative **just noticeable difference** <term>just noticeable difference (JND): the smallest amount of change in a stimulus that a person can detect</term>—that is, the increment added to a stimulus which is just barely noticeable (or has just been made to disappear from perception), divided by the absolute magnitude of the stimulus to which the increment occurs.

### 1) Light intensity (without color change).

The most numerous experiments for testing Weber’s Law in general are certainly found in the field of **light sensation**. Regarding the experiments cited in my *Elements* <note>Referring to *Elemente der Psychophysik*, Vol I, p. 139 ff.</note> by **Bouguer**, **Arago**, **Masson**, **Steinheil**, **Volkmann**, and myself, and regarding the results of **star magnitude observations** <note>original: "Sterngrössenbemerkung"; referring to how astronomers categorize stars by brightness</note> (ibid. 158), I wish to return to them here only with the general remark: that from all these experiments and observations, an—admittedly by some too unconditionally stated—at least approximate validity of Weber’s Law follows within more or less wide limits; however, already...

<summary>Fechner summarizes the applicability of Weber's Law across different senses, noting it works best for intensities (light, sound, weight) but fails for qualities like pitch or color. He introduces the mathematical constant epsilon to represent the "just noticeable difference" and begins reviewing historical light experiments.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, stimulus intensity, light sensation, visual estimation, time estimation, just noticeable difference, epsilon, Pierre Bouguer, star magnitudes, psychophysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Weber's Law's validity. Fechner is addressing the experimental findings of other scientists that show deviations from the law, arguing that these deviations occur at the extremes and do not invalidate the law's general application within middle ranges.</meta>

...has been recognized by myself that there are, indeed, experimental limits to its validity. Even if one considers the more recent experiments of **Helmholtz** and **Aubert** <note>Hermann von Helmholtz and Hermann Aubert were leading 19th-century physiologists who investigated how the human eye perceives light intensity.</note> to be more precise than all preceding ones, they still cannot nullify the consistent results of all earlier ones, but only modify them to the extent that the approximation <note>Fechner refers to the "approximation" of Weber's Law, which states that the ratio of a stimulus increment to the background stimulus is constant.</note> does not appear as extensive as it seemed according to the former; but it would not have been possible at all to express the law even without restriction, as has partly occurred, if the deviation from it within the maintained limits had been a gross one. Furthermore, different eyes may behave differently in this respect; and since practice in every field contributes to making differences easier to recognize, it should not be too surprising from the outset that the eyes of **Helmholtz** and **Aubert**, so practiced in light experiments and occupied in the consistent pursuit of them, recognized finer differences than the eyes of other observers who only occasionally engaged in observations in this field.*) But let us look a bit more closely at the relevant results of the Helmholtzian and Aubertian observations and experiments.

**Helmholtz** himself (p. 312 ff. <note>original: "p. 312 ff." stands for page 312 and following.</note>) found himself prompted to concede the approximate character of the law by facts of daily experience, particularly by the fact that copperplate engravings under differently bright illumination (provided it is not changed beyond certain limits) still make much the same impression. If, however, in more precise experiments according to the method of just noticeable differences <term>just noticeable differences: the smallest change in a stimulus that a person can detect</term> by means of an advantageously modified principle of the **Masson disc** <term>Masson disc: a rotating white disc with a black radial line or segment used to determine the threshold of light intensity discrimination</term>

---

*) **Dobrowolsky** <note>Wladislaw Dobrowolsky was a Russian ophthalmologist who studied how we perceive the colors of the spectrum.</note> notes on the occasion of his experiments on the relative **sensitivity to differences** <term>sensitivity to differences: the capacity to detect a change in intensity between two stimuli</term> for modified intensity of **spectral colors** <term>spectral colors: the pure colors found in a rainbow, produced by passing light through a prism</term> (Contributions to Physiological Optics p. 87): "At the beginning of my investigations, the sensitivity of my eyes to all spectral colors was much lower than at their conclusion; at the beginning of the investigations, my [relative] sensitivity to red in line $B$ <note>Line B refers to a specific Fraunhofer line in the solar spectrum, used as a landmark for a particular shade of red.</note> was $1/12.5$ and to blue about $1/180$, while at the conclusion of the investigations, the values resulted in $1/29.7$ and $1/268$." <note>These fractions represent the Weber fraction; a smaller fraction (like 1/268 compared to 1/180) indicates that the eye has become more sensitive through practice, detecting a much smaller relative change.</note>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Weber's Law against criticisms from Helmholtz and Aubert. The author argues that while deviations exist at extreme light intensities, the law remains a valid approximation for general use, citing his own mathematical re-analysis of Aubert's data.</meta>

the just noticeable relative difference in light $\varepsilon$, which according to **Weber’s Law** <note>Weber’s Law states that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant proportion of the original stimulus, rather than a fixed amount.</note> should remain constant regardless of the absolute intensity of the illumination. Instead, it was found to vary according to this illumination (within limits not precisely measured) between 1/117, 1/133, 1/150, and 1/167. However, this does not provide a completely accurate measure of the actual variation, insofar as **Helmholtz’s** <note>Hermann von Helmholtz was a 19th-century polymath whose work on the physiology of sight was foundational.</note> own accounts state that the differences in question were not perceived with equal ease and clarity; in particular, the difference of 1/167, which was perceptible "only for moments," cannot be considered comparable to the others.

The experiments of **Aubert**—conducted using the **method of just noticeable differences** <term>The method of just noticeable differences is a sensory testing technique used to find the smallest change in a stimulus (like light or sound) that a person can detect.</term>, partly with shadows and partly with rotating discs, and reaching through a very large scale of light intensities down to the smallest—might seem to speak quite strictly against the law upon a superficial examination of the very strong deviations shown at the upper and even more so at the lower ends. This is especially true given the manner in which **Aubert** presented them and asserted them against the law; they are therefore also preferred by opponents as a weapon against it. But first and foremost, as already noted, they cannot invalidate the factual evidence of the law's approximation, which is found in all earlier experiments, in the **estimates of stellar magnitudes** <note>Astronomers had long noted that the perceived brightness of stars followed a scale that we now recognize as logarithmic, supporting Weber's Law.</note>, and in the appearance of **copperplate engravings** <note>Engravings use lines and dots to create shades; Fechner argued that the fact we recognize the same image under different lighting proves we perceive relative, not absolute, light differences.</note>. Secondly, in a discussion of Aubert's experiments contained in the **Reports of the Saxon Society of Sciences, 1864** <note>original: "Berichten der sächs. Soc. 1864"</note>, and mentioned previously on p. 17, I have demonstrated that even in Aubert's experiments—within an interval far exceeding the limits of ordinary eye use, where the light intensity rose from 1 to 96.67 times its value, nearly a hundredfold*)—the still-just-noticeable relative stimulus difference $\varepsilon$ changed only in a ratio of 1 : 1.8, thus not even doubling.

\*) In this case, the lowest light intensity in this interval was 1/6.79 of that at which we usually read in the evening; the highest was that produced by a rotating disc with 250° white and 110° black in sunshine.

<meta>This page continues the author's mathematical defense of Weber's Law against the experimental data provided by Aubert. The author acknowledges that while there are fluctuations in the relative threshold (ε) at different intensities, these fluctuations are minor compared to the massive changes in the light stimulus itself, supporting the law as a functional approximation.</meta>

In this regard, it must certainly be admitted that this approximation appears considerably less significant if one examines the results on each side of the minimum of ε <note>ε represents the "relative threshold," or the smallest percentage change in light intensity that a person can detect.</note> individually. For even when one rises from an intensity of 1 to approximately 17-fold, ε changes in roughly that ratio of 1 : 1.8; it changes by a similar amount in the opposite direction if one increases that 17-fold intensity by another 5 to 6 times. Nevertheless, it remains a fact that while the intensity increases in a ratio of approximately 1 : 97—an interval that encompasses the limits of ordinary eye use—the value of ε only rises and falls in a ratio of 1 : 1.8. While this is not the absolute constancy required for the **strictness** of **Weber’s Law** <note>Weber's Law suggests that the ratio of the increment threshold to the background intensity is constant.</note>, it is nevertheless to be understood as a considerable **approximation** of constancy in relation to the vast change in light intensities over which it holds; conversely, it would represent an enormous deviation from the proportionality of the increase in sensation with the increase in stimulus.

To be sure, Aubert’s experimental results as they stand would not allow one to derive the lower deviation in the realm of the light-sense solely from the existence of the faint internal excitation represented by the brightness of the <term>intrinsic light of the eye</term> <note>Original: *Augenschwarz*. This refers to the sensation of gray or "black" light perceived by the eye even in total darkness, caused by the internal activity of the visual system.</note> (as an addition to the external brightness), as I have already explicitly admitted in my treatise against **Aubert**. For to do so, the light-value of the intrinsic light of the eye would have to be introduced into the calculation as a value that is far too large and highly variable depending on the levels of stimulus. But firstly, our knowledge of the internal conditions of the psychophysical system is not sufficient to guarantee that other factors might not also be involved in this lower deviation; secondly, the degree of brightness of the intrinsic light—which Aubert himself acknowledged—must at least play a part. Thirdly, after new insight into Aubert’s experiments, I cannot help but find it questionable—judging by pages 55 and 80 (with consideration of page 74)—that his experiments were generally conducted in a one-sided manner: while the brighter light source was held constant, only the darker one was altered until the brightness difference between the shadow and the background disappeared; consequently, the brighter component was always used as the object of comparison for the darker one, and not also

<summary>The author argues that while Weber's Law is not perfectly constant in Aubert's data, it serves as a strong approximation given the vast range of light intensities studied. He also critiques Aubert's experimental methodology, specifically regarding the role of the eye's intrinsic light and the one-sided nature of his light comparisons.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, Aubert, intrinsic light of the eye, Augenschwarz, light intensity, psychophysics, relative threshold, experimental method</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s (Gustav Fechner) critical evaluation of Hermann Aubert’s experiments. Fechner is arguing that Aubert’s data, which seems to contradict Weber’s Law at low light intensities, might be skewed by systematic experimental errors that Aubert failed to account for.</meta>

conversely, occurred where the <term>constant error</term> <note>A "constant error" is a systematic bias in an experiment that consistently pushes results in one direction, such as a researcher always reacting slightly too late to a signal.</note>—which I described in *Elements II, 140* as being dependent on the method of preparation—could hardly have been avoided. Furthermore, the statement (p. 55)—"that the more distant light was moved gradually and with frequent pauses until the shadow was at the limit of perceivability"—does not allow us to conclude that the limit was reached just as often from a distance that was too small as from one that was too large\*). Nor do I find it mentioned that the experiments were systematically conducted just as often with the intensities ascending as descending. In both cases, however, causes of constant errors may once again be found. 

Now, regarding these experiments—which are inherently difficult to compare—I find no precautions taken to avoid constant errors, or at least nothing said about them. This is especially concerning after I have convinced myself, through my own weight-lifting, visual measurement, and tactile experiments, of the significant role such errors play in research of this kind. Furthermore, the experiments of **Delboeuf** (mentioned in the footnote) and **Vierordt** (to be mentioned later) have proven that these errors also occur in the realms of light sensation and time perception—in short, they are indisputably common to all sensory fields. Therefore, despite all the credit I give to **Aubert’s experiments** for their care and prudence in other respects, I do not know why I should still place sufficient trust in them regarding the elimination of constant errors.

One might object that other observers, whose experiments I myself cite as confirmation of **Weber’s Law** in the realm of light, generally took just as little care to avoid constant errors. This is true; and had such care been taken, they would likely align even better with the law. However, none of these other experiments descend to such low intensities as Aubert’s experiments do; and it is natural that of the ex—

In one column, he arrives at the "right point" where a difference disappears by starting from a brightness that is too great; in the other, he starts from a brightness that is too small, in order to derive the average from both. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Aubert’s experimental methodology. He argues that the apparent failures of Weber’s Law at low light intensities are not failures of the law itself, but rather the result of "constant errors" and the physiological phenomenon of "retinal noise" that becomes dominant in the dark.</meta>

constant errors dependent on experimental conditions—insofar as they do not themselves change proportionally to the intensities—must gain a greater influence when testing <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>A fundamental principle in psychophysics stating that the smallest perceivable change in a stimulus is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> the smaller the intensities are. Thus, my opinion is that **Aubert’s experiments** regarding low intensities should be disregarded entirely concerning the question of the validity of **Weber's Law**, whereas for higher intensities, the stated approximation is found. Indeed, experiments at such low intensities that the light value of the <term>retinal noise</term> <note>Original: "Augenschwarz" (literally "eye-black"). This refers to the faint sensation of light or grayness experienced even in total darkness, caused by internal neural activity in the retina and brain.</note> can no longer be neglected may be hardly usable for testing the law at all. This is because the variable influence of <term>adaptation</term> <note>The eye's ability to adjust its sensitivity to different levels of light.</note>—which I deny is of significant concern at higher intensities (p. 48)—can then certainly no longer be neglected; however, due to its variability, it cannot allow for a calculation of the increase in brightness provided by the retinal noise. Should it be possible to eliminate it, however, it could likely only happen through a systematic combination of experiments conducted with both ascending and descending intensities.

A truly methodically conducted series of experiments calculated for the greatest possible elimination of <term>constant errors</term> <note>Systematic biases in an experiment that consistently skew results in one direction.</note>, such as those I have performed regarding **sensitivity to weight and touch**, does not yet exist at all for light experiments. I must continually return to the point that if, despite the inadequacy of these experiments, a significant approximation to **Weber’s Law** has been found at intensities that are not too low, then one must conclude from the inadequacy of the experiments only an inadequacy of the *proof*, not of the *law* itself.

To this, I add the following remark. Shadow experiments for testing **Weber’s Law** are naturally conducted in such a way that the darker area (the shadow) is made to disappear within a larger, surrounding bright area. The experiments with the <term>Masson disk</term> <note>A device used to test light sensitivity consisting of a white disk with black segments that, when rotated, appear as gray rings of varying intensities.</note> were conducted accordingly by **Masson** himself and by **Helmholtz** (p. 314), but in reverse by **Aubert** (p. 74). In short, these are performed in a one-sided direction everywhere, so that either the smaller, darker area disappears into a larger, brighter one, or conversely, a smaller, brighter area disappears—or comes close to disappearing—into a larger, darker one. Could not a constant error also depend on this one-sided direction of the experiment, which would need to be compensated for by experiments in the opposite direction while keeping all other circumstances equal? In any case, it would be necessary to decide the question through experiments rather than through assumption. In **Plateau’s method** (p. 22), the occasion for a

<summary>Fechner argues that discrepancies in Weber’s Law at low light levels are due to experimental flaws and "retinal noise" (Augenschwarz), rather than a failure of the law itself. He advocates for more rigorous, multi-directional experimental designs to eliminate systematic biases.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Aubert’s experimental methods. He remains focused on how "retinal noise" (internal light sensation) affects the validity of Weber’s Law at low light intensities, comparing Aubert's results with those of Delboeuf.</meta>

removes such an error. To be sure, the experiment with the <term>Masson disk</term> <note>A rotating disk used to test the eye's sensitivity to contrast; by spinning a disk with a small black or white sector, a faint gray ring appears.</note> cannot be simply reversed by placing a black sector on a white disk in one instance and a white sector of equal angular width on a black disk in another. This is because, in the latter case, the <term>retinal noise</term> <note>Original: "inneres Augenlicht" or "Augenschwarz." Fechner refers to the internal neural "glow" or grayness we see even in total darkness.</note> can no longer be neglected relative to the objective brightness of the black disk as it could in the first case. However, **Aubert's modification** of the Masson disk offered a means to make the bright ring stand out and disappear—or come close to disappearing—not against a black background, but against a merely less bright one.

The suspicion that **Aubert’s experiments** generally involved un-eliminated sources of error of this kind is significantly strengthened by the experiments of **Delboeuf** (*Study*, p. 54 to 65). Although Delboeuf’s experiments do not reach as low an intensity as Aubert’s, they were conducted according to a completely different procedure, namely <term>Plateau’s method</term> <note>A method of "equal-appearing intervals" where a subject tries to find a gray shade exactly halfway between two others.</note>. Thus, even if they are not entirely free of objections themselves, they do not suggest sources of error in the same direction. In these trials, **Weber's Law** is confirmed with the most desirable approximation, provided one imagines the brightness of the "eye-black" added to the external light stimulus as a very small value—a value even smaller than one might expect based on the role attributed to retinal noise in other experiences. It has been noted previously that these experiments can be regarded as a very welcome confirmation of **Weber’s Law**. Opponents of the law will find it impossible to ignore this, unless they wish to be guilty of a kind of constant error themselves by always only considering, one-sidedly, what seems to speak against the law. In Section 17, these experiments will be found discussed in detail. However, if Delboeuf himself, in his *Study*, still claimed his experiments spoke against Weber’s Law, enough has been said on this matter on pages 27 and 28.

...width of the slit [with which the absolute objective brightness grew] decreased somewhat; but it is a quite well-known fact that sensitivity decreases under strong illumination. The specific results of the aforementioned observers regarding the minimum value of ε for the different colors are provided in the following insertion. In their absolute values, their reports differ significantly from one another, yet they agree that the minimum value of ε is very different for various colors. This result could indicate that the value <term>$K$</term> <note>A constant in the fundamental psychophysical formula representing the inherent sensitivity of a sensory system.</note> in the fundamental formula is different for different colors, but it could also be related to the reasons for experimental deviation from <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>A psychological law stating that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note>, which already exist for each color individually.

Dobrowolsky provides the minimum values of ε for the following points of the spectrum, designated by the <term>Fraunhofer lines</term> <note>Specific dark lines in the solar spectrum used as standard reference points for different wavelengths/colors of light.</note>, obtained with the assistance of two co-observers, Dr. Berthold and Dr. Garza. <summary>This page presents experimental data from researchers Lamansky and Dobrowolsky, showing that the human eye's sensitivity to light changes (the Weber fraction) is not constant across the color spectrum, with the highest sensitivity found in yellow and green light. These findings challenge the idea that Weber's Law applies identically to all colors and suggest that our retinas "dull" or lose sensitivity at very high intensities.</summary>

In the case of violet between lines G and H, the author <note>original: "der Verf." (der Verfasser)</note> finds it doubtful whether the maximum of the <term>relative difference sensitivity</term> <note>The eye's ability to detect a small change in light intensity relative to the background intensity.</note> had already been reached; for violet at line H, it certainly had not been reached, which is why the value of <term>ε</term> <note>Epsilon (ε) refers to the Weber fraction, or the proportional threshold of perception.</note> found there has no significance.

A very fine—that is, within wide limits, a very approximate—confirmation of <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>A principle stating that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is proportional to the magnitude of the stimuli.</note> was obtained by Dobrowolsky (*Contributions* <note>original: "Beitr." (Beiträge zur Lehre von den Gesichtsempfindungen)</note> p. 92 ff.) for the case where the difference between a spectral color light, mixed with white light in a given proportion, and an adjacent **pure** color light is observed. If Weber's Law is to hold, then the color light mixed with white must always become indistinguishable from the pure color light at the same intensity ratio between the two, regardless of how the absolute intensity of both changes; this was indeed found to be very approximately the case. The experiment showed, for example, in the case of Red, during a change of absolute intensities from 1.0000 to 0.0302 instead of a

...of a constant ratio, as would have been required for the strict accuracy of the law, we find a ratio that only changed from **2.2335** to **2.0303**. For further details, see the following insertion.

By means of a spectral apparatus <note>A spectroscope or similar device used to split light into its constituent colors (a spectrum).</note>, a color spectrum as bright as possible is produced. Using a suitable device, a white strip of light (produced by a petroleum lamp placed in front of a slit) is projected onto a specific spot in this spectrum; the light of this strip thus adds to the light of the color at that spot. Following this, experiments are conducted as follows: 1) While the colored light is at its maximum brightness (set as 1), the intensity of the white strip is weakened (by rotating one of two Nicol prisms <note>Nicol prisms are crystals used to polarize light; by rotating them, one can precisely control how much light passes through, effectively acting as a "dimmer" switch.</note>) until the strip disappears into the colored background. Let *v* be the ratio of its attenuation. 2) The colored light is now weakened by the ratio *m*, which makes the strip visible again; now its light is once more weakened until it disappears anew. Let the ratio of its attenuation found here (calculated against the first maximum intensity) be *n*. 3) Correspondingly, by means of further associated attenuations, the associated values *m'*, *n'*, then *m''*, *n''*, and so forth, are found; always calculated against the original maximum intensity. If <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> is to be valid, then the ratios 1/*v*, *m*/*n*, *m'*/*n'*, *m''*/*n''* etc., generally designated as *a*, must be found equal to one another, which was indeed the case to a very close approximation. Specifically, if *i* is the intensity of the colored light, the following values of *a* were found as the average of 6 to 10 observations for each degree of light intensity *i*:

| *i* | Red | Green | Violet |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 1.0000 | 2.2335 | 2.028 | 1.6841 |
| 0.8830 | 2.5155 | 2.3285 | 1.7842 |
| 0.5868 | 2.2338 | 2.3308 | 1.7999 |
| 0.2500 | 2.3061 | 1.9555 | 1.8462 |
| 0.1170 | 2.0954 | 2.0369 | |

### 2) Sound Intensity (without change of pitch).

Regarding the applicability of Weber's Law to sound intensities, there are currently only the experiments by <note>Alfred Wilhelm Volkmann (1801–1877), a German physiologist who contributed significantly to psychophysics.</note> Volkmann, conducted according to the <term>method of just noticeable differences</term> <note>A technique where a researcher finds the smallest change in a stimulus (like sound or light) that a person can actually perceive.</note>, which are mentioned in the *Elements of Psychophysics* Volume I, page 176 and following. Perhaps the method used there was not refined or certain enough to make a controlling re-examination of the experiments by more reliable methods seem undesirable; such a re-examination would likely reveal analogous deviations from the accuracy of the—

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the validity of Weber's Law, moving from experiments on light to those concerning sound and taste. It addresses the observation that perceived intensity does not increase linearly with physical intensity.</meta>

...law's validity in the field of sound experiments matches the results seen in light experiments, which did not come to light in those previous studies; at the very least, they suggest the law holds true to an approximate degree, just as it does in experiments with light.\*) Furthermore, the fact noted in the *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's own seminal work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics), Volume 1, page 183.</note>—that a choir of 400 male voices does not make a significantly stronger impression than one of 200—aligns perfectly with the logarithmic <term>measurement law</term> <note>Fechner’s Law, which states that the intensity of a sensation increases as the logarithm of the stimulus.</note> derived from <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note>. This law implies that physically doubling a strong light source does not produce a significant increase in the perception of light.

If our ability to distinguish sound levels depended on *absolute* differences rather than *relative* ones, there would be no reason why we have to raise our voices to be understood by someone nearby while riding in a train carriage or standing near a roaring waterfall.

### 3) *Taste*.

Regarding the sensation of taste, we currently only have the experiments conducted by *Keppler* using the <term>method of right and wrong cases</term> <note>A statistical method in early psychology where a subject compares two stimuli, and the frequency of correct versus incorrect judgments is recorded to determine sensitivity.</note> found in his inaugural dissertation: "The discriminative power of the sense of taste for concentration differences of tastable substances" (Bonn, 1869). The author himself finds his results (p. 11) to be in contradiction with Weber's Law. However, this is based on a use of experimental data that I do not understand, despite his claim that my fundamental table for this method—provided in the *Elements*, Volume I, p. 108—serves as his basis. After recalculating the experimental data myself, I find the following.

The experiments were conducted using solutions of common salt, basic quinine sulfate, phosphoric acid, and glycerin. By their nature, these experiments are difficult and hard to keep comparable, even for the same substance; furthermore, the author fails to provide essential details for a proper evaluation. However, the [data] contain

\*) When *Langer* (p. 84) states that no certain conclusion regarding sound intensity can be drawn from the height from which a ball is dropped (as was the case in Volkmann’s experiments), I would like to ask why not—unless one were working with balls of very small mass from very great heights, where air resistance would have a noticeable impact.

For each substance, I tried a specific way to examine whether they are consistent enough among themselves to be used for testing the law.*) If I subject these to this test, only the experiments with common salt prove valid, while those with the other substances are completely unusable; however, the experiments with the former agree with <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> as well as one could possibly expect given the difficulty of these experiments; meanwhile, the experiments with the other substances agree as poorly with Weber's Law as they do with each other, and are certainly not fit for testing the law at all.

To provide the definitive numbers that matter for the salt solution: based on the setup and principle of the experiments—grouping together the numbers that can be combined for the results—the following three numbers for $\sum \frac{r}{n}$ should be equal for the following three percentage concentrations of a main solution (against which a solution weakened by a specific ratio, the comparison solution, was compared), provided Weber's Law holds. Here, *r* represents the number of correct cases, and *n* the total number of cases <note>This refers to the "method of right and wrong cases," where the ratio of correct identifications indicates the subject's sensitivity.</note>.

| Percentage content of the main solution | 1.13 % | 3.2 % | 5.43 % |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **$\sum \frac{r}{n}$** | **221.7** | **230.1** | **241.1** |

---

*) In short, this method consists of the following. From the experiments conducted under four different positional relationships, three values can be calculated for each substance, which I designated as *h*, *p*, and *q* in the discussion of the method in the *Elements* <note>Referring to *Elemente der Psychophysik*, Volume 1, page 114.</note>, Vol. I, p. 114. The four positions yield four equations for determining these values, so that each value can be found in two ways from two equations each. If the experiments are to be considered consistent, *h*, *p*, and *q* must—when averaged over the trials to be combined for a definitive result—give nearly identical values according to both methods of calculation, or *p* and *q* (as <term>constant errors</term> <note>Systematic biases in an experiment, such as a subject's tendency to favor one side, that must be accounted for to find the true threshold of sensation.</note>) must be found to be very small. Where neither is the case, the experiments are unusable for drawing a conclusion.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of experimental data regarding the sense of taste, specifically addressing the minor discrepancies in the previously listed values for salt solution concentrations and explaining the statistical methodology used to validate Weber's Law.</meta>

...the following two circumstances must be considered. First, the most deviant value, 241.1, has a lower reliability than the others because it is based on a smaller number of observations; secondly, if, as is to be expected, the tongue is naturally saturated with saline fluid, this must have the same influence on the experiments as the presence of the natural brightness of the <term>eye-black</term> <note>original: "Augenschwarz." Fechner refers to the "intrinsic light" of the retina—the faint sensations of light present even in total darkness. In taste, this would be the "background" saltiness of saliva.</note> has on light experiments—namely, it leads to a slight increase in the values of the <term>relative difference sensitivity</term> <note>The ability to detect a change in stimulus intensity relative to the initial intensity.</note> as one moves from lower to higher degrees of concentration.

To ensure the method by which the above numbers were obtained is not left entirely unclear, I further note the following.

The experiments were, as noted, conducted with three main solutions of varying salt content—specifically 1.13%, 3.2%, and 5.43%. For each of these, four comparison solutions were used, which were weaker than the respective main solution by 2.5%, 5%, 7.5%, and 10% of the main solution's content. These were examined in four different test positions (two of which were the inverse of the other two) to determine whether the stronger main solution or the weaker comparison solution seemed to have a stronger taste; from this, the "right" and "wrong" cases were determined. I cannot go into a more detailed description here of these experiments, which appeared very difficult from the outset. Since the experiments were by no means numerous enough to perform calculations for each individual comparison solution, two steps were taken: first, the experiments with the comparison solution that was 2.5% weaker than the main solution were left aside entirely. Because of the slightness of this difference, the <term>r/n ratio</term> <note>The ratio of right cases (r) to the total number of trials (n).</note> fluctuated around 50%, which would have required a disproportionately larger number of trials to yield any reliable result—though including them would not significantly change the outcome. Second, the $r/n$ values for the other three comparison solutions were combined, which was permissible because they correspond to the same percentage differences across all three main solutions. In this way, determined as the average of the four test positions, they yielded the above-mentioned $\sum r/n$ values.

The summation and averaging of the $r/n$ values for the four test positions is not the most precise way to compensate for the <term>constant errors</term> <note>Predictable biases in an experiment, such as a tendency to favor the first sample tasted.</note> arising from these positions. Rather, one proceeds more accurately if one sums the $t$ values—which correspond to the $r/n$ ratios for the specific test positions (according to the <term>Fundamental Table</term> <note>A mathematical table, likely based on the Gaussian distribution, used by Fechner to convert success rates into units of sensation.</note> in *Elements* Volume I, page 108)—instead of summing the $r/n$ ratios themselves...

### 4) Weights.

Regarding the weight experiments, one may ask from the outset, as previously noted, whether they can fundamentally be classified under the same law as experiments on light and sound. Indeed, gravity, through which weights act, is even viewed by some as the polar opposite of light. There are also peculiar differences: while an eye fatigued by a <term>light stimulus</term> <note>The physical energy (light) that triggers a sensory response.</note> perceives light as weaker, a hand fatigued by loads perceives a weight as heavier. Furthermore, while practice contributes nothing to seeing a light as brighter, one can, through practice, come to find a weight lighter. However, according to the remark on page 121, it is very possible that the <term>weight stimulus</term>—no less than light and sound stimuli—influences sensation by awakening or strengthening <term>oscillations</term> <note>Fechner hypothesized that sensory nerves communicated through rhythmic vibrations or "oscillations."</note> in the sensory nerves; and the differences just noted do not necessarily preclude subordination under our law. 

Ultimately, only experience can decide the matter; and according to the discussions in Section 18, despite the opposing view of <term>Hering</term> <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a prominent physiologist who often criticized Fechner’s mathematical approach to sensations.</note>, I can find no reason to view his experiments—using the <term>method of just noticeable differences</term> <note>A technique where a subject adjusts a stimulus until a change is barely detectable.</note>—as anything other than a confirmation of my earlier results using the <term>method of right and wrong cases</term> <note>A statistical method where a subject compares two stimuli and the researcher records the percentage of correct identifications.</note>. In their general progression, Hering’s results agree with mine, providing prevailing reasons for, rather than against, the fundamental validity of <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>The principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus.</note>. Indeed, as will be seen in Section 18, Hering’s experiments—no less than my own—can be brought into surprising agreement with the law by applying a <term>correction</term> to the weight. This involves a constant addition, which I consider justified by taking into account the weight of the arm and other potentially contributing circumstances. This agreement holds as long as one does not descend to excessively small weights, where an as-yet-unexplained anomaly appears in the general course of the experiments—an anomaly whose cause is evidently outweighed by the effect of the weights themselves when they are larger.

<summary>Fechner argues that weight perception follows Weber's Law, despite differences in how practice and fatigue affect the senses compared to light or sound. He defends his methods against the critiques of Ewald Hering, suggesting that accounting for the weight of the arm stabilizes the experimental data.</summary>

<keywords>weight perception, Weber's Law, Ewald Hering, psychophysics, sensory fatigue, experimental correction, method of right and wrong cases</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of Weber’s Law, transitioning from the perception of weight to the perception of temperature and the distinction between stimulus intensity and frequency (color/pitch).</meta>

Aside from the lower experimental deviation from the law, which must indeed be acknowledged here, there will undoubtedly also be an upper deviation. As noted previously, this might take the opposite direction compared to other sensory fields; however, no specific experiments are available regarding this.

->5) **Temperature.**<-

In the field of **temperature sensations**, the experiments which I myself published in *Elements* <note>Referring to Gustav Fechner’s *Elemente der Psychophysik* (1860), a foundational text in psychology.</note> Vol. I, pp. 201 ff., do not yet provide a definitive conclusion. I myself have assumed that the <term>temperature stimulus</term> <note>The physical thermal energy that triggers a sensation.</note> should be viewed not as the absolute temperature, but rather as the difference between a given temperature and a "mean" (neutral) temperature. To maintain <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note>, the <term>just noticeable differences</term> <note>Often abbreviated as JND, the minimum change in sensation that a person can detect.</note> must be considered in relation to this difference. Under this assumption, the calculated values recorded in Section II of the table *) on page 203 of the *Elements* align well enough with the observed values within an interval of 19° to 31° <term>Réaumur</term> <note>A historical temperature scale where water freezes at 0° and boils at 80°. 19° to 31° R is roughly 24° to 39° Celsius.</note>, if one treats the increasing stimuli as the increasing differences of these temperatures from 14.77° R—this being the midpoint between <term>freezing</term> <note>original: "Frostkälte"</note> and <term>body heat</term> <note>original: "Blutwärme"</note>. However, this starting point is somewhat arbitrary. In the vicinity of freezing, the law holds just as poorly as it undoubtedly would if the experiments were increased so far beyond body heat that a sensation of burning occurred. I do not consider the question of the law's relationship to these sensations to be resolved yet.

->6) **Changes** in **Color** or **Pitch.**<-

All observations regarding light and sound mentioned under 1) and 2) referred to changes in the intensity of the stimulus, which depends on the **amplitude** *a* of the vibrations, without changing the **vibration frequency** *n*. Even in the experiments with different colors, only the brightness was varied for each, not the color itself; however, the question arises whether Weber's Law is confirmed for changes in *n* <note>The number of vibrations per second, which determines the pitch of a sound or the hue of a color.</note> just as it is for *a*.

---

*) Section I is not useful for drawing conclusions at all, due to the almost negligible values.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of Weber’s Law, transitioning from the perception of temperature to the perception of color and the distinction between stimulus intensity and frequency (pitch versus hue).</meta>

Already in the *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's own work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (1860).</note> Vol. I, p. 175, I acknowledged with the following remark that this is not the case regarding color.

> »Regarding the analogy between <term>pitches</term> <note>The perceived highness or lowness of a sound.</note> and colors, which is otherwise usually considered valid, it is a noteworthy circumstance—one that departs entirely from this analogy—that <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> does not hold in the domain of colors as it does in the domain of pitches. That is, equally noticeable differences in <term>vibration frequencies</term> <note>original: "Schwingungszahlen"; the number of waves per second.</note> are by no means proportional to the frequencies of the colors. Indeed, at the edges of the <term>spectrum</term> <note>The band of colors produced by separating light into its constituent wavelengths.</note>, the eye perceives almost no change in color even at intervals of a minor or major <term>third</term> <note>A musical interval; Fechner uses musical distances to describe the "distance" between colors.</note>. Meanwhile, in the region of yellow and green, noticeable color transitions follow one another so rapidly that all the transitional steps between yellow and green are squeezed into the interval of a small <term>half-tone</term> <note>A semitone; the smallest interval in common Western music.</note>.*) Moreover, there are other points in which the analogy between tones and colors fails.«

Newer experiments, the results of which are shared in the following insertion, provide more specific data for evaluating these relationships.

Regarding the differences in sensitivity to relative changes in color hues in various parts of the spectrum, earlier experiments by *Mandelstamm* (*Archive for Ophthalmology* Vol. XIII, Part 2, p. 399) are already available. These agree well in the general trend of their results with the later experiments by *Dobrowolsky* (*Contributions to Physiological Optics* no. III, p. 66 and V, p. 98), except that the values for ε found by *Dobrowolsky* are generally nearly half as small as those from *Mandelstamm*. The experiments of both were conducted using an <term>ophthalmometer</term> <note>An instrument traditionally used to measure the eye's curvature, here adapted for precise color observation.</note> following a suggestion by *Helmholtz*. However, *Dobrowolsky* made several modifications to its construction that appeared advantageous compared to the setup used by *Mandelstamm*, so his data should be regarded as the more accurate of the two.

If ε is the fraction that must be added to a given value of the <term>wavelength</term> <note>The physical distance between wave crests, which determines color hue; it is the inverse of frequency.</note> (which stands in inverse proportion to the vibration frequency *n*) so that our eye can perceive the difference in color corresponding to this wavelength, then the following values of ε were found by the two observers for the following points in the spectrum, designated by the letters of the <term>Fraunhofer lines</term> <note>Specific dark lines in the solar spectrum used as standard reference points for specific colors.</note> **).

Regarding *Mandelstamm’s* experiments, according to his report (p. 400), the rotation of the <term>ophthalmometer</term> <note>A medical instrument usually used to measure the curvature of the eye; here, the researcher uses its glass plates to precisely overlap and compare colors.</note> plates began from the position where no difference in their coloring appeared, and continued until "the first difference in the coloring of the plates" became perceptible. Indisputably, therefore, in these trials—just as in the earlier experiments by *Dobrowolsky*, whose results are combined with those of *Mandelstamm* in the same table above—any consideration of equal brightness between the compared color tones was omitted.

In the meantime, the previously missing analogy between colors and tones is recently restored in this regard. The validity of <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>A fundamental principle in psychophysics stating that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is proportional to the intensity of the original stimulus.</note> for the perception of <term>pitch differences</term> <note>The perceived "highness" or "lowness" of a sound.</note> is just as little confirmed by the newer experiments of *Preyer*\*\*) as it is for the perception of color differences. The previous assumption regarding the law's validity for pitch was based essentially on the fact that, to the musical sense, a pitch interval appears to be of equal size if the ratio of the <term>vibration frequencies</term> <note>The number of times a sound wave oscillates per second; higher frequencies result in higher pitches.</note> remains the same despite different absolute pitches. However, for small pitch differences, such as those considered in the method of "just noticeable differences," this law fails according to *Preyer’s* experiments. This leads one to suspect that the musical ear judges tone intervals more by the ratios of the recurrence or non-recurrence of <term>overtones</term> <note>Secondary tones that sound at higher frequencies alongside the main fundamental pitch, giving a sound its specific "timbre" or character.</note> than by the rise and fall of the vibration frequency.\*\*\*)

\* ) Provided that the difference in brightness here could already be below the threshold, these experiments for the middle part of the spectrum would certainly not be fully comparable to those for the boundaries of the spectrum regarding brightness.
\*\*) "On the Limits of Tone Perception by *Preyer*," Jena, Dufft 1876 p. 26 ff.
\*\*\*) One can raise a difficulty against this, which seemingly <unclear>stands</unclear> against the most fundamental views of *Helmholtz* in the <term>theory of sound</term> <note>Acoustics or the scientific study of how sound is produced and perceived.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the sensitivity of color perception to investigating the perception of musical pitch, specifically testing whether Weber's Law—which suggests our sensitivity to change is proportional to the intensity of the stimulus—holds true for sound frequencies.</meta>

Preyer’s experiments were conducted using the method of <term>just noticeable differences</term> <note>The smallest change in a stimulus that a person can detect.</note> with tones produced by metal reeds. He involved several observers and compared his findings with earlier studies by Delezenne and Seebeck, resulting in the following data.

Let $n_1$ and $n$ be two vibration frequencies <note>The number of oscillations per second, determining the pitch.</note> where the difference $n_1 - n$ is to be detected, $d$ be the absolute just noticeable difference $n_1 - n$, and $\epsilon = d/n$ be the relative just noticeable difference. According to Preyer (p. 31, 32), the results under the most favorable circumstances are:

| $n_1$ | $n$ | $d$ | $\epsilon$ |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 120.209 | 119.791 | 0.418 | 0.00349 = 1/286 |
| 440 | 439.636 | 0.364 | 0.000827 = 1/1209 |
| 500.3 | 500 | 0.300 | 0.000600 = 1/1666 |
| 1000.5 | 1000 | 0.500 | 0.000500 = 1/2000 |

As can be seen, $\epsilon$ varies enormously. According to <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>A psychological principle stating that the ratio of the increment threshold to the background intensity is constant.</note>, this value should remain constant across different values of $n$. Meanwhile, the absolute differential sensitivity—measured by the reciprocal value of $d$—varies significantly less within these frequency limits. However, according to the author's experiments, this sensitivity decreases rapidly below these limits and slowly above them.

It is, of course, regrettable that the important support that Weber's Law previously found through the intuition of musical feeling in the realm of pitch is lost due to Preyer’s experiments. However, three factors must be considered here, just as with color sensations.

**First**, the pitch and intensity of tones, as well as the color and brightness of light, are different things; thus, different laws may apply to each. If the law fails for one, it does not necessarily fail for the others.

**Second.** The confirmations of Weber’s Law for the dependence of the intensity of light and sound sensations on—

<column-break/>

—reveals that even objectively simple tones—and those from rods and plates, which do not have the same <term>harmonic overtones</term> <note>Secondary frequencies that sound along with the main pitch, giving a sound its specific "color" or timbre.</note> as those from strings or the human voice—still produce the same melodies and harmonies for our perception. However, as noted previously, every objectively simple tone (as well as every tone from a rod or plate) carries its own harmonic overtones within the ear, due to <term>second-order terms</term> <note>A reference to mathematical components in the equations of sound waves that account for distortions or additional vibrations created by the physical structure of the ear itself.</note> in the mathematical expression.

<summary>This page examines experiments by Preyer that challenge Weber's Law regarding pitch perception, showing that relative sensitivity to frequency changes is not constant. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between physical stimuli (vibrations) and psychological sensation, specifically addressing why Preyer's experiments might not align perfectly with Weber's Law due to the interplay between frequency and amplitude.</meta>

...of the amplitude *a* are generally found with the constancy of the vibration frequency *n* <note>The number of oscillations per second, which determines pitch.</note>. It could be imagined that confirmations of the law for the dependence of pitch and color sensation on the frequency *n* would, conversely, only be found with a corresponding constancy of the amplitude *a* <note>The maximum extent of a vibration, which determines loudness or intensity.</note>, for which constancy Preyer's experiments offer no guarantee.

In the meantime, little weight should be given to this conjecture, for the relative force with which we strike tones seems to have no distinct influence on the sensation of their pitch relationship. All the more reason to take the following remark into consideration.

**Thirdly.** With a change in the frequency *n*, two things change. First, the **number of repetitions of vibrations in a given time** changes—in short, the **period** of the vibration—on which (considering also the composition of the periods) the **sensation of pitch, tone quality, and color** depends; in short, the **qualitative side of sensation**. Secondly, the **velocity of the vibrating motion at every moment** changes, and consequently the **kinetic energy <term>vis viva</term> *a*²*n*² of the vibrations** <note>original: "lebendige Kraft"; a historical term for kinetic energy, though here expressed as a function of amplitude and frequency.</note>. According to the most obvious assumption, the **intensity or quantitative side of the sensation** is made dependent on this, as is also *an*², a function which, according to the discussions under XX, might be more relevant to the quantity of sensation than kinetic energy; in short, whichever function of *a* one wishes to consider here, not only *a* but also *n* will have an influence on the quantity or total intensity of the sensation. A tone—to use a distinguishing expression for it—will sound all the louder the more *a* increases, and all the more piercing the more *n* increases, although in the impression of intensity itself one cannot distinguish in any way what is due to *a* and what is due to *n*. Similarly, with a color, the **brightness** will depend on both elements simultaneously without a distinction between them. And it is now still to be investigated whether Weber's Law—regarding the total intensity of sensation dependent on both *a* and *n*—holds true not only for the case of a change in *a* with constant *n* (to which previous confirmations refer), but also for the case where *n* changes, as a fun-

It distinguishes between qualitative aspects (like pitch) and quantitative aspects (like loudness or brightness).</summary>

...to be regarded as fundamentally valid. This is, of course, not decided by Preyer's experiments <note>William Preyer (1841–1897), a physiologist who studied how we perceive the limits of sound and color.</note>, insofar as these relate to differences in the sensation of pitch, not intensity; and to my knowledge, no decisive experiments on this matter exist at all. It is generally difficult to accurately judge the equality and inequality of the **total intensity** of the impression of tones and colors, in dependence on *a* and *n* simultaneously <note>The author uses *a* to represent amplitude (the "height" of a wave, related to loudness/brightness) and *n* for frequency (the "speed" of vibrations, related to pitch/hue).</note>, when both *a* and *n* differ between the compared components; yet the comparison is not impossible, only not as precise as when *n* is the same for both. No one will be in doubt as to which of the two components makes the **stronger** total impression when comparing a **very** bright blue to a very dark red, or a **very** forcefully struck low tone to a **very** faintly struck high tone, or in the reversal of these conditions. Furthermore, in the experiments mentioned above, Dobrowolsky <note>Wladimir Dobrowolsky, a Russian ophthalmologist who studied light sensitivity.</note> knew how to bring two different shades of color to the same impression of brightness; and Helmholtz, in his experiments (*p. 317*), where he gave more precise expression to earlier observations by **Dove** and **Purkinje** <note>The "Purkinje effect" describes how the human eye becomes more sensitive to blue light compared to red light in low-light conditions.</note> (*see below, p. 171*), proceeded from an equal impression of brightness for blue and red.

Now it is certain that with tones as with colors—whether one considers the total intensity of the impression dependent on *a*²*n*² or *an*²—every decrease in *a* can be compensated for by an increase in *n*, or vice versa, such that the total intensity of the impression remains the same, while during this maintenance of equality, the impression of the pitch or color changes due to the change in *n*. Conversely, with the sensation of pitch or color tone held constant because *n* is kept the same, the total intensity of the sensation can change due to a modification of *a*. Thus, generally speaking, the **difference threshold** <term>Unterschiedsschwelle</term> <note>The "difference threshold" is the smallest change in a physical stimulus that a person can actually detect.</note>, that is, the **point** where equality between two sensations is just barely reached, does not coincide for the total intensity of the impression of tones or colors with the difference threshold for pitches or color tones.*) And when with two...

colors or tones through a corresponding modification of their *a* and *n* <note>As established previously, *a* refers to the amplitude (intensity/brightness) and *n* refers to the frequency (hue/pitch).</note> all difference between both the total strength of their impression and their pitch or color tone has just disappeared, it will generally be said (apart from special cases) that only one of the two differences is exactly on the threshold, while the other is already below the threshold; for otherwise, because the two thresholds do not coincide, one would still notice a difference in one sense or the other. The fact that two colors or tones have been brought to the point where no difference at all is perceived between them is therefore no proof that the difference in the total strength of their impression—expressed mathematically as a function of their *a* and *n*—has been brought to the same value, the **threshold value**; it could also have fallen more or less below the threshold value. Therefore, in my opinion, experiments according to the method of just noticeable differences—where one brings the difference between two tones or colors to complete disappearance, or close to it, by changing their *a* or *n*—cannot decide with certainty on the applicability of Weber's Law <note>Weber’s Law suggests that our ability to perceive a change in intensity is relative, not absolute. For example, adding one candle to a room with ten candles is noticeable, but adding one candle to a room with a hundred is not.</note> to differences between the total strength of tones or colors in general. This remark seems applicable to the interesting and ingenious experiments of *v. Zahn* <note>Gustav von Zahn, a 19th-century researcher who studied the physics of sound and light.</note>, which would otherwise speak against this applicability in the field of colors. However, in order to leave the judgment to everyone themselves, I communicate these experiments specifically under XIX.

From another side, the following circumstance seems to contradict the applicability of Weber's Law to the total strength of the impression in colors.

According to the more precise experiments of Helmholtz (p. 317 f.)—provoked by well-known earlier reports by Purkinje and Dove <note>The "Purkinje effect" describes how the human eye becomes more sensitive to blue light compared to red light as light levels drop.</note>—with spectral colors, red and blue (generally, a color of low refractivity and one of high refractivity, i.e., with small and large *n*) which appear equally bright under a certain illumination, no longer appear so when the illumination, and thus the *a*, is weakened or strengthened for both in the same proportion,

---

disappeared between color tones that are close to each other in *n*, without the difference in brightness having already disappeared everywhere.

Given the many other mysterious differences between the behavior of color impressions and tone impressions, such a discrepancy might possibly exist here as well. On the other hand, the fact that colors change their hue when their brightness is altered—appearing more "whitish" the lighter they become (see p. 136)—proves that complex relationships exist for colors. This makes the interpretation of the facts in this regard generally uncertain.

After all is said and done, it must be admitted that neither the changes in the sensation of pitch nor color, in their dependence on changes in the **vibration frequency** *n*, follow Weber's Law <note>Weber’s Law states that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus. The author argues here that while this works for brightness, it doesn't work for changes in frequency/wavelength.</note>, and it does not seem possible to blame this on experimental errors. <meta>This page transitions from discussing the limitations of Weber's Law in the perception of color and pitch to its application in "extensive magnitudes," specifically spatial and temporal perception. The author is evaluating whether the same mathematical rules that govern sensory intensity (like brightness) also govern our perception of physical size and time duration.</meta>

### 7) **Extensive Magnitudes.** <note>original: *Größen*; refers here to things with physical extension, such as length, area, or duration, as opposed to "intensive" sensations like loudness.</note>

It remains to ask: how do the so-called extensive sensations relate to the **Law** regarding the perception of **magnitudes of space and time**?

It was previously noted (p. 61) and is obvious from the outset that one cannot **postulate** for these sensations a law of dependence on the magnitude of distance or duration of time identical to the one that exists for **intensive sensations** regarding the intensity of the underlying **psychophysical activity**. <note>The author distinguishes between how we feel the "strength" of a stimulus versus how we perceive its "size."</note> Rather, nothing can be decided about this *a priori* <note>Latin: "from the former"; referring to knowledge that proceeds from theoretical deduction rather than from observation.</note>. What I stated in the 7th section regarding extensive spatial sensations on the retina summarizes as follows: **Weber’s Law** and the **logarithmic law of measurement** dependent upon it could very well exist regarding changes in the **total extension of the retina**, without preventing parts of the retina—distances upon it—from being estimated according to a simple ratio to the total extension (the number of **sensation circles** <term>sensation circles: an early physiological concept describing the smallest areas of the skin or retina that can distinguish two separate points of contact</term>); only, experiments involving changes to the size of the retina, such as those performed by nature, cannot be conducted by us. In my and Volkmann’s **visual estimation experiments** <note>original: *Augenmassversuche*; experiments where a subject judges lengths or distances by eye.</note>, **Weber’s Law** (with a downward deviation in Volkmann’s micrometric experiments) has certainly proven itself very well over wide intervals; however, it remains doubtful whether these results should be attributed directly to extensive sensations and not rather to **sensations of movement or muscle feeling**. — For extensive sensations on the **skin**, where muscle feeling is not called upon, I have certainly not obtained any confirmation of Weber’s Law equivalent to that found in the visual estimation experiments.

Regarding the **sense of time**, **Mach** <note>Ernst Mach (1838–1916), a physicist and philosopher who conducted early research on how humans perceive time.</note> (in Treatise no. 1) finds the **Law** inapplicable according to experiments using the method of just noticeable differences while employing the **sense of hearing**. The average results obtained from different experimental arrangements and with different subjects, summarized on p. 10, yielded the following on the whole: where ***t*** signifies the primary time in **seconds**, and Δ*t/t* signifies the just-noticeable relative time difference *ε*, which according to **Weber’s Law** should be equal for all primary times ***t***.

<summary>This page explores whether Weber's Law applies to the perception of space and time, noting that while visual estimations often follow the law, perceptions of time and skin sensitivity do not. The author questions whether visual accuracy stems from pure sensation or the physical movement of the eye muscles.</summary>

The numbers marked with * are to be regarded, according to the author, as too small compared to the others (as a lower limit), insofar as the difference $\Delta t$ was no longer noticeable at all in these cases, whereas it was still noticeable for the other values of $t$. <note>Here, $t$ represents the duration of time (time interval) and $\Delta t/t$ represents the relative threshold—the smallest change in time a person can detect relative to the total duration.</note>

According to this, the values for $\frac{\Delta t}{t}$ do indeed deviate completely from the equality required by **Weber’s Law** <note>Weber's Law suggests that the ratio $\Delta t/t$ should remain constant regardless of the size of $t$.</note>, and at $t = 0.375$ there is a minimum of $\frac{\Delta t}{t}$, and therefore a maximum of **relative differential sensitivity**. <note>"Differential sensitivity" refers to how good we are at telling two things apart. A "minimum" in the ratio means we are at our most precise in perceiving time intervals around 0.375 seconds.</note>

Let us now observe, however, that the main times <note>original: *Hauptzeiten*; in these experiments, the "standard" time interval presented to the subject for comparison.</note> within which the strong variation of $\frac{\Delta t}{t}$ occurs—0.016 to 1.153 seconds—are everywhere quite small; meanwhile, the values of $\frac{\Delta t}{t}$ for the two largest values of $t$ (i.e., 4.52 and 8.00), despite their great distance from one another, give a result for $\frac{\Delta t}{t}$ that is only inconsistent insofar as the value for $t = 8.00$ is declared by the author to be slightly too small. One might not place much weight on this approximate agreement between the two values if it were not confirmed on a much larger scale in **Vierordt's** <note>Karl von Vierordt (1818–1884), a German physician who conducted pioneering experiments on the human perception of time.</note> very extensive experiments using the **method of mean errors** (in his work: *The Sense of Time*, Tübingen 1868). <term>method of mean errors: a scientific procedure where a subject attempts to replicate a stimulus (like a sound's duration) exactly, and the researcher measures the average amount by which they miss the mark.</term>

In general, Vierordt found the following: in the experiments, a **constant error** is committed, which is perceptible in the ratios of the raw errors; specifically, small main times (standard times) are translated into larger error times, while large main times are translated into smaller error times. In short: small intervals are estimated as too large, and large intervals as too small. <note>This phenomenon is often called "Vierordt’s Law" or the "indifference point" effect.</note> By transitioning from raw errors to **pure variable errors**, this circumstance—which complicates experiments on differential sensitivity—is eliminated, and one must adhere to the pure mean error when testing our Law. There it is shown, in experiments where the error times <note>original: *Fehlzeiten*; the durations reproduced or guessed by the test subject.</note> were determined immediately after the main time—

<meta>This page continues the empirical analysis of Karl von Vierordt’s research on the "sense of time," specifically testing the validity of Weber's Law. It builds on the previous page's discussion by introducing more complex datasets and comparing them to Ernst Mach's earlier observations on how our perception of time changes as intervals grow longer.</meta>

...then <note>This refers back to the discussion of the "mean error" on the previous page.</note> \*), if the percentage-based—that is, the pure mean error calculated in relation to the **main time** <note>original: *Hauptzeit*; the standard duration presented to the subject for comparison.</note>—is called *$\varepsilon$*, we find that *$\varepsilon$* does indeed decrease as the duration *t* increases within the range of small intervals, until it reaches a minimum. This is exactly what <term>Mach (Ernst Mach, a physicist who studied how humans perceive physical sensations)</term> discovered. However, after one has increased the duration more or less beyond that point, *$\varepsilon$* remains approximately constant as *t* continues to rise; one must only calculate the average of several consecutive values of *t* to smooth out the quite significant **random** fluctuations of *$\varepsilon$*.

Let us examine the experimental table on page 36. An assistant provided two beats at a specific interval; Vierordt attempted to repeat them immediately with the exact same interval. The times were recorded using a suitable auxiliary attachment on a <term>Kymograph (a device that recorded motion, such as a heartbeat or a finger tap, as a line on a rotating drum)</term>. In this case, starting from *t* = 0.204 seconds (where *$\varepsilon$* = 16.6) and moving upward with the durations, the minimum error of *$\varepsilon$* = 8.5 occurred at *t* = 1.129. After this, *$\varepsilon$* = 8.7 at *t* = 1.363, and as the durations continued to increase, the following mean *$\varepsilon$* values were found for the average of every four consecutive *t* values from 1.5 up to 8.860 seconds:

| *t* | 1.98 | 3.09 | 4.99 | 7.30 |
|-----|------|------|------|------|
| *$\varepsilon$* | 13.5 | 14.4 | 15.1 | 15.1 |

In the experiments on page 49, the durations *t* were increased much further. The minimum *$\varepsilon$* = 4.5 was found at *t* = 0.887; following this, and including this minimum, the average values for every four increasing *t* values were as follows:

| *t* | 1.346 | 4.096 | 11.000 | 19.288 | 42.620 |
|-----|-------|-------|--------|--------|--------|
| *$\varepsilon$* | 5.45 | 11.85 | 16.02 | 15.20 | 17.65 |

Thus, in both series of experiments, there is a strong deviation from the law at the lower end, but a strong approximation to it at higher main times *t*.

\*) <note>Vierordt’s experiments, which are overall very interesting, include many more variations; however, for the time being, I cannot go into further citation or discussion of them beyond what has already been provided above.</note>

<summary>This page analyzes Karl von Vierordt's experimental data on time perception, showing that while our precision fluctuates at very short intervals, it stabilizes and follows Weber's Law more closely as the time intervals increase. It highlights the "minimum error" point where human time perception is most accurate, roughly around 0.9 to 1.1 seconds.</summary>
