<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of temporal perception, specifically looking at data from Karl von Vierordt’s laboratory. It builds on the previous discussion of the "mean error" (represented by the Greek letter ε) and how it fluctuates depending on the duration of the time interval (t) being measured. Fechner is attempting to prove that while humans are less accurate with very short durations, our perception stabilizes for longer durations, supporting the validity of Weber's Law in the realm of time.</meta>

In yet another series of experiments, conducted by the student Höring under Vierordt's supervision (p. 44), eight successive beats were provided by a metronome—thus creating seven equal time intervals—which then had to be repeated immediately on the <term>Kymograph (a device that recorded the timing of physical movements onto a rotating drum)</term>. The results were as follows, where *t* represents the duration of a beat interval in seconds:

| *t* | 0.183 | 0.234 | 0.306 | 0.365 | 0.454 | 0.600 | 0.833 | 1.428 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| *$\epsilon$* | 3.9 | 4.5 | 3.0 | 2.5 | 3.1 | 4.1 | 5.2 | 5.4 |

Once again, we see a minimum of *$\epsilon$* <note>The "mean error" expressed as a percentage of the total time; lower numbers indicate higher accuracy.</note> at *t* = 0.365, followed by an approximate constancy from *t* = 0.833 onward.

Experiments entirely comparable to these, but conducted according to the <term>method of right and wrong cases (a procedure where a subject compares two stimuli and judges which is greater, rather than trying to replicate one)</term>, are presented by Höring in his inaugural dissertation, "Experiments on the Discriminatory Power of the Sense of Hearing for Temporal Magnitudes," Tübingen 1866. These are reproduced and discussed by Vierordt in his work on the *Sense of Time* (p. 62 ff.). However, I would prefer to approach their calculation differently than Vierordt did. Consequently, I do not consider the figures Vierordt derived from them on pages 73 and 75—the progression of which does not particularly align with the figures cited above—to be entirely authoritative. I will, however, refrain from going into further detail for now, as I have not yet performed a new calculation of my own. Furthermore, the essential conclusion is already supported by the substantial agreement between the experimental results of <term>Mach (Ernst Mach)</term> and Vierordt, despite their use of two completely different methods.

In any case, one must admit that if the deviations from the Law <note>Referring to Weber’s Law, which suggests that our ability to perceive a difference between two things stays constant relative to the size of the things being compared.</note> in this type of experiment still require an explanation within the range of small *t* values, then the strong approximation to that Law at higher *t* values also demands an explanation. It is easier to believe that the former (the deviations) depend on external circumstances that mask the pure lawfulness of nature, rather than the latter (the adherence to the law).

<summary>Fechner analyzes data from Höring and Vierordt regarding the accuracy of timing beats, noting that while accuracy fluctuates at very short intervals, it stabilizes at longer ones. This consistency supports Fechner's belief that Weber's Law applies to the human sense of time despite some initial deviations.</summary>
<keywords>Vierordt, Höring, sense of time, Weber's Law, mean error, psychophysics, metronome, temporal perception</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of temporal perception in Vierordt’s experiments to a new section focusing on light perception experiments conducted by Joseph Delboeuf. It introduces the methodology Delboeuf used to test how the human eye perceives equal differences in brightness, building upon principles established earlier in the book regarding Weber’s Law and Plateau’s research.</meta>

## XVII. Delboeuf’s Light Experiments.

Delboeuf’s procedure, established according to <term>Plateau’s Principle (the theory that a perceived "middle" brightness between two stimuli corresponds to a specific physical ratio; see p. 22)</term>, was essentially this (according to <note>original: "Ét." referring to Joseph Delboeuf's *Étude psychophysique* (1873)</note> p. 50 ff.).

To produce measured values for *A, B, and C*, he had three circular sectors made of white <term>vellum paper (a high-quality, smooth paper with a uniform surface)</term> of graduated angular widths rotate in front of a background of black velvet. He arranged the setup so that the daylight or candlelight illuminating the white sectors could not strike the black background. Thus (in my view), the brightness of the background was represented purely by that of the <term>eye-black (the "intrinsic light" or baseline sensation of grayness the eye perceives even in total darkness)</term>; furthermore, any side light reflected from the walls was kept away from the apparatus. The circular sectors were attached to the circumference of a disc *C'* (labeled *A* by Delboeuf)—which was white in some experiments and black in others—in such a way that, when rotated together, they appeared as three gray rings enclosing the disc and each other. The innermost ring, produced by sector *C* with the largest angular width, was the brightest and directly surrounded disc *C'*. The outermost ring, produced by sector *A* with the smallest angular width, was the darkest. Let $\delta, \delta', \delta''$ be the angular widths of *A, B, and C*, which, under constant and uniform illumination, can serve as a comparative measure of the physical brightness of the rings, insofar as this is determined by the **external illumination**. Accordingly, the experiment consisted of taking fixed values for $\delta$ and $\delta'$ and then adjusting the angular width $\delta''$ of the innermost, brightest sector *C* until the difference between *C* and *B* appeared equal to the difference between *B* and *A*. If we now imagine that each of the brightnesses $\delta, \delta', \delta''$ (which depend on the angular width under the influence of external light) is increased by a small constant value *c* to represent the full appearance of the rings' brightness—a value which I believe depends on the internal brightness of the eye-black, whereas Delboeuf assigns a slightly different meaning to it in his *Étude* (see p. 34)—then, regardless of this value, at the point where both differences appear equal, according to <term>Weber’s Law (the principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus)</term>, it would have to be

<summary>This page describes Joseph Delboeuf’s experimental setup using rotating discs and white paper sectors to create rings of varying brightness. Fechner explains how Delboeuf attempted to find the physical intensities required for a subject to perceive equal intervals of brightness, incorporating a correction factor for the eye's internal "baseline" light.</summary>
<keywords>Joseph Delboeuf, light perception, Plateau's Principle, circular sectors, eye-black, Weber's Law, brightness intensity, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Joseph Delboeuf's light experiments. The previous page ended mid-word while defining $\delta, \delta', \delta''$ as the angular widths of white sectors on a rotating disc used to measure perceived brightness. This page introduces the mathematical formulas used to test Weber's Law and discusses the experimental problem of "contrast effects" where neighboring shades of gray influence how a specific ring is perceived.</meta>

$\frac{\delta'}{\delta} = \frac{\delta''}{\delta'}$, but with regard to $c <note>The variable "c" represents a constant value in Fechner and Delboeuf’s formulas, usually interpreted as the "intrinsic light" of the eye—the baseline level of sensation present even in total darkness.</note>$, according to Delboeuf’s formulas in his *Étude* <note>original: "Étude psychophysique" (1873) by Joseph Delboeuf</note> as well as my formula (*Elements II, p. 195*):

-> $\frac{\delta' + c}{\delta + c} = \frac{\delta'' + c}{\delta' + c}$ <-

from which follows

-> $\delta'' = \frac{\delta'^2 - c \delta + c \delta'}{c + \delta'}$ (1) <-

-> $c = \frac{\delta'^2 - \delta \delta''}{\delta + \delta'' - 2 \delta'}$ (2) <-

It was now possible to investigate whether the values of $\delta''$ determined through experiment sufficiently match Formula (1)—and thus the condition for the validity of <term>Weber’s Law (the principle that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus)</term>—when $\delta$ and $\delta'$ (i.e., different absolute brightnesses) are changed, provided a small value for $c$ is assumed as a constant. Furthermore, one could see whether $c$, when calculated from the values of $\delta, \delta', \delta''$ using Formula (2), actually remains constant as those values change across different absolute brightnesses.

Delboeuf himself mentions as a flaw in the procedure (Et. p. 50, 71) that a non-uniform <term>contrast effect (the phenomenon where the perceived brightness of an object is altered by the brightness of the area surrounding it)</term> was not sufficiently avoided. Some anomalies in the experiments might depend on this, which is why he intended to repeat the trials but never got around to it. Specifically, in the experiments testing Weber’s Law (Ét. p. 54–66), the innermost gray ring—produced by the white sector with the widest angle—surrounded a completely white disc, labeled $C'$ above, to which all the sectors were attached, while the outermost ring stood out against a completely black background. In the special experiments to determine $c$ (p. 72, 88), $C'$ was black, meaning both $C$ and $A$ were set against black. Since every ring now has a neighbor of a different brightness on either side, it splits into two zones of varying brightness according to the laws of contrast and, overall, adopts a modified brightness depending on its surroundings*) (see Section XI). Delboeuf notes that with sufficiently wide rings—for example, 2 to 3 centimeters—the resulting disturbance is less noticeable, and adds (p. 71) that it is easy "to partially avoid this disadvantage" if one considers the background against which the combination of $\delta, \delta', \delta''$ is set,

---

*) <margin>Undoubtedly, due to the far-reaching effects of contrast, one must consider more than just the immediate neighborhood. Thus, the middle ring will be influenced not only by the contrast with its two neighboring rings but also by the sum or difference of the inherently weaker contrast effects from disc $C'$ and from the background, depending on whether the disc and background are of the same or different types.</margin>

<sig>12*</sig>

<summary>This page presents the mathematical verification of Delboeuf’s brightness experiments, focusing on whether a constant value 'c' (intrinsic light) can reconcile experimental data with Weber's Law. It also highlights a significant experimental error regarding "contrast effects," where the background and neighboring rings distort the perceived brightness of the gray sectors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Joseph Delboeuf’s experiments regarding light perception. The previous page ended while explaining that a non-uniform contrast effect occurs when the rings on a rotating disc influence one another's perceived brightness; the author was arguing that the background should be adjusted so that it</meta>

gives the same brightness to both the inner and outer sides as the middle ring.

I would like to add to the previous point that, to reliably prevent a <term>constant error (a systematic bias in measurement that consistently shifts results in one direction)</term>, not only should ring *C* have been adjusted, but *A* should just as often have been modified until the differences between *C* and *B*, and *B* and *A* appeared equal to the eye. At the very least, one should have investigated whether a constant error arose from this one-sided experimental method.

In the meantime, since the experiments to test Weber's Law on page 54 and following—where the disc *C'* was white—no less than the experiments on pages 73 and 88—where it was black—yielded only very small, and in some cases almost vanishing, values for *c* according to Formula (2), it follows that only errors of a very small order can have arisen from the irregularities of non-uniform contrast when testing Weber’s Law according to Formula (1). Thus, the smallness of *c* <note>As noted previously, "c" represents the eye's internal "intrinsic light" or baseline sensation.</note> itself can be considered established; however, one should not count on an accurate absolute determination of *c* based on these results.

From the entirety of the experiments—summarized below in a master table—it is evident that Formula (1), assuming a very small constant value for *c*, is indeed followed with a most desirable approximation across wide variations of *δ*, *δ'*, and *δ''*. Delboeuf provides four experimental tables (p. 54, 60, 62, 65) for various test subjects and under different lighting conditions. In all four, the same 14 different combinations of *δ* and *δ'* were consistently used, which are listed in the master table of experiments below; and in each experimental series, the corresponding value of *δ''* for each of these combinations was determined by an average of 2 to 5 observation values (specified in the original). Delboeuf now compares these average observed values of *δ''* with those calculated from Formula (1) using the given values of *δ* and *δ'*, setting *c* first at $0.5^\circ$ and then at $0.12^\circ$. These values almost vanish when compared to the applied values of *δ*, *δ'*, and *δ''*, of which the smallest *δ* was $9^\circ$. I have also convinced myself through the first three tables that one can set *c* directly to zero without significantly altering the agreement between observation and calculation—provided one disregards experiment no. 1 in each table (where *δ* $= 9^\circ$ and *δ'* $= 47^\circ$). In that specific case, the value of *c* carries the most weight because *δ* is at its weakest, but Delboeuf himself (according to p. 60) does not consider this experiment to be very reliable at all.

<summary>This page evaluates the accuracy of Delboeuf’s formulas in relation to Weber’s Law, concluding that the variable for internal eye-light (c) is negligible in most experimental conditions. Fechner notes that while contrast effects and experimental bias exist, they do not significantly undermine the mathematical approximation of the law.</summary>

However satisfactory, indeed conclusive, the agreement of the previous experiments regarding the validity of **Weber’s Law** <note>A fundamental principle in psychophysics stating that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> appears, it must be noted regarding the determination of the small value ***c*** that these experiments agree within themselves and with the experiments specifically conducted for this determination (on pages 73 and 88) only insofar as they all yield a very small but, depending on circumstances, highly variable value. This variability does not inherently prevent relating ***c*** to the brightness of the **intrinsic light of the eye** <note>original: "Augenschwarz." This refers to the sensation of "gray" or light one perceives even in total darkness, caused by the internal activity of the visual system.</note>, since this brightness itself, according to earlier remarks, must be considered significantly variable. However, it may also partly stem from the uncertainty of the procedure noted in the insertion on page 179. Even if ***c*** was set to 0.5 throughout the calculation of the previous experimental tables—as the author himself did according to one of his calculation methods—it is not to be assumed that ***c*** held this value constantly everywhere. Rather, the consistent close agreement between calculation and observation is explained by the fact that a value which is quite negligible in the calculation can be doubled, tripled, or even shift from positive to negative without the resulting calculation changing significantly.

Fundamentally, every single experiment number in each of the tables combined in the previous general table provides an opportunity to calculate the value ***c*** using the given values *δ*, *δ'*, and the observed *δ''* <note>These Greek letters represent the intensities of light used in the three-shadow experiment setup.</note> by means of formula (2), except that every individual determination of such a small value is very uncertain. If I now take the first seven numbers from Table I together—against whose *δ* the value ***c*** does not vanish to the same degree as it does against the following seven—and determine ***c*** summarily through

-> ***c*** = $\frac{\Sigma (\delta'^2 - \delta \delta''^2)}{\Sigma (\delta + \delta'' - 2 \delta')}$ <-

I find ***c*** = 0.48, which noticeably agrees with the 0.5 assumed for the calculation. But the other tables yield other values; and since ***c*** cannot be found precisely from these experiments at all, *Delboeuf* has conducted further experiments on page 73,

...were carried out by the observer **A** using a constant application of $\delta = 13^\circ$ and $\delta' = 41^\circ$. Five different levels of illumination were used, and for each level, **24** (well-consistent) trials were performed. Half of these were conducted by moving from a $\delta''$ that was too large toward one that appeared correct, and the other half by moving from one that was too small toward the correct value. The illuminations were as follows and yielded the following mean values for $\delta''$ and, consequently, $c$.

**1)** In the evening, by candlelight, surrounded by a black half-cylinder at a distance of **25** centimeters from the apparatus: $\delta'' = 127.8$; $c = 0.333$.
**2)** A very dark <note>original: "très-sombre"</note> March sky between **4** and **5** o'clock in the afternoon: $\delta'' = 122.3$; $c = 1.71$.
**3)** A gray <note>original: "gris"</note> sky in the same month at **3** o'clock: $\delta'' = 126.3$; $c = 0.682$.
**4)** A completely bright sky, the sun shining in full glory, measured indoors: $\delta'' = 129.4$; $c = 0.00$.
**5)** Then (on this same bright day) in the open air, though without direct sunlight: $\delta'' = 137$; $c = -5.814$.

If one disregards the trials under **1)**, insofar as trials conducted by candlelight seem insufficiently comparable to the others, then $c$ decreases as the intensity of illumination increases, and in the case of **5)** even crosses into a negative value. The decrease of $c$ following the calculation during increased illumination does not necessarily signify an absolute decrease in value. Rather, if the value were truly constant (meaning the <term>psychophysical value</term> <note>The internal, mental measurement of a physical stimulus.</note> it represents), such a decrease would be expected. This is because a constant $c$ naturally constitutes a smaller fraction of the total illumination the larger that illumination becomes. In the method of determination employed here, a sector of 1° multiplied by the intensity of its external illumination serves as the unit of brightness. Only the occurrence of negative values for $c$ under the strongest illumination cannot be explained by this; and naturally, negative values for $c$ do not align at all with the view that $c$ represents a positive brightness of the <term>intrinsic light of the eye</term> <note>original: "Augenschwarz," the sensation of "dark gray" perceived by the eye even in total darkness.</note>. <meta>This page continues the technical analysis of experimental data regarding light perception and the constant "c," which represents the internal light of the eye. ...deviations from <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>Weber’s Law suggests that the smallest change in a stimulus that can be perceived is a constant ratio of the original stimulus. This text discusses why this rule fails at very high or low light intensities.</note> begin to become more noticeable as one ascends to higher levels of brightness. And that this circumstance really comes into play here is evident from the following remark made by the author <note>Joseph Delboeuf, a Belgian psychologist</note> on page 72: "We wanted [with $\delta = 13^\circ, \delta' = 41^\circ$] to conduct experiments while illuminating the apparatus with full sunlight; these experiments did not succeed, for the observation subject was blinded and accepted all values of $\delta''$ if they exceeded $100^\circ$ or $105^\circ$, or went below $150^\circ$." Naturally, such a disturbance must have already made itself felt in bright daylight.

Since the brightness levels of the various illuminations in the previous experiments were not compared with each other, and since Delboeuf furthermore (according to page 84) did not yet consider the previous method precise enough for determining such a small value as *c*, he conducted four more series of experiments—each with 58 to 84 observations following a completely consistent procedure—partly by himself and partly through the observer *A*. In these, $\delta, \delta'$, and $\delta''$ <note>These symbols represent the angular sizes of white sectors on a rotating disk, used to create specific shades of gray.</note> were given as constants equal to $13^\circ, 41^\circ$, and $100^\circ$ (in one of the series, actually $102^\circ$). Thus, $\delta''$ was not the value being sought; instead, the illumination was modified by changing the distance of a candle until the condition of a seemingly equal difference between the pairs ($\delta, \delta'$) and ($\delta', \delta''$) was fulfilled. The candle was almost entirely surrounded by a cylinder blackened on the inside. Half of the experiments were conducted by moving the candle further away to reach the point of equality, while the other half were conducted by bringing the candle closer. When this point was reached, the distances measured during the "moving away" phase were generally smaller than those from the "moving closer" phase. The mean of these was taken and used as the basis for calculating the brightness according to the <term>inverse square law</term> <note>A physics principle stating that the intensity of light decreases in proportion to the square of the distance from the source.</note> of distance.

Through the 58 observations he conducted himself, he found, by means of appropriate reduction, the value of *c* to be equivalent to the brightness produced by the rotation of a white sector of only $0.04^\circ = \frac{1^\circ}{25}$ on a completely light-deprived background, when this sector is illuminated by a candle (the specific nature of which is not specified in the experiments) placed 25 centimeters away. In place of $0.04^\circ$, according to entirely corresponding experiments by observer *A*...

<summary>This page details Delboeuf's experiments to precisely measure 'c' (internal eye light) using a candle-lit apparatus and rotating disks, noting that extreme brightness causes blindness that invalidates Weber's Law. He establishes a method of moving a light source to find the exact point where differences in gray shades appear equal.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, Delboeuf, light intensity, experimental psychology, psychophysics, internal light of the eye, inverse square law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Joseph Delboeuf's light perception experiments, specifically focusing on the constant "c" which represents the eye's internal brightness (its "intrinsic light"). (with **84 observations in each series**), in the three series, the values were 0.05, 0.1372, and 0.1587. Of these, the first was found a few days earlier than the last two, which followed from two series conducted on the same day of testing; from this, Delboeuf (p. 84) draws the following results:

**1)** "that *c* varies from one individual to another,
**2)** that *c* itself varies at different times even in the same individual,
**3)** that *c* [under constant conditions] remains noticeably constant during a fairly substantial interval of time, which can extend over several hours [namely during the duration of the 58 or 84 trials in each series], if this interval is chosen appropriately."

He considers the latter point in particular to be established, although (according to p. 90) the differences noted in **1)** and **2)** might have also been influenced by a difference in the brightness of the candles used for the experiments.

In any case, the insignificance of *c* that emerges from all these experiments remains striking. As well as the experiments align with <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>Weber’s Law states that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is proportional to the original stimulus. For example, if you can tell the difference between 10 and 11 pounds, you would need 20 and 22 pounds to notice the same relative change.</note>, they align just as little with the role that, according to other experiences, one must attribute to the brightness of the <term>eye-black</term> <note>original: "Augenschwarz"; this refers to the "internal light" of the eye, or the faint sensation of light we see even in total darkness.</note>; except that for the stated reason they do not appear reliable enough for its determination.

## XVIII. Hering's Weight Experiments.

<note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918) was a famous physiologist and a major rival to Fechner’s theories on how we perceive physical sensations.</note> Hering asserts two types of weight experiments against Weber’s Law: one set conducted by himself in a peculiar manner with larger weight differences (p. 14 ff.), and the other (p. 33 ff.)—henceforth to be counted as Hering’s—carried out at his suggestion and unquestionably under his direction by the medical students Biedermann and Löwit. These were executed according to a method that, as I believe I can infer from correspondence with Hering, is a modification of the method of <term>just noticeable differences</term> <note>A classic psychological testing method where researchers determine the smallest change in a weight or light that a person can actually feel or see.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of Delboeuf’s light experiments, noting that the "internal light" of the eye varies by individual and time, before introducing Ewald Hering’s weight-based challenges to Weber's Law.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, Joseph Delboeuf, Ewald Hering, eye-black, intrinsic light, weight perception, psychophysics, Biedermann, Löwit</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of sensory perception experiments, specifically moving from light perception to Ewald Hering's critiques of Weber's Law regarding weight and pressure. It explores how the physical weight of one's own body affects the perception of external weights.</meta>

...differences to be considered, regarding which further communications from him are still to be expected.

There are two experiments of the first kind, which can be summarized briefly in the following schema:

1) If I lift a weight of 3 pounds <note>original: "Pfd.", an abbreviation for *Pfund*</note> and also calculate the weight of the arm lifted with it as approximately 3 pounds, the addition of 3 pounds to the 3-pound arm weight seems smaller to me than if I add 7 pounds to an arm weight of 3 pounds plus another 4 pounds (making 7 pounds total)—even though, according to the law <note>Referring to Weber's Law, which suggests that the ratio of the increase to the total weight should determine the sensation.</note>, they should appear equal.

2) The hand is placed on a support to exclude the influence of the weight of the arm. If I add another plate to a <term>galvanic plate</term> <note>original: "galvanische Platte"; these were metal plates used in early physics and physiology experiments because of their uniform size and weight.</note> lying on the hand, the increase appears smaller to me than if I add five plates to five already lying there. This likewise contradicts the law.

In my opinion, the first experiment allows for the following interpretation. Usually, when we take a weight in hand and weigh it, it does not occur to us to grasp it as an **increase** to the arm weight and to, so to speak, subtract the latter in order to perceive the former on its own. Instead, we judge the impression made by the **total weight** of the arm and the object, whether correctly or incorrectly, and we will do the same in the experiment above. Therefore, on one side, we are not estimating an increase of 3 pounds to 3 pounds; rather, we have an absolute weight of 6 pounds on one hand, which we compare with the 14 pounds on the other side after the weight increase has been added to that side. Thus, we will certainly feel that 14 pounds is heavier than 6 pounds, but no further conclusions can be drawn beyond this self-evident fact.

Regarding the second experiment, in which consideration of the weight of the arm is omitted, the following should be noted first. When the hand rests on a table and a plate is placed upon it, the <term>sensation of pressure</term> <note>original: "Druckempfindung"</note> felt by the hand depends not only on the pressure of the plate but also on the counter-pressure the hand experiences from the table it is pressing against. This counter-pressure must be added to the pressure of the plate, just as the weight of the arm was previously added to the weight being lifted. This counter-pressure, however, will be at least—

<summary>The author critiques Ewald Hering's experiments on weight perception, arguing that the human mind perceives the "total weight" (including the arm's own weight or table resistance) rather than isolated mathematical increments.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, weight perception, arm weight, pressure sensation, counter-pressure, experimental psychology, Ewald Hering</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Ewald Hering’s experiments regarding Weber’s Law. The previous page ended while discussing how subjects often struggle to distinguish between a total weight and the incremental increase added to it.</meta>

...estimating it to be at least equal to the pressure of a single plate. Because this factor is neglected, the entire experiment loses its **evidentiary value** <note>original: "Beweiskraft"</note>.

However, it seems to me that a common objection can be raised against both of the previous experiments: that the comparison of the differences cannot be kept independent of the comparison of the absolute weights. To exclude the objection made regarding the low weight of the plate itself, I had a person—who was unaware of the purpose of the experiments—lay both hands with the backs on the table. On one hand, I placed a **lead plate** of 500 grams, covering about half the hand; on the other, I placed a plate of 1000 grams. I had the person close their eyes, then added a second plate of 500 grams to the first, and a second 1000 grams to the second. I asked which **increase** <note>original: "Zuwachs"</note> seemed larger to them. I received the answer that the weight on the second hand had become much heavier than the one on the first, just as it had already been heavier before. 

To my remark, "But I do not want to know on which side the weight became heavier, but rather on which side the **increase** experienced by the previously existing weight was greater," I received the reply: "Yes, but they (the subject) only knew how to compare the one weight that had become heavier with the other." I was unable to bring them to concede that the increases could also be compared independently of the total weights. It seemed the same to me when I had the comparison performed on myself—or rather, it seemed one way or the other depending on the direction of my imagination—though I do not wish to place much weight on my own **judgment** <note>original: "Urtheil"</note> as it may be biased. I must simply recommend caution, based on the outcome of previous trials; if one allows the experiment to be repeated by others, one must be truly certain that they do not confuse the sensation of a greater weight with the sensation of a greater increase—a certainty that, admittedly, seems quite difficult to me.

Should this nonetheless succeed, I would most readily admit that a substantial objection to the applicability of the law to **sensations of weight** would arise from it. In any case, this experimental method suffers from a complication that is difficult to eliminate, and which **Hering's** other experiments involving <term>just noticeable differences</term> <note>original: "eben merklichen Unterschieden"; this is a foundational concept in psychophysics referring to the smallest detectable change in a stimulus.</note> do not suffer from in the same way.

<summary>The author describes an experiment where a subject fails to distinguish between the total weight felt and the specific increase added, suggesting that Hering's critiques of Weber's Law may be flawed due to this subjective confusion. He argues that while the math of the "increase" should be perceived as equal or proportional, the human mind struggles to separate the increment from the total pressure.</summary>

<keywords>Ewald Hering, Weber's Law, weight perception, lead plates, psychophysics, just noticeable differences, sensation of weight, experimental methodology</keywords>

Before I go into more detail regarding these further experiments by Hering, I must, for comparison, refer back to the weight experiments based on the <term>method of right and wrong cases</term> <note>A standard psychophysical procedure where a subject compares two stimuli and the researcher records the frequency of correct versus incorrect identifications of the heavier one.</note>, which I published myself in the *Elements of Psychophysics*, Volume I, page 186 and following.*) The most significant result of these experiments is that the summed numbers *r/n* (where *r* is the number of correct cases and *n* is the total number of cases), as well as the summed *h D* values <note>In psychophysics, *h* represents the "measure of precision" or sensitivity, and *D* is the difference between stimuli. This product is a statistical value used to determine if a person's sensitivity remains constant across different weight levels.</note> derived from them, are not actually equal—even though, according to <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus.</note>, they should be equal for the various primary weights (*P*) which I varied from 300 to 3000 grams. However, they approach equality more closely the higher the weights increase. I was unable to increase these to even higher primary weights than I did, partly due to the excessive physical effort that would have resulted from continued trials, and particularly because the apparatus itself would have suffered from the strain. Nevertheless, I have demonstrated through special experiments (*Elements*, Vol. I, p. 305 ff.) that the <term>fatigue</term> of the arm caused by weights does not hinder the validity of the law itself any more than the fatigue of the eye by light stimuli does; the outcome is simply the same as if both weights being compared were altered in the same proportion by that fatigue.

Indisputably, the results of these experiments of mine can be used just as well against as for Weber's Law. Against the law, insofar as they show the most striking deviation from it at the lower end; for the law, insofar as they show an equally striking <term>approximation</term> <note>A mathematical term meaning the results get closer and closer to the predicted value without perfectly reaching it.</note> to it as the weights increase. Naturally, my opponents interpret them in the former sense, while I interpret them in the latter, asserting the following considerations.

The fact that the experiments with smaller weights do not align with the law at all only shows that the law, in the realm of...

---

*) In addition to these series of experiments, which were aimed directly at testing Weber's Law under varied circumstances, and the supplementary ones regarding the influence of <term>fatigue</term> (*Elements*, Vol. I, 305), I have large, as yet unpublished series of experiments concerning the influence of the duration of individual <term>lifts</term> <note>The physical act of lifting a weight during a trial.</note> and the time interval between two lifts belonging to a comparison, as well as lifts without a weight difference (*D*). Perhaps I will eventually have the opportunity to make them known. For the present purpose, however, they are of no significant importance.

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of Weber’s Law against the criticisms of Ewald Hering. The previous page discussed how experimental data tends to align more closely with the law as weights increase, though physical limitations prevented testing at even higher magnitudes.</meta>

That weight experiments are subject to a significant **lower deviation** <term>lower deviation</term> <note>In psychophysics, this refers to the tendency for experimental data to deviate from a mathematical law at the lowest levels of stimulus intensity—in this case, very light weights.</note> just as in other fields, proves no more here than in those other fields against the pure law, as such a law would assert itself were it not for the causes of experimental deviations. This is especially true provided that a very specific reason for this lower experimental deviation can be given—one which loses its relative influence the higher the weights increase. Furthermore, other secondary circumstances may potentially be more relatively disruptive for experiments with small weights than with large ones. The specifically identifiable reason lies in the fact that, in addition to the external weights factored into the calculation as the primary weight *P* and comparison weight *P'*, a **weight-moment** <note>The mechanical force or influence exerted by the weight of the limb itself during the act of lifting.</note> of the lifted arm must be added to make the calculation consistent. However, it is partly questionable how much should be added in this regard, and partly how it should be factored in if—as is Weber's view—these experiments only apply to a composite **sensation** <term>sensation</term> <note>The subjective experience resulting from the stimulation of a sense organ.</note> of pressure and heaviness, since the arm’s weight could only increase the heaviness. In any case, one cannot neglect the weight of the arm simply because it belongs to our own body; for everyone feels the weight of their own body when they are to climb a mountain or a staircase, and one lets a tired arm hang because they feel its heaviness.

In general, however, one can say: insofar as it is at all probable that our sensory nerves are naturally "charged" to a certain degree (mostly below the **threshold** <term>threshold</term> <note>The "Schwelle" or threshold is the minimum level of stimulus intensity required to produce a perceptible sensation. Fechner suggests that even without an external weight, there is internal "activity" in the nerves.</note>) with the same **psychophysical activity** that can be awakened or increased in them by external stimuli, then the nerves through which we feel pressure and heaviness will be no exception. Therefore, an increase from this perspective must be added to the external weight, which may vary under different circumstances and in different individuals. Additionally, the weight-moment of the lifting arm itself will naturally be factored in differently, depending on whether the whole arm, or merely the forearm, or even just the hand is employed to lift the external weight.

But even if it may be that the addition of the arm's weight is not sufficient, or not sufficient everywhere, to cover the lower deviation, Hering himself (see above, p. 49) has asserted a whole series of circumstances which cause disturbances in the experiments—

He suggests that as external weights increase, these "internal" factors become mathematically insignificant.</summary>

...could have been, and even if I have not been able to admit that some of these circumstances apply to my experiments, I still cannot claim that others do not truly come into consideration. However, I must always return to the point that, in my view, these factors tend to disturb the law and bring it to an **approximation** <term>approximation</term> <note>In this context, an approximation means the experimental data gets close to the ideal mathematical law but is slightly skewed by physical reality.</note>, rather than being the cause of this approximation as it appears in my experiments with larger *P*. Meanwhile, his experiments—which we will consider next—although conducted using a completely different method, align so well with the general progression of my own experiments as I could ever wish. Hering himself says (p. 35) regarding his experiments with weights from 250 to 3000 grams, they are: 
> "generally in beautiful agreement with the results of Fechner's experiments according to the **method of right and wrong cases** <term>method of right and wrong cases</term> <note>A classic psychophysical procedure where a subject compares two stimuli and the researcher records the percentage of correct judgments; now more commonly called the "method of constant stimuli."</note>; the deviations from **Weber's Law** <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The rule stating that the smallest change in a stimulus that can be felt is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> are quite analogous here and there,"
which indeed they are.

However, I have not conducted any experiments corresponding to Hering's tests with small *P* from 10 to 500 grams—mine do not go below *P* = 300 grams—because experiments with very small *P* did not seem significant for the question of the law. This is due to the relatively large influence of disruptive circumstances and, in particular, because of an **anomaly** <note>Fechner previously noted that at very low weight levels, the human ability to distinguish differences doesn't follow the usual rules, likely due to the "noise" of one's own muscle tension.</note> I noticed in the range of small weights (*Elements* I. 197). Yet when Hering says (p. 33): 
> "that Fechner's experiments do not provide a sufficient overview of the essential facts, because the experiments cannot be sufficiently varied in his method unless one wants to spend years on it,"
I cannot find this statement justified. I have indeed spent years of daily work on it (*Elements* I. 93), and I do not know which essential facts Hering could mean that did not fall under the variations of the experiments I undertook. In any case, the preliminary reports on Hering’s experiments thus far leave open the question of whether they were equally varied regarding the essential points to be considered—chief among which I count the **constant errors** <term>constant errors</term> <note>Systematic biases in an experiment, such as a person always perceiving the second weight lifted as heavier than the first regardless of its actual mass.</note> that must be eliminated.

Let us now take Hering's experiments themselves into consideration, of which Hering gives a preliminary report. In the main, they offer only a new application of the remarks made previously regarding my own experiments; however, a few points therein require special discussion.

<meta>On the previous page, Fechner began his rebuttal to Ewald Hering’s criticisms. He argued that apparent deviations from Weber's Law in weight-lifting experiments are not failures of the law itself, but are caused by the weight of the person's own arm, which must be added to the external weight to get an accurate measurement of the total stimulus.</meta>

There were three experimental series, of which Hering only briefly mentions one (p. 36), noting that the weights fell upon a suitably supported fingertip from a constant, albeit minimal, height. He notes that these results, like the other two series, did not align with **Weber's Law** <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>A fundamental principle in psychophysics stating that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note>; however, he does not provide the specific numerical results for this series at this time.

In another experimental series, "a towel was gripped at both folded ends, while a wooden plate, attached to three cords and carrying the weights, hung in the loop thus formed. The towel, cords, and plate together weighed 250 grams." The results were as follows. In these results, *P* represents the main weight in grams, *D* represents "the smallest added weight in grams clearly recognized by the perceptible increase in weight," ε = *D*/*P* is the **just noticeable relative difference in weight** <term>just noticeable difference</term> <note>Often abbreviated as JND, this is the smallest change in a physical stimulus that can be detected by a human observer.</note> according to the observations, and ε' is the same value calculated by me according to the formula ε' = *D* / (*P* + 2273). In this formula, 2273 grams represents the weight of the arm along with whatever else must be added to the weight *P* as a determining factor.*) To satisfy Weber's Law, Hering demands—without considering any addition to be made to *P*—the approximate agreement of the values of ε for different weights of *P*. This, of course, fails completely. I, however, require the agreement of the values of ε', which is found to be excellently confirmed. The numbers for experiments no. 3, no. 9, and no. 11 are placed in parentheses because the corresponding observed *D* values immediately prove to be erroneous; for by all rights, no. 3 cannot give the same *D* as no. 2, and no. 9 cannot give the same *D* as no. 10, because the *P* values are different. Furthermore, in no. 11, the value suddenly jumps from *D* = 22 to *D* = 28. For these experiment numbers, there is therefore no agreement—

---

*) The value 2273 is calculated from the experiments themselves. By excluding the three most erroneous observations, I combined the remaining eight—the first four and the second four—to obtain two equations from which the above value is found. Namely, if *x* is the value to be added to *P*, and *c* is a constant, then according to my hypothesis for Hering's experiments, we must set: 
*D* / (*P* + *x*) = *c*
consequently *D* = *c P* + *c x* for an individual case, and Σ *D* = *c* Σ *P* + 4 *c x* <note>original: "Σ" is the Greek letter Sigma, used here to represent the mathematical sum of the values.</note> for four cases taken together as a sum. From two such equations, both *c* and *x* can be calculated.

The close agreement of the values for ε' <term>epsilon prime</term> <note>Fechner’s adjusted Weber fraction, which includes the constant weight of the arm in the calculation.</note> across such extraordinarily different values of **P** is all the more surprising because it was not to be expected from the outset, given that the observed **D** progressed only in whole gram increments. <meta>Continuing the discussion from page 193 regarding the "constant error" in Ewald Hering’s weight-lifting experiments. Fechner is defending Weber’s Law by arguing that the weight of the human arm itself must be factored into the mathematical equations as a constant value, which he designates as x.</meta>

...my experiments find this approximate constancy <note>Fechner is referring to the stability of the ratio between the weight and the perceived difference.</note> at much lower values of *x*.

In any case, judging by the degree of agreement shown by the *ε'* <term>epsilon prime</term> <note>Fechner’s adjusted Weber fraction, which includes the constant weight of the arm in the calculation.</note> values here and elsewhere, one can hardly escape the conviction that the following two assumptions, upon which their calculation is based, are correct—insofar as such agreement can only be explained by their combined accuracy: 1) that <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> is fundamentally valid; 2) that the weight *P*, however, due to external causes which co-determine its effect, must be corrected by adding a constant *x* to find the observations in agreement with the law. This holds regardless of what this *x* depends on, as long as one must admit that the observed *D* <term>difference threshold</term> <note>The smallest change in weight a person can actually detect.</note> does not depend on the magnitude of *P* alone, even if the co-determining causes remain partly in the dark.

Hering himself has notably called attention to causes that can have a co-determining effect; on page 35, he even generally conceded that by adding a certain amount—perhaps to be attributed to the weight of the arm*)—to each primary weight *P*, "the experimental series comes into fairly good agreement with the requirement that the primary weight and the added weight should have the same ratio [i.e., with Weber's Law]." However, he provides no calculation for this and leaves the justification for including the arm's weight undecided for the time being. Nevertheless, he does not feel hindered by that concession from using his experimental series to argue against the law. But by what law and what assumptions regarding the co-determining circumstances does he then explain that agreement of the *ε'*? Until a more satisfactory representation of them is given, I believe that they speak, and indeed very decisively, in favor of it.

It must be conceded that 2273 grams might be too much for the mere weight of the arm; but as long as the suspicion of a constant error is not excluded by the more specific communication of the experiments still to be expected, it must be all the more permissible to consider the addition of such an error to the weight of the arm as possible.

---

*) Hering assumes 1750 instead of 2273 grams for this, likely because he included the three faulty experiments that I excluded when calculating *x*.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to Ewald Hering. Hering adds the following remarks:

> "One cannot speak of factoring in the weight of the lifting arm here, for the simple reason that it could only be assumed to be roughly 100 grams if one wanted to bring the series of experiments even somewhat into harmony with Fechner's view. Moreover, anyone who conducts experiments according to the last-mentioned method notices that, when comparing different weights, one directs one's attention solely to the sensations arising at the fingertips; therefore, the so-called **muscle sense** <term>muscle sense</term> <note>A 19th-century term for proprioception, the internal sense of muscle effort and position, which Fechner believed contributed to weight perception.</note> cannot be significantly considered here. Factoring in the arm's weight would only make sense if the comparison were carried out on the basis of this alleged **muscle sense**."

In response to this, I find it necessary to remark regarding this series of experiments that, while it is not without interest concerning the **factual question** of how the values of ε <note>epsilon (ε): the ratio of the difference threshold to the total weight, often called the Weber fraction.</note> behave in the range of small weights and weight increments, it appears irrelevant to the question of our **Law** <note>Weber’s Law: The principle that the "just noticeable difference" between two stimuli is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note>, as mentioned earlier. We know without these experiments that the law does not hold experimentally in the range of small weights; and if that could be used to decide against the law when it is freed from experimental disturbances, my own experiments would have decided against it long ago. Hering’s experiments do, in **fact**, only confirm the occurrence of the **anomaly**—already found in my own experiments—in the transition from P = 300 to P = 500 <note>P refers to the primary weight being lifted, measured in grams.</note>, insofar as the **denominator** of ε decreases instead of increasing, whereas it increases again after experiments with larger P *); and so long as the **fact** of this **anomaly** must be acknowledged without its cause being known, nothing at all can be said about the significance and utilization of **results** obtained at even smaller values of P. The fact that by adding 99.5, or as Hering sets in round numbers,

at which the **denominator** of ε decreases, were set aside, and the calculation was not continued to these.

*) To be sure, in the above experiments by Hering with larger weights, this **anomaly** is not found in the transition from 250 to 500 grams, and in my experiments it also did not occur under all experimental circumstances; but it occurs predominantly according to my experiments, as can be seen from the experimental tables in *Elements of Psychophysics* [Elem.] I. p. 190 ff. <note>original: "p. 190 ff." refers to page 190 and the following pages.</note>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to Ewald Hering regarding the "weight of the arm" and its effect on psychological measurements. ...roughly 100, an approximation—albeit always a very crude one—of the Law <note>Weber's Law: the theory that the "just noticeable difference" between two weights is a constant ratio of the starting weight.</note> is obtained for *P*, even though 100 grams cannot truly represent the weight of the arm. This may be interpreted either to mean that the influence felt with very small weights, upon which the anomaly depends, acts in the opposite sense to the increase of weight caused by the arm; or that, with small weights, only the hand lifting the weight from the wrist was involved, rather than the entire arm.*) The fact that attention during these experiments is directed solely toward the sensations arising at the fingertips does not seem, in itself, to prove anything against the idea that the weight of the arm or hand plays a part in the creation of these sensations.

Following this, I return to my experiments from the perspective of two questions: 1) whether they—like Hering’s experiments with larger weights—can be brought to a consistent *ε'* <note>The corrected Weber fraction, representing the ratio of the threshold of difference to the total weight felt (including the arm).</note> for the various values of *P* by correcting *P* by means of a constant *x*; 2) what the magnitude of this *x* is.

In my *Elements* <term>Elements of Psychophysics</term> <note>Fechner’s foundational 1860 book, *Elemente der Psychophysik*.</note>, I initially refrained from attempting to make the experiments consistent in this way because I believed that, due to the anomaly noted above and the composition of the sensation from both pressure and weight sensations <note>Fechner distinguished between the sensation of a weight pressing on the skin and the sensation of the effort needed to lift it.</note>, such a calculation could lead nowhere (*Elem.* I. 197).**) However, after the calculation of Hering's experiments yielded such a well-fitting result, I subsequently subjected those of my own series (*Elem.* I. 193) which seemed suitable (due to not having too great irregularities) to the calculation. Naturally, I left aside the experiments at *P* = 300 grams because of the anomaly that appears in the transition from there to 500 grams. Accordingly, I find the following as the addition to be made to each *P*, to be designated as *x*, for the following series (*Elem.* I. 193):

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of Ewald Hering's experimental methods. Fechner is arguing that some of Hering’s results, which seem to contradict Weber’s Law, are actually flawed because they fail to account for the physical pressure of the equipment used or the subjective confusion of the test subjects.</meta>

...at the very least must be considered equal to the pressure of a plate. Because this factor has been neglected, the entire experiment loses its evidentiary power.

However, a common objection against both previous experiments seems to me to be that the comparison of differences cannot be maintained independently of the comparison of the absolute weights. To exclude the objection made regarding the low weight of the plate itself, I conducted an experiment with a person who knew nothing of the purpose of these trials. I had them place both hands with the backs on the table. I placed a lead plate of 500 grams on one hand, covering about half of it, and a plate of 1000 grams on the other. I had the person close their eyes, then added a second plate of 500 grams to the first and another 1000 grams to the second. I then asked which **increase** <note>original: "Zuwachs"</note> seemed larger to them. To this, I received the answer that the weight on the second hand had become "much heavier" than on the first—just as it had already been heavier to begin with.

Upon my remark—"But I do not wish to know on which side the weight has become heavier, but rather on which side the *increase* experienced by the previously existing weight is greater"—I received the response: "Yes, but they <insert>the subject</insert> only ever knew how to compare the one weight that had become heavier with the other." I was unable to bring them to concede that the increases could be compared independently of the total weight. It seemed the same to me when I performed the comparison on myself; or rather, it seemed now one way, now the other, depending on the direction of my focus <note>Fechner is noting that subjective expectation can influence the perception of the experiment.</note>, though I do not wish to give much weight to my own judgment here as it might be biased. 

However, based on the outcome of previous trials, I must recommend caution: if one has others repeat the experiment, one must be truly certain that they do not confuse the *sensation of a larger weight* with the *sensation of a larger increase* in their statement—a certainty that, admittedly, seems quite difficult to me to achieve.

Should this certainty nevertheless be achieved, I would most readily admit that a substantial objection against the applicability of the Law <note>Weber's Law: the principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus.</note> to sensations of weight <term>weight sensations</term> would arise from it. In any case, this experimental method suffers from a complication that is difficult to eliminate, whereas Hering’s other experiments involving **just noticeable differences** <term>just noticeable differences</term> <note>original: "eben merkliche Unterschiede," the smallest detectable difference between two stimuli.</note> do not suffer from this same issue.

<summary>Fechner describes a control experiment where a subject fails to distinguish between the total weight felt and the incremental increase added to it. He uses this to argue that Hering’s experimental objections to Weber's Law may be based on a subjective confusion between absolute and relative weight sensations.</summary>

Also, the series of trials IX. in **Elements [of Psychophysics] Vol. I. 196**, which were conducted only with $P = 2000$ and $P = 3000$, one-handed and two-handed, with $D = 0.04 P$ and $0.08 P$, provide an opportunity to calculate $x$; however, one must not stick to the individual series, as each offers only two data points for calculation, and $x$ is small compared to such large values of $P$. If one adheres to the sums for all series, according to which

->The $\Sigma h D$ at $P = 2000$ is 31186<-
->The » » » at $P = 3000$ is 32938<-

then one finds $x = 397.8$, which comes quite close to the values mentioned above. <note>In Fechner's formulas, $P$ usually represents the initial weight and $x$ often represents a constant related to the internal weight of the limb or experimental resistance.</note>

It remains somewhat mysterious which internal reasons determine the differentiation of weight magnitudes at all, and whether, in Weber's sense <note>Refers to Ernst Heinrich Weber, whose law states that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus</note>, it really depends on two different factors. **Bernhardt** writes (in the **Archive for Psychiatry III. 1872 p. 632**):

> "In patients who had lost their skin sensation <term>skin sensation: German 'Hautgefühl', the tactile sense of the skin</term> to the highest degree, who could report nothing about passively executed movements, had no conception of the position of their limbs, and felt nothing from the strongest induction currents <term>induction currents: electrical pulses used to stimulate nerves or muscles</term> which vigorously set their muscles into contraction, I frequently found the so-called sense of force <term>sense of force: German 'Kraftsinn', the ability to perceive muscular effort or resistance</term> (specifically in two such patients), the ability to distinguish weights, to be relatively well preserved: a result which **Leyden** also reached in **his Investigation on Muscle Sense and Ataxia**."

The Leyden experiments (in **Virchow's Archive XLVII. p. 327**) are indeed decisive in showing that "patients who had entirely or almost entirely lost sensitivity in the muscles of the lower leg [insofar as they had no or almost no sensation from the strongest induced currents according to previous tests] distinguished the weight of different weights with the same sharpness as healthy people." The experiments on this were, in fact, conducted by means of a specific apparatus attached to the foot, in comparison with several healthy individuals using the method of just noticeable differences <term>just noticeable differences: the smallest detectable difference between two stimuli</term>, and the values found are mostly remarkably close in agreement. The skin sensitivity was also partly greatly and partly only moderately diminished in the test subjects.

## XIX. von Zahn’s Experiments on Color Combinations.

Since von Zahn’s experiments—which were mentioned on page 172 in the interest of an important question—are contained in a little-known journal (*Proceedings of the Leipzig Natural Research Society* 1874 no. 3 p. 25 ff.), but may still require further discussion regarding this question, I will share them here. I will first use the author's own words, followed by a schematic representation to facilitate understanding, where *a* represents the amplitude <note>The maximum extent of a vibration or oscillation, measured from the position of equilibrium; in light, this corresponds to intensity or brightness.</note> and *n* represents the vibration frequency, upon which the color depends.

The author’s intention was to use experiments based on the method of just noticeable (or disappearing) differences to determine whether, when colored light is added to reinforce a spot on a differently colored background, this surplus (referred to by the author as the "measuring surplus") may be of any arbitrary color if <term>Weber's Law</term> <note>The principle that the change in a stimulus that will be just noticeable is a constant ratio of the original stimulus.</note> is to be confirmed upon changing its strength (which depends on the amplitude *a*).

> "At a suitable distance from the objective of a <term>Zöllner astrophotometer</term> <note>A 19th-century instrument used to measure the brightness of celestial bodies by comparing them to a standard light source.</note>, a sufficiently constant light source (a petroleum lamp) was placed. This lamp uniformly illuminates the focal plane of the telescope, in which the side tube projects the image of an illuminated circular opening by reflection off a glass plate. — One then had to use the front Nicol prism <note>A Nicol prism is an optical device made of calcite used to create and analyze polarized light.</note> in the side tube to dim the image of the small disk until it just ceases to be visible; the degree of attenuation can then be determined from the angle of rotation in the known manner. — Whether the light surplus used as a measure acted only through its (physiological) intensity, and not through the quality of its coloring, had to be revealed as follows. After placing, for example, a blue glass and then a yellow glass in front of the telescope, one sees a small disk on a blue or yellow background, respectively, glowing in whatever color the <term>colorimeter device</term> (a quartz plate with a Nicol prism) has just assigned to it. In both cases, the disk is then dimmed until it just disappears, and the relative degree of its light intensity is determined. To the ratio of these two intensities..."

<summary>This page introduces von Zahn’s 1874 experiments on color perception, specifically testing if Weber's Law applies to the intensity of colored light when superimposed on different colored backgrounds. It describes the use of a Zöllner astrophotometer and Nicol prisms to measure the threshold at which a colored disk becomes invisible against a background.</summary>

...could have been, and even if I could not admit that some of these circumstances applied to my experiments, I still cannot claim that others do not truly come into consideration. However, I must always return to the point that, in my view, these circumstances are more likely to disturb the law and reduce it to a mere approximation, rather than being the *cause* of the approximation itself, as it appears in the larger values of *P* <note>In psychophysics, *P* (from the German *Pfund* or Latin *pondus*) typically represents the mass or magnitude of the initial weight or stimulus being tested.</note> in my experiments. Hering holds a different view, and this contradiction between us will remain; meanwhile, his experiments, which we will consider next, although conducted using a completely different method, agree so well with the general course of my experiments as I could ever wish. They only differ in that the addition to the external weights—which in my view are still necessary to correct the results—is significantly larger in his findings than what will be found in mine based on the corresponding calculations below.

Hering himself says (p. 33) regarding his experiments with weights from 250 to 3000 grams, they are "generally in beautiful agreement with the results of Fechner's experiments using the **method of right and wrong cases**; <term>the deviations from Weber's Law</term> <note>Weber's Law states that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant proportion of the original stimulus. "Deviations" refer to cases where this ratio does not stay constant.</note> are quite analogous here and there," which indeed they are.

However, I have not performed any experiments corresponding to Hering's trials with small *P* values of 10 to 500 grams—mine do not go below *P* = 300 grams—because experiments with very small *P* did not seem important to me for the question of the law. This is due to the relatively large influence of disturbing circumstances and, in particular, an anomaly I noticed in the range of small weights (*Elem. I. 197* <note>A reference to Fechner's foundational work, *Elemente der Psychophysik* (Elements of Psychophysics), Volume 1.</note>). Yet, when Hering says (p. 33) "that Fechner's experiments do not provide a sufficient overview of the essential facts because the experiments in his method cannot be varied enough unless one wants to spend years on them," I cannot find this statement justified. I have indeed spent years of daily work on it (*Elem. I. 93*), and I do not know which essential facts Hering could mean that were not subject to the variations I performed in the experiments. In any case, the preliminary reports on Hering’s experiments published so far leave open the question of whether his own experiments were equally varied with regard to the essential points to be considered, among which I count, above all, the <term>constant errors</term> <note>In 19th-century psychology, "constant errors" referred to systematic biases in perception, such as being more sensitive to weights held in the right hand versus the left.</note> that must be eliminated.

If we now take Hering's experiments themselves into consideration, of which Hering gives a preliminary report, they essentially represent only a new application of the remarks made previously regarding my experiments; however, a few points deserve special discussion.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of Ewald Hering’s experiments. On the previous page, Fechner noted that while Hering’s results seem to deviate from Weber’s Law, they actually align with Fechner's own predictions if certain physiological factors are taken into account.</meta>

There were three series of experiments, one of which Hering mentions only briefly (p. 36). In this series, weights were dropped onto a suitably supported fingertip from a constant, though minimal, height. He notes that these results, like the other two series, did not align with **Weber’s Law**; however, he does not provide the specific numerical data for them at this time.

In another series of experiments, "a towel was held by its two ends brought together, while a wooden plate, attached by three cords and carrying the weights, hung in the loop thus formed. The towel, cords, and plate together weighed 250 grams." The results were as follows. In these results, *P* represents the <term>main weight</term> <note>The initial weight or stimulus magnitude.</note> in grams, *D* represents "the smallest <term>additional weight</term> <note>Also known as the Just Noticeable Difference (JND), the smallest change a person can detect.</note> in grams recognized with certainty as a perceptible increase in weight," and $\epsilon = \frac{D}{P}$ represents the <term>just noticeable relative weight difference</term> according to the observations. Finally, $\epsilon'$ represents the same value as calculated by me using the formula $\epsilon' = \frac{D}{P + 2273}$, where 2273 grams represents the **arm-weight** along with whatever else must be added to the weight *P* as a determining factor.*) 

Hering maintains that for Weber’s Law to be valid—without considering any addition to be made to *P*—there must be an approximate agreement of the $\epsilon$ values for the various weights of *P*. In this case, the law certainly fails. I, however, maintain that the agreement should be found in the $\epsilon'$ values, which is indeed found to be excellently confirmed. The numbers for experiments no. 3, no. 9, and no. 11 are placed in parentheses because the corresponding observed *D* values prove to be immediately erroneous. This is because, by rights, no. 3 cannot give the same *D* as no. 2, nor can no. 9 give the same as no. 10, since the weights of *P* are different; furthermore, in no. 11, the value suddenly drops from *D* = 22 to *D* = 28. For these experiment numbers, there is therefore no agreement—

---

*) The value 2273 is calculated from the experiments themselves. By excluding the three most erroneous observations and using the remaining eight, I combined the first four and the second four observations to obtain two equations from which the above value is found. Namely, let *x* be the value to be added to *P*, and *c* be a constant. According to my hypothesis for Hering's experiments, we must set $\frac{D}{P + x} = c$; therefore, *D* = *c P* + *c x* for an individual case, and $\Sigma D = c \Sigma P + 4 c x$ for four cases taken together as a sum. From two such equations, both *c* and *x* can be calculated.

my attempts to find this approximate constancy again at much lower values of *x*.

In any case, given the degree of agreement shown by the $\varepsilon'$ <note>Epsilon prime, the adjusted ratio of the difference threshold to the weight</note> values here and there, one can hardly escape the conviction that the following two assumptions, upon which their calculation is based, are correct—insofar as their combined accuracy is the only way to explain such an agreement: 1) that <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>The principle that the perceived change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of the stimulus</note> fundamentally holds true; 2) that the weight *P* must be corrected by adding a constant *x* due to external causes that help determine its effect. This correction is necessary to bring the observations into alignment with the law, regardless of what this *x* depends on, provided one admits that the observed *D* <note>The difference threshold or "just noticeable difference"</note> does not depend on the magnitude of *P* alone, and that the contributing causes are still partly shrouded in mystery.

Hering himself has, as noted, drawn attention to causes that may have a contributing effect; he even (p. 35) admitted in general that by adding a value\*)—perhaps to be attributed to the <term>arm weight</term>—to each main weight *P*, "the experimental series comes into fairly good agreement with the requirement that the main weight and the additional weight should have the same ratio [i.e., with Weber’s Law]." However, he provides no calculation for this and leaves the justification for including the arm weight undecided for the time being; nevertheless, he does not find himself hindered by that admission from using his experimental series as an argument against the law. But by what law and what assumptions regarding the contributing circumstances does he then explain that agreement of the $\varepsilon'$? Until a more satisfactory representation of them is given, I maintain that these values speak—and quite decisively—in favor of the law.

It must be admitted that 2273 grams might be too much for the mere weight of the arm; but as long as the suspicion of a <term>constant error</term> <note>A systematic error that consistently skews results in one direction</note> is not excluded by the more specific communication of the experiments still to be expected, it must be all the more permissible to consider the addition of such a value to the arm weight as possible.

---

\*) Hering assumes 1750 instead of 2273 grams for this, probably because he included the three erroneous experiments that I excluded when calculating *x*.

<meta>This page continues the technical debate between Fechner and his contemporary, Ewald Hering, regarding the validity of Weber’s Law at low weight levels. Fechner is addressing Hering’s critique that the weight of the arm should not be a factor in calculating sensory thresholds.</meta>

Hering adds the following remarks:

> “There could be no talk of including the weight of the lifting arm here, if only because it could only be assumed to be approximately 100 grams, provided one wanted to bring the series of experiments even somewhat into harmony with Fechner’s conception. Moreover, everyone who conducts experiments according to the last-mentioned method notices that, when comparing different weights, one directs one's attention solely to the sensations arising at the fingertips; therefore, the so-called <term>muscle sense</term> <note>The perception of movement and effort in the muscles, later known as proprioception</note> cannot significantly come into consideration here. Including the weight of the arm would only make sense if the comparison were carried out on the basis of this alleged muscle sense.”

In response, I find myself noting that while this series of experiments is not without interest regarding the factual question of how the values of <term>$\epsilon$ (epsilon)</term> <note>The ratio of the difference threshold to the total weight, used to measure sensory sensitivity</note> behave in the range of small weights and <term>weight increments</term>, it appears irrelevant to the question of our law, as previously mentioned. We know without these experiments that the law does not hold experimentally in the range of small weights; if that were enough to decide against the law when it is freed from experimental disturbances, my own experiments would have already decided against it. Hering’s experiments actually only confirm the occurrence of the anomaly—already found in my own experiments—in the transition from $P = 300$ to $P = 500$, insofar as the denominator of $\epsilon$ falls instead of rising, whereas it rises again after experiments with a larger $P$ <note>The letter $P$ represents the initial weight or stimulus intensity</note>\*); and as long as the fact of this anomaly must be acknowledged, but its cause is unknown, nothing at all can be said about the meaning and utility of results obtained at even smaller values of $P$. The fact that by adding 99.5, or as Hering sets it in round numbers,

...approximately *100*, for *P* <note>The external weight being lifted, from the Latin *pondus*</note> only a very crude approximation of the law will be obtained, though *100* grams cannot truly represent the weight of the arm. This might be interpreted either to mean that the influence noticeable at very small weights, upon which the anomaly depends, acts in the opposite sense than the increase of weight by the arm, or that with small weights, it was rather just the hand—lifting the weight from the wrist—that was involved instead of the whole arm.*) The fact that one directs one's attention during these experiments solely to the sensations arising at the fingertips seems to me in itself to prove nothing against the idea that the weight of the arm or hand plays a part in the creation of these sensations.

Based on this, I return to my experiments from the perspective of two questions: 1) whether they, like Hering's with the larger weights, can be brought to a consistent $\epsilon'$ <note>Epsilon prime: the corrected Weber fraction, representing the ratio of the threshold of difference to the total weight (external + internal)</note> for the various values of *P* by correcting *P* using a constant *x*; 2) what the magnitude of this *x* is.

In my *Elements* <note>Fechner’s foundational 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik*</note>, I initially refrained from attempting to make the experiments consistent in this way, because I believed that, due to the anomaly noted above and the composition of the sensation from both pressure and weight sensation, such a calculation could lead to nothing (*Elements* Vol. I, p. 197).**) However, since the calculation of Hering's experiments yielded such a well-fitting result, I have subsequently subjected those of my series (*Elements* Vol. I, p. 193) which seemed suitable due to not having excessive irregularities to the calculation; naturally, however, I set aside the experiments at *P* = 300 grams because of the anomaly that appears in the transition from there to 500 grams.
