*) The calculation of $x$ was conducted as follows: If I designate the five experiments from each series included in the calculation (from $P = 500$ upward) as 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 respectively, then to determine $x$, I combine 1 and 2 in summed values with 3, 4, 5; a second time I combine 1, 2, 3 with 4, 5, and take the average of both determinations.

**) This correction is justified as follows: According to the principle of the method, all values of $h D$ derived from $\frac{r}{n}$ <note>The ratio of right/correct answers ($r$) to the total number of trials ($n$)</note> must turn out equal if Weber's Law holds and no addition of $x$ needs to be made to the applied weights $P$. If, however, such an addition must be made, then every $D$ and therefore $h D$ (taken as proportional to $P$) must be modified by the ratio of $P + x : P$ in order to expect constant values of $h D \cdot \frac{P + x}{P}$ across the different values of $P$.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical analysis of experimental weights and the internal constant "x" (representing the arm's own weight). It transitions from statistical calculations to a physiological discussion on whether the "sense of force" is independent of skin and muscle sensitivity.</meta>

Experimental series IX in *Elements [of Psychophysics] Vol. I, p. 196*, which were conducted solely with *P* = 2000 and *P* = 3000 <note>*P* represents the external weight in grams</note>, one-handed and two-handed, with *D* = 0.04 *P* and 0.08 *P* <note>*D* represents the difference between two weights being compared</note>, also provide an opportunity for the calculation of *x* <note>The constant internal weight of the limb</note>; however, one must not strictly rely on the individual series, as each offers only two data points for calculation, and *x* is small compared to such large values of *P*. If one looks at the sums for all series, according to which:

->$\sum h D$ <note>The sum of the precision measure multiplied by the weight difference</note> at *P* = 2000 is 31186<-
->"  "  " at *P* = 3000 is 32938<-

then one finds *x* = 397.8, which comes quite close to the values mentioned above.

It remains somewhat mysterious upon which internal grounds the discrimination of weight magnitudes actually depends, and whether—in **Weber's** <note>Ernst Heinrich Weber (1795–1878), the physician whose work on sensation thresholds led to Weber’s Law</note> sense—it must truly be made dependent on two different causes. **Bernhardt** writes (in the *Archive for Psychiatry* III. 1872 p. 632):

> "In patients who had lost their skin sensitivity to the highest degree, who knew nothing to report regarding movements carried out passively <note>Passive movements: movements performed on a patient's limb by an external force without the patient's own muscle contraction</note>, had no idea about the position of their limbs, and felt nothing from the strongest induction currents <note>Electrical stimulation used to trigger muscle contractions</note> which set their muscles into energetic contraction, I frequently found the so-called **sense of force** (specifically in two such patients), the ability to distinguish weights, to be **relatively well preserved**: a result which **Leyden** also reached in his investigations on muscle sense and ataxia <note>Ataxia: a lack of muscle control or coordination of voluntary movements</note>."

Leyden's experiments (in **Virchow’s Archive XLVII. p. 327**) are indeed decisive in proving that "patients who had entirely or almost entirely lost sensitivity in the muscles of the lower leg [insofar as they had no or almost no sensation from the strongest induced currents during previous tests], distinguished the heaviness of different weights with the same sharpness as healthy individuals." Specifically, the experiments regarding this were conducted using a dedicated apparatus attached to the foot, compared with several healthy subjects according to the method of just noticeable differences; the values found are, for the most part, remarkably consistent. Furthermore, the skin sensitivity in the test subjects was partly severely and partly only moderately diminished.

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the mathematical constant "x," representing the internal weight or resistance of the limb. It moves from specific statistical data to a broader theoretical explanation of why weight-lifting experiments often deviate from mathematical laws at lower values.</meta>

The fact that weight experiments are subject to a significant lower deviation <note>A "lower deviation" refers to experimental results failing to match mathematical predictions at very small stimulus values</note>, just as in other fields, does not prove any more here than elsewhere against the pure law as it would manifest without the causes of experimental error. This holds true insofar as a very specific reason can be given for this lower experimental deviation—a reason that loses its relative influence the higher the weights increase. Furthermore, other incidental circumstances may potentially be more relatively disruptive for experiments with small weights than with large ones.

The specific, identifiable reason is that one must add a <term>weight-moment</term> <note>Weight-moment refers to the effective force exerted by the weight of the limb itself as it moves</note> of the arm being lifted to the external weights—accounted for as the primary weight *P* and the comparison weight *P'*—in order to find a consistent calculation. However, it is partly a question of how much should be added in this regard, and partly how it should be added if, as is **Weber's** <note>Ernst Heinrich Weber (1795–1878), the physician whose research into the "just noticeable difference" founded the field of psychophysics</note> view, these experiments only apply to a composite sensation of pressure and weight, since the weight of the arm could only increase the sensation of heaviness.

In any case, one cannot neglect the weight of the arm simply because it belongs to our own body; after all, everyone feels the weight of their own body when they must climb a mountain or a staircase, and lets a tired arm hang because they feel its weight. Generally, however, one can say: if it is at all probable that our sensory nerves are naturally "charged" to a certain degree (mostly below the **threshold** <note>The "threshold" is the point at which a stimulus is strong enough to be consciously perceived</note>) with the same <term>psychophysical activity</term> that can be awakened or increased by external stimuli, then the nerves through which we perceive pressure and weight will be no exception. Therefore, from this perspective, an addition must be added to the external weight, which may vary under different circumstances and for different people. Also, the weight-moment of the lifting arm itself will naturally be factored into the calculation in different ways, depending on whether the entire arm, just the forearm, or even just the hand is used to lift the external weight.

But even if the addition of the arm's weight does not, or does not always, suffice to account for the lower deviation, **Hering** <note>Ewald Hering (1834–1918), a physiologist known for his work on color vision and perception, who often challenged the specific mathematical formulations of Weber and Fechner</note> himself (see above p. 49) has asserted a whole series of circumstances through which disturbances in the experiments are caused—

<summary>This page explains why psychological laws of weight perception seem to fail at low weights, arguing that the physical weight of the human arm itself acts as a constant internal stimulus that must be added to the external weights in any calculation.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, weight perception, arm weight, weight-moment, psychophysical activity, sensory threshold, Ewald Hering, experimental deviation</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical sensation of weight (discussed on page 200) to the visual perception of light and color, testing if the Weber-Fechner law applies universally across different senses.</meta>

...would have to be equal to that of the various colored ray bundles entering the telescope; the same ratio would always have to result regardless of the color of the small disc, if the hypothesis of the unrestricted validity of the <term>Weber-Fechner Law</term> <note>The Weber-Fechner law describes the relationship between the physical intensity of a stimulus and its perceived intensity; it suggests that "just noticeable differences" are proportional to the initial stimulus.</note> were to lead to determinations of *objective value*.

My experiments have now yielded the *opposite*. By comparing, for instance, a blue and a yellow illuminated field of view through a yellow luminous disc, I obtained a different ratio for the *intensities* than I did through a blue disc. For example, in one case:

The former, therefore, is four times the latter.

| | | |
|:---|:---|:---|
| Ratio of Green to Red, measured by | Green : | 8.96 |
| » » » » » » » | Red : | 3.08 |

One recognizes from these significant differences that the *color quality* of the *luminous excess* <note>Luminous excess (original: *Lichtüberschuss*) refers to the additional amount of light required for a viewer to perceive a change in brightness against a colored background.</note> has the greatest influence on its *noticeability*, and thus an *objective definition* of qualitatively different <term>light quanta</term> <note>Units or quantities of light.</note> through the *Fechner law* is not possible.

The totality of the experiments allowed me to clearly recognize that those differences are based on a *one-sided preference* for certain *color contrasts*. For instance, one recognizes even slight traces of yellow on a blue ground and both colors do not merge into a single impression, whereas blue on yellow is soon no longer recognized as a distinct *color nuance*. Quite the same applies to green and red.

I carried out further determinations by removing the <term>colorimeter</term> <note>An instrument used to measure the intensity and quality of color.</note> from the side tube and coloring the disc using pieces of glass placed in front of the <term>objective</term> <note>The primary lens of the telescope that collects light.</note> of the telescope. Here, the same differences emerged, in some cases even more decisively. For example:

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Weber-Fechner Law as applied to light intensity, presenting data on how different colored filters yield wildly inconsistent ratios when measuring the same light sources.</meta>

From other determinations, I still cite:

| | | | | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ratio | Blue | to | Yellow , | measured | by | **Red :** | **3.56** |
| » | » | » | » | » | » | **Yellow :** | **2.59** |
| » | » | » | » | » | » | **White\*) :** | **2.42** |
| » | » | » | » | » | » | **Blue :** | **1.70** |
| » | » | » | » | » | » | **Green :** | **1.09** |

My experiments thus leave it beyond doubt that when using different colored light excesses, no comparable measure of given light sources is provided, and the concept of <term>light intensity</term> <note>The perceived brightness or strength of a light source.</note> therefore remains completely undetermined in the physiological sense. One might ask, however, whether—renouncing an objective definition—one could at least arrive at practically comparable results if all measurements were referred to a normal color (lamp light, sodium flame, and the like). This assumption is also inadmissible. — Specifically, by placing various <term>smoked glasses</term> <note>Rauchgläser: darkened or tinted glass used to reduce the intensity of light entering an instrument without significantly altering its color.</note> in front of the telescope of the <term>photometer</term> <note>An instrument used to measure the intensity of light or to compare the relative intensity of different light sources.</note>, I obtained (as is immediately evident) pairs of light bundles through the exchange of the colored glasses whose intensities always had the same ratio, but differed in their absolute magnitude. The ratio calculated from the experiments according to <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>Also known as the Weber-Fechner law; it suggests that the smallest perceptible change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of that stimulus.</note> then proved to be highly dependent on the absolute quantity; for example, the ratio of Green to Red measured by green was 2.4, 3.8, 7.0 times as large as the same ratio measured by red. The increase in these numbers corresponds to the decrease in light strength, as I had expected from the outset, since colors lose <term>saturation</term> <note>The purity or vividness of a color; as light becomes more intense, colors often appear more "washed out" or white.</note> with increasing intensity. My experiments do not yet permit the determination of the more precise connection between the mentioned changes; also, results of general validity in this regard could apparently only be achieved using <term>spectral colors</term> <note>Colors produced by a single wavelength of light, such as those seen in a rainbow.</note>, which presupposes a specially constructed apparatus, and given the relative inaccuracy\*\*) of the method used, very extensive and frequently repeated series of observations would be required.

\*) "Lamp light."
\*\*) "This was much greater than the existing data led me to expect."

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Weber-Fechner Law as applied to light intensity, following a table that demonstrated how different colored filters result in inconsistent measurements of the same light source.</meta>

### The following is the scheme of the first experimental method.

1) First, onto a blue background of unknown intensity *B*, and another time onto a yellow background of unknown intensity *G*, a **y e l l o w** small light disk <note>original: "Lichtscheibchen"; a small, circular patch of light used in optical experiments to test the limits of human perception.</note> of unknown intensity *g* is projected. Its intensity is added to that of the background, so that in the first case the intensity ratio between the bare background and the background reinforced by the disk is $\frac{B}{B + g}$, and in the second case $\frac{G}{G + g}$. The *g* of the disk is then weakened (without changing its *n* <note>Likely referring to the refractive index or a specific optical constant.</note>) in a known ratio until the disk disappears into the background for the eye. In the first case, *g* is reduced to *mg*, and in the second case to *m'g*; thus the ratio is reduced:

->from $\frac{B}{B + g}$ to $\frac{B}{B + mg}$, and from $\frac{G}{G + g}$ to $\frac{G}{G + m'g}$<-

2) Next, once onto a blue background of the previous intensity *B*, and another time onto a yellow background of intensity *G*, a **blue** disk of intensity *b* is projected and the same procedure is followed as before. In the first case *b* is reduced to $\mu b$, and in the second case to $\mu'b$; thus the ratio is reduced:

->from $\frac{B}{B + b}$ to $\frac{B}{B + \mu b}$, and from $\frac{G}{G + b}$ to $\frac{G}{G + \mu'b}$<-

Now, according to <term>Weber’s Law</term> <note>A principle of psychology stating that the smallest perceivable change in a stimulus is a constant proportion of the original stimulus.</note>, all ratios where the disappearance occurs must be equal; therefore:

->$\frac{B}{B + mg} = \frac{G}{G + m'g} = \frac{B}{B + \mu b} = \frac{G}{G + \mu'b} \qquad (1)$<-

This gives as the condition for Weber’s Law:

->$\frac{G}{B} = \frac{G + m'g}{B + mg}$ measured by yellow,<-

->$\frac{G}{B} = \frac{G + \mu'b}{B + \mu b}$ measured by blue.<-

According to this:

->$\frac{m'}{m} = \frac{\mu'}{\mu}$<-

whereas <term>v. Zahn</term> <note>Wilhelm von Zahn (1845–1904), a German physicist who specialized in the study of light and color perception.</note> found:

->$\frac{m'}{m} = 6.44, \quad \frac{\mu'}{\mu} = 1.59,$<-

consequently, a strong inequality instead of equality.

## XX. On the Question of the Fundamental Measure of Psychophysical Activity.

If the intensity of sensation is to be placed in functional dependence on the intensity of psychophysical activity\*), the question naturally arises as to what one looks for as the measure of the <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>The physical or physiological processes (such as neural firing) that correspond to a mental sensation.</note> itself. Now, this activity is in any case

---

\*) The view of Bernstein, that sensation should be made dependent on the disappearance rather than the existence of <term>living force</term> <note>original: "lebendiger Kraft"; a 19th-century term for kinetic energy, here used to describe the energy of neural processes.</note>, is considered on p. 147.

<summary>The author concludes a technical critique of optical experiments before transitioning into a theoretical discussion on how to measure the physical energy (psychophysical activity) that produces mental sensations.</summary>
<keywords>difference threshold, ratio threshold, Weber's Law, psychophysical activity, living force, Bernstein, sensation intensity</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's introductory heading "XX. On the Question of the Fundamental Measure of Ps...", completing the term as "Psychophysical Activity." The author is moving from experimental critique to a theoretical discussion on how to mathematically model the physical basis of sensation.</meta>

to be understood as a process of motion, and thus the most natural approach is to seek the measure of <term>psychophysical activity</term> <note>The physical process in the brain or nervous system that corresponds to a mental sensation.</note> in the <term>living force</term> <note>original: "lebendigen Kraft"; a historical term used in the 19th century for kinetic energy, calculated as $mv^2$.</note>, the product of mass *m* and the square (or half-square) of the velocity *v* developed within the process of motion; however, the observation immediately arises that if our psychophysical formulas are valid, both the mass and the squared power of the velocity are eliminated from them. For if we substitute $mv^2$ for *β* and $mV^2$ as the <term>threshold value</term> <note>The minimum intensity of a stimulus required to produce a sensation.</note> of $mv^2$ for *b* in the fundamental and measurement formulas, then $\partial\beta/\beta$ transforms into $2mv\partial v/mv^2 = 2\partial v/v$, and $\log \beta/b$ into $2 \log v/V$; the coefficient 2, however, merges with the constant coefficient *K* or *k*. Furthermore, the sign of the velocity—whether it moves in one direction or the opposite—is eliminated, as the same sign appears in both the numerator and denominator of our expressions, and thus only the absolute value of the velocity is considered. Therefore, the measure of psychophysical activity could also simply be sought in the simple velocity developed therein, and thus *v* could be substituted into the formulas according to its absolute value for *β*.

Fundamentally, in the mathematical treatment of the <term>measure of sensation</term> <note>original: "Empfindungsmasses"; Fechner's method for quantifying the intensity of mental experiences.</note>, one will have to return to the velocity at a given point at a given moment and make the element of a sensation dependent upon this as the measure of elementary psychophysical activity; however, since in reality we can observe neither elementary velocities nor elementary sensations, we must, for empirical purposes, determine the sum of the velocities developed over a finite time at a given number of points through <term>integration</term> <note>The mathematical process of finding a total sum, such as the total energy over a period of time.</note> from the elementary velocities, and make the sensation developed during that same time through those same points functionally dependent on this, as I have sought to show using the example of a simple <term>oscillation</term> <note>original: "Schwingung"; a repetitive back-and-forth motion, like a sound wave or light wave.</note> in the *Elements of Psychophysics*, Vol. II, Chapter 32.

From another perspective, it is known (*Elements* II, 166) that the living force of oscillations occurring during a given time—that is, the sum $mv^2$—is proportional to $a^2n^2$, where *a*

the amplitude, and $n$ is the <term>frequency</term> <note>original: "Schwingungszahl"; literally the number of oscillations.</note>. Accordingly, when applying our formulas to <term>psychophysical oscillations</term> <note>The theoretical vibrations of particles in the brain or nervous system that Fechner believed corresponded to mental experiences.</note>, one could set $\beta = a^{2} n^{2}$ or—if the power is eliminated—set it as $= an$, provided that the elementary measure of psychophysical activity is truly to be sought in the simple measure of velocity. This would be the case if this assumption sufficiently corresponds to the facts of observation.

However, there stands another, and perhaps inherently more probable, assumption (Elements II. 223. 235) against the possibility of the first: that instead of absolute velocities, <term>changes in velocity</term> <note>In modern physics, this is known as acceleration.</note>—which I also call velocities of the second order and designate briefly as $v$—should be substituted for $\beta$ in our formulas as the elementary measure of psychophysical activity. This would allow the elementary measure of sensation to depend on these changes, and from there, through summation (<term>integration</term>), one could proceed to the measure of an observable sensation linked to vibrations. According to the "velocity hypothesis," to speak briefly, sensation would even be linked to psychophysical movements of constant velocity above the <term>threshold</term>; according to the "change-in-velocity hypothesis," however, sensation would only be linked to movements insofar as they include changes in velocity—a condition which oscillatory movements generally satisfy perfectly.\*) Insofar as we assume that psychophysical movements truly have the nature of oscillations, it is indifferent for the measurement ratios of the sensations dependent upon them whether we set the <term>living force</term> <note>original: "lebendige Kraft"; a historical term for kinetic energy ($mv^2$).</note> $m v^{2}$, simple velocity $v$, or the changes in velocity $v$ for $\beta$. This remains true as long as the speed of the oscillations $n$—meaning the <term>pitch</term> for sound or <term>color</term> for light—remains constant and the form of the oscillations remains the same. Consequently, changes in the intensity of the sensation would depend merely on changes

Indeed, in *Elements II. § 18. 223*, I have shown that according to the hypothesis of *v*, the intensity of a sensation tied to the simplest form of oscillation must be assumed proportional to $\log an$; whereas according to the hypothesis of *$\mathfrak{v}$*, it is proportional to $\log an^2$. In our formulas for sensations of finite magnitude linked to the simplest oscillations, *an* must be substituted for $\beta$ according to the first hypothesis, and $an^2$ according to the second.

Namely, like this:

Let us first assume that sensation depends on *v*, and suppose that every value of *v* exceeding a certain <term>threshold value</term> <note>original: "Schwellenwerth"; the minimum intensity required for a stimulus to be consciously perceived.</note> *b* gives a positive contribution to the sensation tied to a complete, simple, rectilinear oscillation. Then the element of sensation $\gamma \partial t$, which occurs during the time element $\partial t$, will be given—based on the <term>measure formula</term> <note>original: "Massformel"; Fechner's law, which states that sensation is proportional to the logarithm of the stimulus intensity.</note>—by

->$\gamma \partial t = k \log \frac{v}{b} \cdot \partial t$<-

But according to a well-known formula, *v* is

->$= \frac{2 \pi a}{\tau} \sin \frac{2 \pi t}{\tau}$<-

where *a* is the amplitude, $\tau$ the <term>period of oscillation</term> <note>original: "Schwingungsdauer"; the time it takes for one complete cycle.</note>, *t* the time elapsed since the beginning of the oscillation, and $\pi$ is <term>Ludolf's number</term> <note>original: "Ludolfsche Zahl"; an older name for the mathematical constant pi ($\pi \approx 3.14159$).</note> or half the circumference of a circle where the radius $= 1$.

-> $S_\tau = k \int_{0}^{\tau} \log \left( \frac{2 \pi a}{\mathfrak{b} \tau} \sin \frac{2 \pi t}{\tau} \right) \partial t$ <-

-> $= k (P + Q)$ <-

accordingly, by means of the same treatment,

-> $S_\tau = k \tau \log \frac{4 \pi^2 a}{\mathfrak{b} \tau^2}$ <-

Since $\tau$ <note>The period of one complete oscillation.</note> is inversely proportional to the frequency *n*, one can substitute $\frac{1}{n}$ for $\tau$ in these formulas.

This latter possibility does not occur to me as likely, but it would nonetheless allow Weber's Law to remain valid for the case of constant *n* with varying *a*.

### XXI. Summary.

1) To my knowledge, at least until now, no explicit fundamental objection has been raised against the principle I established in the *Elements of Psychophysics* <note>original: "Elem. der Psychophysik"</note> regarding the **measurement of sensation** <term>measurement of sensation: the attempt to quantify mental experiences based on their physical causes</term> based on the functional dependence of sensation on the stimulus. However, objections begin with the question of which law of dependence should be used as a basis—since the measurement itself must change according to the nature of that law—and continue to the question of whether the dependence of sensation on the stimulus, which can be inferred from experiments (in **outer psychophysics** <term>outer psychophysics: the study of the relationship between an external physical stimulus and the resulting sensation</term>), can be translated into a corresponding dependence of sensation on **psychophysical activity** (for **inner psychophysics** <term>inner psychophysics: the study of the relationship between sensation and the underlying physiological or neural activity</term>) (Section I.).

2) Regarding the first point, based on both external and my own experimental investigations, I have taken **Weber’s Law** <term>Weber’s Law: the principle that the smallest detectable change in a stimulus is a constant proportion of the original stimulus</term> (p. 8) as the basis for the dependence of sensory differences on relative stimulus differences. From this, by incorporating the **Threshold Law** <term>Threshold Law: the principle that a stimulus must reach a minimum intensity, the "threshold," before any sensation is perceived at all</term> (p. 7), I derived the **logarithmic law of measurement** <term>logarithmic law of measurement: also known as Fechner’s Law, it states that the intensity of a sensation increases as the logarithm of the stimulus intensity</term> (p. 8), along with several other consequential laws, through mathematical means. This was done, however, with the concession that the former law—Weber's—is confirmed experimentally (that is, regarding external stimuli) only approximately and within certain limits, specifically within the limits of the ordinary use of the senses; nor does it find unrestricted application for the entire field to be considered by psychophysics.

3) With this concession, in Sections XVI, XVII, and XVIII, I have presented the evidence that has emerged so far regarding Weber’s Law, particularly concerning the sensation of differences in light intensity, sound intensity, weight magnitude, and the strength of taste stimuli. While the experiments of **Helmholtz** and **Aubert** (p. 150) make the approximation of Weber's Law in the field of light sensation appear less precise than it seemed according to earlier experiments by others, they do not invalidate it. Furthermore, the experiments of **Delboeuf** (Section XVII) and **Dobrowolsky** (p. 159) provide all the more beautiful confirmations of the law in this

<summary>Fechner summarizes the theoretical foundations of his work, defending the use of Weber's Law and the Threshold Law to derive a logarithmic measurement of sensation while acknowledging their practical limits.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, Psychophysics, measurement of sensation, Threshold Law, logarithmic law, light intensity, Helmholtz, Delboeuf</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Weber's Law, specifically addressing empirical evidence from various researchers and defining the limits of the law's applicability to different types of sensation, such as space, time, color, and pitch.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>

domains; meanwhile, **Hering's** weight experiments (Section XVIII), although he himself argued against the law, align with my earlier ones in support of the law when properly considered. Similarly, **Keppler’s** taste experiments (p. 161), when correctly discussed, speak in its favor.

4) However, **Weber’s** law <term>Weber's Law: the principle that the smallest perceptible change in a stimulus is proportional to the initial intensity of that stimulus</term> can generally and reliably only be applied to differences in the *intensity* of sensation. Extensive spatial sensations only fall under it under a certain hypothetical assumption (p. 60 ff.), and extensive temporal sensations offer an approximation of it, though with very strong deviations at the lower end (p. 174 ff.). It has already been admitted in the *Elements* <note>Referring to Fechner's 1860 work, "Elements of Psychophysics"</note> that **Weber’s** law is not applicable to color differences, and this is shown even more clearly by the newer experiments of **Lamansky** and **Dobrowolsky** (p. 166). Meanwhile, the newer experiments of **Preyer**, contrary to previous assumptions, also make it appear inapplicable to differences in pitch (p. 168). Whether it applies to the **total intensity** of sensation—which depends simultaneously on amplitude *a* <term>amplitude: the maximum extent of a vibration or oscillation</term> and frequency *n* <term>frequency: the number of occurrences of a repeating event per unit of time; in this context, the number of vibrations per second</term>—remains an open question, as the evidence so far has focused essentially on changes in *a* while *n* remains constant (p. 170 and Section XIX.).

5) Ultimately, it remains just as certain that **Weber’s** law, with its derived laws, can provide a useful guide within certain limits and in several sensory domains for judging the proportional relationships of sensation based on its dependence on the stimulus—as it is certain that it cannot provide such a guide beyond certain limits or in application to every domain. Truly, there should be no dispute about this, even though the dispute has extended to this point. One can only argue about the degree and limits of the experimental approximation of the law and the reasons for its deviations, without being able to deny either the one or the other.

6) That **Weber’s** law was incorrectly inferred from the facts, and that a confused understanding of the conditions of the external world would emerge from it, as especially—

He argues that while the law has clear boundaries, it remains a fundamental tool for understanding the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensation.</summary>
<keywords>Weber's Law, intensity of sensation, spatial sensation, temporal sensation, color differences, pitch, amplitude, frequency, Hering, Preyer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his psychophysical theories, moving from the empirical validation of Weber’s Law to its deeper philosophical and physiological implications. It addresses criticisms from contemporaries like Hering, Delboeuf, and Mach regarding "negative sensations" and the distinction between external and internal psychophysics.</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>

...what is specifically claimed by **Hering** is just as little to be conceded (Sections V. and VII.), as the objections are to be granted which have been raised against the negative sensation values <note>Fechner used "negative sensations" to mathematically represent mental states that exist below the threshold of conscious perception.</note> to which the measurement formula leads, or against my interpretation of them, raised in particular by **Delboeuf** and **Langer** (Section X.).

7) However, the interest in knowing a functional relationship between stimulus and sensation by no means rests solely on its usability for a measure of sensation; rather, a much deeper interest, touching upon the more general doctrine of the relations between **body and soul**, attaches to the second main question (p. 211). This is because the knowledge of this relationship, drawn from experiments, provides a basis for conclusions about the functional relationship between sensation and the <term>psychophysical activity: the internal physical process, likely neural or chemical, that directly triggers a conscious sensation</term> directly underlying it, and thus about the fundamental dependence of the mental on the physical.

8) I myself now assume that not only is the **threshold law** <term>threshold law: the principle that a stimulus must reach a certain intensity before any sensation is perceived</term> transferable from external psychophysics to internal, but also that the validity of **Weber's law** points toward an exact and unlimited validity within **internal psychophysics**. While in <term>external psychophysics: the study of the relationship between an outer stimulus (like light or weight) and the resulting sensation</term> the law only holds approximately and within certain limits, I believe it holds perfectly for the relationship between sensation and the psychophysical activity directly underlying it. Consequently, sensation stands in a **logarithmic dependence** on psychophysical activity. The experimental deviations, however, are based on causes which diminish the proportionality of internal psychophysical activity with the stimulus the more the stimulus rises above middle limits, or the lower it sinks below them (Section V.).

Opposed to this (on the part of **Mach**, **Hering**, and others) is the view that sensation depends instead on a simple, direct relationship with psychophysical activity, and that the experimental approximation to **Weber’s law** depends instead on the fact that—within the same limits in which the law is approximately confirmed—the psychophysical activity itself is dependent on the stimulus in a logarithmic relationship (p. 71 ff.). I have (in Section VIII.) stated the reasons...

<summary>Fechner argues that while Weber's Law is only approximate in the physical world, it represents an exact logarithmic relationship between the soul and the internal workings of the body. He defends his "negative sensation" values against critics like Hering and Mach.</summary>

<keywords>internal psychophysics, external psychophysics, logarithmic relationship, body and soul, sensation threshold, Weber's Law, Hering, Mach, Delboeuf, psychophysical activity</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensation, specifically focusing on the "internal" psychophysical process—the activity within the brain or nervous system that corresponds to conscious experience. He is arguing against colleagues who disagree with his mathematical formulations of the "threshold."</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>

which lead me to decidedly prefer the first view, without, however, believing that the dispute is settled thereby.

9) Depending on one or the other of the two previous assumptions, the whole of **internal psychophysics** <term>internal psychophysics: the study of the relationship between the soul (mind) and the immediate physical activities of the nervous system</term> must take a different shape. In particular, it must be considered that with the assumption of a simple proportionality between sensation and psychophysical activity—which would eliminate an **internal threshold**—an appropriate representation of the **unconscious mental life** <note>Fechner argues that for a mind to have an "unconscious," there must be a mathematical "threshold" (Schwelle). If sensation were perfectly proportional to nerve activity without a cutoff point, every tiny vibration in the brain would result in a conscious feeling.</note> would be lacking. The importance of the question regarding whether the threshold can be transferred into the internal workings of the mind from this perspective is discussed on pages 70 and 85, and the factual reasons speaking for this transferability are asserted in Section IX.

10) The question remains as to how the fundamental measure of psychophysical activity is to be determined. In Section XX, it was shown that if one takes the **living force** <term>living force: (Latin: *vis viva*) a historical term for what we now call kinetic energy, or the energy of motion</term> of the movement as the measure, one can also substitute simple **velocity** for it, provided our formulas remain valid. However, it is possible that mental activities are fundamentally dependent on **changes in velocity** <note>In modern physics, this refers to acceleration.</note> rather than simple velocity in the sense of our formulas. In this case, considerations come into play that are validated in Section XX, according to which the latter view has a prevailing probability.

11) Between the **oscillation hypothesis** <note>The theory that nerve activity consists of microscopic vibrations or waves.</note> and the **chemical hypothesis** of psychophysical activity, there is no contradiction in itself, except that I (unlike **Hering**) can see in the chemical process merely the mediator of oscillations, upon which the sensations themselves are made dependent (Section XIII). — I am unable to share the **difference view** of sensations, according to which a sensation can exist only in distinction from other sensations (Section XII).

12) In place of the psychophysical laws and formulas established by me, others have been put forward by **Helmholtz**, **Aubert**, **Bernstein**, **Plateau**, **Brentano**, **Delboeuf**, **Hering**, and **Langer**, which, however, generally speaking, agree even less among themselves than they do with mine. **Helmholtz's** formulas are basically only an extension of my own, insofar as they incorporate the upper deviation from **Weber's Law** <note>Weber’s Law suggests that our ability to notice a change in a stimulus is proportional to the original intensity, but it often fails at extremely high intensities (the "upper deviation").</note> (p. 17); the law established by **Aubert** is...

<summary>Fechner defends his model of internal psychophysics, arguing that a "threshold" is necessary to explain unconscious mental states and discussing whether brain activity should be measured as energy, velocity, or acceleration.</summary>

<keywords>internal psychophysics, unconscious mental life, living force, kinetic energy, oscillation hypothesis, Weber's Law, Helmholtz, internal threshold</keywords>

--- Page 239 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s technical comparison of his psychophysical laws against those of his contemporaries. He argues that his model—specifically his inclusion of a "threshold"—is more robust than competing theories. This page concludes the main body of the work with a defiant postscript.</meta>

<page-num>215</page-num>

...was found by him himself to be only approximately correct within certain limits (p. 18), and both formulas can only be claimed for **outer psychophysics** <term>outer psychophysics: the study of the relationship between physical stimuli (like light or sound) and the resulting sensation</term>. The laws established by **Plateau** and **Brentano**, which essentially agree with one another, show themselves to be deficient simply because they do not leave room for the **threshold law** <term>threshold law: the principle that a stimulus must reach a minimum intensity before it is consciously perceived</term>, and they are further dismissed by **Plateau’s** own admission, as they were not confirmed by **Delboeuf's** experiments (p. 21 ff. and 24 ff.). The formulas of **Langer** deviate from my own based on viewpoints that I cannot find sufficiently grounded, either theoretically or empirically, according to pp. 37 ff. and 96 ff. To date, the most extensive developments from their authors have been found in the fundamental views of **Bernstein**, **Delboeuf**, and **Hering**, which deviate fundamentally from my own. The presentation of these views, together with my counterarguments against them, can be found regarding **Bernstein** on p. 20 and in Section XV, regarding **Delboeuf** on pp. 27 ff., and regarding **Hering** on p. 35 f. and in Section XIV.

## XXII. Postscript.

The Tower of Babel <note>original: "babylonische Thurm"; Fechner uses this biblical metaphor to suggest that while his critics all disagree with him, they also disagree with each other, preventing them from successfully overturning his theory</note> was not completed because the workmen could not understand one another regarding how they should build it; my psychophysical edifice should remain standing, because the workmen will not be able to agree on how they should tear it down.

---

<summary>Fechner concludes his treatise by dismissing competing psychophysical formulas and asserting that his "edifice" of thought will endure precisely because his critics cannot agree on a single alternative. This marks the final defense of his mathematical relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensation.</summary>

# XXIII. Some Corrections to the Elements of Psychophysics.

1) I have subsequently to report the following unnoted printing errors or oversights.

Vol. I. p. 166 line 8 from bottom: instead of green read gray.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 186 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; in Table II., first col. read 1500 instead of 1800.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 192 » 20 from top: after "row" insert "on the following page".
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 221 » 12 from top: instead of $\mu = 1$ read $\mu = 4$.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 221 » 4 from bottom: instead of 2908.5 read 2998.2.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 221 » 5 from bottom: instead of 1479.3 read 1563.0.
Vol. II. » 166 » 8 from bottom: instead of $an^2$ read $a^2n$.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 223 » 7 from bottom: the two terms of the expression for $S_t$ are still to be multiplied by $\partial t$.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 281 » 21 from top: after "Agreeing" insert "and Differing".
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 283 » 14 from top: instead of how read what <note>original: "wie l. was"</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 283 » 5 from bottom: instead of also read after <note>original: "auch l. nach"</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 330 » 12 from bottom: instead of some read more <note>original: "manche l. mehr"</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 347 » 17 from bottom: instead of 141 read 140.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 357 » 14 from top: instead of $\varepsilon'$ read $\varepsilon_{,}$.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 360 » 19 from top: instead of 2.728 read 2.718 <note>Fechner is correcting a value for the mathematical constant *e*, the base of natural logarithms</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 374 » 9 from bottom: instead of $S \Delta$ read $\Sigma \Delta$, and instead of $\Sigma \Delta$ read $S \Delta$.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;» 567 » 10 from bottom: instead of "of $\gamma$" read "of sensitivity" <note>original: "von $\gamma$ l. der Empfindlichkeit"</note>.

2) In the formulas of the first 3 lines of Vol. II. 213, the factor $\frac{2 \pi}{\tau}$ is missing before $G \cos \frac{2 \pi t}{\tau}$, and $\mathfrak{v}$ has a value of zero instead of a finite value. These oversights have no influence on what follows.

3) The correction of $\varepsilon$ or $\frac{\Sigma \Delta}{m}$ by $\frac{\pi m}{\pi m - 1}$ given in Vol. I. 126, Vol. II. 368 is (despite checking by a specialist mathematician) incorrectly derived and factually incorrect; it should instead be replaced by $\sqrt{\frac{m}{m-1}}$, or if $m$ <note>The variable *m* usually refers to the number of observations or measurements in Fechner's statistical work</note> is not too small

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s technical corrections to his 1860 work, *Elements of Psychophysics*. He is currently discussing the statistical formulas used to calculate the "mean variation" (a measure of how much data points differ from the average) and correcting errors in his previously published data tables on tactile sensitivity.</meta>

noticeably consistent with the expression $\frac{2 m}{2 m - 1}$ <note>Fechner is discussing a statistical correction factor used when a series of observations is divided into $m$ groups. This factor helps account for biases that arise when estimating variability from small samples.</note>. Decisive for this are the discussions and observations that I later shared in the *Reports of the Royal Saxon Society of Sciences* <note>original: "sächs. Soc." referring to the *Berichte der Königlich Sächsischen Gesellschaft der Wissenschaften zu Leipzig*</note> 1861, p. 57 ff., to which I could add as further confirmation the investigations of other series of observations undertaken since then.

However, a series of observations divided into different groupings ($m$) <term>Fractionirung</term> <note>The process of dividing a large set of data into smaller subsets or "fractions" to check for consistency.</note> can only be brought to consistent values of $\Sigma \Delta$ <note>The sum of the differences (deviations) from the mean.</note> through a correction factor if the so-called "constant error" of these fractions varies only within the limits of chance. This condition is not met, for example, by the series of tactile experiments <note>Experiments involving the sense of touch, often using a compass to test the distance at which two points are felt as one.</note> in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 358, which was extended over a very long period of time, as shown by an examination of its groupings.

In the aforementioned place (in the *Reports*) p. 93 ff., I have shown that the correction given in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 373 regarding the size of the intervals <note>This refers to the "class intervals" or bins used when grouping data into a frequency distribution.</note> is also incorrect. The correction substituted for it there is indeed correct in itself, but upon closer consideration, I have convinced myself that it is not general enough. It applies only to the case where the mean value of the observations coincides with the beginning or end of a division; if it coincides with the middle of such a division, the correction even has the opposite sign <note>Positive vs. negative (+/-).</note> than that of the previous position. And even though the correction for all positions can be captured in a general mathematical expression, I have not yet been able to derive a practically usable simple correction from it. Thus, for now, nothing remains but to make the intervals of the division small enough that any correction for them can be neglected.

4) The tables regarding tactile experiments on the forehead, in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 354, suffer from several small calculation errors <term>Rechenversehen</term>. While these do not have a significant impact on the utility of the tables, they are corrected in the following reproduction.

->*c*<-

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

Based on this, the values for $\varepsilon$, $\varepsilon'$, $q$ and $p$ <note>These symbols refer to various measures of "probable error" and "precision" used in 19th-century statistics to determine the reliability of experimental data.</note> calculated from the tables must also be corrected.

5) The value given in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 360 for a $\mathfrak{P}$ derived from a finite $m$ <note>The letter $m$ represents the number of groups or "fractions" into which the data has been divided.</note> is based on a false theoretical assumption. Instead, according to a theoretical and experimental investigation I conducted in the Anniversary Volume of *Poggendorff’s Annals* <note>original: "Poggend. Ann.", referring to the *Annalen der Physik und Chemie*</note>, p. 66 ff., one has:

->$\mathfrak{P} = \frac{2 m \Sigma (\Delta)^2}{(\Sigma \Delta)^2} = \frac{2 \pi (m-1)}{2 m + \pi - 4}$<-

This value transforms into $\pi$ <note>The mathematical constant pi (approx. 3.14159).</note> when $m$ is infinite ($m = \infty$), and into 2 when $m = 2$. This latter value can be found from the outset <note>original: "a priori"</note>, provided that for $m = 2$, both deviations ($\Delta$) are necessarily equal to each other, and consequently $(\Sigma \Delta)^2 = 2 \Sigma (\Delta)^2$. For intermediate values of $m$ between 2 and infinity, I have given experimental determinations for $\frac{\Sigma (\Delta)^2}{(\Sigma \Delta)^2}$—denoted there as $\frac{\mathcal{C}}{B}$—in the place cited <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angegebenen Orte)</note> (p. 81), which agree entirely with the theory.

6) In *Elements* Vol. II, p. 461 and 462, I viewed dreams from the perspective that the entire "main wave" or "total wave" of consciousness sinks below its threshold (the total threshold) during sleep, but that beneath this threshold, "upper waves" <term>Oberwellen</term> <note>Fechner uses a wave metaphor for mental energy; "upper waves" are specific thoughts or sensations riding on top of a broader "total wave" of general consciousness.</note> still play upon the low "under-wave" <term>Unterwelle</term>, rising above their own specific thresholds. This is undeniably not valid, insofar as consciousness must be extinguished entirely if the total wave sinks below the total threshold; yet dreams are still phenomena of consciousness. 

It would be more correct to say that in dreams, the under-wave—as the carrier of voluntarily shiftable attention—sinks below its threshold, as it seems appropriate to grant attention its own threshold just like the upper waves; meanwhile, the upper waves, as carriers of dream-images, still rise high enough so that the total wave projects beyond the total threshold. — In *Elements* Vol. II, p. 459 1/2, the level of attention active in a particular region was instead related to the height of the total wave rather than the under-wave specifically; however, the latter now appears more appropriate.

<summary>Fechner corrects a mathematical formula for statistical probability and revises his theory of consciousness during sleep, arguing that dreams occur because specific mental "waves" remain above the threshold of awareness even when voluntary attention has subsided.</summary>
<keywords>statistical correction, Poggendorff’s Annals, consciousness theory, threshold of consciousness, total wave, upper waves, attention, dream theory, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s technical corrections to his work "Elements of Psychophysics." Here, he moves from purely statistical corrections to refining the conceptual "wave schema" he uses to describe the rise and fall of consciousness and mental activity.</meta>

If one, in order to have a certain foothold in reality for the entire schematic way of representation introduced in Section 42 of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Gustav Fechner's foundational 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik*.</note>, compares the composition of the **base wave** <term>Unterwelle: the primary wave representing a general state of consciousness</term> and **harmonic waves** <term>Oberwelle: smaller, faster waves representing specific thoughts or sensations</term> into a **total wave** with the combination of a periodic motion of a large period with periodic motions of small periods, one must nonetheless not forget that what is composed in the schema as the height of the base wave and harmonic wave into the height of the total wave is not to be referred to height values (or depressions, respectively) of the ordinates of the *shape* of waves. Rather, it merely expresses, in a schematic way, magnitudes of **kinetic energy** <term>lebendige Kraft: literally "living force," a 19th-century term for kinetic energy ($mv^2$)</term> with which the periodic movements take place (insofar as one wishes to seek the measure of **psychophysical activity** in **kinetic energy**). 

The **kinetic energies** of two periodic movements of unequal periods are, however, additive (see Grailich in the *Vienna Session Reports* 1854 <note>Joseph Wilhelm Grailich (1833–1859), an Austrian physicist and crystallographer.</note>), and thus the harmonic waves are indeed to be imagined as wave crests merely *elevated* above the base wave, but not reaching into it with troughs; meanwhile, above the base wave, they can sink *below* their *own threshold* <note>Fechner’s "threshold" (Schwelle) is the point at which a stimulus or mental activity becomes perceptible to the mind.</note>. These relationships, too, are not clearly—and thus not validly—presented in *Elements* Vol. II, p. 456 f., though the conclusions attached to the wave schema are not affected in any essential points by this lack of validity.

<summary>Fechner clarifies his "wave schema" of consciousness, explaining that mental "waves" represent levels of kinetic energy that add together, rather than physical shapes with peaks and troughs.</summary>
<keywords>wave schema, kinetic energy, psychophysical activity, threshold, base wave, harmonic wave, Grailich</keywords>
